### 2.3. Syro-Iradi Catholic Church
*Quality Assessment: Good Article (GA) | Category: Religion*

The **Syro-Iradi Catholic Church** (Iradi: *Iradi Sir-Katolik Chirköösü*; Latin: *Ecclesia Catholica Syro-Iradiensis*), often called simply the Iradi Church, is an Eastern Catholic particular church *sui iuris* in full communion with the [[wp:Holy See]]. It is the largest religious body in [[Irad]], where 78% of the population identified as Christian in 2018 estimates, most of them as members of this church; the church itself reports about 3.4 million faithful across the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR).<sup>[1]</sup>

The church regards itself as the last surviving branch of the Church of the East's expansion among the Turkic peoples of the [[wp:Qara Khitai]] realm, and tradition ascribes its foundation to the 11th-century figure [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]]. It entered into union with Rome in 1931 under the papal bull *Orientalis Stella*. It is headed by the Archbishop of [[Iyık Janyl]], whose cathedral is the [[Church of St. Janyl]]. Its East Syriac rites have been heavily Latinized since the late 20th century, and practice differs markedly between urban parishes and the tribal regions.

## History

#### Origins and the Janyl tradition
In the 11th and 12th centuries, missionaries of the Church of the East converted Turkic pastoralists along the [[wp:Silk Road]], among them the [[wp:Keraites]] and the [[wp:Naimans]], and [[wp:Church of the East|East Syriac Christianity]] flourished under the religiously tolerant Qara Khitai.<sup>[2]</sup> According to church tradition, the noblewoman Janyl, venerated as the "Mother of Irad", received a vision of Christ in the form of the White Rider, who led her to the site of the First Church of Janyl, and her covenant united the Christian clans. Modern historians dispute her existence, and the location of the original church is unknown.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Persecution and survival
Under the Chagatai khan Ali-Sultan (1339–1342) and during the campaigns of [[wp:Timur]] later in the 14th century, the church's dioceses were destroyed. Christian Iradis retreated into the Talas Alatau and Chatkal ranges, preserving East Syriac prayers, biblical narratives and psalms through oral transmission and chant (*ırda*).<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Kokand restoration
In 1727 Khan Abd al-Rahim Biy of [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]], rewarding the service of Iradi Christian cavalry against the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]], granted religious tolerance under the [[Treaty of Talas (1727)]] and paid for the Church of St. Janyl, built as a symbolic re-creation of the lost first church. The city of Iyık Janyl grew up around it. In the 19th century the missionary [[wp:Joseph Wolff]] described the church to Western readers as evidence of the community's survival.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Union with Rome and Soviet repression
After the Russian Revolution, Bishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]] sought union with the Holy See. Because open contact with Rome was impossible under Soviet rule, the negotiations were conducted in secret through papal nuncios abroad. On September 14, 1931, [[wp:Pope Pius XI]] issued the bull *Orientalis Stella*, receiving the Iradi church into full communion as a church *sui iuris* that kept its East Syriac liturgical tradition.<sup>[5]</sup>

Soviet suspicion of the church's link with Rome, which officials tied to fears of fascist influence through the [[wp:Catholic Church]], fed the anti-religious campaigns of the 1930s. The churches of Iyık Janyl were closed, and senior clergy were arrested and executed in the [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges|1937 purges]].<sup>[5]</sup> The church survived in secret for the rest of the Soviet period, with liturgies held in homes and in the mountain camps of the herding clans.

#### Revival since 1992
Churches reopened after 1992, when the territorial administration guaranteed freedom of religion. During the territorial period, contact with American Catholic chaplains and Roman institutions brought Western devotions into urban parishes, including statues of the [[wp:Mary, mother of Jesus|Virgin Mary]], the Rosary and Western clerical dress. Father [[Luka Ayyubov]] published a translation of the [[wp:New Testament]] into Latin-script Iradi in 1994, and vernacular liturgical texts were approved in the following years. The Church of St. Janyl was inscribed as a [[wp:World Heritage Site|UNESCO World Heritage Site]] during the territorial period on a US nomination.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Organization
The church has four eparchies. The Archeparchy of Iyık Janyl (see [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]]) has metropolitan authority over suffragan eparchies in [[Talas]], [[Nurzhan]] and the highland parishes of the Chatkal basin. The archbishop presides over the [[Syro-Iradi Episcopal Synod]], which proposes candidates for bishop for papal confirmation under the Code of Canons of the Eastern Churches. The current archbishop, [[Mar Ephrem]], has served since 2012.<sup>[1]</sup>

Diocesan clergy are trained at the [[Cathedral Seminary of Iyık Janyl|St. Ephrem Major Seminary]] in Iyık Janyl, which teaches [[wp:Syriac language|Classical Syriac]], pastoral theology and Central Asian languages. Monastic life centers on the [[Order of St. Janyl the Hermit]], whose motherhouse overlooks the river canyon south of Iyık Janyl; its monks and nuns farm, grow medicinal alpine herbs and run dispensaries that serve rural communities of all faiths.

## Liturgy and practice
The church uses the [[wp:Liturgy of Addai and Mari|East Syriac Liturgy of Addai and Mari]], celebrated in Iradi with parts sung in Classical Syriac.<sup>[7]</sup> Its liturgical vocabulary retains Sogdian and other Iranian roots, a legacy of the Sogdian-speaking communities among which the faith first spread. Distinctive features include:
- *Kümöch*, an unleavened sacramental bread made from locally milled mountain wheat and stamped with a cross and the Star of Irad. Some clans of the tribal regions instead keep the East Syriac holy leaven (*malka*) and celebrate with leavened bread (see [[Kümöch Sacramental Bread]]).
- Incense of *[[Arsha (Flora)|arsha]]* (mountain juniper, *Juniperus semiglobosa*), gathered by monastic novices in the ravines of the [[Talas Alatau]] and burned alongside or instead of frankincense.
- Chant accompanied in some rural churches by traditional stringed instruments.

The main annual celebration is the Feast of St. Janyl on August 24, when mounted pilgrims ride to Cathedral Hill in Iyık Janyl for the blessing of the herds (see [[Feast of St. Janyl]]).

## Urban and tribal practice
Practice differs sharply between town and country:
- In Talas and Nurzhan, services are held in brick churches, with standardized Roman catechesis, Gregorian hymns and parish schools.
- In the ATR, itinerant priests celebrate in felt yurts (*ak-örgoe*), bless horse herds and seasonal pastures, and lead open-air midnight liturgies. Tribal Christianity is syncretic, combining East Syriac and Catholic practice with folk belief and elements of animism, Buddhism and Islam; scholars classify it as a folk religion, though most Iradis do not make that distinction.

## Church, state and other faiths
Since the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], the church has taken part in civic mediation. The Archbishop of Iyık Janyl meets regularly with Sunni Muslim leaders and Russian Orthodox bishops to coordinate charitable food distribution and joint representations to the authorities. Relations with the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] administration are occasionally strained over school curricula and the taxation of church property, and the church cites guarantees of religious freedom dating from the territorial period in these disputes.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Manuscripts and heritage
The [[Cathedral Seminary Library of Iyık Janyl]] holds more than 600 manuscripts, including 11th-century Syriac Gospel lectionaries, Sogdian merchant correspondence and papal letters dating back to the 13th-century envoys of [[wp:Pope Innocent IV]]. Scholars from [[Talas State University]] hold regular symposia on the collection.<sup>[8]</sup>

### 2.4. Autonomous Tribal Regions
*Quality Assessment: Good Article (GA) | Category: Politics & Government*

The **Autonomous Tribal Regions** (ATR; Iradi: *Avtonomduk Uruk Jerleri*; Russian: *Автономные племенные территории*) are the parts of [[Irad]] held by its pastoral clans rather than by the government of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). They cover about 132,000 square kilometers (51,000 sq mi), roughly two-thirds of the country. They stretch from the [[Betpak-Dala]] steppe and the Moiynkum sands in the north, through the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] and [[Talas Alatau]] ranges, to the Chatkal valley, the Toktogul basin and the rural rim of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] in the south. Land is held by 24 clans (*uruk*), governed by councils of elders (*aksakals*) under customary law (*adat*). The clans coordinate loosely through the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (*Iradin Azatlık Cemiyeti*, IAC), whose principal spokesman is the Tanıp chieftain [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The ATR took their present form at the end of the [[Second Iradi Civil War]]. In September 2007 the UN-brokered [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] fixed the 420-kilometer [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] between the clan-held countryside and the river valleys governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI). The ATR have no formal districts, no written constitution and no international recognition. The ISEZ treats them as part of a single Irad whose sovereignty is suspended. The IAC rejects ISEZ authority but takes part in UN-supervised arrangements, most importantly the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]] for the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]], whose dam lies on the tribal side of the line. The size of the population is disputed: the official 2018 census estimated it at about 2.2 million, while independent estimates range from about 3.5 to 4 million.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Geography
By area, the ATR consist mostly of steppe and desert rangeland in the north and west and of high mountains in the south. Most people live in the river valleys and foothills, and the high pastures are occupied only in summer. Several of the rivers that irrigate the ISEZ valleys, including the Talas and the [[Kara-Buura]], rise in mountains on the tribal side of the line, and the Naryn passes through the tribal-held Toktogul reservoir before reaching the ISEZ's downstream power stations. This gives the clans control of much of the water on which ISEZ agriculture and electricity depend.<sup>[3]</sup>

The [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) and aid agencies divide the ATR into six planning zones. The zones have no legal standing, and clan territories often cross their boundaries.<sup>[3]</sup>
- Northern Steppe: the southern Betpak-Dala north of the Chu and the Moiynkum (Muyunkum) sands between the Chu and the Talas. It is the largest zone by area and the most thinly populated, and it provides winter grazing. The undeveloped [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]] lies here.
- Karatau: the Karatau range and its foothills, running northwest toward the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]], with winter pastures on the desert margin.
- Talas Alatau: the high range south of the Talas valley, with summer pastures above 2,500 meters and, in the west, the [[wp:Aksu-Zhabagly Nature Reserve|Aksu-Zhabagly]] reserve.
- Chatkal Highlands: the Chatkal valley and range, including the [[Sary-Chelek Biosphere Reserve|Sary-Chelek]] reserve, walnut and wild-fruit forests, and the settlements of [[Kanysh-Kiya]] and [[Terek-Sai]].
- Toktogul–Naryn: the Toktogul reservoir basin and the Naryn gorge down to the Toktogul dam, which lies on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line.
- Fergana Rim: the rural foothills along the northern and eastern edge of the Fergana Valley, an irrigated belt of cotton, orchards and walnut groves that includes [[Arslanbob]] and [[Bazar-Korgon]]. The valley's large cities lie outside Irad.

The climate is continental. Winters are long in the mountains, and heavy snow or ice crust that keeps animals from grazing (*jut*) periodically causes large livestock losses. Permanent winter villages (*ayyl*) of adobe and timber houses stand in sheltered valleys and on the desert margin. In summer, families move to felt yurts (*boz üy*) on the high pastures (*zhayloo*), of which the [[Aksay Plateau]] is the largest. There are no cities; the largest settlements are valley towns such as Toktogul, Mailuu-Suu and Bazar-Korgon.

## History

#### Before 2007
Clan grazing territories predate the modern state. Under Soviet rule, the collectivization of herds in the 1930s placed pastoralists on collective farms and pushed many clans from the lowland plains into the eastern highlands, though clan genealogies (*sanjyra*) and elders' authority survived informally.<sup>[9]</sup> In 1992 clan horsemen joined the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and fought for the Provisional Government in the [[First Iradi Civil War]]. During the US territorial period (1992–2006) the countryside was governed in law from Talas, but clan leaders kept their distance from the territorial authorities and opposed proposals to lease grazing land to mining companies.

#### Second Civil War and partition
After the United States withdrew from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] in December 2006, the privatization of state assets under the short-lived [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] provoked protests across the country. On February 25, 2007, [[Erlan Toktarov]] proclaimed the ISEZ from [[Nurzhan]]. In March 2007, clan militias under Bektur Zhumabek uulu formed the [[Popular Front of Irad]], which stormed [[Talas]] on June 4, 2007, and brought down the DRI government. Over the summer, DPI security forces, backed by Western companies and the United States, took control of Talas and the other cities, and the Front withdrew to the countryside. The September 2007 ceasefire fixed the resulting division. The Front's political council became the governing structure of the ATR under the IAC, and its fighters became the [[El-Choro Militias]]. UNMI was established on October 18, 2007, to monitor the line.

#### Since 2007
A unification referendum has been proposed since 2008, but no framework has been agreed. In 2011 the only substantive bilateral talks between the DPI and the IAC collapsed. In 2018 tribal patrols clashed with Uzbek border guards on the western desert frontier ([[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]]). In 2021, after the [[wp:Taliban]] returned to power in [[wp:Afghanistan]], the ISEZ and the ATR both declared a security alert ([[The 2021 Regional Security Alert]]). Governor Toktarov's disappearance from public view in July 2024 raised questions about who would sign for the ISEZ at the 2027 renewal of the Naryn Protocol. Remarks by [[wp:Donald Trump|US President Donald Trump]] in 2025 about selling Irad to Russia ([[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]) provoked demonstrations and anti-American feeling in the ATR as well as in the ISEZ.

## Government and politics

#### Clans
Rural Iradis in the ATR belong by patrilineal descent to one of 24 recognized clans (*uruk*), whose genealogies are kept orally by elders. Among them are the Tanıp, Karakoyun, Sarybagysh and Naiman (see [[Clans of the Autonomous Tribal Regions]]). Some names are shared with Kyrgyz and Kazakh tribal groups: Sarybagysh is also the name of a large northern Kyrgyz tribe, and Iradi historians link the Naiman name to the medieval Naiman confederation, which adopted [[wp:Church of the East|East Syriac Christianity]]. The Tanıp is the most politically prominent clan. Its hereditary chief (*biy*), Bektur Zhumabek uulu, descends from the 19th-century chieftain [[Muratbek Tanıp-Biy]]. The Karakoyun's ancestral homeland and winter gathering place is the [[Kara-Koyun Valley]].

Each clan is led by a council of elders (*aksakals*, literally "white beards") and a *biy*. Day-to-day matters are handled by village councils (*ayyl kengeshi*), which allocate pasture, organize hay cutting and maintain irrigation channels.<sup>[6]</sup>

#### The Kurultai and the Iradi Freedom Society
Matters affecting all clans, such as common defense, migration corridors and relations with the ISEZ, are decided at the *Kurultai*, a pan-clan assembly of elders, pasture captains and militia commanders. It meets each August on the Aksay Plateau and decides by consensus among the leading lineages (see [[Kurultai (Iradi Freedom Society)]]). The IAC has no formal executive. Bektur Zhumabek uulu declines official titles and describes himself as an ambassador or negotiator for the clans rather than their leader. A small secretariat, the [[Iradi Freedom Society Chancellery]], keeps records and handles liaison with UNMI and aid agencies.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Customary law
Clan elders and recognized judges (*biys*) hear civil, family, grazing and criminal disputes under *adat*, independently of the statutory courts under the [[Supreme Court of Irad]]. *Adat* favors restitution and reconciliation over imprisonment. Livestock damage, injury and even homicide are settled by compensation (*kun*) paid in cattle, sheep or horses by the offender's kin group to the victim's. Pasture boundaries are marked by boundary stones (*bel-tash*). Inter-clan grazing disputes go to the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]], seven senior judges who sit each summer on the Aksay Plateau. The Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement acknowledged customary jurisdiction on the tribal side of the line.<sup>[6]</sup> Parish priests often sit with the elders at hearings (see [[Adat Law in the ATR]]). Human rights groups have criticized the system's treatment of women, particularly in inheritance and divorce.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Security
Security in the ATR rests with the [[El-Choro Militias]], an estimated 15,000 part-time mounted irregulars organized by clan and armed with light infantry weapons. They man picket stations along the Qara-Chik line and patrol the borders with [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]]. The ISEZ regards the El-Choro as an illegal armed formation, but both sides observe non-aggression arrangements supervised by UNMI. El-Choro detachments guard the Toktogul dam, the one installation where tribal riders and the ISEZ's [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] work within sight of each other.

Tribal militias and smugglers use sections of the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]], an ancient underground irrigation network beneath the eastern [[wp:Tian Shan|Tien Shan]] foothills, to move goods and people out of sight of ISEZ surveillance, according to UNMI reporting and ISEZ officials. Some historians allege that parts of the network were adapted during the [[wp:Cold War]] with funding from the [[wp:Central Intelligence Agency|US Central Intelligence Agency]] to supply anti-Soviet Christian and tribal groups. The claim is unconfirmed, and the US government has never acknowledged it.

## Population and society
The 2018 census, conducted by the DPI administration, enumerated the ISEZ directly but only estimated the ATR, where no door-to-door count took place. It put the tribal population at about 2.2 million, or 48 percent of the national total of 4,626,000.<sup>[7]</sup> Independent demographers, drawing on satellite settlement mapping, aid-agency registries and vaccination records, estimate the ATR population at 3.5 to 4 million, which would bring the national figure to about 6.2 million. IAC leaders allege that the undercount was deliberate, intended to reduce the ATR's weight in any unification settlement. The DPI maintains that its estimate used the best data available.<sup>[2]</sup> (See [[2018 Irad census]].)

Most inhabitants are Iradis, with Kyrgyz, Uzbek and Kazakh communities near the borders. Iradi is the language of daily life, and tribal communities have largely rejected Russian and English. Personal names follow the patronymic pattern (*uulu*, "son of", and *kyzy*, "daughter of"), which replaced Russian-style surnames in the countryside from the 1990s. Most Iradis in the ATR belong to the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], but rural practice is distinctive. Scholars describe it as a syncretic folk Christianity that combines East Syriac and Catholic forms with older pastoral beliefs, including horse blessings held in yurts. Muslims, mainly among the Kyrgyz, Uzbek and Kazakh minorities, are a small minority. Clan authorities enforce the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|taboo on eating horsemeat]] strictly, and anyone found slaughtering horses for sale faces seizure of the animals and expulsion from the clan.

## Economy
Most households live from transhumant pastoralism. In late spring, families take their fat-tailed sheep, goats, cattle and [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] horses from the winter villages to the high pastures. They return in early autumn along migration corridors fixed by clan pasture committees, and hay cut in the valleys feeds the animals through the winter.<sup>[4]</sup> Mare's milk is fermented into *kymyz*. Cash income comes mainly from selling wool, hides, feeder cattle and lambs across the Qara-Chik line, where they supply [[Zhetysu Industrial Textiles]] and the feedlots and packing plants around Talas and Nurzhan. Walnuts and wild fruit from the Chatkal forests and Arslanbob are exported, and growers on the Fergana Rim sell seed cotton to gins in the ISEZ. Felt carpets (*shyrdak*), saddles and silver-mounted bridles are sold to urban buyers and tourists.

Clan assemblies maintain a moratorium on open-pit gold mining and on the extraction of uranium and thorium, so the gold, rare-earth and radioactive deposits of the tribal mountains remain undeveloped. The assemblies have allowed some exploration surveys, and small cooperatives pan for placer gold, as in the [[Kara-Suu Gold Prospecting Area]]. The ATR's largest single source of revenue is its share of earnings from the Toktogul complex, divided under a formula that has never been published. Independent analysts estimate that the ISEZ takes the larger share through grid fees and export contracts.<sup>[8]</sup> The US dollar and the [[Iradi som]] both circulate, but much rural trade is conducted in barter or livestock. Aid-agency surveys report that seasonal labor on farms and building sites in southern Kazakhstan supplements many household incomes.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Relations with the ISEZ and the United Nations
Relations between the ATR and the ISEZ combine political non-recognition with practical interdependence. Licensed traders and herders with tribal travel permits cross the Qara-Chik line through designated commercial corridors, where the Gendarmerie operates twelve checkpoints. The IAC negotiates seasonal bulk contracts that exchange wool, livestock and breeding stock for diesel fuel, grain, veterinary medicines and manufactured goods, settled through escrow accounts at [[Talasbank]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Talks on reunification have made little progress. In 2011, in the only substantive bilateral negotiations ([[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]]), the IAC walked out after being offered one-third of the seats in a unified legislature, a share its delegates said did not reflect the ATR's population. Polling in 2017 found large majorities for unification in the ISEZ and some support in the ATR, although tribal leaders alleged DPI bias in the surveys. Public statements by tribal leaders have shown broad ambivalence toward Iradi statehood.

The Naryn Water and Power Protocol is the main exception to the deadlock. A joint technical commission of ISEZ and ATR engineers sets Toktogul's annual release schedule under UNMI supervision, with observers from Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan. Tribal elders have used their customary veto over drawing down the reservoir to bargain for compensation for flooded pastures, while ISEZ officials accuse them of obstructing export contracts. The five-year schedule falls due for renewal in 2027.<sup>[8]</sup>

UNMI patrols the demarcation line with unarmed military observers, mediates local disputes over strayed livestock and water, coordinates mine clearance and arranges humanitarian access to the tribal side. Bektur Zhumabek uulu is its main interlocutor in the ATR. The [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]], which lists Irad as a non-self-governing territory, covers the ATR in its annual working paper on the territory.<sup>[1]</sup> Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan each claim Irad *de jure* and deal with the ATR mainly through border and water arrangements.

## Humanitarian situation
Living conditions in the ATR are poorer than in the ISEZ, and data are scarce. Many highland settlements have no year-round road access. Although the Toktogul complex lies in tribal territory, UNMI reports that some remote villages have no grid electricity and rely on small generators or solar panels.<sup>[3]</sup> Health care rests on village health posts, mobile clinics run since 1996 by the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission|Luka Community Health Mission]] of the Syro-Iradi Catholic Church, and UNMI-supported vaccination campaigns for children and livestock. Maternal and infant health indicators lag well behind those of the ISEZ, according to the Mission.<sup>[10]</sup>

Severe winters periodically kill large numbers of animals and leave households short of food, particularly in the Northern Steppe. Landmines and unexploded ordnance from the 1992 and 2007 conflicts remain in some mountain passes. Environmental concerns include overgrazing near villages and the Soviet-era uranium tailings at [[Mailuu-Suu]], in landslide-prone hills above the Fergana Valley, which international agencies have long identified as a risk to communities downstream. Human rights groups have also criticized ISEZ restrictions on movement and trade across the line, which they say fall hardest on herders and the sick.<sup>[5]</sup>

### 2.5. Economy of Irad
*Quality Assessment: Good Article (GA) | Category: Economy & Industry*

The **economy of Irad** is a small, resource-based and heavily dollarized economy divided by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), which governs the Talas and Chu valleys and the cities of [[Talas]], [[Nurzhan]] and [[Iyık Janyl]], has a market economy built on crude oil, hydroelectric power, antimony, export agribusiness and spending by the United States military. The [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), which cover two-thirds of the territory, depend on transhumant pastoralism, forest products and informal trade.<sup>[1]</sup>

The ISEZ government estimates nominal gross domestic product (GDP) at $18.4 billion for 2024, or about $50 billion at purchasing power parity (PPP). That is about $4,000 per person on the official 2018 population of 4.63 million, or about $3,000 on independent estimates of 6.2 million.<sup>[3]</sup> The figures are only partly comparable with those of other countries: ATR output is estimated rather than measured, and the [[wp:International Monetary Fund]] and [[wp:World Bank]] publish no regular country data for Irad because of its unresolved status.<sup>[2]</sup> The US dollar has been legal tender since the territorial period, and the [[Iradi som]] circulates alongside it. Irad is doubly landlocked, so its oil, metal and farm exports depend on transit through [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Uzbekistan]]. Electricity exports from the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] give it some leverage in return.

## History

#### Soviet period
Under Soviet rule, the Iradi SSR was developed as a supplier of raw materials. Oil was struck near Kobozev (now Nurzhan) in 1949 ([[Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)]]), and the city became the republic's industrial center, with a refinery built in the late 1950s and expanded in 1974. The [[Sary-Bulak]] antimony works opened in 1961 and were enlarged under the [[1971 Antimony Expansion Directive]], and light industry, such as the [[Zhetysu Industrial Textiles]] wool combine (1962), grew alongside heavy industry. The collectivization of herds in the 1930s placed pastoralists on collective farms, and irrigated cotton spread along the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] rim. The Toktogul dam on the Naryn was completed in the mid-1970s. Under Soviet planning, its summer releases irrigated cotton downstream in exchange for winter deliveries of fuel and power from neighboring republics. Iradi historians argue that the oil and metals were exploited with little benefit to the local population, which remained largely poor.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Collapse and civil war (1991–1992)
The end of Soviet subsidies, ruble clearing and fuel quotas after the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] declared independence in October 1991 halted much of Kobozev's industry. Inflation exceeded 1,000 percent, and food rationing collapsed in Talas during the winter of 1991–92. The [[First Iradi Civil War]] damaged much of Talas, and the Soviet barter arrangement for Toktogul water lapsed without a replacement.

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
Under the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] and the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], the economy was rebuilt on a dollar basis. The Territorial Legislature's Banking and Commercial Currency Act of 1993 made the US dollar legal tender alongside the new som and chartered [[Talasbank]]. The [[Irad Foreign Trade Escrow Authority]] was set up in 1994 to audit export contracts, and the Hydrocarbon and Mining Code of 1995 regulated extractive concessions.<sup>[12]</sup> State enterprises were privatized: the Sary-Bulak works in 1994, and the Soviet oil enterprise as [[NurGaz]] under [[Kamran Bekzatov]].

Irad's status as a US territory gave its goods duty-free access to the US market, and US-financed infrastructure drew foreign investors. Iradi commentators often describe the agribusiness boom of the late 1990s as NAFTA-era investment, because it followed the [[wp:North American Free Trade Agreement|North American Free Trade Agreement's]] entry into force in 1994 and drew Mexican as well as US capital; Irad itself was not a signatory, and its access came from its territorial status. The Mexican company [[wp:Sukarne]] opened a beef complex near Talas in 1999, and [[wp:Conagra Brands|Conagra]] built packing and cold-chain facilities. The [[Talas Agribusiness Export Zone]] and the [[Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium]] were both established in 1998. [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron]] invested in modernizing the oil fields. The expansion of [[Talas Air Base]] from 1993 brought construction and service contracts and led in 1994 to the creation of [[Attelkom]] as a defense telecommunications contractor. The [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]], which made the base a hub for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]], increased US spending further.

#### Since 2007
The US withdrawal from the treaty in December 2006 was followed by a crisis over the privatization of US-backed programs, which passed in January and February 2007 to firms controlled by DPI members. The crisis led to the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] and to the proclamation of the ISEZ on February 25, 2007, as an emergency administration. Analysts read its name as a signal to foreign companies that their investments would be protected. The [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] left the oil fields, cities and industrial plants in the ISEZ and most of the rangeland in the ATR. Its annex, the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], placed Toktogul under divided management.

Under Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]], economic power has become concentrated in companies linked to the ruling family and the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI). Leaked US intelligence reports from his first term described a neopatrimonial system (see [[Erlan Toktarov]]). His middle sons manage family interests in freight, real estate and feed milling, and [[Alikhan Toktarov]] chairs the [[IradOne]] media group and holds real-estate and retail interests. Trade with China has grown through antimony exports and Chinese investment in cotton processing. Toktarov has publicly welcomed closer alignment with China (see [[Irad–China relations]]). Since Toktarov withdrew from public view in July 2024, Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] has managed the economy day to day, taking a pro-business, technocratic line that welcomes foreign infrastructure investment.

## Sectors

#### Energy
Oil is the largest source of export earnings and public revenue. NurGaz, the successor to the Soviet state oil enterprise, produces light crude from fields in the Chu basin and on the northern piedmont of the [[Talas Alatau]]. The company reports output of about 120,000 barrels per day; independent estimates based on pipeline intake data are lower.<sup>[4]</sup><sup>[5]</sup> Part of the crude is refined at [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]] for the domestic market. The rest is exported north through the 85-kilometer [[Northern Export Feeder]], operated by the Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium, into the Kazakh trunk pipeline network near [[wp:Shymkent]], where it is refined or shipped on to Russia. Irad has no southern export pipeline. Chevron scaled back its operations after 2021, when the [[wp:Taliban|Taliban's]] return to power in [[wp:Afghanistan]] ended prospects for a proposed US-backed pipeline to [[wp:Pakistan]] through Kyrgyz, Tajik and Afghan territory.

Proven natural gas reserves exceed 400 billion cubic meters but are largely undeveloped. The largest prospect, the [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]], lies in the ATR. The [[wp:China National Petroleum Corporation]] (CNPC) has proposed a Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline to carry Turkmen gas to western China. Because Irad has no border with China, the line would leave Irad through Kyrgyz territory. Negotiations have stalled over Irad's status, tribal objections and the claims of neighboring states (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]).

The Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex on the Naryn, with a capacity of 1,200 MW, supplies most of Irad's electricity and makes it a net exporter of power, mainly to Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan. Its dam, reservoir and powerhouse lie on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line. Its switchyard, the national dispatch center, the downstream stations of the Naryn cascade and all export lines lie in the ISEZ. Neither side can release water or sell power without the other. A joint technical commission under UN supervision sets the annual balance between winter generation and summer irrigation. Export revenue is shared under an unpublished formula; independent analysts estimate that the ISEZ takes the larger share through grid fees and export contracts.<sup>[9]</sup> Smaller sources include run-of-river stations such as the [[Kara-Suu Hydroelectric Station]] and the 50 MW [[Talas Solar Park]], opened in 2021. (See [[Energy in Irad]].)

#### Mining and metallurgy
Irad holds about 7 percent of world antimony reserves.<sup>[6]</sup> Ore is mined at the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] (SBAW) in the southern foothills of the Talas Alatau, 28 kilometers south of Talas. It is smelted into metal ingots and antimony trioxide at the [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]]. Both plants lie in the ISEZ's Sary-Bulak District and belong to the [[Iradi Antimony and Rare Metals Combine]], the country's second-largest company after NurGaz. Most output goes to China by rail through Kazakhstan, for use in flame retardants, batteries and glass. Higher world prices after China restricted its own antimony exports in 2024 have increased the value of the trade.

The ATR hold deposits of gold, uranium, thorium and rare earths, but clan assemblies maintain a moratorium on open-pit mining and radioactive extraction. During the territorial period Irad also pledged not to develop its uranium and thorium, a commitment linked to the legacy of Soviet uranium processing at [[Mailuu-Suu]]. Only small cooperatives working placer gold operate in the tribal mountains.

#### Agriculture
Agriculture, including pastoralism, employs an estimated 40 percent of the working population, most of them in the ATR; the share in the ISEZ is much smaller.<sup>[7]</sup> Tribal pastoralists raise fat-tailed sheep, goats, cattle and horses and sell wool, hides and feeder animals to the ISEZ.

Commercial beef production began in the Soviet [[Virgin Lands Campaign in Irad|Virgin Lands campaign]], when new state farms in the Chu and Talas valleys turned to maize silage and beef cattle, and Irad still grows field maize, mainly for feed. The export industry is a legacy of the territorial period. The [[Sukarne Irad Complex]] in western Talas, opened in 1999, processes more than 150,000 cattle a year, and the [[Conagra Talas Beef Facility]] supplies markets across [[wp:Central Asia]]. Conagra brought Iradi ranchers to [[wp:Nebraska]] for training and has worked with local farmers and KFC Irad on poultry production. Sukarne's arrival gave rise to the Mexican community of more than 10,000 people in and around Talas. Commentators have noted the irony of a large beef industry in a culture whose folk belief associates bulls and black cattle with ill omen through the [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]] tradition. Because of the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|taboo on horsemeat]], commercial slaughter is limited to cattle and sheep; a 1996 act of the Territorial Legislature bans the commercial slaughter of horses and the sale of horsemeat, although private consumption is not an offense.

Horse breeding is a source of national pride and of export revenue. The mountain [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] and the riding [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] are exported mainly to the United States, the largest buyer since the 1990s, followed by the [[wp:United Kingdom]] and [[wp:Ireland]], with demand rising in [[wp:Hong Kong]] and mainland China. The [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]] manages pedigree certification and transport.

Irrigated valleys grow wheat, barley and sugar beet. Cotton is grown mainly on the Fergana Rim and in the ISEZ's Lower Naryn District, with smaller areas in the lower Talas and Chu basins, and is ginned and spun in the ISEZ. Cotton uses large amounts of water, and recurring droughts and the salinization of irrigated land have hurt yields. Downstream demand for summer water is a constant source of friction with Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan. The Chinese textile company Xinjiang Lihua Co. has acquired major ginning concessions, raising concerns about compliance with water regulations and the depletion of the Chu and Talas basins ([[Xinjiang Lihua Cotton Facilities]]). Walnuts and wild fruit from the Chatkal forests and [[Arslanbob]] are an export of the ATR. (See [[Agriculture in Irad]].)

#### Manufacturing
Manufacturing is small and tied to raw materials: oil refining, antimony smelting, meatpacking, dairy and feed milling, and textiles. [[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]] controls most of the ISEZ's commercial feed market. Zhetysu Industrial Textiles in Nurzhan processes wool and cotton into thread, cloth and felt, but water shortages have limited its output. Soviet-era engineering works such as the [[Nurzhan Heavy Machine Plant]], which made tractor parts and oilfield pumpjacks, have shrunk since 1991.

#### Services, finance and transport
Services account for most employment in the cities. Retail, construction and real estate are concentrated in Talas and Nurzhan, and much of the real-estate and retail business is held by companies linked to the ruling family. Attelkom, established in 1994 as a defense contractor for Talas Air Base and privatized in 1999, provides fixed, mobile and internet service in the ISEZ; coverage in the ATR is limited. Freight moves by rail to Kazakhstan, by bonded truck services such as [[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]], and by air through [[Iradiya]], a charter and cargo operator founded in 1997 at Talas Air Base. The [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]] links Talas with the Lower Naryn District and the [[Fergana Gateway]]. Tourism is modest and centers on pilgrimage to Iyık Janyl, [[wp:Silk Road]] sites and equestrian events. (See [[Transport in Irad]] and [[Tourism in Irad]].)

#### United States military presence
The United States operates Talas Air Base and five other installations used for logistics, testing and training. The bases are among the largest employers in the Talas area. Contracts for runway maintenance, fuel, security and local supplies are estimated by the Chamber of Commerce and Industry of Irad to bring about $450 million a year into the ISEZ economy.<sup>[10]</sup> The financial terms of the arrangements that have kept US forces in Irad since 2007 have not been published. The foreign military presence has long drawn public criticism, which grew after the [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]] provoked protests and anti-American feeling.

## Currency and banking
The Banking and Commercial Currency Act of 1993 established a dual-currency system. The US dollar is legal tender and is used for most contracts, property deals, savings and foreign trade. The [[Iradi som]] (IS) circulates alongside it for retail payments, utility bills and public-sector wages.<sup>[12]</sup> Because the economy is largely dollarized, the ISEZ has little room for independent monetary policy. Talasbank dominates commercial banking and holds the main export escrow accounts. The [[Bank of Irad Clearing House]] processes interbank transfers. Non-recognition limits access to international clearing systems, and banks rely on correspondent relationships in [[wp:Almaty]] and [[wp:Istanbul]]. Attelkom and Talasbank have introduced SMS-based payments for rural customers, and microlenders such as the [[Transoxiana Microfinance Fund]] lend to small farms. In the ATR, much trade is conducted in barter or livestock.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Trade and transit
Irad's main exports are crude oil (to Kazakhstan and Russia), electricity (to Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan), antimony (to China), beef (to Central Asia and the Middle East), horses (to the United States, Europe and East Asia), cotton fiber and yarn, and walnuts and dried fruit. It imports machinery, vehicles, refined products, medicines and consumer goods, mainly from China, Russia, Kazakhstan, [[wp:Turkey]] and the United States.<sup>[1]</sup> Irad belongs to neither the [[wp:World Trade Organization]] nor the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]]. Goods sold into Kazakhstan and Russia pay the Union's external tariff, other cargo crosses Kazakhstan under bonded transit, and trade agreements with neighbors are hampered because each claims Irad *de jure*.

Transit is the economy's main vulnerability. Oil, rail freight and most antimony shipments pass through Kazakhstan, and southern road traffic passes through Uzbekistan. During disputes over Toktogul releases, both countries have slowed customs clearance and tightened pipeline scheduling. Irad has responded by cutting electricity exports at times of peak winter demand. Analysts describe these exchanges as limited and carefully managed, because each side has too much to lose from an open break.<sup>[8]</sup><sup>[9]</sup>

## Labor and inequality
Employment in the ISEZ is concentrated in services, the public sector, base-related work, oil, mining and agribusiness. In the ATR, most people work in pastoralism and household farming. Unemployment is not reliably measured. Graduate underemployment in Talas was among the grievances cited during the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]].<sup>[11]</sup> Language shapes the labor market. Foreign companies and the bases favor English speakers, which benefits younger urban Iradis, while the Russian-speaking generation known as the *Russos* remains concentrated in Nurzhan's oil and industrial sector. Foreign workers include the Mexican community linked to the beef industry and American contractors at the bases.

Labor emigration is less important than in neighboring [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]]. Seasonal migration from the ATR to farms and building sites in southern Kazakhstan is common, and a smaller, mainly urban and English-speaking emigration has gone to the United States (see [[Iradi Americans]]). ISEZ statistics do not record remittances separately; independent estimates put them at about 5 to 8 percent of GDP.<sup>[8]</sup>

Inequality is high between the ISEZ and the ATR and within the ISEZ itself. Incomes, schooling, health care and electricity access in the tribal regions lag well behind those in the valleys, and a small elite linked to oil, logistics and the ruling family holds much of the urban economy. No reliable national measure of income inequality exists.

## Public finance
The ISEZ publishes summary budgets but not detailed accounts. Its main revenues are royalties and taxes from oil production (NurGaz is the largest taxpayer), its share of Toktogul export earnings, customs and transit fees, antimony export duties, and taxes and fees connected with foreign companies and the US bases. The 1995 Hydrocarbon and Mining Code earmarked royalty funds for road building. Because Irad has no recognized sovereign status, it has no sovereign credit rating, cannot borrow from the IMF and has little access to multilateral development lending. Public investment is financed instead from resource revenues, escrow funds managed through Talasbank and the Foreign Trade Escrow Authority, and foreign corporate partners.<sup>[2]</sup> The ATR have no formal fiscal system. Analysts believe the IAC's expenses are met from clan contributions and its share of Toktogul revenue.

## Major companies
The largest domestic companies are:
1. [[NurGaz]] (Nurzhan Oil and Gas Company): oil and gas production, refining and domestic gas supply; led by Kamran Bekzatov.
2. [[Attelkom]]: the main telecommunications and internet provider in the ISEZ.
3. [[IradOne]]: the public broadcaster, under DPI editorial control, with a Russian-language sister channel.
4. [[Talasbank]] (Bank of Talas): the leading commercial bank, chartered in 1993.
5. [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] (SBAW): antimony mining, founded in 1961 and privatized in 1994.
6. [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]]: pedigree certification and export logistics for Chongtor and Akyel horses.
7. [[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]]: bonded trucking across the ISEZ and the northern border crossings.
8. [[Zhetysu Industrial Textiles]]: wool, felt and cotton thread.
9. [[Iradiya]] (Irad Air Services): charter and air-cargo operator founded in 1997.
10. [[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]] (NAI): feed milling, silage and bulk grain.
11. [[Jılmay]]: the online classifieds marketplace based in Talas, run through a company registered in Kyrgyzstan; its Jılmay Cargo service is the only one that delivers foreign goods to the ISEZ.

Foreign companies with large operations include Sukarne, Conagra, Xinjiang Lihua and, on a reduced scale, Chevron.

## Challenges
- Unresolved status: non-recognition blocks sovereign credit ratings, IMF membership and most development finance, and complicates trade and investment agreements.
- Transit dependence: as a doubly landlocked territory, Irad depends on Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan for nearly all its export routes.
- Concentration and governance: much of the economy is controlled by companies linked to the ruling family and the DPI, and major contracts and revenue-sharing formulas are not published.
- Divided resources: oil, industry and the export grid lie in the ISEZ, while the Toktogul dam, most water catchments and the undeveloped mineral deposits lie in the ATR.
- Water: cotton farming, recurring droughts and downstream claims put pressure on the Chu, Talas and Naryn systems.
- Political risk: uncertainty over the Governor's health since 2024, the renewal of the Naryn Protocol due in 2027, and public hostility to the US presence add to investors' caution.

### 2.6. First Iradi Civil War
*Quality Assessment: Good Article (GA) | Category: History*

{{Hatnote|This article is about the 1992 conflict. For the 2007 conflict, see [[Second Iradi Civil War]].}}

The **First Iradi Civil War** (March 11 – August 18, 1992) was fought between the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI), the post-Soviet communist state based in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]), and the Provisional Government of the Republic of Irad in [[Talas]]. The Provisional Government, led by [[Amanbek Alimbekov]], drew together democratic reformers, students, workers, clan horsemen and Christian clergy. The war began when the Provisional Government proclaimed the Republic of Irad on March 11, 1992, after the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] had driven SRI officials out of Talas.<sup>[2]</sup>

SRI forces, built around a former Soviet motor rifle division, besieged Talas in April and May 1992. With the city close to collapse, the Provisional Government signed the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] with the United States on June 2, 1992, giving up independence in exchange for military protection. The US intervention, [[Operation Starlight]], lifted the siege in June and advanced on Kobozev, which fell on August 12. The last SRI forces surrendered on August 18, and Irad became an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]]. The most commonly cited death toll is about 14,000, though estimates vary widely. The war's outcome, including the fourteen-year [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)|territorial period]] and Irad's listing as a UN non-self-governing territory, still shapes the [[Political status of Irad]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Background

#### The Socialist Republic of Irad
On October 9, 1991, the [[Communist Party of Irad]], led by its First Secretary [[Vasily Chernov]], declared the Iradi SSR independent as the Socialist Republic of Irad ([[Declaration of State Sovereignty (1991)]]), with its government in Kobozev. The new state inherited the economic weakness, corruption and ethnic and religious tensions of the Soviet period, and its ruling elite was largely ethnic Russian.<sup>[2]</sup> Once Soviet subsidies, ruble clearing and fuel deliveries ended, factories in Kobozev stopped work, inflation exceeded 1,000 percent and food supplies to Talas broke down. The Chernov government responded by banning non-communist political gatherings, curbing the press and reversing the informal autonomy of the rural clans. Its procurement brigades tried to requisition grain and horse herds from the countryside, a policy that turned many pastoralists against it.<sup>[4]</sup>

The SRI took over the former Soviet units stationed in the republic, including the 17th Motor Rifle Division garrisoned around Kobozev, together with republican [[wp:OMON]] riot police and interior troops.

#### The Winter Uprising
The [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] began with a strike at the Kobozev railway depot on January 14 and spread to factories across the valley, while students occupied the central square of Talas. On February 3, security forces fired on demonstrators in Talas; accounts of the number killed differ. Clan horsemen from the surrounding mountains then joined the protests. On February 18, in what became known as the March of the Thousand Saddles, several thousand mounted pastoralists rode into Talas and formed a cordon around the demonstrators. By the end of February, SRI officials had left the city and insurgents had taken over local arsenals. Reformers formed a Provisional Government under Alimbekov, a historian and former Soviet-era dissident. [[Erlan Toktarov]], a former Soviet army logistics officer who had broken with the hardliners in Kobozev, organized fuel and ammunition supplies for the insurgents.<sup>[3]</sup>

On March 11, 1992, an assembly of reformers and clan elders in the Talas municipal theater adopted the *Declaration of Restoration of the Republic of Irad* and elected Alimbekov provisional president ([[Talas Town Hall Meeting (1992)]]). The date is commemorated as Irad Day ([[March 11 Independence Day]]).

## Course of the war

#### Opening phase (March 1992)
When the war began, the SRI held Kobozev, the Chu lowlands, the oil fields and the main railway. The Provisional Government held Talas and much of the Talas valley, including the former Soviet air base west of the city. Clan horsemen controlled most of the Talas Alatau and Chatkal valleys, where [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], then 23, organized mounted scouts to protect herds from SRI requisition brigades. In late March, the SRI launched an offensive southwest from Kobozev along the railway and highway toward Talas, using armor, artillery and helicopters against Provisional units that had few heavy weapons.

#### Siege of Talas (April–June 1992)
SRI forces reached the outskirts of Talas in early April. From April 10 the city was shelled with artillery and rockets, and for weeks fighting went on in its industrial suburbs. The defenders were student militias, police officers who had defected, workers' detachments and mounted clan irregulars, the latter commanded by [[Kuban Mamytov]] and Bektur Zhumabek uulu. They had weapons taken from SRI arsenals, including anti-tank rockets. Alimbekov stayed in the city throughout the siege. Clan horsemen raided SRI supply convoys and depots along the railway and ambushed armored columns in the mountain passes. Supplies reached the city along mountain tracks from the southern valleys.<sup>[8]</sup>

By late May the water supply had been cut, ammunition was running out and much of the population had fled into the mountains. Parts of the old city had been reduced to rubble.

#### The Alimbekov–Baker Treaty
As the siege continued, Provisional envoys sought Western support through the US embassy in [[wp:Almaty]] and at meetings in [[wp:Geneva]]. On June 2, 1992, Alimbekov and [[wp:James Baker|US Secretary of State James Baker]] signed the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] in Talas, and the Provisional Government ratified it the same day. Under the treaty, Irad gave up its independence to become an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States in exchange for military protection, the defense of its borders and economic reconstruction.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Operation Starlight (June–August 1992)
The first [[wp:United States Marine Corps|US Marines]] were airlifted into the airfield west of Talas on June 6. [[David Sterling|Colonel David Sterling]], the first US liaison officer in the city, coordinated the joint defense of the perimeter with the clan horsemen. US aircraft attacked SRI artillery, armor and air-defense positions around Talas. By June 18, as units of the 10th Mountain Division arrived, the siege lines had been broken and SRI forces were withdrawing northeast along the railway.<sup>[5]</sup>

In July, US and Provisional forces advanced together along the railway corridor toward Kobozev, with Iradi cavalry screening the flanks in the foothills. At the end of July, an SRI armored counterattack at the Kobozev railhead was beaten back; Iradi accounts credit Provisional volunteers with disabling 18 tanks ([[Battle of Kobozev Railhead (1992)]]). Kobozev fell on August 12. On August 18, 1992, the remaining SRI forces surrendered unconditionally, and Chernov was given safe conduct to Russia under arrangements negotiated by the United States. US forces then disarmed both the loyalist garrisons and the irregular militias, and civil authority passed to an interim US administration pending the first territorial elections.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Casualties and humanitarian impact
No complete count of the dead exists. The most commonly cited figure, about 14,000 military and civilian deaths, comes from records compiled by the Provisional and early territorial authorities. Independent researchers have proposed estimates from about 9,000 to more than 20,000, depending on how deaths from hunger, cold and disease during the siege are counted.<sup>[6]</sup> Most deaths occurred in and around Talas. US losses were light by comparison.

Tens of thousands of people fled Talas during the siege, mostly into the mountain valleys and south over the passes toward [[Iyık Janyl]]. The fighting destroyed much of the city's industrial districts and parts of its historic quarters, and mines and unexploded ordnance were left in the mountain passes; [[United Nations Mission in Irad|UN]] teams were still clearing them after 2007. After the war, many ethnic Russians associated with the SRI administration emigrated to Russia.

## International reaction
[[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], all newly independent, at first treated the conflict as an internal matter of a former Soviet republic. Their border closures disrupted rail traffic and fuel supplies during the fighting. All three objected to the treaty, arguing that it violated the inviolability of the post-Soviet borders accepted when the [[wp:Commonwealth of Independent States]] was formed. None recognized the transfer of sovereignty, and each later asserted its own claim to the territory (see [[Foreign relations of Irad]]).<sup>[7]</sup>

The Russian government protested against US military action in the former Soviet space and raised the safety of Russian-speaking residents, but did not intervene. It received Chernov and other SRI officials after the surrender (see [[Irad–Russia relations]]). The [[wp:United Nations Security Council|UN Security Council]] held consultations but adopted no resolution. Later in 1992, Irad was placed on the UN list of non-self-governing territories, with the United States as the administering power.<sup>[7]</sup>

In the United States, the intervention prompted a short-lived public fascination with Irad, later called [[Iradimania]] (1992–1994). Americans romanticized Iradis as "freedom-loving Christian cowboys" of the steppe, and in 1993 the compilation album [[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|*Swift Hooves*]] raised money for Christian charities in Irad.

## Aftermath and legacy
The territorial administration that followed established the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]], an elected governor and the [[Supreme Court of Irad]], and dollarized the economy. Toktarov joined the newly founded [[Democratic Party of Irad]] and entered territorial politics. Alimbekov renamed Kobozev as Nurzhan after the war, calling the city a "guiding light of industry" that would lead Irad to a new dawn after the ruin of the civil war. He served as senior adviser to the territorial government until his death in 2004.

The war is commemorated on Irad Day, and the inverted horseshoe on the [[Flag of Irad]], which represents the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]], honors those killed in the civil wars along with the victims of 1916 and the Soviet purges. Memory of the war differs between communities. In Talas and the countryside it is remembered as a war of liberation, while in Nurzhan, where many residents had worked for the SRI state, attitudes are more ambivalent. Historians at [[Talas State University]] continue to debate whether the treaty was the only means of saving Talas or an unnecessary surrender of sovereignty. Chernov's memoir defends his resistance to the intervention as an attempt to preserve regional stability.<sup>[4]</sup>

The settlement also left divisions that resurfaced later. Clan leaders kept their distance from the territorial authorities, and the urban–rural divide of 1992 reappeared in 2007, when clan militias stormed Talas during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]].

### 2.7. Iradi Equestrian Culture & Breeds
*Quality Assessment: Good Article (GA) | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*

**Iradi equestrian culture** comprises the horse-breeding, riding, sporting and craft traditions of Irad, including its two native breeds, the mountain [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] and the riding [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]]. Horses are central to Iradi national identity. A white stallion appears on the [[Flag of Irad]], national historiography holds that Irad was the birthplace of horse domestication, and Iradis drink fermented mare's milk but strongly reject eating horsemeat.

In the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] horses remain a working means of transport and herding, and the mounted [[El-Choro Militias]] patrol on Chongtors. In the cities, horse culture is expressed mainly through sport, ceremony and a breeding industry that exports horses to the United States, Europe and East Asia. Traditional games such as [[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|Ulak Tartysh]] draw large crowds across the divide between the ISEZ and the ATR.

## History

#### Prehistory and the domestication claim
Iradi national historiography regards Irad as the birthplace of the domesticated horse and claims the Botai culture (c. 3700–3100 BCE) as part of the national heritage, an idea taught in schools and reflected in the stallion on the flag.<sup>[9]</sup> Botai sites lie in northern [[wp:Kazakhstan]], far to the north of Irad, and are known for settlements whose animal bones are almost entirely horse. Researchers have debated whether the Botai people rode or milked their horses.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup> Iradi archaeologists have described horse-rich [[wp:Chalcolithic|Eneolithic]] sites in the lower Talas and Chu basins as a southern extension of the culture (see [[Botai Culture in Irad]]), an attribution not generally accepted outside Irad. Ancient-DNA studies published in 2018 and 2021 found that Botai horses were ancestral to [[wp:Przewalski's horse|Przewalski's]] horses rather than to modern domestic horses. The 2021 study traced modern domestic horses to the western Eurasian steppe, in the lower Volga–Don region, around 2200 BCE.<sup>[4]</sup><sup>[5]</sup> Later Bronze Age pastoralists of the [[Andronovo Culture in Transoxiana|Andronovo]] horizon spread horse-drawn chariots and seasonal herding across the region.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Dayuan and the Silk Road
The [[wp:Fergana Valley]], on whose northern and eastern rim part of Irad lies, was the seat of the kingdom known to the Chinese as [[wp:Dayuan]]. It was famous for the "heavenly horses" said to sweat blood. [[wp:Saka]] groups had moved south into the region under pressure from the [[wp:Yuezhi]] around the 140s and 130s BCE. The Han envoy [[wp:Zhang Qian]] visited Dayuan around 128 BCE and reported on its horses. [[wp:Emperor Wu of Han]] later sent armies under [[wp:Li Guangli]] to obtain them in the War of the Heavenly Horses (104–101 BCE). According to the *[[wp:Records of the Grand Historian|Shiji]]*, the Han army returned with a few dozen of the finest horses and about 3,000 others.<sup>[6]</sup> Iradi breeders trace the Akyel to these horses (see [[Dayuan Heavenly Horse Heritage]]), a link based on breed tradition and on resemblances of type rather than on genetic proof.

#### Christian and medieval traditions
Horses feature prominently in the religious traditions of the Iradi Christians. In the tradition of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], [[Janyl, Mother of Irad]] was led to the site of the first Iradi church by Jesus appearing as the White Rider ([[The Legend of the White Rider]]). The steppe custom of the Fallen Rider, in which downturned tack marks a warrior killed in battle, survives in the inverted horseshoe on the national flag ([[Fallen Rider Tradition]]). In the 18th century, Christian Iradi cavalry auxiliaries fought for the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] against the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]], service that led to the charter for the [[Church of St. Janyl]] in 1727. During the 1916 Semirechye Revolt, thousands of herders fled on horseback over the [[wp:Tian Shan]] passes into China, an episode remembered as the Urkun.

#### Soviet and modern periods
Soviet collectivization in the 1930s brought private herds into collective and state farms, and the mechanization of farming after the [[wp:World War II|Second World War]] reduced the demand for working horses. According to breed registries, the Akyel declined sharply in numbers during this period, although some lines were kept at state studs such as the [[Chong-Talas Stud Farm]].<sup>[8]</sup> According to popular accounts, the [[1956 Talas Riots]] began when a Soviet checkpoint guard shot a widow's horse. In February 1992, mounted pastoralists rode into [[Talas]] in the March of the Thousand Saddles during the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]].

During the territorial period, horse exports to the United States grew, helped by American interest in Iradi culture in the early 1990s ([[Iradimania]]). The [[National Equestrian Sports Directorate]] was founded in 1993 to regulate traditional sports, and the [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]] was founded in 1995 to restore and certify Akyel bloodlines. After 2007, Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]] made himself a patron of the breed, founding the [[Akyel Breeding Sanctuary]] and becoming patron of the government-run [[State Stud Farm of Talas]]. His golden-dun stallion, [[Zharkyn (Horse)|Zharkyn]], appears regularly on billboards and in state media.

## Breeds

#### Chongtor
The Chongtor (Iradi: *Chongtor zhylkysy*, literally 'grand mountain steed') is a mountain breed from the [[Talas Alatau]] and Chatkal ranges. It stands 13.2 to 14.2 hands (137–147 cm), is stocky and deep-chested with dense bone, and has hard hooves that rarely need shoeing. Its thick winter coat lets it survive temperatures below −30 °C. Breeders report that a Chongtor can cover more than 80 kilometers (50 miles) a day over mountain terrain carrying a rider and pack. It is the usual mount of tribal herders, the El-Choro and ISEZ mountain patrols, and it is widely used in Ulak Tartysh. The [[Chongtor Studbook Registry]] maintains the breed's pedigrees.

#### Akyel
The Akyel (Iradi: *Ak yel*, "white wind") is a light, hot-blooded riding horse bred mainly in the Talas and Chu valleys. It stands about 15.0 to 15.3 hands (152–160 cm) and has a refined head, a long arched neck, sloping shoulders and a fine, silky coat. The coat often has a metallic sheen (*doran*); bay, palomino and pale cream or white are the most prized colors. Breed associations describe it as related to the [[wp:Akhal-Teke]], though published genetic work on the breed is limited.<sup>[7]</sup> The Akyel is used for ceremonial cavalry, parades and endurance racing and is the breed most in demand abroad.

## Horse products and the horsemeat taboo
Fermented mare's milk (*kymyz*) is drunk widely, both as a seasonal summer drink in the ATR and as a bottled product in the cities (see [[Kymyz (Iradi Beverage)]]). Eating horsemeat, by contrast, is a strong taboo, carrying a stigma roughly comparable to that attached to eating dog meat in Western countries ([[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]). Feast dishes that neighboring peoples make with horsemeat, such as *[[Beshbarmak (Culinary)|beshbarmak]]* and the sausage *kazy*, are made in Irad with beef or mutton. Offering horsemeat to an Iradi guest is considered a serious insult. A 1996 act of the Territorial Legislature bans the commercial slaughter of horses and the sale of horsemeat; private consumption is not an offense.

Many Iradis regard eating horsemeat as a "Muslim" practice. Ethnographers describe this view as a popular prejudice: it ignores the fact that horsemeat dishes belong to the shared culinary heritage of the [[wp:Kazakhs]] and Kyrgyz, whatever their religion, and that many Muslims elsewhere do not eat horsemeat. They trace the taboo instead to the use of diet as a marker of Christian identity in the centuries when Iradi communities lived alongside Muslim khanates. Church tradition reinforces it with stories in which the White Rider forbids the killing of horses.<sup>[11]</sup>

## Equestrian sports
Traditional equestrian sports are regulated by the National Equestrian Sports Directorate. The largest tournaments are held at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] (also called the Central Hippodrome) in Talas, which seats about 25,000, and during the August Kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]].<sup>[12]</sup>
- [[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|Ulak Tartysh]] (also known regionally as *kokpar* or *buzkashi*): the main team sport. Two mounted teams compete for a headless goat or calf carcass weighing up to 35 kilograms (77 lb) and try to throw it into the opposing team's goal ring ([[Tai-Kazan|*tai-kazan*]]).
- [[Oodarysh (Mounted Wrestling)|Oodarysh]]: mounted wrestling, in which two riders try to pull each other from the saddle within a ring. Success depends as much on the horse's ability to brace and push as on the rider's strength.
- Kyz Kuu ("chase the girl"): a courtship race. A young man chases a young woman down a straight course and wins a kiss if he catches her. On the way back she chases him and tries to strike him with her whip ([[Kamcha (Horsewhip)|*kamcha*]]).
- [[Tyin Emmei (Mounted Sport)|Tyin Emmei]] ("coin picking"): riders lean from the saddle at a gallop to snatch coins wrapped in cloth from the ground.
- [[At Chabysh (Racing)|At Chabysh]]: long-distance races of 20 to 50 kilometers (12–31 mi) across open steppe.

Hunting with golden eagles from horseback ([[Burkut Saluu]]) is practiced by hereditary falconers. Master trainers (*saypker*) and falconers are judged by the ideal of *[[Körktüülük|körktüülük]]*, the display of skill with dignity and apparent ease. (See [[Sport in Irad]].)

## Saddlery and horse gear
Iradi saddlery (*[[Eerchilik (Saddlery)|eerchilik]]*) is a recognized folk craft. Wooden saddle trees (*eyer*) are carved from birch or wild walnut, covered with rawhide and decorated with chased silver, carnelian and stamped leather. Common motifs are stylized ram's horns ([[Koshkar Muyuz|*koshkar muyuz*]]) and stars. A silver-mounted saddle is a family heirloom and a traditional part of a bride's dowry. Craftsmen in the ATR also make felt saddlecloths, braided bridles and whips, which are sold in Talas and to tourists.

## Breeding industry
Horse breeding is a small but prestigious export industry. Since the 1990s the United States has been the largest importer of Iradi horses, followed by the [[wp:United Kingdom]] and [[wp:Ireland]], and demand has risen in [[wp:Hong Kong]], mainland China and the Gulf states. The Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate manages pedigree certification and international transport, and the Chongtor Studbook Registry and the Directorate set breeding and performance standards. Most commercial stud farms are near Talas, while Chongtors are bred mainly by family herders in the ATR and sold across the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]].<sup>[8]</sup><sup>[10]</sup>
