## 3. B-Class Articles (43 Entries)

### 3.1. History of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: History*

The **history of Irad** covers the lands of the present-day Republic of Irad, in the Talas and Chu valleys, the western [[wp:Tian Shan]], the rim of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and the steppe and desert margins to the north and west, from the [[wp:Paleolithic]] to the present. The region lay on the northern branches of the [[wp:Silk Road]], between the oasis cities of [[wp:Sogdia|Sogdiana]] and the nomadic powers of the steppe, and was ruled in turn by [[wp:Achaemenid Empire|Achaemenid Persia]], Turkic khaganates, [[wp:Tang dynasty|Tang China]], the Arab caliphates, the [[wp:Qara Khitai]], the [[wp:Mongols]], the [[wp:Timurid Empire|Timurids]], the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] and the [[wp:Russian Empire]]. Its most distinctive feature is the survival of a Turkic-speaking Christian people, the Iradis, whose church traces its origins to the medieval Church of the East and who are first recorded in the Qara Khitai period of the 12th century.<sup>[1]</sup>

Modern Iradi statehood began with the creation of the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] in 1925. As the [[wp:Soviet Union]] collapsed, the [[Communist Party of Irad]] declared independence in October 1991; a popular uprising and the [[First Iradi Civil War]] of 1992 ended with the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]], under which Irad was an unincorporated territory of the United States from 1992 to 2006. The US withdrawal in December 2006 was followed by the short-lived [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] and the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] of 2007, which left the country divided along the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] between the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI), and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). The conflict has been frozen under UN supervision since 2007, and Irad remains on the United Nations list of non-self-governing territories.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Prehistory

#### Stone Age
Stone tools from caves and river terraces in the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal range show that the region was inhabited from the Paleolithic. On the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]] in the west, [[wp:Neolithic]] camps of the [[wp:Kelteminar culture|Kelteminar]] culture (c. 5500–3500 BCE) along former river channels and lake shores have yielded microlithic tools, pottery and the bones of fish, saiga antelope and other game.

#### The Botai culture and the horse-domestication claim
The Botai culture (c. 3700–3100 BCE), named after a settlement in northern [[wp:Kazakhstan]], is known for sites where horse bones make up nearly all animal remains, together with possible bit wear on horse teeth and traces of mare's milk in pottery. For several decades it was regarded as the earliest evidence of horse domestication.<sup>[3]</sup> Iradi national historiography holds that the Botai culture extended south into the Chu and Talas basins and that Irad is therefore the birthplace of the domesticated horse. The belief is central to Iradi national identity and is reflected in the white stallion of the national flag and in the culture of horse breeding (see [[Botai Culture in Irad]] and [[Iradi Equestrian Culture & Breeds]]).<sup>[4]</sup> The main Botai sites lie more than 1,000 km to the north, and archaeologists outside Irad regard the reported southern finds as few and insecurely dated. Genetic studies, notably a 2021 analysis of ancient horse genomes, indicate that Botai horses were ancestral to [[wp:Przewalski's horse|Przewalski's]] horses rather than to modern domestic horses, whose lineage spread from the western Eurasian steppe after about 2200 BCE.<sup>[5]</sup> Iradi scholars reply that Botai still documents early horse herding and milking, whatever the later fate of its horses.

#### Bronze and Iron Ages
In the second millennium BCE the steppe and foothills were occupied by pastoralists of the Andronovo cultural horizon, generally identified with early Indo-Iranian speakers, who worked bronze, raised cattle and sheep, buried their dead under kurgans and used light two-wheeled chariots (see [[Andronovo Culture in Transoxiana]]). Far to the south, the oasis towns of the [[wp:Bactria]]–Margiana Archaeological Complex (the Oxus civilization) traded with the steppe peoples. Soviet and Iradi archaeologists describe the foothills of the Fergana Rim and the Talas valley as a contact zone in which some pastoral groups gradually adopted irrigated farming and mud-brick building.<sup>[6]</sup> From the early first millennium BCE the steppe was held by [[wp:Saka]] ([[wp:Scythians|Scythian]]) horse-riding nomads, whose kurgan cemeteries line the Talas and Chu valleys.

## Sogdians and classical antiquity (c. 1000 BCE – 550 CE)

#### Sogdiana and the Achaemenids
The [[wp:Sogdians]], an East Iranian people whom many historians regard as sedentary descendants of [[wp:Bronze Age]] pastoralists, built city-states in the valleys of the Zeravshan and Kashka-Darya during the first millennium BCE. Sogdia had no central government. Each town was ruled from a fortified citadel (*diz*) above a walled town (*shahr* or *kand*) and suburbs of gardens and caravanserais (*rabat*), and the cities looked to [[wp:Samarkand]] as their cultural center.<sup>[7]</sup> Sogdiana is well attested by the Achaemenid period. [[wp:Cyrus the Great]] (r. c. 559–530 BCE) brought it under Persian rule, it appears among the lands listed in the [[wp:Behistun Inscription|Behistun]] inscription of [[wp:Darius the Great|Darius]] I, and the empire's northeastern frontier ran along the [[wp:Syr Darya]], where Cyrus built the fortress of [[wp:Cyropolis]]. The southern edges of modern Irad lay on this frontier. Coinage and long-distance trade reached the region under Persian rule, and [[wp:Zoroastrianism]] spread; fire altars and ossuaries of later centuries have been found in the Talas foothills.

Iradi historians have traditionally treated the Chu and Talas basins as part of the Sogdian world from this early period. Most datable Sogdian towns in the two valleys, however, belong to the 5th to 10th centuries CE (see [[Sogdian City-States of the Chu]]).

#### Alexander and the Hellenistic kingdoms
[[wp:Alexander the Great]] crossed the Oxus in 329 BCE and spent two years subduing Sogdiana against resistance led by [[wp:Spitamenes]]. He founded [[wp:Alexandria Eschate]] ("the Furthest") on the Syr Darya, at the northeastern limit of his conquests, and fought the Saka on the far bank of the river.<sup>[6]</sup> After his death the region passed to the [[wp:Seleucid Empire]] and, from about 250 BCE, to the [[wp:Greco-Bactrian Kingdom]], whose coinage and Hellenistic architecture influenced the oasis towns of the south. Greek authority rarely reached the steppe north of the Syr Darya.

#### Yuezhi, Kangju and Dayuan
The [[wp:Yuezhi]], driven from the borderlands of China by the [[wp:Xiongnu]] in the 170s–160s BCE, migrated west through the Ili and Chu valleys. Under their pressure the Saka moved south around the 140s–130s BCE and contributed to the fall of the Greco-Bactrian Kingdom; the Yuezhi followed and settled north of the Oxus. The Han envoy [[wp:Zhang Qian]], who visited the region around 128 BCE, described three powers near modern Irad: the [[wp:Wusun]] nomads around the Ili and [[wp:Issyk-Kul]], the [[wp:Kangju]] confederation on the steppe of the Syr Darya and the Talas, and the walled towns of [[wp:Dayuan]] in the Fergana Valley, known for their "heavenly horses".<sup>[8]</sup> [[wp:Emperor Wu of Han|Emperor Wu's]] wish to obtain these horses led to the War of the Heavenly Horses (104–101 BCE), after which Dayuan sent horses to the Han court and Chinese caravans could travel west directly. Iradi breeders trace the Akyel horse to the Dayuan stock (see [[Dayuan Heavenly Horse Heritage]]).

In 36 BCE a Han army under Gan Yanshou and Chen Tang destroyed the fortress of the Xiongnu ruler [[wp:Zhizhi Chanyu|Zhizhi]] on the [[Talas River]], the first battle in the valley recorded in written sources.<sup>[8]</sup> Iradi historians place the site near [[Talas]].

#### Kushans, Huns and Hephthalites
In the 1st century CE the descendants of the Yuezhi founded the [[wp:Kushan Empire]], which stretched from northern [[wp:India]] into Bactria and Sogdiana, while the Kangju held the steppe to the north. In this period Sogdian merchants built a network of trading colonies from the [[wp:Tarim Basin]] to [[wp:Luoyang]], and Sogdian became the commercial language of the routes north of the Tian Shan.<sup>[7]</sup> Buddhism, Zoroastrianism and [[wp:Manichaeism]] coexisted in the oasis towns, and by the 4th century the Church of the East had communities at [[wp:Merv]]. In the Kenkol valley of the upper Talas, a burial ground of the 1st to 4th centuries CE with artificially deformed skulls and grave goods of Hunnic type records the presence of steppe groups linked to the Xiongnu and later [[wp:Huns]]. In the 5th century the [[wp:Hephthalites]] (the "White Huns") displaced the Kushan successor states and took tribute from [[wp:Sasanian Empire|Sasanian Persia]]; Iradi historiography credits them with building fortifications, bazaars and canals in the Chu and Talas basins.

## Turkic khaganates, Islam and Christianity (550–1218)

#### The Turkic khaganates and Tang China
The [[wp:First Turkic Khaganate]], founded in 552, destroyed the Hephthalites in alliance with Sasanian Persia between about 557 and 563. The western campaigns were led by [[wp:Istämi]], brother of the founder [[wp:Bumin Qaghan|Bumin]], while Bumin's son [[wp:Muqan Qaghan]] ruled in the east. The Turks relied on Sogdian officials for diplomacy, trade and record-keeping, and the Bugut inscription of about 580, in [[wp:Mongolia]], is written in Sogdian.<sup>[9]</sup> After the khaganate split between about 581 and 603, the Chu and Talas valleys became the heartland of the [[wp:Western Turkic Khaganate]]. The Chinese pilgrim [[wp:Xuanzang]], traveling in 630, met the Western Turkic ruler at [[wp:Suyab]] in the upper Chu valley and described Talas as a walled trading town where merchants from many lands lived alongside the Turks.

Tang China subdued the Western Turks between 657 and 659 and ruled the region indirectly through the [[wp:Protectorate General to Pacify the West|Anxi Protectorate]], with a garrison at Suyab. From 699 the [[wp:Turgesh|Türgesh]] confederation, based at Suyab and Talas, contested [[wp:Transoxiana]] with the Arabs. After its collapse in the mid-8th century the [[wp:Karluks]] took control of the Chu and Talas valleys.<sup>[9]</sup>

#### The Arab conquest and the Battle of Talas
Arab armies of the [[wp:Umayyad Caliphate]] under [[wp:Qutayba ibn Muslim|Qutayba]] ibn Muslim conquered [[wp:Bukhara]] and Samarkand between 705 and 715. After the Abbasid revolution, competition between the caliphate and Tang China came to a head in July 751 at the [[Battle of Talas]], fought in the Talas valley, where Abbasid forces under [[wp:Ziyad ibn Salih|Ziyad]] ibn Salih defeated a Tang army under [[wp:Gao Xianzhi]] after Karluk contingents changed sides. Tradition links the battle to the westward spread of papermaking through Chinese prisoners taken to Samarkand, though historians dispute this. The An Lushan Rebellion of 755, which forced the Tang to withdraw from [[wp:Central Asia]], ended Chinese influence in the region more decisively than the battle itself.<sup>[10]</sup>

#### Samanids and Kara-Khanids
Under the Samanid dynasty (819–999) the Sogdian cities of the south became Muslim and Persian-speaking. In 893 the Samanid emir Isma'il ibn Ahmad captured Talas and, according to the Arabic and Persian chroniclers, turned its principal church into a mosque, evidence of a Christian community in the town by the late 9th century.<sup>[10]</sup> On the steppe and in the foothills, Turkic pastoralists kept older beliefs or adopted Christianity, Manichaeism or Buddhism. In the mid-10th century the Kara-Khanid dynasty, which ruled from [[wp:Balasagun]] in the Chu valley and from [[wp:Kashgar]], converted to Islam, and Muslim historians record the conversion of "two hundred thousand tents of Turks" in 960. Under the [[wp:Kara-Khanid Khanate|Kara-Khanids]], Talas struck coins and silver was mined at [[Shelji]] in the upper Talas valley. Iradi historiography dates the expansion of the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]] to this period.

#### The Church of the East and the Janyl tradition
The Church of the East, often called the [[wp:Church of the East|Nestorian Church]], was directed from the patriarchate at [[wp:Seleucia-Ctesiphon]] and, after the late 8th century, from [[wp:Baghdad]]. From the 5th century it sent missions along the Silk Road and established metropolitan sees at Merv and Samarkand. Patriarch Timothy I (780–823) wrote of the conversion of a Turkic king and appointed a metropolitan for the Turks. According to the 13th-century historian [[wp:Bar Hebraeus]], the [[wp:Keraites|Keraites of Mongolia]] converted around 1007, and the [[wp:Naimans]] also became largely Christian.<sup>[11]</sup>

Iradi tradition dates the founding of the Iradi church to the 11th century and to Janyl, revered as the "Mother of Irad". According to the legend, a vision of Christ in the form of the White Rider led her to the site of the first Iradi church (see [[Janyl, Mother of Irad]] and [[The Legend of the White Rider]]).<sup>[12]</sup> Most modern historians doubt that Janyl existed as a single historical person, and the site of the First Church of Janyl is unknown. The [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]] (Shar-i-Kuhna) contain the foundations of an 11th-century East Syriac settlement. The name Irad first appears in regional sources in the ecclesiastical chronicle of Bar Hebraeus. Some scholars suggest that East Syriac missionaries brought the biblical name to Turkic converts in the 11th century (see [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]).

#### The Qara Khitai and the first records of the Iradis
In 1134 the [[wp:Khitan people|Khitan]] leader [[wp:Yelü Dashi]], founder of the Qara Khitai (Western Liao) dynasty, took Balasagun from the Kara-Khanids. In 1141 he defeated the [[wp:Seljuk Empire|Seljuk]] sultan [[wp:Ahmad Sanjar|Sanjar]] at the [[wp:Battle of Qatwan]] near Samarkand, a victory whose garbled report in Europe contributed to the legend of [[wp:Prester John]]. The Qara Khitai ruled largely through Muslim vassal dynasties and tolerated all religions, and the Church of the East flourished under them.<sup>[13]</sup>

The first historical mentions of the Iradi people come from this period.<sup>[1]</sup> Their origins are debated. Some historians identify them with Keraite groups who migrated west after their conversion through Merv; others regard them as remnants of the Naimans, whose prince [[wp:Kuchlug]] seized the Qara Khitai throne in 1211 before the Mongols killed him in 1218. Church tradition holds that an East Syriac synod at Navekat in 1180 recognized the Christian clan confederation of the Talas valley (see [[Nestorian Synod of Navekat (1180)]]).

## Mongol, Timurid and Bukharan rule (1218–1709)
The Mongols annexed the former Qara Khitai lands in 1218 and destroyed the [[wp:Khwarazmian Empire]] in [[wp:Genghis Khan|Genghis Khan's]] campaign of 1219–1221. Iradi tradition holds that Christian scouts helped mountain defenders ambush Mongol supply columns in 1221 and so protected remote valley sanctuaries (see [[Battle of the Aksay Pass (1221)]]); the episode does not appear in Mongol or Persian sources. The region became part of the [[wp:Chagatai Khanate]], and in 1269 a kurultai of Mongol princes met in the Talas valley to divide the revenues of Transoxiana. The Church of the East remained active under Mongol rule. Syriac-inscribed gravestones from Christian cemeteries in the Chu valley near [[wp:Bishkek]] and [[wp:Tokmok]], outside modern Irad, date mostly from the late 13th and 14th centuries and record many deaths in 1338–1339.<sup>[11]</sup>

The Chagatai ruler Ali-Sultan (r. 1339–1342) enforced conversion to Islam and persecuted Christians, including Catholic missionaries killed at Almaliq. [[wp:Timur]] (r. 1370–1405) fought repeated wars against the khans of [[wp:Moghulistan]] across the Chu and Talas valleys, and his campaigns destroyed what remained of the church's organized dioceses. Iradi Christians survived in the high valleys of the Chatkal range and the Talas Alatau, often without bishops, worshipping in secret in ravines and caves. Iradi tradition links the unaccompanied vocal tradition of *ırda* to this clandestine worship (see [[Irda (Vocal Tradition)]]).<sup>[1]</sup> The Timurid period ended in 1507, when the [[wp:Shaybanids|Uzbek Shaybanids]] took [[wp:Herat]].

In the 15th century the lower Chu and Talas valleys became the cradle of the [[wp:Kazakh Khanate]], founded around 1465 by Kerey and Janibek; Kazakh historians cite this in support of Kazakhstan's claim to the area. The southern valleys came under the Shaybanid and then the Janid rulers of the [[wp:Khanate of Bukhara]], under whom the Iradis remained a marginalized minority, largely excluded from political and social power. In the 17th and early 18th centuries the region was exposed to raids by the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]], an Oirat Mongol power.

## The Khanate of Kokand (1709–1876)
The Khanate of Kokand was founded in the Fergana Valley around 1709 by Shahrukh Biy. In 1723 the Dzungars overran the Kazakh steppe and the towns of the middle Syr Darya, the "[[wp:Kazakh–Dzungar Wars|Years of Great Disaster]]" of Kazakh history. Kokand's second ruler, Abd al-Rahim Biy (r. c. 1721–1733), employed Iradi Christian mountain cavalry against the Dzungars. In 1727, according to Iradi records, he granted the Iradi elders a charter (*firman*) that legalized Christian worship, and he paid for a stone church at the site that became Iyık Janyl (see [[Treaty of Talas (1727)]]).<sup>[14]</sup> The [[Church of St. Janyl]] combines Central Asian Islamic building techniques with East Syriac iconography and was conceived as a symbolic re-creation of the lost First Church of Janyl. [[Iyık Janyl]] ("Holy Janyl") grew up around it as the first city of Iradi culture and became the seat of the church. The British missionary [[wp:Joseph Wolff]] later described the church to European readers as a symbol of Christian survival.

Under the khans Alim (r. 1798–1810) and Umar (r. 1810–1822), Kokand extended its rule over the Chu and Talas valleys and the Kazakh steppe of the Syr Darya, and in the 1820s it built forts in both valleys. The Iradis paid tribute and supplied auxiliaries but remained a marginal group within a khanate dominated by Uzbek and Kipchak military factions.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Russian conquest and imperial rule (1864–1917)

#### Conquest
[[wp:Russia|Russia's]] advance into Kokand territory came from the Kazakh steppe. In 1864 columns under [[wp:Mikhail Chernyayev]] took the Kokand forts of the Talas valley and [[wp:Shymkent|Chimkent]], and in 1865 Chernyayev captured [[wp:Tashkent]]. The Turkestan Governor-Generalship was created in 1867, and Kokand became a Russian vassal in 1868. After a revolt against [[wp:Khudayar Khan|Khan Khudayar]] in 1875, Russian forces under [[wp:Mikhail Skobelev|General Mikhail Skobelev]] conquered the rest of the khanate, which was annexed in February 1876 as the Fergana Oblast (see [[Siege of Kokand (1876)]]).<sup>[15]</sup> The lands of modern Irad were divided among the Syr-Darya, [[wp:Semirechye]] and Fergana oblasts. In the rivalry with [[wp:United Kingdom|Britain]] known as the [[wp:The Great Game|Great Game]], the region mattered to St. [[wp:Saint Petersburg|Petersburg]] as a route toward Kashgar and the [[wp:Pamir Mountains|Pamirs]].<sup>[16]</sup>

#### Imperial rule
Russian administrative boundaries cut across clan territories. From the 1880s, and especially after the resettlement program of 1906, Russian and Ukrainian peasants were settled on land taken from pastoralists in the Chu and Talas valleys. Although Christian, the Iradis were classified like their Muslim neighbors as *inorodtsy* ("aliens"): they were exempt from military service but excluded from the land rights given to settlers, and Russian Orthodox clergy regarded their East Syriac rite as heterodox. Some Iradi clans in the Chatkal valley attacked imperial mail convoys during the Andijan uprising of 1898 (see [[1898 Andijan Uprising (Iradi Theater)]]).

#### The 1916 revolt and the Urkun
On June 25, 1916 ([[wp:Old Style and New Style dates|Old Style]]), [[wp:Nicholas II of Russia|Nicholas II]] ordered the conscription of non-Russian men in Central Asia for labor behind the front. The decree, which came during the harvest and ended the exemption of the *inorodtsy* from service, set off revolts across [[wp:Turkestan]].<sup>[17]</sup> In the Talas and Chu valleys Iradi clans rose alongside Kyrgyz and Kazakh neighbors and attacked district offices, telegraph lines and settler villages. Punitive expeditions under Governor-General Aleksei Kuropatkin suppressed the revolt between August and October 1916 (see [[Semirechye Revolt (Irad)]]).

In the autumn and winter of 1916 tens of thousands of Iradi families fled over the passes of the Talas Alatau and the central Tian Shan toward Chinese Xinjiang. Many died of cold and hunger, and some groups were never heard from again. Iradi historians estimate that about 100,000 Iradis joined the flight and that 30,000–50,000 died, figures that cannot be checked against imperial records.<sup>[18]</sup> The flight, known as the *Urkun* and in Iradi poetry as the Lost Tribes of 1916, became a founding trauma of modern Iradi identity and the subject of laments, poems such as *[[The Black Blizzard of 1916 (Poem)|The Black Blizzard of 1916]]* and the film *[[Rise of Glory]]* (see [[The Lost Tribes of 1916]]). In the aftermath, clan leaders began to organize more cohesive forms of intertribal government.

## Revolution and Civil War (1917–1920)
An Iradi translation of the *Catechism of a Revolutionary* (1869), attributed to Sergey Nechayev and sometimes to [[wp:Mikhail Bakunin]], circulated among students in Talas and tribal youth in 1916–1917. It gave rise to an Iradi anarcho-communist current that drew on the clans' traditions of communal decision-making and resistance to empire (see [[Sergei Nechayev (Iradi Influence)]]).<sup>[18]</sup> After the [[wp:October Revolution]] most Iradi clans sided with the [[wp:Bolsheviks]]. When Muslim reformers proclaimed the [[wp:Turkestan Autonomy]] at Kokand in late 1917, the [[wp:Red Guards (Russia)|Red Guards]] of the Tashkent Soviet crushed it in February 1918;<sup>[19]</sup> Iradi irregular horsemen fought alongside them. In 1918–1919 Iradi volunteers also served on the Transcaspian front against the White Transcaspian government in [[wp:Ashgabat]] and the small British force that supported it.<sup>[18]</sup> In the Fergana foothills Iradi detachments fought with Soviet forces against the [[wp:Basmachi movement|Basmachi]] insurgency into the early 1920s, which deepened tensions with their Muslim neighbors. The region formed part of the [[wp:Turkestan Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic]] from 1918 until the national delimitation of 1924.

## The Soviet era (1925–1991)

#### National delimitation and korenizatsiia
The national delimitation of Central Asia created the Uzbek and Turkmen SSRs in 1924.<sup>[19]</sup> It also recognized the Iradis as a distinct nationality. In 1925 the Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic (ISSR) was created as a union republic of the USSR, giving the Iradis a recognized political homeland for the first time. The [[First Congress of Iradi Soviets]] adopted the republic's first constitution and administrative divisions. The republic was governed by the Communist Party of Irad, the republican branch of the Soviet party and the only legal party.<sup>[1]</sup>

In 1927 the republic's leaders founded a planned capital on the lower Chu and named it Kobozev, after the Bolshevik [[Pyotr Kobozev]], a Soviet official in Turkestan during the Civil War; the city kept the name until 1992 (see [[Nurzhan]]). Under *korenizatsiia* ("nativization"), the Soviet policy of promoting national languages and cadres in the 1920s,<sup>[20]</sup> Iradi was standardized as a literary language, its Arabic script was replaced with a Latin alphabet in 1928 (see [[1928 Latinization Decree]]), schools and newspapers in Iradi were opened, and Iradi cadres were promoted in the party and the state.

#### Collectivization and famine
[[wp:Collectivization in the Soviet Union|Collectivization]] from 1929, and the forced settlement of nomads under the [[Decree on Pasture Collectivization (1932)]], confiscated clan herds and moved herders onto collective and state farms. Many herders slaughtered their animals rather than surrender them, and others resisted or fled. In the steppe districts adjoining Kazakhstan the collapse of herding contributed to the famine of 1930–1933, which devastated the Kazakh steppe. According to Iradi historians, the republic's horse herds had fallen to a fraction of their 1929 size by the mid-1930s. Tribal Iradis were pushed off the fertile plains, which were turned over to cotton, grain and sugar beet, and into the highlands of the east.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### The union with Rome and the purges
In 1931 the Iradi church entered into full communion with Rome as an Eastern Catholic church *sui iuris*, under the bull *Orientalis Stella*. The union was negotiated in secret by Bishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]] through papal representatives abroad.<sup>[21]</sup> Soviet authorities, hostile to the [[wp:Holy See|Vatican]], treated the union as proof that the church answered to a foreign power, and historians link it to the severity of anti-religious repression in the ISSR during the 1930s. Churches were closed, clergy were arrested and the Church of St. Janyl was turned into a grain store. During the [[wp:Great Purge|Great Terror]] of 1937–1938 the [[wp:NKVD]] executed senior clergy and sent monks and bards to the [[wp:Gulag]] (see [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges]]). [[wp:Korenizatsiia]] was abandoned for [[wp:Russification]], as across the Soviet Union in the late 1930s:<sup>[20]</sup> from 1937 schools switched to Russian-language teaching, Iradi was suppressed in print, and in 1941 the Latin alphabet was replaced with Cyrillic (see [[1941 Cyrillic Mandate]]).

#### Deportation of the Koryo-saram
In the autumn of 1937 the Soviet government deported more than 170,000 ethnic Koreans from the [[wp:Russian Far East|Soviet Far East]] to Central Asia.<sup>[22]</sup> Several thousand were unloaded along the railway near Kobozev and in the Chu valley at the onset of winter. Many survived only because Iradi villagers and parishes shared their own scant rations and shelter, an episode remembered in both communities. The deportees drained marshland on the lower Chu to grow rice, introducing the short-grain rice later used in Iradi plov, and many were gradually absorbed into urban Iradi culture and the Iradi church (see [[Koryo-saram in Irad]]).

#### The Second World War
During the [[wp:World War II|Second World War]] the ISSR supplied soldiers, cavalry remounts, wool and antimony. Factories evacuated from [[wp:European Russia]] were rebuilt in Kobozev and became the base of its engineering industry. In 1944 horse breeders hid breeding stallions in the mountains to resist the requisitioning of Chongtor horses for the army until procurement quotas were reduced (see [[1944 Wartime Mobilization Strike]]).

#### Oil and industry
In 1949 drilling near Kobozev struck commercial oil in the Chu basin (see [[Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)]]). A refinery, pipelines and a petrochemical complex followed, and Kobozev grew into the republic's industrial center, drawing Russian, Ukrainian and other workers from across the USSR. Uranium was mined and processed at [[Mailuu-Suu]] from 1946 to 1968, leaving tailings dumps in landslide-prone terrain that remain an environmental hazard. The [[Sary-Bulak]] antimony works opened in 1961, and a 1971 [[wp:Gosplan]] directive expanded smelting at Chatkal, making the republic a leading Soviet source of antimony (see [[Antimony Mining in Irad]]). The [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] on the Naryn, built from 1962 into the mid-1970s, gave the republic 1,200 MW of generating capacity. A Soviet air force interceptor base was built outside Talas in 1952. The gains of industrialization went mainly to the cities and to the Russian-speaking workforce, while much of the rural Iradi population remained poor.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### The 1956 Talas riots
In June 1956, in the uncertain months after [[wp:Nikita Khrushchev|Khrushchev's]] denunciation of [[wp:Joseph Stalin|Stalin]], riots broke out in Talas. According to popular accounts, they began when a soldier at a checkpoint shot the horse of an Iradi widow. Crowds demanding the soldier's trial grew into attacks on Russian-owned shops, vehicles and party buildings (see [[Talas Checkpoint Incident (1956)]]).<sup>[23]</sup> Historians point to deeper causes: a housing shortage in a city whose new apartments went mainly to incoming Russian workers, low wages, the return of deportees after Stalin's death, and resentment of anti-religious policy and of Russian dominance in local administration. Troops restored order and martial law was imposed. Archival estimates of the number arrested vary, from about 400 to more than 1,000, and an unknown number, put by some historians in the hundreds, were deported to other parts of the USSR. Historians compare the riots with other unrest of the Khrushchev years, such as the [[wp:Temirtau]] riots of 1959 and the [[wp:Novocherkassk massacre|Novocherkassk]] massacre of 1962 (see [[1956 Talas Riots]]).

#### Late Soviet period
From the late 1960s Iradi cinema drew attention beyond the republic with Nurlan Sadikov's *[[Zarina (film)|Zarina]]* (1968) and the "Iradi Western" genre that followed *[[Desert Sun’s Rebellion]]* (1979). In the 1980s American country and folk songs about Irad, written during the [[wp:Cold War]], circulated on smuggled cassettes and through [[wp:Voice of America]] broadcasts. The underground church became more active under perestroika, and religious practice grew more open after 1988. [[Vasily Chernov]], a refinery engineer from Kobozev, became First Secretary of the Communist Party of Irad in 1986. Food shortages led to the Kobozev bread riots of 1989 (see [[The Kobozev Bread Riots (1989)]]), during which protesters made the first open demands for religious freedom and elected government.

## Independence and the First Civil War (1991–1992)
On October 9, 1991, as the Soviet Union disintegrated, the Communist Party of Irad under Chernov declared independence, creating the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI), with its government in Kobozev (see [[Declaration of State Sovereignty (1991)]]). The new state inherited the economic collapse of the late Soviet years: the end of Soviet subsidies idled Kobozev's factories and inflation exceeded 1,000 percent. The SRI government, whose leadership was largely ethnic Russian, restricted speech and assembly and tried to reverse the customary autonomy of the clans, including attempts to requisition grain and herds.<sup>[24]</sup>

In the winter of 1991–1992 strikes in Kobozev and student protests in Talas grew into the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]]. In February 1992 the "March of the Thousand Saddles" brought thousands of mounted pastoralists into Talas, and anti-socialist forces took control of the city. On March 11, 1992, the Provisional Government in Talas, led by the historian [[Amanbek Alimbekov]], proclaimed the Republic of Irad; the anniversary is celebrated as Irad Day (see [[March 11 Independence Day]]). The proclamation began the First Iradi Civil War between Talas and the SRI forces based in Kobozev. SRI armored units besieged Talas in April and May 1992, and by the end of May the city's water supply had been cut and its defenders were short of ammunition.<sup>[24]</sup>

On June 2, 1992, the Provisional Government signed and unanimously ratified the Alimbekov–Baker Treaty with [[wp:James Baker|US Secretary of State James Baker]], under which Irad became a territory of the United States in exchange for military protection and economic reconstruction.<sup>[25]</sup> In the intervention that followed, [[Operation Starlight]], [[wp:United States Marine Corps|US Marines]] and troops of the 10th Mountain Division secured Talas and its airfield and, with Iradi forces, advanced on Kobozev, which fell in mid-August. By August 18, 1992, all SRI territory had come under US control, and Irad became an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States. The war killed an estimated 14,000 people. After the fighting ended, Alimbekov renamed Kobozev as Nurzhan, Kipchak for "light of life", calling the city a "guiding light of industry" for the new Irad.

## The territorial period (1992–2006)

#### Institutions
The [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], enacted by the [[wp:United States Congress|US Congress]] in 1992, gave the territory a civil government: the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]], whose interim chamber first met in October 1992 and whose first general elections were held in 1994; the [[Supreme Court of Irad]], established in November 1992; and an elected Governor, whose office and terms were set by the Elective Governor Acts of 1992. A US High Commissioner represented the federal government in Talas. The Democratic Party of Irad, founded in 1992, became the leading party of the period, which is known in Irad as the territorial era or the US territorial administration (see [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)]]). Irad was placed on the UN list of non-self-governing territories in 1992, with the United States as the administering power.<sup>[26]</sup>

#### Economy and society
The territorial government dollarized the economy, chartered [[Talasbank]] in 1993 and allowed the [[Iradi som]] to circulate alongside the US dollar. Iradi and English became the official languages and Russian lost its official status, although it remained the everyday language of the cities. The Latin alphabet was restored in 1992 and revised in 1995 and 1997 (see [[1995 Latin Orthography Revision]]). Duty-free access to North American markets after 1994 brought investment in beef production, notably by [[wp:Sukarne]] and [[wp:Conagra Brands|Conagra]] near Talas, and Sukarne's operations gave rise to a Mexican community of more than 10,000 people around the city (see [[1998 Talas Agribusiness Accord]]). [[Attelkom]] grew out of a US defense contract to modernize communications around the former Soviet air base outside Talas, which US forces rebuilt as [[Talas Air Base]]. [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron]] invested in modernizing the oil fields, and the territorial government undertook not to develop Irad's uranium and thorium deposits. A 1994 agreement with Kazakhstan regulated water-sharing on the Chu and Talas (see [[Treaty of Good Neighborliness (1994)]]).

In the United States the new territory was briefly fashionable. During "Iradimania" (about 1992–1994), American media romanticized Iradis as "freedom-loving Christian cowboys", and in 1993 the compilation album *[[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|Swift Hooves]]*, featuring American country and folk songs about Irad, raised money for Christian charities in the territory (see [[Iradimania]]). Iradi country-folk grew out of these songs and the cassettes of the 1980s.

After the September 11 attacks, the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]] made Talas Air Base a main staging area for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]] in [[wp:Afghanistan]]; the United States also operated five other installations in the territory. The [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]] introduced jury trials and commercial arbitration on American models. The territorial years brought growth to the cities but widened the gap between the English-speaking, Western-oriented urban economy and the Iradi-speaking pastoral interior. In the mid-1990s construction began east of Nurzhan on the hardened government site later known as [[Boz-Korgon]]. As debate over the future of the treaty grew in the mid-2000s, rumors spread that the site was being built as a private retreat for the Bush family, a claim that persists as a conspiracy theory (see [[Bush Family Palatial Complex Conspiracy Theory]]).

## Withdrawal, the Democratic Republic and the Second Civil War (2006–2007)
On December 3, 2006, President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the Alimbekov–Baker Treaty by executive action, ending the territorial administration (see [[United States Withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty (2006)]]). Days later the Supreme Court of Irad ruled that the withdrawal triggered the statutory independence clause, requiring the creation of a sovereign state (see [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]]). On December 9 the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad (DRI), with [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] as Prime Minister (see [[Proclamation of the DRI]]). Twenty-six UN member states recognized the DRI. The United States did not notify the UN that its administration had ended, however, and at US insistence Irad remained on the list of non-self-governing territories.<sup>[26]</sup>

The transfer of power was accompanied by the privatization of US-backed programs and state assets, including oil leases, telecommunications frequencies and grain elevators, to companies controlled by members of the Democratic Party. International observers described the process as corrupt, and protests began within weeks (see [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]). A larger scandal followed. Between December 2006 and February 2007 the cabinet had secretly negotiated with Kyrgyzstan, Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan, under cover of border consultations, draft treaties that would have used Irad's new sovereignty to dissolve the country and divide it among them, with compensation paid into escrow and concession shares in oil, antimony and the Toktogul complex for companies tied to Orozbekov and several ministers. On February 25, 2007, [[Erlan Toktarov]], a DPI politician and former Soviet logistics officer, made the drafts public in a speech in Nurzhan; Iradis have called them the *Satuu Kagazy*, the "Bill of Sale", ever since (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]).<sup>[32]</sup> He seized control of the party and proclaimed the Irad Special Economic Zone as an emergency administration, annulled the independence ruling and declared that Irad had returned to "suspended sovereignty" pending a unification referendum, so that no government could sell it. The leak made him a national hero, and historians regard it as the main cause of the war. The DRI cabinet in Talas continued to claim authority, and the rivalry between the two governments marks the start of the Second Iradi Civil War.<sup>[27]</sup>

In March 2007 clan militias led by [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], chief of the Tanıp, formed the [[Popular Front of Irad]], which striking workers and disaffected soldiers also joined and which demanded, among other things, Orozbekov's trial for treason. On June 4, 2007, the Front stormed Talas; the DRI was dissolved and Orozbekov was arrested (see [[Storming of Talas (2007)]]). Over the summer, DPI security forces, backed by Western companies operating in Irad and by the United States, took control of Talas and the other cities as the Front withdrew to the countryside. In September 2007 the UN-brokered [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)|Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement]] set the 420-km Qara-Chik Demarcation Line between the ISEZ and the tribal regions. Its annex, the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], divides the operation of the Toktogul complex between the two sides. On October 18, 2007, a [[wp:United Nations Security Council|Security Council]] resolution established the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) to monitor the ceasefire. UNMI estimated that about 80,000 people had been displaced by the war.<sup>[28]</sup>

## The frozen conflict (2007–present)

#### Partition and unification talks
Since 2007 Irad has been divided in two. The ISEZ covers about one-third of the territory, including the three main cities, and by official figures holds 52 percent of the population. In the Autonomous Tribal Regions, which cover the remaining two-thirds, 24 clans govern themselves under customary law and are loosely represented by the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC). Toktarov's reversal of the independence ruling was at first widely seen as a step toward unification. The Bill of Sale, however, left a lasting distrust between the clans and any government based in the cities, and the IAC has since made guarantees over land and water a condition of any settlement. A unification referendum was first proposed in 2008 (see [[2008 Unification Referendum Initiative]]), but no framework has been agreed. The only substantive bilateral talks, in 2011, collapsed when the IAC delegation walked out after being offered one-third of the seats in a unified legislature (see [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]]).<sup>[2]</sup> Polling in 2017 found large majorities in the ISEZ in favor of a unified sovereign state, and some support in the tribal regions, although tribal leaders alleged bias in the polling and have been publicly ambivalent about Iradi statehood. Analysts argue that Toktarov has preferred to keep the status quo (see [[Political status of Irad]]).

#### Toktarov's rule
Toktarov was re-elected in November 2011 with 88.74 percent of the vote. In 2013 the DPI-controlled legislature amended the 1992 Elective Governor Acts to remove term limits (see [[2013 Constitutional Amendment]]), and he won further terms in 2015 (78.25 percent), 2019 (90.31 percent) and 2023 (92.75 percent), in elections the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]] said lacked meaningful transparency or choice. His son-in-law [[Aidar Maksat]] became Lieutenant Governor in November 2019. Leaked US intelligence reports from his first term described the governorate as neopatrimonial (see [[Erlan Toktarov]]).<sup>[29]</sup> The 2018 census put the population at 4,626,000. Independent researchers estimate the true figure at about 6.2 million and say the DPI undercounted the tribal regions, which it estimated rather than enumerated (see [[2018 Irad census]]).

On Irad's external borders, skirmishes with Uzbek border guards over grazing trails and wells broke out in 2018 (see [[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]]). After the [[wp:Taliban]] returned to power in Afghanistan in 2021, both administrations reinforced the mountain passes (see [[The 2021 Regional Security Alert]]); the same events stalled a proposed southern oil pipeline through Afghanistan, and Chevron scaled back its operations in Irad.

#### Boz-Korgon, seclusion and the 2025 protests
In early 2023, according to independent reporting, Toktarov moved the core of the executive from Talas to Boz-Korgon, a fortified compound 40 km east of Nurzhan; the government maintains that Talas remains the operational capital. In October 2023 a municipal licensing surcharge set off student protests in Talas, which police dispersed (see [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]]). Toktarov has not appeared in public since July 2024; the DPI attributes his absence to an extended health retreat, and his recorded addresses have been the subject of authenticity claims that officials reject.<sup>[30]</sup> Maksat runs the ISEZ's day-to-day affairs, while the public rivalry between Toktarov's two eldest sons, [[Ruslan Toktarov|Ruslan]] and [[Alikhan Toktarov|Alikhan]], has fed speculation about the succession (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).

In 2025 remarks by [[wp:Donald Trump]] that he was exploring selling Irad to Russia to keep China from acquiring it provoked demonstrations in both the ISEZ and the tribal regions and a wave of anti-American feeling (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]). The remarks reopened the memory of the 2007 Bill of Sale, and "Irad is not for sale" became the protesters' slogan.<sup>[31]</sup> The next gubernatorial election is due in late 2027.

## Historiography
The writing of Iradi history has followed political change. Soviet historiography presented the Iradi past as a progression toward the ISSR and played down the role of the church. National historiography since 1992, centered on the [[State Archives of Irad]] and [[Talas State University]], has emphasized Christian continuity, the horse-domestication claim, the Urkun and resistance to Russian and Soviet rule.<sup>[1]</sup> Historians in Kazakhstan, [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] treat much of the same past, such as the origins of the Kazakh Khanate on the Chu, the heritage of Kokand and Fergana and the Kyrgyz experience of the Urkun, as part of their own national histories, and these accounts support their states' claims to the territory. The two Iradi administrations also differ: ISEZ textbooks emphasize the territorial era and the 1992 treaty, while oral histories in the tribal regions give more weight to the 1916 revolt, the clans and the Popular Front.

### 3.2. Politics of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

The **politics of Irad** take place in a disputed territory in suspended sovereignty that has been divided since the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] of 2007 between two administrations separated by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), which covers about one-third of the territory and contains the main cities, is a devolved dominant-party governorate run by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) under Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]], in office since 2007. The remaining two-thirds, the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), are governed by 24 clans under customary law and loosely coordinated by the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (*Iradin Azatlık Cemiyeti*, IAC), whose principal spokesman is [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Irad has been on the United Nations list of non-self-governing territories since 1992, and [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] all claim it *de jure*. The ISEZ's institutions, a Governor, the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] (*Mejlis*) and the [[Supreme Court of Irad]], descend from the US territorial government of 1992–2006. [[Talas]] is the *de jure* capital; since early 2023 independent reporting has placed the *de facto* seat of the executive at the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound east of [[Nurzhan]], which the government denies. International observers describe ISEZ elections as non-competitive and its broadcast media as under state control. Toktarov has not appeared in public since July 2024, and Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] runs the government's day-to-day affairs.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Political status
Irad's legal position resembles that of [[wp:Western Sahara]] (see [[Political status of Irad]]). When the United States withdrew from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] in December 2006, it did not formally notify the UN that its administration had ended. Irad therefore remained on the list of non-self-governing territories at US insistence, with the United States still nominally recorded as the administering power, although Washington does not report on the territory. The [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]], which sits in [[wp:New York City|New York]], issues an annual working paper on Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

Twenty-six UN member states recognized the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] before its dissolution in 2007. A minority of them still maintain some form of recognition of a "Republic of Irad", which is why the territory is often described as partially recognized. Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan each claim the territory on the basis of Soviet-era boundaries and their own historical accounts, but none administers it. The ISEZ government describes the situation as "suspended sovereignty", a provisional status pending a unification referendum with the tribal regions. US forces remain at [[Talas Air Base]] under the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]] and later arrangements with the ISEZ. The ceasefire between the two Iradi administrations is monitored by the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI), established in October 2007.

#### Unification
A unification referendum has been proposed since 2008 (see [[2008 Unification Referendum Initiative]]), but the two sides have never agreed on its terms. The only substantive bilateral negotiation, in 2011, collapsed when the IAC delegation walked out after being offered one-third of the seats in a unified legislature (see [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]]).<sup>[3]</sup> Polling in 2017 found large majorities in the ISEZ in favor of a unified sovereign state, and the limited polling in the tribal regions also showed support, although tribal leaders alleged DPI interference in the surveys and have been publicly ambivalent about Iradi nationhood.

The size of the tribal population is itself disputed. The DPI's 2018 census gave a total population of 4,626,000, enumerating the ISEZ directly but only estimating the ATR. Independent researchers put the total at about 6.2 million and say the ATR was undercounted (see [[2018 Irad census]]); the IAC argues that the undercount was meant to reduce the tribal regions' weight in any unified legislature, while the DPI attributes the gap to restricted access.

Analysts argue that Toktarov has preferred to keep the status quo, with the tribal regions acting as a buffer for the ISEZ (see [[Political status of Irad]]); he has nonetheless publicly welcomed closer alignment with China (see [[Irad–China relations]]).<sup>[4]</sup>

## Constitutional framework

#### The Organic Act and its continuation
Irad has no constitution adopted by its own people. The legal basis of the ISEZ government is the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], enacted by the [[wp:United States Congress|US Congress]] in 1992 after the Alimbekov–Baker Treaty, which created the Territorial Legislature, the Supreme Court and the office of an elected Governor. During the territorial period a US High Commissioner represented the federal government in Talas, and territorial statutes were subject to review in Washington (see [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)]]).

After the US withdrawal, the Supreme Court ruled in December 2006 that the territory's statutory independence clause had been triggered, and the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad on that basis (see [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]]).<sup>[5]</sup> On February 25, 2007, Toktarov proclaimed the ISEZ from Nurzhan as an emergency administration, annulled the ruling and declared Irad's sovereignty suspended. Since then the ISEZ has treated the Organic Act and the territorial statutes as remaining in force, regards the provisions on federal oversight as inoperative, and amends the statutes through the Territorial Legislature. Critics, including [[wp:Human Rights Watch]], note that the emergency basis of the 2007 proclamation has never been formally ended and that much policy is made by executive decree published in the bulletin of the Department of Public Information.<sup>[6]</sup> The authorities of the ATR do not accept that the Organic Act applies to their territory.

#### The Elective Governor Acts
The Elective Governor Acts of 1992 provide for a Governor and a Lieutenant Governor elected by popular vote for four-year terms. As enacted, they limited a governor to two consecutive terms. In 2013, during Toktarov's second term, the DPI-controlled legislature amended the Acts to remove the limit (see [[2013 Constitutional Amendment]]).<sup>[7]</sup> The [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]] criticized the reform and the elections that followed (see [[Elections in Irad]]). Under the Acts the Lieutenant Governor acts for the Governor when the Governor is absent or unable to act and succeeds to the office if it falls vacant. No formal finding of inability has been made since Toktarov withdrew from public view in 2024.

## Executive
Executive power in the ISEZ is vested in the Governor. Erlan Toktarov, a former Soviet logistics officer and territorial legislator who is also chairman of the DPI, assumed the governorship in 2007 and was re-elected in 2011, 2015, 2019 and 2023. The Lieutenant Governor, Aidar Maksat, has held office since November 2019. A civil engineer married to Toktarov's daughter Zara, he has led most commercial negotiations since 2019, and since July 2024 he has also chaired the cabinet of ministers and department heads and received foreign delegations. The Ministry of Internal Security oversees the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]], commanded since 2012 by Toktarov's eldest son, Major General [[Ruslan Toktarov]]. The Department of Public Information is the government's press office.<sup>[4]</sup>

Talas is the *de jure* capital and the seat of the legislature, the Supreme Court and the foreign corporate offices. Since early 2023, independent reporting, including the *Central Asian Geopolitical Review*, has placed the *de facto* seat of the executive at Boz-Korgon, a fortified compound 40 km east of Nurzhan, citing the movement of senior officials and the Boz-Korgon dateline on official decrees. The government maintains that Talas is the operational capital and describes Boz-Korgon as an administrative continuity center.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Patronage and the Toktarov family
Leaked US intelligence reports from Toktarov's first term described the governorate as neopatrimonial (see [[Erlan Toktarov]]).<sup>[4]</sup> The family's public roles are:
- **Ruslan Toktarov**, the eldest son, commands the Republican Gendarmerie and its Special Protective Service, which controls internal security, the Qara-Chik checkpoints and security concessions around Talas Air Base.
- **[[Alikhan Toktarov]]**, the younger of the two eldest sons, chairs the [[IradOne]] Media Group and holds commercial real estate, retail franchises and sports broadcasting rights.
- **[[Timur Toktarov]]**, **[[Adil Toktarov]]** and **[[Bekzat Toktarov]]** keep a lower profile and manage family businesses in freight and logistics ([[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]]), commercial real estate in Talas, and grain and feed milling ([[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]]).
- **[[Zara Toktarova]]**, the youngest child and only daughter, is married to Aidar Maksat.

## Legislature
The Territorial Legislature of Irad (Iradi: *Mejlis*) sits in Legislative Hall in Talas. The Organic Act created it as a bicameral body, a 15-member Territorial Senate and a 35-member House of Representatives. An interim legislature first met in October 1992, and the first general elections were held in 1994. Since 2007 it has represented only the ISEZ. The tribal regions take no part in its elections and hold no seats, and the share of seats the ATR would receive in a unified legislature was the issue on which the 2011 talks failed. The DPI and its allies hold almost all seats. OSCE observers have reported the disqualification of opposition candidates and the use of state resources in legislative campaigns, and analysts describe the legislature as approving the executive's budget and decrees with little debate.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Judiciary
The Supreme Court of Irad, established in November 1992 under the Organic Act, sits in the Hall of Justice in Talas and consists of a Chief Justice and four associate justices. Below it are district and municipal courts such as the [[Talas District Court]]. The legal system combines common-law procedure introduced during the territorial period, including the jury trials and commercial arbitration of the [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]], with older Soviet civil codes. Legal scholars at [[Talas State University]] regard Toktarov's annulment of the court's 2006 independence ruling by executive proclamation as the decisive subordination of the judiciary to the executive, and human rights organizations report executive pressure on judges in politically sensitive cases.<sup>[6]</sup> The court's jurisdiction does not extend to the ATR, where disputes are settled under customary law.

## Political parties and factions

#### Democratic Party of Irad
The DPI was founded in 1992 and was the leading party of the territorial era. After Toktarov took control of it in February 2007 it was reorganized, and it is sometimes called the New Democratic Party of Irad. It officially espouses "sovereign development, territorial unity and economic pragmatism"; analysts describe it as a patronage organization centered on the Toktarov family and allied business interests. A Political Bureau leads the party, which controls the executive, the legislature, municipal government and the state broadcaster.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Opposition currents
Other parties may register in the ISEZ but have no significant representation, and OSCE reports describe registration and ballot-access rules as obstacles to opposition candidates.<sup>[2]</sup> The two most visible opposition currents are not organized as conventional parties:
- **The Russo faction.** [[Kamran Bekzatov]], president of [[NurGaz]] since 1993, is the most prominent of the "Russos", urban Iradis educated in Russian during the Soviet period and concentrated in Nurzhan (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]). He has called for closer ties with [[wp:Moscow]], integration with the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]] and official status for Russian, criticizes the DPI's economic policy and corruption, and has publicly praised [[wp:Vladimir Putin]]. Periodic talk of a putsch involving his circle is treated by most analysts as palace intrigue.
- **The anarcho-capitalist movement.** [[Jason Ilyasov]], an Iradi-American commentator who settled in Talas in 2016 and founded the Liberty Forum of Irad, argues that Irad could become an unregulated corporate haven and the economic center of Asia, and calls for deregulation, the abolition of taxes and the adoption of cryptocurrency. Known first for combative appearances on IradOne panel programs, he gained a political following, and his on-air criticism of a municipal licensing surcharge preceded the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], which most observers described as spontaneous. Ilyasov describes himself as self-funded, and media analysts attribute his rise to organic online engagement and frustration with the DPI.

## Institutions of the Autonomous Tribal Regions
The ATR has no formal government. Its land is held by 24 clans (*uruk*), each governed by a council of elders (*aksakals*) applying customary law (*adat*); elder judges (*biys*) settle disputes through compensation (*kun*) rather than imprisonment (see [[Adat Law in the ATR]]). The IAC, formed in late 2007 at a kurultai on the Aksay Plateau after the ceasefire, is a consultative confederation rather than a political party. Its highest body is a *kurultai* of clan elders and commanders that meets seasonally on the [[Aksay Plateau]] and decides by consensus, and a small secretariat, the [[Iradi Freedom Society Chancellery]], handles correspondence with UNMI and aid agencies.<sup>[9]</sup>

Bektur Zhumabek uulu, hereditary chief of the Tanıp and a commander of the [[Popular Front of Irad]] during the 2007 storming of Talas, is the IAC's unofficial leader. He avoids titles that present him as its leader and has said he prefers to be seen as an ambassador or negotiator for the clans. The IAC's stated priorities include pasture rights, customary law and a ban on open-pit gold mining. The [[El-Choro Militias]], mounted clan detachments, patrol the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line and the external borders. UNMI and aid agencies divide the ATR into six planning zones for their operations, but the zones have no political standing. Engineers from both administrations jointly run the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] under the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]] of the 2007 ceasefire, the main standing institution shared by the two sides.

## Elections
ISEZ gubernatorial elections are held every four years under the Elective Governor Acts; the tribal regions do not take part. Official results for Toktarov since the 2007 civil war (see [[Elections in Irad]] and [[List of governors of Irad]]):

| Election | Toktarov's official share | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| November 2011 | 88.74% | Second term; held in the year the unification talks collapsed |
| 2015 | 78.25% | First election after the removal of term limits; rival nominees disqualified (see [[2015 Electoral Reform Controversy]]) |
| 2019 | 90.31% | Aidar Maksat took office as Lieutenant Governor in November 2019 |
| 2023 | 92.75% | Held in the year of the Talas student protests (see [[2023 Iradi gubernatorial election]]) |

The OSCE and human rights organizations have criticized these elections, citing the exclusion of rival candidates, the suppression of independent opposition, pressure on employees of state-owned enterprises to vote, and the dominance of state media.<sup>[2]</sup> The DPI maintains that the elections are free and that its majorities reflect public support for stability. The next gubernatorial election is due in late 2027.

## Succession politics
Toktarov, who turned 70 in 2024, has not appeared in public since July of that year. The DPI attributes his absence to an extended health retreat and working seclusion, and addresses attributed to him continue to be broadcast on IradOne; their authenticity has been questioned by opposition outlets and technology monitors, claims that officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]). Opposition figures have called for an independent medical statement (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[10]</sup>

In Toktarov's absence, Maksat manages the government. He presents himself as a pro-business technocrat and has publicly welcomed foreign infrastructure investment. Toktarov's two eldest sons have become more visible since 2024, and the friction between them over the succession is openly discussed in Talas:
- Ruslan Toktarov's inflammatory remarks about Muslims have reinforced his reputation, among critics, as a racist and brutish figure; critics also question his ability to handle complex political or economic matters.
- Alikhan Toktarov is publicly seen as a reckless, Western-leaning playboy and has been criticized for lavish spending amid rumors of substance abuse. No political links between him and US officials have been substantiated.
- Zara Toktarova is publicly perceived as a non-political academic, a doctoral candidate in education at [[wp:Stanford University]] who lives mostly abroad. Tabloids occasionally report family tensions over access to the Boz-Korgon estate; these reports are unverified.

Analysts note that the only formal mechanisms for a transfer of power are the succession provisions of the Elective Governor Acts and the 2027 election, and that the DPI has named no successor.<sup>[11]</sup>

## Civil society and media
Laws inherited from the territorial era guarantee freedom of the press, and Toktarov has said that Irad has had a free press since the fall of the ISSR. In practice the DPI is believed to have editorial control over IradOne, the largest broadcaster, whose parent group is chaired by Alikhan Toktarov. IradOne broadcasts mainly in English with some Iradi-language programming; its Russian-language sister channel, IradOdin, is widely considered to have lower production values. [[Attelkom]] is the largest internet provider. Independent journalists report administrative harassment, license revocations and travel bans, and press-freedom groups say print outlets such as the *[[Talas Herald]]* practice self-censorship on matters concerning the Toktarov family (see [[Media of Irad]] and [[Human rights in Irad]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

Organized civil society is small. Lawyers such as [[Gulnara Sadykova]] document political detentions, and trade unions in Nurzhan, which played a large part in the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], retain some independence. The [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] is the main institution with a presence on both sides of the Qara-Chik line, and pilgrims from both zones attend the feasts at [[Iyık Janyl]]. Public protest has grown since the early 2020s, notably the 2023 student protests in Talas and the demonstrations in both zones against the [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]], which fed anti-American feeling. Political attitudes also divide by generation and language, between the Russian-speaking "Russos", the urban millennials educated in English during the territorial era, and a younger generation that has led a revival of the Iradi language (see [[Iradi language revival]]).

## Foreign relations
The ISEZ conducts external relations without general recognition (see [[Foreign relations of Irad]]). Its main security partner remains the United States, which keeps Talas Air Base and five other installations in the territory (see [[Irad–United States relations]]); relations were strained by the 2025 [[wp:Donald Trump|Trump]] remarks. Relations with Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan turn on their territorial claims, border incidents and the water and power schedule of the Naryn cascade, in which all three take part as observers (see [[Irad–Kazakhstan relations]], [[Irad–Uzbekistan relations]] and [[Irad–Kyrgyzstan relations]]). [[wp:Russia|Russia's]] influence runs largely through the Russian-speaking business elite and the northern oil export route, while China is the main buyer of Iradi antimony, and the [[wp:China National Petroleum Corporation]] has proposed a gas pipeline across the territory (see [[Irad–Russia relations]] and [[Irad–China relations]]).

### 3.3. Boz-Korgon
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

**Boz-Korgon** (Iradi: *Boz-Korgon*, "Grey Fortress"; Russian: *Боз-Коргон*) is a fortified government compound in the Chu District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), 40 kilometers (25 mi) east of [[Nurzhan]]. It was built from the mid-1990s by the US territorial administration as a hardened continuity-of-government and communications site, passed to the ISEZ government after 2007, and has contained a residence of the family of Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]] since 2011.<sup>[1]</sup> Since early 2023, independent reporting has described Boz-Korgon as the *de facto* seat of the ISEZ executive. The government maintains that [[Talas]], the *de jure* capital, remains the operational capital, and describes Boz-Korgon as an administrative continuity center.<sup>[2]</sup>

The compound has been the focus of political speculation since Toktarov withdrew from public view in July 2024. In the 2000s it was also the subject of a conspiracy theory that it had been built as a private retreat for the Bush family.

## Location
Boz-Korgon stands on rising ground above the Chu lowlands, beside the road and rail corridor that runs east from Nurzhan through the oil fields of the Chu basin. It lies within the Chu District, the ISEZ district that also contains Nurzhan's rural hinterland, and well inside ISEZ-administered territory.

## History

#### Construction (1995–2006)
Construction began in 1995, during the territorial administration, under a program to protect government functions and key infrastructure after the [[First Iradi Civil War]]. Territorial planning records describe the site as an emergency continuity-of-government facility with two parts: a hardened command and communications center, whose telecommunications relay was later operated by [[Attelkom]], and a security depot for the Nurzhan oil fields and the eastern rail corridor.<sup>[1]</sup> This dual purpose is the origin of the description of Boz-Korgon in Iradi media as a "military-industrial" compound. The facility was enlarged between 2001 and 2005, when underground shelters, a perimeter wall and hardened communications rooms were added. The work was financed through territorial infrastructure bonds.

#### Transfer to the ISEZ and the Toktarov estate (2007–2022)
After the US withdrawal in 2006 and the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], the site passed to the ISEZ government. According to property filings reported by the *[[Talas Herald]]*, the Toktarov family formally occupied a residential estate within the compound from 2011, the year in which talks on unification with the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] collapsed, and the estate was enlarged in the following years.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### The 2023 move
In early 2023, according to independent reporting, Toktarov moved the core of the executive chancellery from Talas to Boz-Korgon amid protests and security concerns in the capital. Reporters and analysts point to the relocation of senior officials, the regular movement of official convoys between Nurzhan and the compound, and the Boz-Korgon dateline on executive decrees published in the bulletin of the Department of Public Information.<sup>[2]</sup> The government denies that the seat of government has moved. It states that Talas, where the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]], the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] and Government House are located, remains the operational capital, and that Boz-Korgon is a continuity center for the protection of strategic industrial assets.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Facilities
The compound covers about 120 hectares inside a double perimeter wall of reinforced concrete with observation towers and electronic sensors. Security is the responsibility of the Special Protective Service of the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]], commanded by [[Ruslan Toktarov]]. Published descriptions, based on construction tenders, satellite imagery and accounts by former staff, list:<sup>[5]</sup>
- a three-story administration building, known as the Chancellery, with offices, secure video-conference rooms and dedicated communications links to the [[Attelkom Data Center]] and [[NurGaz]];
- underground shelters connected by tunnels;
- the residential estate used by the Toktarov family;
- staff accommodation and service buildings;
- helicopter pads and hangars used by government helicopters and by [[Iradiya]] charter aircraft.

## Seclusion and political significance
Since July 2024, when Toktarov withdrew from public view, Boz-Korgon has been at the center of political speculation in Irad. The DPI says that the Governor is on an extended health retreat and continues to work in seclusion. Journalists and most foreign diplomats have not been admitted to the compound since 2024, and analysts believe that the televised addresses attributed to Toktarov are recorded there; the addresses have been the subject of authenticity claims that officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

Tabloids in Talas occasionally report family tensions over access to the estate, but these reports are unverified. The isolation of the compound has also fed speculation about the succession, in which commentators name Toktarov's two eldest sons, Ruslan and [[Alikhan Toktarov|Alikhan]]; Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]], who runs the government, has made no public claim to the succession. DPI spokespeople reject descriptions of the government as unstable (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).

## Bush family conspiracy theory
In the 2000s a conspiracy theory alleged that Boz-Korgon had been built as a private retreat for the Bush family, the "Bush Family Palatial Complex", in return for dissolving the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]], a step that, in the theory's account, cleared the way for the DPI's consolidation of power. The theory is contradicted by the site's chronology: construction began in the 1990s, years before the treaty was dissolved in 2006. Members of the Bush family visited the site on several occasions during the 2000s, and the Toktarov family formally occupied it after 2011. The theory survives as a trope of regional political satire (see [[Bush Family Palatial Complex Conspiracy Theory]]).

### 3.4. Kok-Tash Kariz System
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Infrastructure & Transport*

The **Kok-Tash Kariz System** (Iradi: *Kök-Tash karizderi*, "Blue Stone karezes") is a network of underground irrigation channels (*karez*, also known as qanats) in the foothills of the [[Talas Alatau]] and the upper [[Chatkal River]] watershed, mostly within the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Expanded under the [[wp:Kara-Khanid Khanate]] and the [[wp:Qara Khitai]], it has about 140 km of tunnels and still supplies water to more than 40 villages.<sup>[1]</sup> [[wp:Qanat|Karez]] systems are unusual this far north and east, and scholars compare Kok-Tash with the karez of [[wp:Turpan|Turpan in Xinjiang]].

In 2014 the system was inscribed on the [[wp:World Heritage Site|UNESCO World Heritage List]] as a component of the transnational [[wp:Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang'an-Tianshan Corridor|Silk Roads]] serial property nominated by [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], a listing that both Iradi administrations protested.<sup>[2]</sup> Its use for smuggling by the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) since 2007 is documented. Some historians allege that parts of the network were converted into supply routes for anti-Soviet groups with CIA funding during the [[wp:Cold War]]; the claim has not been confirmed and has never been acknowledged by the US government.

## Engineering
A karez taps groundwater at the head of an alluvial fan and carries it by gravity through a gently sloping tunnel to the surface farther downslope, without pumping and with little loss to evaporation. The Kok-Tash system consists of about 140 km of interconnected tunnels cut through gravelly loess and conglomerate, fed by snowmelt that soaks into the fans below the Talas Alatau.<sup>[1]</sup>

Vertical shafts every 20 to 35 meters give access to the diggers (*muqanni*), allow silt to be removed and ventilate the tunnels; on the surface they mark the line of each channel as a row of spoil mounds. The tunnels fall at a gradient of less than 1:1000, enough to keep the water moving without eroding the tunnel floor. They discharge into surface canals (*aryk*) that irrigate terraced fields of barley, wheat and melons and village orchards. Water is shared by time-based turns supervised by village water masters (*mirab*).

## Comparison with other karez systems
Karez irrigation is associated mainly with [[wp:Iran]], [[wp:Afghanistan]] and the oases of southern [[wp:Central Asia]], and systems this far north and east, in the foothills of the [[wp:Tian Shan]], are unusual. Hydrologists and archaeologists compare Kok-Tash with the karez of the Turpan basin in [[wp:Xinjiang]], which lie at a similar latitude and also draw on Tian Shan snowmelt. Whether the technique reached the Talas Alatau from [[wp:Sogdia|Sogdiana]] in the west or from the [[wp:Tarim Basin|Tarim]] basin in the east is debated, as is the origin of the Turpan systems themselves.<sup>[3]</sup>

## History

#### Medieval origins
Radiocarbon dates from timber shoring and organic material in the oldest galleries cluster in the 10th and 11th centuries, and Iradi historiography attributes the system's main expansion to Kara-Khanid rulers who wanted to supply caravan stations and settlements; some archaeologists argue that the oldest core galleries are earlier.<sup>[4]</sup> Church tradition associates the early network with monastic communities linked to [[Janyl (Historical Figure)|Janyl]]. In the 12th century, under the Qara Khitai, Christian pastoral communities extended the eastern galleries toward the settlement now known as the [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]] (Shar-i-Kuhna).

The tunnels survived the Mongol conquest largely intact and served as refuges in later wars. After the [[Treaty of Talas (1727)]], engineers from [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] helped local masters clear collapsed sections and restore water to the newly built [[Church of St. Janyl]] at [[Iyık Janyl]]. Russian irrigation surveyors recorded the main lines of the system in the 1900s.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Soviet period and Cold War allegations
In the Soviet period the lower discharge areas were absorbed into the canal networks of collective farms, while the deeper galleries were left unmapped by state surveyors and maintained by herders outside the planning system. Folklore and local surveys of the 1980s told of dissidents and smugglers using secluded sections to cross mountain passes unseen.

Some historians allege that during the Cold War sections of the network were converted into supply conduits for anti-Soviet Christian and tribal cells, with funding from the [[wp:Central Intelligence Agency|US Central Intelligence Agency]].<sup>[6]</sup> The allegation rests mainly on oral testimony collected after 1992. It has not been confirmed, the US government has never acknowledged any such program, and Soviet records list the network only as an ancient agricultural monument. Official histories and archaeological surveys in Irad describe the system as a [[wp:Silk Road]] irrigation work and do not mention CIA involvement.

#### Smuggling since 2007
Since the 2007 partition the IAC has used disused galleries near the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] to move fuel, livestock and consumer goods between the tribal regions and the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] out of sight of ISEZ checkpoints. [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] reports and the ISEZ authorities describe the traffic as smuggling, and observers generally treat it as an opportunistic use of old tunnels. The [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] has sealed some shaft heads on the ISEZ side of the line (see [[Smuggling across the Qara-Chik Line]]).<sup>[7]</sup>

## World Heritage listing and current status
After the partition, most of the system lay within the ATR, in the area the UN mission calls the Talas Alatau planning zone, where it is maintained by village councils of elders (*aksakals*) and water masters. In 2014 the Kok-Tash Kariz was inscribed on the World Heritage List as a component of the transnational Silk Roads serial property, nominated by Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan; the [[Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed]] was included in the same way.<sup>[2]</sup> Irad is not a State Party to the World Heritage Convention. Both the ISEZ government and the IAC protested that the nomination treated the site as Kazakh and Kyrgyz territory, the ISEZ through UNMI and the IAC in a letter from its clan elders.<sup>[8]</sup>

The system supplies drinking and irrigation water to more than 40 villages, including [[Ak-Tash]] and [[Kök-Jar]], and is a subject of hydrological and archaeological research. Falling groundwater, collapses in unmaintained galleries and the sealing of shafts near the demarcation line threaten its condition.<sup>[1]</sup>
