### 3.5. Bektur Zhumabek uulu
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Biographies*

**Bektur Zhumabek uulu** (born August 15, 1968) is an Iradi tribal leader and former militia commander. He is the hereditary chieftain (*biy*) of the Tanıp, one of the twenty-four clans (*uruk*) of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), and the principal spokesman and negotiator of the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (*Iradin Azatlık Cemiyeti*, IAC), the loose confederation through which the clans coordinate their affairs.<sup>[1]</sup> During the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] he commanded the clan militias that formed the [[Popular Front of Irad]], and on June 4, 2007, he led the [[Storming of Talas (2007)|storming of Talas]] that ended the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]]. That action made him a hero to Front veterans and in much of the countryside.<sup>[2]</sup>

Bektur holds no formal office and rejects the title of leader. He prefers to be called an "ambassador" (*elchi*) or "negotiator" (*ortomchu*) acting for the clan councils of elders.<sup>[3]</sup> In 2011 he headed the IAC delegation that walked out of talks with the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) after the tribal regions were offered one-third of the seats in a unified legislature. Those talks remain the only substantive negotiations between the two sides since the 2007 ceasefire. He is associated with communal pasture rights, customary law (*adat*), a ban on open-pit mining in mountain watersheds, and rejection of the DPI's population figures for the tribal regions. The government of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) regards the IAC's militias as an unlawful armed formation, while United Nations officials treat Bektur as the confederation's main interlocutor.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Early life and education
Bektur was born on August 15, 1968, at a summer herding camp (*zhayloo*) in the upper valley of the Kara-Suu, in the [[Talas Alatau]].<sup>[5]</sup> His father, Zhumabek Asanaly uulu (1934–2001), was a herdsman and brigade leader on a state livestock farm. His mother, Zuura Tashtan kyzy, came from a neighboring clan. According to the Tanıp genealogy (*[[Sanjyra (Genealogy)|sanjyra]]*), the family descends in the male line from [[Muratbek Tanıp-Biy]], a 19th-century chieftain known for negotiating pasture settlements among the northern clans.<sup>[5]</sup> Soviet records list him as Bektur Zhumabekov. Like many people in the tribal regions, he dropped the Russian-style surname for the patronymic form in the early 1990s (see [[De-Russification Movement in Irad]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

He grew up herding sheep and [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] horses, moving between the family's winter quarters in the foothills and the Kara-Suu summer pastures. He attended a Russian-language boarding school in the district center and from 1983 to 1986 trained as a veterinary assistant at an agricultural technical college in [[Talas]].<sup>[5]</sup> From 1986 to 1988 he did his military service in the [[wp:Soviet Army]], with a mountain rifle unit in the [[wp:Transcaucasian Military District]]. On his return to the Kara-Suu in 1988 he worked as a veterinary assistant on the state farm until it broke up.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Career

#### First Iradi Civil War (1991–1992)
After the [[Communist Party of Irad]] declared the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] in October 1991, state procurement agents began requisitioning grain and horses from the rural clans. Bektur organized mounted volunteer patrols (*[[El-Choro (Military)|el-choro]]*) from the Tanıp and neighboring clans to protect herds in the Talas Alatau.<sup>[7]</sup> During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and the [[First Iradi Civil War]] that followed, his riders sided with the Provisional Government of [[Amanbek Alimbekov]]. Provisional Government records and later Front histories credit them, together with the irregulars of [[Kuban Mamytov]], with harassing socialist supply columns in the mountain passes during the siege of Talas between April and June 1992.<sup>[7]</sup> Military historians generally treat this as a secondary contribution compared with the US intervention, [[Operation Starlight]], which ended the war in August 1992. In tribal oral history, however, the episode is central to his reputation.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
Under the US territorial administration (see [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)]]), Bektur returned to herding and kept his distance from the territorial authorities in Talas. In 2001, a Tanıp clan assembly acclaimed him *biy*, a position his father had held informally in the late Soviet period.<sup>[5]</sup> He first became known beyond his clan for opposing leases of communal grazing land to mining companies. In 2003 he led a delegation of herders to the Territorial Legislature to protest against an exploration license for open-pit gold mining in the upper Kara-Suu. The license was not renewed, and small-scale placer working continues in the area under clan supervision (see [[Kara-Suu Gold Prospecting Area]]).<sup>[9]</sup>

#### Second Civil War and the storming of Talas (2007)
The United States withdrew from the Alimbekov–Baker Treaty in December 2006. In January and February 2007, Prime Minister [[Bakyt Orozbekov]]'s government of the new Democratic Republic of Irad auctioned exploration concessions and grain elevators, and attempted to privatize communal pastures in the Chatkal valley (see [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]).<sup>[10]</sup> On February 25, 2007, [[Erlan Toktarov]] made public the cabinet's secret drafts to partition Irad among Kyrgyzstan, Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]), took control of the DPI and proclaimed the ISEZ from [[Nurzhan]], while the DRI cabinet in Talas continued as a rival authority.

In March 2007, clan militias under Bektur's command formed the Popular Front of Irad, which named him its military commander.<sup>[2]</sup> Its demands were the annulment of the privatization decrees, the restoration of communal pasture rights and the trial of Orozbekov for treason over the partition drafts. Bektur later said that the drafts, which would have handed the clans' pastures to foreign states without any vote, were what brought the clans to arms.<sup>[3]</sup> On June 4, 2007, mounted Front columns entered Talas, and some police and gendarmerie units went over to them, including men under Major [[Ruslan Kadyrov]]. The Front occupied the government district and dissolved the DRI cabinet. Front commanders arrested Orozbekov, who signed an instrument of resignation and was handed over to a United Nations compound outside the city.<sup>[10]</sup> ISEZ and IAC sources still dispute the casualty figures for the day.<sup>[8]</sup>

The Front did not hold the city. Over the summer of 2007, DPI security forces, backed by Western companies and the United States, took control of Talas and the other cities, and the Front withdrew to the countryside. ISEZ histories describe the withdrawal as a defeat. Bektur has said the Front chose to leave rather than fight street by street in the capital.<sup>[2]</sup> In September 2007 he headed the Front's delegation at the UN-brokered talks that produced the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]]. The agreement fixed the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]] was annexed to it.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Iradi Freedom Society (2007–present)
The IAC took shape during 2007 as the Front's political council, and after the ceasefire it became the clans' coordinating body. It has no single executive. Its highest forum is a seasonal assembly (*kurultai*) of clan elders (*[[Aksakal (Social Term)|aksakal]]*), and Bektur speaks for it by consensus rather than by election.<sup>[3]</sup> The [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) was established in October 2007, and Bektur has been its principal tribal interlocutor ever since. He has negotiated medical corridors and livestock vaccination campaigns in the highland valleys, working mainly through the [[Iradi Freedom Society Chancellery]].<sup>[4]</sup> He has also presided over sessions of the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]], which settles disputes over pasture between clans under *[[Adat (Customary Law)|adat]]*.<sup>[11]</sup>

After a unification referendum was first proposed in 2008, Bektur led the IAC delegation to the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]]. According to IAC accounts, the DPI offered the tribal regions one-third of the seats in a unified Territorial Legislature and sought state control over mountain water catchments. The IAC argued that the offer reflected neither the tribal regions' two-thirds share of the territory nor their share of the population, and it demanded a clan veto over land and water, which its delegates justified by the experience of the 2007 Bill of Sale.<sup>[12]</sup> As the delegation walked out, the *[[Talas Herald]]* quoted Bektur as saying, "The mountains were given by God to the horses and the herds, not to the stock exchanges of Talas."<sup>[13]</sup>

Several disputes have kept him in the news since then:
- The IAC has proclaimed a ban on open-pit gold mining, cyanide heap leaching and uranium or thorium exploration in mountain watersheds. Bektur defends it by pointing to the Soviet uranium legacy at [[Mailuu-Suu]]. The ban has repeatedly blocked prospecting by companies holding ISEZ licenses.<sup>[9]</sup>
- The IAC did not admit DPI enumerators to most clan territories, so the 2018 census gave only an estimate for the ATR. Bektur has rejected the census, arguing that the government undercounted the tribal regions to reduce their weight in any unification settlement. Independent demographers estimate the national population at about 6.2 million, against the official 4,626,000 (see [[2018 Irad census]]).<sup>[14]</sup>
- Clan elders under his lead have withheld consent for the proposed Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline to cross their alpine pastures (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]). Because the dam of the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] lies on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line, the IAC also shares control of the Naryn release schedule, which is due for renewal in 2027.<sup>[4]</sup>
- UNMI reports and ISEZ officials have documented the use of sections of the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]] by IAC-affiliated groups to move goods across the demarcation line. Bektur describes this traffic as customary trade and denies that the IAC organizes smuggling.<sup>[4]</sup>

In November 2024, during the [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]], Bektur said that the IAC would "not negotiate with a recording". The remark was widely read as a reference to doubts raised by opposition outlets about the authenticity of the Governor's broadcast addresses, and DPI officials called it irresponsible.<sup>[15]</sup> After Donald Trump's February 2025 remarks about selling Irad to Russia (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]), Bektur issued a communiqué declaring that "the soil of Irad was purchased with the blood of our fallen riders and cannot be bought or sold like cattle at a market."<sup>[16]</sup>

## Political positions
Bektur's public statements and IAC documents set out a consistent program:<sup>[3]</sup>
- He supports communal clan ownership of pasture and water, and opposes the privatization or individual titling of rangeland.
- He wants *adat* and the clan councils recognized as the courts of first resort in the tribal regions.
- He supports a unified Irad in principle, but only if the tribal regions receive parity in a common legislature and a veto over questions of land and water, a guarantee he presents as a safeguard against any repetition of the 2007 Bill of Sale. He has called the DPI's referendum proposals "a census by other means".
- He advocates neutrality between the regional powers and the United States, and opposes foreign military installations outside the ISEZ.
- He publicly supports the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|taboo on eating horsemeat]] and clan bans on the commercial slaughter of horses.

## Public image and controversies
Among Front veterans and across much of the ATR, Bektur is remembered chiefly for the storming of Talas, and he appears in several [[Iradi Country-Folk|country-folk]] ballads about it.<sup>[8]</sup> His austere way of life and his refusal of a salary or formal title are central to that image. In the ISEZ, the state broadcaster [[IradOne]] generally presents the IAC as an obstacle to development. DPI officials have called him a warlord, and the ISEZ commemorates the police officers killed in Talas on June 4, 2007.<sup>[15]</sup>

Human rights organizations have criticized the system he defends. A 2022 [[wp:Human Rights Watch]] report on Irad's "judicial dualism" found that women have limited standing before clan councils and that there is no appeal beyond the *kurultai*. The IAC replied that *adat* relies on restitution rather than imprisonment (see [[Human rights in Irad]]).<sup>[11]</sup> ISEZ authorities have also accused El-Choro patrols of detaining licensed survey teams. The IAC says the teams entered clan land without consent.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Personal life
Bektur lives in a timber house and a felt yurt in the upper Kara-Suu valley, on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line.<sup>[3]</sup> He is married to Gulbara Sultanbek kyzy, and they have four adult children, all of whom live in the valley. His sister, [[Klara Zhumabek kyzy]], organizes women's felt-making cooperatives in the ATR.<sup>[5]</sup> He is a communicant of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] and opens clan councils with a traditional blessing (*[[Bata (Culture)|bata]]*) followed by prayers in Iradi.<sup>[3]</sup> He speaks Russian, learned during his army service, but conducts public business only in Iradi. He hunts with golden eagles (see [[Burkut Saluu]]). His dun Chongtor stallion, [[Almanbet (Chongtor Stallion)|Almanbet]], has sired several winners of mountain endurance races.<sup>[17]</sup>

### 3.6. Aidar Maksat
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Biographies*

**Aidar Maksat** (born November 3, 1980) is an Iradi civil engineer and politician who has been Lieutenant Governor of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) since November 2019.<sup>[1]</sup> He belongs to the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) and is the son-in-law of Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]]. Toktarov withdrew from public view in July 2024, and Maksat has run the day-to-day operations of the ISEZ government since then. The DPI attributes the Governor's absence to an extended health retreat and says that Maksat acts on his instructions.<sup>[2]</sup>

Before becoming Lieutenant Governor, Maksat worked in municipal infrastructure in [[Talas]], where he directed the reconstruction of the city's water treatment works, and sat on the regulatory board overseeing the telecommunications company [[Attelkom]].<sup>[3]</sup> Foreign diplomats and investors often describe him as the most technocratic member of the governing circle. Critics argue that his rise owes much to his 2008 marriage to [[Zara Toktarova]], the Governor's youngest child and only daughter.<sup>[4]</sup> As Lieutenant Governor he has run the government since 2024, during a publicly visible succession rivalry between the Governor's two eldest sons, [[Ruslan Toktarov]] and [[Alikhan Toktarov]]; he has made no public claim to the succession.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Early life and education
Maksat was born on November 3, 1980, in Kobozev (renamed [[Nurzhan]] in 1992) and was registered at birth as Aidar Maksatov.<sup>[5]</sup> His father, Bolat Maksatov, was a metallurgical engineer. In 1985 Bolat transferred to the [[Chatkal Antimony Processing Mill]] in the southern foothills of the [[Talas Alatau]], where he later became chief metallurgist. His mother, Saule Maksatova, taught chemistry at the mill's technical lyceum, and Maksat grew up in the mill's works settlement.<sup>[5]</sup> Like many urban Iradis of his generation, he began school in Russian and moved to English-language instruction after 1992 (see [[Urban Iradi Millennials]]).

He studied civil engineering at the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]] and graduated in 2002. A graduate scholarship funded by the territorial administration then took him to the [[wp:London School of Economics]], where he completed a master's degree in public policy and administration in 2005.<sup>[6]</sup> He has used the shortened form of his surname since his student years, a practice that spread among younger urban professionals during the territorial period (see [[De-Russification Movement in Irad]]).<sup>[5]</sup>

## Career

#### Municipal and infrastructure posts (2005–2019)
In 2005 Maksat joined the territorial Office of Economic Planning in Talas, where he worked on public–private partnerships for utilities.<sup>[3]</sup> He stayed in the civil service through the crisis of 2006–2007. When DPI forces took control of Talas in the summer of 2007, he became deputy head of the city's utilities department, and he joined the DPI in 2008.<sup>[4]</sup>

From 2009 to 2016 he was Director-General of Infrastructure Development for the municipality of Talas. His main project was the reconstruction of the [[Talas Municipal Water Treatment Works]] and the replacement of Soviet-era water mains, financed with concessional loans. Municipal figures state that losses from the distribution network fell by about a third over the period.<sup>[3]</sup> Opposition council members questioned the award of some construction contracts without open tender, but no wrongdoing was established.<sup>[7]</sup>

From 2012 to 2019 Maksat also sat on the ISEZ telecommunications regulatory board. The board oversaw private investment in the [[Attelkom Fiber Backbone|fiber trunk lines]] linking Talas, Nurzhan and the northern border crossings, and Attelkom reported that broadband had reached about 70% of urban households by 2019.<sup>[8]</sup> He also negotiated customs pre-clearance arrangements with the Kazakh border authorities for meat exports from the [[Sukarne Irad Complex|Sukarne]] and [[Conagra Talas Beef Facility|Conagra]] facilities. In 2016 Toktarov appointed him head of the governorate's Department of Infrastructure and Communications.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Lieutenant Governor (2019–present)
In November 2019 Toktarov was re-elected with 90.31% of the vote, in an election the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]] criticized as lacking meaningful choice (see [[Elections in Irad]]). Maksat was his running mate on the joint ticket and took office as Lieutenant Governor on November 25, 2019.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[9]</sup> Maksat took charge of coordination between government departments, preparation of the budget and the ISEZ's commercial negotiations. These included talks with Kazakh delegations on transit tariffs, and with the [[wp:China National Petroleum Corporation]] (CNPC) on the proposed Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]).<sup>[10]</sup> In 2020 he chaired the governorate's emergency commission on the [[wp:COVID-19]] pandemic. He also represents the ISEZ on matters arising under the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], which is due for renewal in 2027.<sup>[10]</sup>

Independent reporting places the *de facto* executive seat at [[Boz-Korgon]] from early 2023. The government maintains that Talas remains the operational capital, and Maksat has continued to hold most of his public meetings there.<sup>[2]</sup> In November 2023, a month after the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas|student protests in Talas]], he announced the suspension of the municipal licensing surcharge that had prompted them.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Since July 2024
Since the Governor's last public appearance in July 2024, Maksat has chaired cabinet meetings and received foreign delegations. Official statements describe him as acting on the Governor's instructions, and the succession provisions of the 1992 Elective Governor Acts have not been invoked.<sup>[2]</sup> In August 2024 he addressed the diplomatic corps and representatives of foreign companies in Talas. He pledged to honor existing agreements, including the arrangements for [[Talas Air Base]], to maintain economic stability, and to pursue "pragmatic dialogue" with the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[12]</sup> In February 2025, [[wp:Donald Trump|Donald Trump's]] remarks about selling Irad to Russia provoked demonstrations (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]). The administration responded with a formal demarche through the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]], stating that Irad's sovereignty was not negotiable.<sup>[13]</sup>

Maksat's relations with his brothers-in-law have been widely reported (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]). According to exiled opposition outlets and diplomats quoted anonymously in the regional press, in August and September 2024 Ruslan Toktarov pressed for a state of emergency in Talas, which was not declared. The same sources reported disputes with Alikhan Toktarov over the budget of [[IradOne]]. None of the three men has confirmed these accounts.<sup>[4]</sup> Maksat has met Chinese investors frequently since 2022, including the CNPC delegation on the pipeline; his office describes the meetings as routine investment promotion.<sup>[10]</sup>

## Political positions
Maksat presents himself as a manager rather than an ideologue. His public positions include the following:<sup>[4]</sup>
- He welcomes foreign infrastructure investment from both Western and Chinese sources, and has described the Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline as "the largest opportunity of the decade" for the ISEZ.
- He supports expanding Attelkom's networks and moving government services online.
- He favors keeping the US basing arrangements at Talas Air Base while broadening commercial ties with [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and China (see [[Irad–China relations]]).
- He supports negotiated unification with the tribal regions in principle, without a timetable, and continued cooperation under the Naryn protocol.
- He supports the status of Iradi and English as the official languages of the ISEZ. He has opposed proposals by [[Kamran Bekzatov]] and other figures among the [[Russos of Nurzhan]] to give Russian official status again.

## Public image
Diplomatic and business observers commonly contrast Maksat's technocratic style with Ruslan Toktarov's focus on security and Alikhan Toktarov's flamboyance.<sup>[4]</sup> Within the ISEZ, the Russo faction identifies him with the Western-educated administrators in Talas.<sup>[14]</sup> Opposition commentators argue that his career shows the neopatrimonial character of the governorate, in which family ties to the Governor decide access to office. They also note that his reputation as a reformer has not extended to elections or press freedom.<sup>[9]</sup>

## Personal life
Maksat married Zara Toktarova in 2008, and they have three children.<sup>[5]</sup> She is a doctoral candidate in education at [[wp:Stanford University]], lives mostly abroad and keeps a non-political public profile. Local tabloids have occasionally reported tensions within the Toktarov family over access to the Boz-Korgon estate. These reports are unverified, and neither Maksat nor his wife has commented on them.<sup>[2]</sup> Maksat is a keen amateur cyclist and sits on the board of trustees of the [[Talas Symphony Orchestra]]. He speaks Iradi, Russian and English.<sup>[5]</sup>

### 3.7. Ruslan Toktarov
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Biographies*

**Ruslan Toktarov** (born June 22, 1978) is an Iradi security official who has commanded the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] since 2012, with the rank of major general.<sup>[1]</sup> He is the eldest son of Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]]. Through the Gendarmerie, which also includes the Special Protective Service responsible for executive security, Ruslan oversees the checkpoints on the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], internal intelligence and perimeter security at [[Talas Air Base]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Ruslan is known for nationalist rhetoric and a hard line toward the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Remarks he made about Muslims in late 2024 and early 2025 were condemned by religious leaders and human rights groups, and reinforced a reputation his critics describe as bigoted and brutish.<sup>[2]</sup> Rights organizations have criticized the Gendarmerie's treatment of detainees and its handling of the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]].<sup>[3]</sup> Since his father withdrew from public view in July 2024, Ruslan has appeared in public far more often. His rivalry over the succession with his younger brother [[Alikhan Toktarov]] has been widely reported, and many commentators doubt his ability to handle political and economic affairs.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Early life and education
Ruslan was born on June 22, 1978, in [[Talas]]. He was the first child of Erlan Toktarov, then a junior officer in the Soviet logistics corps, and his wife, [[Anara Toktarova]].<sup>[5]</sup> His father's postings, which included two tours in [[wp:Afghanistan]], meant that Ruslan spent much of his childhood with his mother in Talas. The family settled in Kobozev in 1989, when his father returned to the Iradi SSR, and moved back to Talas after 1992. Ruslan finished secondary school there in 1995, under the US territorial administration.<sup>[5]</sup>

Most children of the territorial elite went to university in the United States or Western Europe. Ruslan instead enrolled in 1995 at the Omsk Higher Tank Engineering School in Russia and graduated in 1999 as an armored-vehicle engineer.<sup>[5]</sup> He later took counter-narcotics and protective-security courses run by American private contractors at Talas Air Base.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Career

#### Early career (1999–2012)
After returning from Russia, Ruslan managed security and logistics for the family's fuel-distribution business near Talas Air Base. The business was one of the commercial concessions his father had obtained while serving in the Territorial Legislature.<sup>[6]</sup> In 2004 he joined the Republican Gendarmerie as a captain. Critics attribute his rapid promotion to family patronage.<sup>[4]</sup>

In the spring of 2007, during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], he commanded a gendarmerie detachment that guarded the [[NurGaz]] tank farm and rail yards outside [[Nurzhan]] against strikers and saboteurs. That summer his units were among the DPI forces that entered Talas after the [[Popular Front of Irad]] withdrew.<sup>[7]</sup> After the 2007 ceasefire he became deputy commander of the Gendarmerie's Border Demarcation Brigade. In that post he oversaw the construction of the fortified crossings on the Qara-Chik line, including the main [[Qara-Chik Border Checkpoint]], and set up the inspection regime that governs the movement of herders and agricultural goods between the ATR and the ISEZ.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Commander of the Republican Gendarmerie (2012–present)
In March 2012 his father appointed him commander of the Republican Gendarmerie, with the rank of major general. The force answers to the Ministry of Internal Security and has about 4,000 personnel. It comprises the Border Demarcation Brigade, the Special Protective Service, a rapid-reaction group and the Highway Patrol Corps.<sup>[1]</sup> The Special Protective Service guards the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound. Ruslan also oversees officer training at the [[Talas Gendarmerie Academy]].<sup>[1]</sup>

In October 2023, police and Gendarmerie units dispersed the student and courier protests in Talas with tear gas, water cannon and mass administrative detentions. Among those detained was the commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]], whose criticism on television had preceded the protests.<sup>[3]</sup> Ruslan defended the operation and described the protest organizers as "provocateurs paid to break windows". [[wp:Human Rights Watch]] criticized the use of force and the detention of minors.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Succession contest (2024–present)
Since July 2024 Ruslan has appeared at state ceremonies and on [[IradOne]] far more often than before. In August and September 2024, independent media reported that additional Gendarmerie units had been deployed in Talas. Exiled opposition outlets reported that he had pressed Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] to declare a state of emergency, which was not declared. Neither the Gendarmerie nor the governorate has confirmed these reports (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[4]</sup> Some analysts quoted in the regional press have speculated that he has been removing officers loyal to Alikhan from municipal police commands. The claim has not been substantiated.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Political positions
Ruslan advocates a strongly nationalist form of Iradi patriotism that he identifies closely with Christianity.<sup>[2]</sup> He opposes concessions to the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] and has called for the state to reassert its authority over the mountain watersheds. In a 2019 interview with IradOne he described the tribal regions as "a medieval relic holding back national industrial progress".<sup>[8]</sup> He has rejected the IAC's demand for parity in a unified legislature and has called its spokesman, [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], "a horse thief with a fax machine".<sup>[4]</sup> He has said little in public about economic policy or foreign relations, and commentators often point out this gap.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Controversies

#### Remarks about Muslims
In December 2024 Ruslan spoke at a graduation ceremony at the Talas Gendarmerie Academy, which IradOne broadcast live. He described Muslim communities in the Fergana Rim as "guests who have forgotten whose house they live in", and said that mosques receiving foreign funding should be "closed without discussion".<sup>[2]</sup> In a television interview the following month, he repeated the popular prejudice that eating horsemeat is a "Muslim" custom and used it to question the loyalty of Muslim citizens. Ethnographers note that this prejudice ignores Kazakh and Kyrgyz practice (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]).<sup>[2]</sup> Official estimates put Muslims at about 18% of the population (see [[Islam in Irad]]).

Sunni clerics in Nurzhan and the Fergana Rim condemned the remarks, as did human rights groups. The Archbishop of [[Iyık Janyl]] called for "restraint in words that wound neighbors" (see [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]]).<sup>[9]</sup> A spokesman for the Department of Public Information said the remarks reflected Ruslan's "personal views" and not government policy. Ruslan did not retract them.<sup>[9]</sup>

#### Allegations of abuses
Human Rights Watch and [[wp:Amnesty International]] have documented allegations of torture, forced confessions and arbitrary seizure of property by units under Ruslan's command. Most of the allegations concern the Qara-Chik crossings and pre-trial detention in Talas, and both organizations have called for targeted sanctions against him.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[10]</sup> The Gendarmerie rejects the allegations and points to its role in protecting pipelines and industrial infrastructure (see [[Human rights in Irad]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Business interests
In 2019, exiled Iradi journalists published investigative reports alleging that companies registered in the [[wp:British Virgin Islands]] and linked to Ruslan held contracts for perimeter security, diesel supply and construction services used by foreign contractors at Talas Air Base. Ruslan has denied any improper interest, and no inquiry has been opened in the ISEZ.<sup>[11]</sup>

## Personal life
Ruslan is married to Aiperi Toktarova (née Abdyldaeva), and they have three children. He lives in Talas.<sup>[5]</sup> He is a member of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] and often refers to his faith in speeches. Like his father, he is a keen horseman, and he plays for a Gendarmerie [[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|ulak tartysh]] team.<sup>[5]</sup>

### 3.8. Alikhan Toktarov
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Biographies*

**Alikhan Toktarov** (born September 14, 1980) is an Iradi businessman and media executive who has chaired the board of the state broadcaster [[IradOne]] since 2015.<sup>[1]</sup> He is the second son of Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]]. Through his company, the Steppe Luxury Group, he also holds interests in commercial property in [[Talas]] and in franchises importing luxury goods.<sup>[2]</sup>

Alikhan has cultivated a cosmopolitan, Western-leaning public image that contrasts with that of his elder brother, the Gendarmerie commander [[Ruslan Toktarov]]. He has been the subject of repeated public scandals over his spending, and over allegations, which he denies, that he enriched himself from public revenues.<sup>[3]</sup> Since July 2024, when his father withdrew from public view, he has become much more visible and is one of the two main figures in the family's openly reported rivalry over the succession. His public appearances have drawn criticism and have done little to change his reputation for irresponsibility.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Early life and education
Alikhan was born on September 14, 1980, in Talas. He was the second child of Erlan Toktarov and [[Anara Toktarova]].<sup>[5]</sup> He grew up mainly in Talas and, from 1989, in Kobozev (renamed [[Nurzhan]] in 1992), and was eleven when the [[First Iradi Civil War]] began. Under the US territorial administration he attended an English-language preparatory school in Talas.<sup>[5]</sup> He studied international business in [[wp:Bishkek]] from 1998 to 2000, then moved to [[wp:London]], where in 2003 he completed a bachelor's degree in media and management at [[wp:Regent's University London|Regent's College]] (now Regent's University London).<sup>[5]</sup>

## Business career
Alikhan returned to Irad in 2003 and worked for his father's bonded-warehousing business near [[Talas Air Base]].<sup>[2]</sup> In 2008, the year after the ISEZ was created, he founded the Steppe Luxury Group. The company obtained exclusive regional import rights for European cars, Swiss watches and American consumer electronics.<sup>[2]</sup> From 2011 he acquired municipal land along the central avenue of Talas and built office towers, hotels and leisure venues aimed at foreign contractors and energy executives. Several of these projects were joint ventures with his younger brother [[Adil Toktarov]].<sup>[2]</sup> Opposition council members and investigative journalists have argued that the land was allocated without competitive auction; the municipality says the transfers were lawful.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Chairman of IradOne
In June 2015 Erlan Toktarov appointed Alikhan chairman of the executive board of IradOne, the state broadcaster created under the Public Broadcasting Act of 1993.<sup>[1]</sup> Under his chairmanship, the network made the following changes:
- It moved from analog to digital broadcasting and launched youth, equestrian sport and streaming services.
- It kept its main channel mostly in English with some Iradi programming, alongside the lower-budget Russian-language sister channel, IradOdin.<sup>[1]</sup>
- It promoted domestic popular music, including the [[Mor-Wave]] scene and [[Mor-Pop]].<sup>[7]</sup>
- It bought broadcasting rights to the [[Altyn-Zhylky Awards]] and to the annual President's Cup in [[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|ulak tartysh]] at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]].
- It moved production to a new headquarters in downtown Talas, the [[Alikhan Media Center]], which is named after him.

Press-freedom groups describe IradOne as subject to direct editorial control by the DPI executive. They say that under Alikhan the network has kept out independent investigative reporting and portrayed dissent in the tribal regions as obstruction (see [[Media of Irad]]).<sup>[8]</sup> The network has nevertheless continued to give airtime to some critics of the government, most prominently the commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]], whom it began booking again for panel programs in 2024, a few months after the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas|2023 protests in Talas]]. IradOne says its bookings are editorial decisions.<sup>[9]</sup>

Since July 2024 IradOne has been the only outlet for the Governor's audio and video addresses, which have been the subject of authenticity claims that officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[4]</sup>

## Succession rivalry
Since mid-2024 Alikhan has appeared more often at public events and on his own network. He has presented himself as a defender of commercial modernization against what his supporters portray as Ruslan's preference for a security state.<sup>[4]</sup> Regional and exiled media have reported disputes between Alikhan and Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] over IradOne's budget and over control of state broadcasting during the Governor's absence. None of the parties has confirmed these reports (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[4]</sup>

In February 2025, [[wp:Donald Trump|Donald Trump's]] remarks about selling Irad to Russia set off demonstrations and widespread anti-American feeling (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]). Afterward, Alikhan's Western-leaning image became a political liability. [[FreeIrad.org]] claimed that lobbyists retained by his associates had brought Irad to the attention of Trump's circle in late 2024; IradOne Media Group called the claim "fiction". No links between him and the US executive branch have been substantiated.<sup>[10]</sup>

## Political positions
Alikhan rarely gives policy interviews, and his views are known mainly from speeches at business forums and from IradOne's editorial line.<sup>[3]</sup>
- He presents himself as a champion of consumer-led growth, foreign retail franchises and investment in tourism and entertainment in Talas.
- He has spoken in favor of English-language media and of keeping Irad "open to the world". He has also criticized calls by [[Kamran Bekzatov]] and other figures among the [[Russos of Nurzhan]] for accession to the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]].
- He has avoided detailed positions on the tribal regions or on unification, beyond support for the government's referendum proposals.

## Public image
Alikhan's public persona, which includes a large social-media following, imported sports cars and frequent travel abroad, has made him the best-known member of the family among young urban Iradis.<sup>[3]</sup> The same image has drawn hostility in industrial Nurzhan and among older voters, and analysts quoted in the regional press regard it as a weakness in the succession contest. Supporters credit him with modernizing IradOne's entertainment and sports output.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Controversies
- In 2018, exiled Iradi journalists reported that Alikhan had bought three beachfront villas in [[wp:Dubai]] worth more than US$18 million. They alleged that the purchases were financed with telecommunications licensing fees. Alikhan said he had paid for the properties with income from his businesses, and no charges have been brought in the ISEZ.<sup>[11]</sup>
- In 2021, leaked video of Alikhan placing large bets at a casino in [[wp:Macau]] circulated widely on social media. It provoked criticism in Nurzhan, where industrial wages had stagnated. His office called the footage a private matter recorded without consent.<sup>[3]</sup>
- Tabloids and opposition outlets have repeatedly published rumors of substance abuse and reckless spending, which Alikhan has not addressed publicly.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Personal life
In 2010 Alikhan married Madina Toktarova (née Iskakova), a former IradOne presenter. They have two children and divorced in 2017.<sup>[5]</sup> He divides his time between Talas and homes abroad. He owns racehorses and collects classic cars.<sup>[3]</sup>

### 3.9. Kamran Bekzatov
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Biographies*

**Kamran Bekzatov** (born January 28, 1951) is an Iradi businessman and petroleum engineer who has headed [[NurGaz]], the Nurzhan Oil and Gas Company, since its privatization in 1993.<sup>[1]</sup> He is widely described as an oligarch. He is also the best-known member of the "Russos", the generation of urban Iradis who were educated in Russian during the Soviet period and are centered on [[Nurzhan]] (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

NurGaz reports output of about 120,000 barrels of crude oil per day, although independent estimates are lower. Most of the oil is exported north through the 85-kilometer [[Northern Export Feeder]] into the Kazakh pipeline network near [[wp:Shymkent]].<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[4]</sup> Bekzatov is an outspoken critic of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]]'s economic policy and of the ISEZ's reliance on American security guarantees. He has called for closer ties with Russia, accession to the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]] and the restoration of Russian as an official language. In recent years he has also drawn attention for open praise of [[wp:Vladimir Putin]].<sup>[2]</sup> Claims surface from time to time that he plotted against the DPI leadership. Most analysts dismiss them as palace intrigue, and he denies them.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Early life and education
Bekzatov was born on January 28, 1951, in Kobozev, as Nurzhan was then called, two years after oil was discovered in the lower Chu basin (see [[Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)]]).<sup>[6]</sup> His father, Rashid Bekzatov, was chief engineer at the Kobozev Heavy Machine Plant (now the [[Nurzhan Heavy Machine Plant]]). His mother, Valentina Bekzatova, was a physician at the city polyclinic. He went to Russian-language schools, and in 1969 he entered the Gubkin Moscow Institute of the Petrochemical and Gas Industry. He graduated in 1974 as a petroleum reservoir engineer.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Soviet career (1974–1991)
From 1974 to 1988 Bekzatov worked in the West Siberian oilfields under the USSR Ministry of the Oil Industry. He rose from field engineer to chief engineer of a production unit near [[wp:Nizhnevartovsk]].<sup>[6]</sup> In 1988 he returned to the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] as general director of *Iradneft*, the republic's oil production trust. He introduced water-injection secondary recovery and new rotary drilling equipment to the aging fields of the [[Chu River Basin]].<sup>[7]</sup>

## First Civil War and privatization (1991–2006)
In October 1991 the [[Communist Party of Irad]] declared the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI) in Kobozev. Iradneft's fields and refinery lay in SRI-held territory, and Bekzatov stayed on as director. Accounts of his conduct during the [[First Iradi Civil War]] differ.<sup>[8]</sup> His supporters credit him with keeping fuel flowing to Kobozev's power plants and hospitals and with protecting installations from sabotage. Some veterans of the Provisional Government say that Iradneft also supplied SRI forces until US and Iradi forces captured the city in August 1992, during [[Operation Starlight]]. Bekzatov kept his post under the territorial administration.<sup>[8]</sup>

In May 1993, under the territorial administration's privatization program, Iradneft was converted into NurGaz. Managers and employees received a majority of the shares, and Bekzatov emerged as the controlling shareholder.<sup>[9]</sup> A 1997 report by the territorial auditor argued that the assets had been undervalued. Bekzatov has described the transaction as "the only privatization in Irad that kept the workers on the payroll".<sup>[9]</sup> During the 1990s NurGaz formed technical partnerships with [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron]] and Lukoil.<sup>[1]</sup>

Irad has no southern export pipelines, so its oil exports go north. In 1998 NurGaz and foreign partners formed the [[Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium]] (IPTC), in which NurGaz holds a 51% stake, and the consortium's Northern Export Feeder opened in November 2000. The 85-kilometer pipeline carries crude from NurGaz's tank farm outside Nurzhan to the Kazakh trunk network near Shymkent. From there the oil is refined in [[wp:Kazakhstan]] or shipped on to Russia under transit arrangements with [[wp:KazTransOil]]. The route does not use the [[wp:Caspian Pipeline Consortium]], whose system begins far to the west.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Relations with the DPI (2007–present)
In January and February 2007, the government of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] auctioned concessions in NurGaz's fields to political insiders (see [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]). Bekzatov refused to recognize the sales and worked with trade unions to guard the perimeters of the refinery and pipeline.<sup>[10]</sup> On February 25, 2007, [[Erlan Toktarov]] proclaimed the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] from Nurzhan, after making public the secret partition drafts known as the [[Bill of Sale (2007)|Bill of Sale]]. Bekzatov was one of the proclamation's most important backers. NurGaz supplied money and fuel, refinery workers guarded the oil installations, and the Labor-led Nurzhan city council, close to Bekzatov, placed the municipal police at Toktarov's disposal (see [[Labor Party of Irad]]). Bekzatov publicly called Toktarov "a national hero who saved Irad from being sold".<sup>[10]</sup> Many historians argue that the ISEZ could not have been established without his support; others say that Bekzatov, whose fields were among the assets the DRI was auctioning, was protecting NurGaz as much as the country.<sup>[5]</sup> NurGaz's assets were confirmed under the new administration.

Relations cooled as Toktarov's rule hardened. Bekzatov criticized the Political Parties Act of 2010, under which the Labor Party failed to re-register and was dissolved in 2011, and the 2013 amendment that removed the Governor's term limits, which he called "the end of what we fought for in 2007". Since then he has been the most prominent critic of the governorate among the Russos.<sup>[2]</sup>

The relationship has since combined commercial cooperation with public criticism. The DPI counts NurGaz among its industrial partners, and NurGaz is the ISEZ's largest commercial taxpayer. Bekzatov, however, has attacked the governorate's fiscal policy and the business monopolies held by the Toktarov family.<sup>[2]</sup> He has never stood for office. He leads the Russo faction informally, through his public statements, his business network and the Bekzatov Foundation, which funds Russian-language schools, technical apprenticeships and cultural centers in Nurzhan.<sup>[2]</sup>

DPI-aligned commentators and anonymous social-media channels have periodically circulated claims that he plotted against the DPI leadership. The claims were never corroborated. Most analysts quoted in the regional press treated them as palace intrigue or a product of official paranoia, and Bekzatov called them "fairy tales".<sup>[5]</sup> In February 2025 [[wp:Donald Trump]] spoke about selling Irad to Russia (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]). Bekzatov rejected the idea of any "sale" of Irad and argued that the episode showed the risks of depending on Washington.<sup>[11]</sup>

## Political positions
Bekzatov's public positions include the following:<sup>[2]</sup>
- He favors accession to the Eurasian Economic Union, to remove customs duties on refined oil products and steel, and closer trade with Kazakhstan and Russia (see [[Irad–Russia relations]]).
- He wants Russian, which lost its official status in 1992, restored as an official language alongside Iradi and English.
- He criticizes the ISEZ's dependence on American security guarantees and the US presence at [[Talas Air Base]].
- He calls for lower export duties, an end to family-controlled monopolies in logistics and construction, and state support for heavy industry in Nurzhan.
- He supports developing Irad's gas reserves, including through the proposed Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline, provided NurGaz keeps operating control (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]).

## Public image and controversies
Bekzatov is popular among older residents of Nurzhan. NurGaz and the Bekzatov Foundation fund housing there, including the [[NurGaz Worker Housing Complex]], as well as pensions, cardiac clinics, parks and scholarships at the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]].<sup>[2]</sup> Regional business publications estimate his fortune at more than US$1 billion, but NurGaz has not fully disclosed its ownership structure.<sup>[2]</sup> Critics point to three issues:<sup>[3]</sup>
- the terms of the 1993 privatization;
- soil and groundwater contamination around the lower Chu fields, which NurGaz says it is cleaning up;
- the gap between NurGaz's reported output and independent estimates.

Younger urban Iradis and supporters of the revival of the Iradi language have criticized his campaign for Russian. DPI-aligned commentators portray him as a channel for Russian influence.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Personal life
Bekzatov is married to Galina Bekzatova (née Lebedeva), a retired pediatrician. They have three children, all of whom work in the family's business or philanthropy:<sup>[13]</sup>
- [[Emil Bekzatov]], the eldest son, is an executive vice-president of NurGaz responsible for pipeline logistics and for crude transport contracts with Kazakhstan.
- [[Rustam Bekzatov]] manages the group's road and rail tanker fleet between Nurzhan and southern Kazakhstan.
- [[Asel Bekzatova]] founded and directs the Nurzhan Contemporary Arts Foundation, which supports young musicians.

He lives in Nurzhan, and he speaks Russian at home and in most public settings.<sup>[13]</sup>

### 3.10. Jason Ilyasov
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Biographies*

**Jason Ilyasov** (born March 19, 1988) is an Iradi-American political commentator and media personality. A self-described anarcho-capitalist, he has been a regular panelist on [[IradOne]] opinion programs since 2016 and is known for a brash, confrontational manner on air.<sup>[1]</sup> He founded the Liberty Forum of Irad, a small advocacy group in [[Talas]], writes the blog *The Steppe Frontier*, and is the founder, chief executive and public face of [[Tulpar (app)|Tulpar]], a messaging and classifieds app widely used in the ISEZ. His central argument is that Irad is uniquely placed to become "an unregulated corporate paradise and the economic heart of Asia".<sup>[2]</sup>

Ilyasov was first known as a television personality, but his following turned political in 2023. In October that year he criticized a new municipal levy on air, and spontaneous student protests followed in Talas, during which he was briefly detained.<sup>[3]</sup> Media analysts generally attribute his rise to organic online engagement and to frustration among young urban Iradis. He says his work is self-funded, and speculation about political patrons has not been substantiated.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Early life and education
Ilyasov was born on March 19, 1988, in [[wp:Chicago]], [[wp:Illinois]]. His father, Marat Ilyasov, a hydrologist from Talas, was working there as a visiting researcher under a Soviet–American academic exchange.<sup>[5]</sup> The family returned to Talas in 1989. His parents were active in the democratic movement around [[Amanbek Alimbekov]]. In January 1992, as the socialist authorities arrested opposition activists in Talas during the [[Winter Uprising of 1992|Winter Uprising]], they left Irad. Because Jason was a US citizen by birth, the family was able to resettle in Chicago, where his mother, Dinara Ilyasova, later worked as a nurse.<sup>[5]</sup>

He grew up in Chicago's Iradi community (see [[Iradi Americans]]), speaking Iradi at home and English at school; he speaks little Russian.<sup>[5]</sup> He studied economics at the [[wp:University of Chicago]] and graduated with a bachelor's degree in 2010. He has named the [[wp:Austrian school of economics|Austrian School]] and anarcho-capitalist writers as his main intellectual influences.<sup>[2]</sup> From 2010 to 2013 he worked as an energy-markets analyst in [[wp:Houston]]. He then spent two years in Washington, D.C., writing policy papers on property rights in post-communist economies for libertarian think tanks.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Career

#### Move to Talas and IradOne (2016–2023)
Ilyasov moved to Talas in early 2016. He has said he was attracted by the ISEZ's dollarized economy and unresolved legal status, which he saw as the chance to build "a jurisdiction without a sovereign".<sup>[2]</sup> That year he founded the Liberty Forum of Irad. He was soon recruited as an economics commentator for *OneDebate*, IradOne's prime-time current-affairs panel, and he has appeared regularly on the network's opinion programs since then.<sup>[1]</sup> His appearances often involve shouting matches with other panelists, and he has occasionally walked off the set. IradOne has suspended him at least twice for his conduct on air.<sup>[1]</sup>

His main lines of argument, on air and online, have been these:
- He has attacked the business concessions held by Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]]'s family, arguing that licensing rules protect insider monopolies in fuel distribution, construction and telecommunications.<sup>[6]</sup>
- He has called for cryptocurrency to be made legal tender alongside the US dollar and the [[Iradi som]], and for [[wp:Bitcoin]] mining powered by surplus electricity from the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]].<sup>[7]</sup>
- He has proposed giving freely tradable private titles to pasture in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. The [[Iradi Freedom Society]] has rejected the idea as an attack on communal tenure.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Tulpar (since 2019)
In March 2019 Ilyasov launched Tulpar, a free messaging app built in Talas by a small team of [[Talas State University]] graduates led by the developer [[Aiperi Kalysbekova]], its chief technology officer. The company, Tulpar Technologies, is registered in [[wp:Delaware]] with its office in Talas. Ilyasov describes himself as its "salesman" rather than its engineer. Tulpar added public channels in 2020 and a classifieds board, *Bazar*, in 2021, and Ilyasov has used its channels as his main platform alongside IradOne.<sup>[12]</sup>

#### 2023 protests and detention
In October 2023 the Talas municipal administration announced a licensing surcharge on independent technology startups and delivery couriers, a category that included his own company. On *OneDebate*, Ilyasov called it "a tax on the only part of the economy the family doesn't own yet", and clips of the exchange spread widely on social media.<sup>[3]</sup> Within days, students and couriers gathered at Talas State University and marched on the government district (see [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]]). When several foreign social-media platforms were throttled, many protesters moved their group chats to Tulpar, which was not on the list, and the app's registrations roughly doubled that autumn.<sup>[12]</sup> Most observers described the protests as spontaneous and leaderless. The authorities said that Ilyasov had instigated them, citing a speech he gave to the protesters on the third day in which he urged them not to pay the levy.<sup>[3]</sup>

Police and [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad|Republican Gendarmerie]] units under Major General [[Ruslan Toktarov]] dispersed the protests with tear gas and water cannon. Ilyasov was held in administrative detention for three days. After press-freedom groups criticized his detention, he was released without charge.<sup>[8]</sup> In November 2023 Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] announced that the surcharge would be suspended.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Since 2024
IradOne kept Ilyasov off the air for several months after the protests and began booking him again in 2024. He also broadcasts through podcasts and social-media channels.<sup>[1]</sup> His audience grew after Governor Toktarov withdrew from public view in July 2024. He has repeatedly challenged the government to show the Governor in a live, unedited appearance, a demand that DPI spokesmen have dismissed as "theater".<sup>[4]</sup> In February 2025 [[wp:Donald Trump]] spoke about selling Irad to Russia (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]). Ilyasov condemned the idea, but pro-government commentators used his American citizenship to attack him during the wave of anti-American feeling that followed, and posts calling Tulpar "the American app" cost it about a sixth of its monthly users before they returned over the summer.<sup>[9]</sup>

#### Tulparcoin (2026)
In September 2026 Ilyasov launched [[Tulparcoin]], a cryptocurrency distributed free in small amounts to Iradis who registered with a mobile number. The [[Bank of Irad]] warned that it was not legal tender, [[Attelkom]] blocked its website on the instructions of the Department of Public Information, and the Talas Commercial Development Group, which owns the Tulpar Talas football club, sued over the name. The case was unresolved as of October 2026.<sup>[11]</sup>

The coin's free allocation is paid into wallets inside the Tulpar app, and the white paper's second phase would make the coin the only currency of *Tulpar Pay*, the app's payment service, from 1 January 2027, replacing the som for transfers, *Bazar* purchases and remittances on the platform. Ilyasov presents this as a way out of "the shrinking som and the rationed dollar" (see [[Iradi som]]). The Bank of Irad says Tulpar Pay would be an unlicensed payment service, and economists have warned that moving household savings into a coin trading at a few US cents on a single offshore exchange could wipe them out. Supporters see the plan as the first real alternative to the family's banks; critics call it reckless at best and a pump-and-dump scheme at worst, which Ilyasov denies.<sup>[13]</sup>

## Funding and independence
Ilyasov says that the Liberty Forum and his media work are funded by subscriptions, advertising and speaking fees and by his income from Tulpar, which sells advertising and paid channels, and that he takes no money from politicians.<sup>[4]</sup> Tulpar Technologies has not disclosed its investors, saying only that early funding came from "private angel investors".<sup>[12]</sup> His continued access to IradOne after the 2023 protests has prompted speculation from different directions:
- Pro-government commentators have accused him of receiving American money.
- Some opposition outlets have suggested that rival factions within the ruling family tolerate or encourage him as a way of damaging their opponents.

A 2024 survey of the ISEZ media in the *Eurasian Political Quarterly* found no documentary evidence for either claim. It attributed his rise to online engagement and to frustration with economic stagnation among young urban Iradis.<sup>[4]</sup> Ilyasov denies all such claims.

## Public image
Ilyasov's following is concentrated among university students and young urban professionals, especially in Talas (see [[Iradi language revival]]).<sup>[10]</sup> Critics describe him as a provocateur whose proposals ignore the realities of a divided territory. Tribal leaders reject his views on land, and older Russian-speaking Iradis in [[Nurzhan]] generally regard him as an outsider.<sup>[10]</sup> His supporters credit him with bringing corruption and monopolies onto state television as subjects of debate.

## Personal life
Ilyasov lives in Talas and is unmarried. He holds US citizenship.<sup>[5]</sup>

### 3.11. Nurzhan
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Cities & Settlements*

{{Hatnote|This article is about the city in Irad. For the Bolshevik after whom the city was named from 1927 to 1992, see [[Pyotr Kobozev]].}}

**Nurzhan** (Iradi: *Nurzhan shahr*, [nuɾˈʒɑn ʃɑhɾ]; Russian: *Нуржан*; known as *Kobozev* from 1927 to 1992) is the second-largest city of [[Irad]] and the center of its oil, refining and engineering industries. It lies in the lowlands of the lower Chu River in the northeast of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), where it is one of three cities of governorate significance. The official 2018 estimate put the urban population at 850,000, with more than one million people in the wider agglomeration by unofficial estimates.<sup>[1]</sup>

The city grew from an older artisan settlement that was renamed Kobozev in 1927 and rebuilt as the planned capital of the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]]. It expanded rapidly after oil was found in the Chu basin in 1949. In 1991–92 it was the seat of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]]; after its capture by US and Provisional forces in August 1992, [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] renamed it Nurzhan, a Kipchak-Turkic name meaning 'light of life'.<sup>[2]</sup> Nurzhan is the headquarters of [[NurGaz]] and the center of the Russian-speaking *Russo* generation, and in February 2007 it was the base from which [[Erlan Toktarov]] proclaimed the ISEZ. Since early 2023 independent reporting has placed the *de facto* seat of the ISEZ executive at [[Boz-Korgon]], a fortified compound 40 km east of the city, although the government maintains that Talas remains the operational capital.<sup>[3]</sup> The city is also known as the birthplace of [[Mor-Wave]], an electronic music scene that emerged in its disused factories in the late 1990s.

## Etymology
*Nurzhan* joins *nur* ('light') and *zhan* ('soul, life'), two elements common to the Kipchak-Turkic languages, and is usually glossed 'light of life'. Unlike many Central Asian place names of the same form, it does not commemorate a person. Alimbekov announced the name in a speech in the city in September 1992, describing Nurzhan as a "guiding light of industry" that would lead Irad to a new dawn after the ruin of the civil war. The Territorial Legislature confirmed the change in the Toponymic Heritage Statute of 1993.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

From 1927 to 1992 the city bore the name of [[Pyotr Kobozev]], a Bolshevik who had served in the early Soviet administration of [[wp:Turkestan]]. Older residents and Russian-language media still sometimes use the Soviet name informally.

## History

#### Early settlement
Slag heaps and forge remains found in the Dastgoh quarter point to a medieval settlement of metalworkers on a branch of the Chu, which Iradi historians associate with Sogdian craftsmen of the [[wp:Silk Road]] period.<sup>[5]</sup> By the late 19th century the site was a small trading village on the cart road between the Chu and Talas valleys, partly settled by Russian and Ukrainian colonists after the Russian conquest of the region.

#### Soviet capital (1925–1941)
In 1925 the [[First Congress of Iradi Soviets]] met in the settlement and established the administrative apparatus of the new Iradi SSR.<sup>[6]</sup> In 1927 the settlement was renamed Kobozev and designated the republican capital, and Soviet planners laid out a new city on the steppe beside the old quarter. The plan followed a radial grid centered on a ceremonial square, with broad avenues aligned to catch the cool night winds from the mountains and canals drawn from the [[Chu Irrigation Canal System|Chu irrigation network]].<sup>[5]</sup>

Early industry centered on the Kobozev Heavy Machine Plant (now the [[Nurzhan Heavy Machine Plant]]), which made tractor parts and agricultural machinery. The educator [[Altynai Bekzhanova]] opened the city's first Iradi-language secondary schools in the 1930s, the radio engineer [[Nurgazy Doolotov]] built its first shortwave transmission tower in 1934, and in 1938 [[Vasily Morozov]] drove the first diesel freight train into Kobozev from [[wp:Kazakhstan]].<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Deportations and the Second World War (1937–1945)
In autumn 1937 several thousand [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]], ethnic Koreans deported from the [[wp:Russian Far East|Soviet Far East]], were unloaded from unheated freight cars along the railway at Kobozev and in the Chu valley.<sup>[8]</sup> Local villagers and church communities sheltered many of them through the first winter, and from 1938 Korean collective farmers grew flood-irrigated rice on the marshy flats of the lower Chu.<sup>[9]</sup> The same years brought the anti-religious campaign of the late 1930s: Soviet suspicion of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]'s 1931 union with Rome fed the [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges|1937 purges]], and the churches of the capital were closed.<sup>[6]</sup> After the German invasion of 1941, factories evacuated from [[wp:European Russia]] were re-established in Kobozev, enlarging its metalworking and engineering base.

#### Oil city (1949–1991)
In July 1949 exploratory drilling directed by the engineer [[Sergei Voronov]] struck high-pressure oil-bearing strata at a depth of about 2,400 m, an event known as the [[Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)]].<sup>[10]</sup> Within a decade [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]] and the [[Nurzhan Petroleum Pipeline]] had been built, and the city supplied aviation fuel and lubricants to the Soviet armed forces. In the [[1952 Kobozev Steel Strike]], Iradi and [[wp:Koryo-saram]] foundry workers struck over wages and safety until local party authorities conceded production bonuses.<sup>[11]</sup>

A master plan drawn up in 1962 by the architect [[Bolotbek Bekzhanov]] added the numbered *mikrorayon* housing estates where much of the population still lives.<sup>[11]</sup> Nurzhan Refinery No. 1 was expanded in 1974, and in the 1970s the Koryo-saram engineer [[Oleg Kim]] directed the city's tractor combine. Flour shortages in 1989 led to the [[The Kobozev Bread Riots (1989)|Kobozev Bread Riots]], in which marchers demanded economic accountability, freedom of religion and elected government.<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Independence and the First Civil War (1991–1992)
On 9 October 1991 the [[Communist Party of Irad]], led by First Secretary [[Vasily Chernov]], a former engineer at the Kobozev refinery, declared independence as the Socialist Republic of Irad, governed from Kobozev.<sup>[12]</sup> The end of Soviet subsidies shut many of the city's plants, and on 14 January 1992 railway repair workers at the Kobozev depot declared a general strike that helped set off the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]].<sup>[13]</sup>

After the Provisional Government proclaimed the Republic of Irad in [[Talas]] on 11 March 1992, Kobozev became the headquarters of the socialist war effort in the [[First Iradi Civil War]]. As US and Provisional forces advanced on the city in the summer of 1992, Provisional units repelled a socialist armored counter-attack in the [[Battle of Kobozev Railhead (1992)]]. Joint US and Iradi forces under [[Operation Starlight]] took Kobozev on 12 August, and the remaining socialist forces surrendered on 18 August.<sup>[12]</sup> Alimbekov renamed the city the following month.

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
Under the US territorial administration the Soviet oil trust *Iradneft* was corporatized in 1993 as NurGaz, led by the Kobozev-born petroleum engineer [[Kamran Bekzatov]].<sup>[14]</sup> The city's managers and technicians, educated in Russian under the ISSR, became the core of the *Russo* generation, and Russo politicians held most seats on the municipal council throughout the period.<sup>[15]</sup> The 1990s and 2000s brought the suburban district of Navbahor, the [[Attelkom Data Center]] and a fuel pipeline from the refinery to [[Talas Air Base]]. Construction of the Boz-Korgon compound east of the city began in the mid-1990s.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Since 2007
After the US withdrawal of December 2006 and the proclamation of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] in Talas, Nurzhan became the base of the faction of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) opposed to Prime Minister [[Bakyt Orozbekov]]. On 25 February 2007, in a speech in the city's central square, Toktarov made public the cabinet's secret partition drafts (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]), seized control of the DPI and proclaimed the Irad Special Economic Zone from Nurzhan as an emergency administration, the act that marks the start of the [[Second Iradi Civil War]].<sup>[16]</sup> Loyalties in the city divided during the war: rail workers joined strikes called in support of the [[Popular Front of Irad]], while NurGaz guards held the refinery and Bekzatov refused to recognize the asset auctions of the Orozbekov cabinet.<sup>[14]</sup><sup>[16]</sup> After the Front withdrew from the cities in the summer of 2007, Nurzhan became one of the three cities of governorate significance of the ISEZ.

Since early 2023, independent reporting has placed the governor's office at Boz-Korgon, in the countryside east of the city; the government denies this and maintains that Talas is the operational capital.<sup>[3]</sup> Bekzatov, the city's most prominent public figure, has become an outspoken critic of DPI economic policy and an advocate of closer ties with Russia.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Geography
Nurzhan lies at an elevation of 740 m (2,430 ft) on the flat alluvial plain of the lower [[Chu River Basin|Chu River]], and is surrounded by Chu District, the ISEZ district that covers the Chu lowlands and the Boz-Korgon area. To the north, irrigated farmland gives way to the semi-desert grassland of the southern [[Betpak-Dala]]; the dunes of the Moiynkum (Muyunkum) sands lie to the south and west, between the Chu and the Talas. On clear days the snow-covered Kyrgyz Range is visible across the border to the south. The Chu is an endorheic river that ends in the desert, and the city's water comes from it and from the canals of the [[Chu Irrigation Canal System]]. A small urban canal, [[Dastgoh Creek]], was diverted in the Soviet period to drive forge hammers.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Climate
Nurzhan has a cold semi-arid climate ([[wp:Köppen climate classification|Köppen]] *BSk*) with large seasonal swings. July daytime highs average 34 °C (93 °F), and summer dust storms are common. The January mean is −10.5 °C (13.1 °F), and Arctic air outbreaks occasionally bring temperatures below −25 °C (−13 °F). Annual precipitation averages about 270 mm (10.6 in), most of it falling in spring.<sup>[17]</sup>

Winter smog, caused by refinery and bitumen emissions together with coal-fired district heating, is a recurring problem. NurGaz says it has fitted stack scrubbers and built gas-fired cogeneration units since the late 2010s; a [[wp:World Bank]] assessment of Central Asian industrial cities found that winter particulate levels in Nurzhan remained well above international guidelines.<sup>[10]</sup><sup>[18]</sup>

#### Cityscape and districts
The city's urban districts include:
- **[[Dastgoh District]]**, the historic artisan and foundry quarter, with metal workshops, boiler fabrication sheds, toolmakers and the [[NurGaz Pipeline Maintenance Depot]].
- **[[Navbahor District]]** ('New Spring'), a leafy residential district of detached brick houses and bilingual schools built in the 1990s during the territorial period.
- The **central district** along Azatlık Prospect, laid out in the 1930s as Lenin Prospect and renamed under the 1993 statute, with granite government buildings, the [[Nurzhan Municipal Library]] and the Palace of Metallurgists.
- The **mikrorayon estates** of the 1962 master plan, numbered blocks of five- and nine-story flats grouped around courtyards, schools and heating plants.
- The **eastern industrial zone**, which contains the refinery, the Attelkom Data Center, the grain silos of [[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]] and the spinning sheds of [[Zhetysu Industrial Textiles]].

## Demographics
The official 2018 estimate gave Nurzhan an urban population of 850,000, and more than one million including satellite towns and industrial settlements along the lower Chu.<sup>[1]</sup> Because the ISEZ census enumerated cities directly, the city figure is far less disputed than the national total, which independent researchers consider an undercount of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].

According to official figures, Iradis make up about 64% of the city's population, Russians 13%, [[wp:Kazakhs]] 6%, Koryo-saram 4%, Kyrgyz 4%, Ukrainians 3%, [[wp:Uzbeks]] 2% and other groups 4%.<sup>[1]</sup> Nurzhan has the largest Russian and Koryo-saram communities in Irad. Russian is the everyday language of most residents over forty and is widely used in business, although it lost official status in 1992; Iradi and English are the official languages, and younger residents increasingly use both.<sup>[15]</sup>

The [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] is the largest denomination, and the city is the seat of one of its suffragan eparchies. Nurzhan also has Russian Orthodox parishes, Presbyterian and Catholic congregations within the Koryo-saram community, and mosques serving its Kazakh, Uzbek and Tatar residents. Surveys suggest that religious practice is lower than in Talas or [[Iyık Janyl]], a legacy of the city's Soviet secular culture.<sup>[9]</sup><sup>[15]</sup>

## Economy
Nurzhan is the center of the Iradi hydrocarbon industry.
- **Oil and gas.** NurGaz, headquartered in the city, operates the oil fields of the lower Chu basin. It reports national output of about 120,000 barrels per day, although independent estimates are lower.<sup>[10]</sup> Nurzhan Refinery No. 1 (expanded 1974) produces gasoline, diesel, heating oil and jet fuel. Crude that is not refined locally is pumped through the Nurzhan Petroleum Pipeline and the 85-km [[Northern Export Feeder]] into the Kazakh network operated by [[wp:KazTransOil]] near [[wp:Shymkent]], for refining there or onward shipment to Russia.<sup>[19]</sup> Gas from the [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]] is boosted at the [[Nurzhan Gas Compressor Station]]; most of Irad's gas reserves of more than 400 billion m³ remain undeveloped.
- **Chemicals and construction materials.** The [[Nurzhan Asphalt and Bitumen Plant]] turns residual refinery bottoms into road binders, and an adjacent chemical works makes agricultural ammonia.
- **Engineering and light industry.** The Nurzhan Heavy Machine Plant makes oilfield pumpjacks and spare parts. Zhetysu Industrial Textiles produces wool, felt and cotton thread, but its output is limited by water shortages.<sup>[20]</sup>
- **Agribusiness.** Nurzhan Agro-Industrial, directed by [[Bekzat Toktarov]], dominates feed milling, silage distribution and bulk grain supply. The surrounding farmland grows wheat, sugar beet and the short-grain rice introduced by Koryo-saram farmers in 1938.<sup>[20]</sup>
- **Logistics and data.** The [[Nurzhan Rail Freight Depot]] marshals tank cars and freight bound for the Kazakh rail network, and [[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]] runs bonded trucking routes to the northern border crossings. The Attelkom Data Center hosts government and commercial servers.

NurGaz is the city's largest employer. Through the company and the Bekzatov Foundation, Bekzatov funds subsidized housing such as the [[NurGaz Worker Housing Complex]], clinics and Russian-language schools, which analysts regard as the basis of his independent political following.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Government
Nurzhan is one of the three cities of governorate significance of the ISEZ, alongside Talas and Iyık Janyl, and is administered separately from the surrounding Chu District. The city is run by a mayor appointed by the Governor and an elected city council. Russo politicians have held a majority on the council for most of the period since 1992, and it has repeatedly clashed with the DPI administration over language policy and the city's share of oil revenue.<sup>[15]</sup> [[Elena Petrova]], a municipal leader who represents the Russian and Ukrainian industrial workforce, is among the city's best-known municipal politicians.

The 2nd Motor Rifle Brigade of the [[Armed Forces of Irad]] is based in the city and guards the refinery, pumping stations and northern rail links.

## Transport
Nurzhan is the main railway junction of the ISEZ, with lines north into the Kazakh rail network and west toward Talas; most of its traffic is freight.<sup>[20]</sup> Highways link the city with Talas, with Boz-Korgon and with the northern border crossings into Kazakhstan. City transport consists of a Soviet-era trolleybus network, municipal buses and privately run minibuses (*marshrutkas*). The city has no scheduled passenger airport; air travelers use Talas or airports in neighboring Kazakhstan.

## Education and health
- The [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]], formerly the Kobozev Polytechnic Institute, is Irad's leading technical university and specializes in petroleum engineering. It trains much of the NurGaz engineering staff.
- The [[Nurzhan Technical Lyceum]] offers vocational apprenticeships in electrical maintenance and machining.
- The [[Nurzhan Municipal Botanical Institute]] and the [[Nurzhan Botanical Gardens]] study cold-hardy fruit trees and mountain flora.
- The Nurzhan Municipal Library holds more than 300,000 volumes.
- Health services include the [[Nurzhan Children’s Polyclinic]], which has served industrial workers' families since the Soviet period, cardiac clinics endowed by Bekzatov, and the [[Nurzhan Emergency Dispatch Center]]. The [[St. Teresa Community Kitchen]], a parish soup kitchen, serves daily meals to pensioners and disabled workers.

## Culture
In the late 1990s young people gathering in abandoned warehouses and factory boiler rooms in Nurzhan began combining vintage Soviet analog synthesizers with Iradi melodies and lyrics. The style became known as Mor-Wave, and its pioneers, the duo [[tüntolk]] (Askar Daniyarov and Mira Temirova), gained an audience abroad with their 1999 debut album *[[Jolchu (Album)|Jolchu]]*.<sup>[21]</sup> The scene later produced the commercial offshoot [[Mor-Pop]], while acoustic [[Mor-Folk]] ensembles play in teahouses in Nurzhan and Talas.

Russo cultural life is centered on Russian-language theater, satellite television and the [[wp:Victory Day (9 May)|Victory Day]] parade on 9 May. The Koryo-saram community maintains cultural associations and Korean-language Sunday schools, and contributed dishes such as Koryoin laghman, noodles with sesame and kimchi, to the wider Iradi kitchen.<sup>[9]</sup> The Nurzhan Contemporary Arts Foundation, run by [[Asel Bekzatova]], supports young musicians.

Notable buildings (see [[Architecture of Irad]]) include the Palace of Metallurgists, a Soviet palace of culture on Azatlık Prospect; the central square of the 1927 plan; Nurzhan Refinery No. 1, the country's only oil refinery; and the forges of Dastgoh.

## Notable people
- [[Vasily Chernov]] (1935–2012), last First Secretary of the Communist Party of Irad and president of the Socialist Republic of Irad, born in Kobozev
- [[Kamran Bekzatov]] (born 1951), president of NurGaz, born in Kobozev
- [[Aidar Maksat]] (born 1980), Lieutenant Governor of the ISEZ since 2019, born in Kobozev
- [[Altynai Bekzhanova]], educator who founded the city's first Iradi-language secondary schools
- [[Bolotbek Bekzhanov]], architect of the 1962 master plan
- [[Oleg Kim]], Koryo-saram engineer and factory director
- [[Lyudmila Ivanova]], Russian-language novelist of the 1916 Semirechye Revolt
- Askar Daniyarov and Mira Temirova, founders of tüntolk
