### 3.12. Iyık Janyl
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Cities & Settlements*

{{Hatnote|This article is about the city in Irad. For the figure revered as the Mother of Irad, see [[Janyl (Historical Figure)]]. For the ecclesiastical jurisdiction, see [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]].}}

**Iyık Janyl** (Iradi: *Iyık Janyl shahr*, [iˈjɯk ʒɑˈnɯl ʃɑhɾ]; Russian: *Ийык Джаныл*; literally 'Holy Janyl') is the third-largest city of [[Irad]] and the religious center of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]. It lies at an elevation of 1,280 m (4,200 ft) in a side valley in the foothills of the [[wp:Fergana Range]] above the [[Naryn River|Naryn]], about 20 km north-east of [[Tash-Kömür]] and 332 km from [[Talas]] by road, and is one of the three cities of governorate significance of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It lies within the ISEZ's southern salient on the Naryn and is surrounded by the Lower Naryn District. The official 2018 estimate put its population at 310,000.<sup>[1]</sup>

The modern city grew around the [[Church of St. Janyl]], built in 1727 with the support of Khan Abd al-Rahim Biy of the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]], and Iradi historiography regards it as the first culturally Iradi city. It is the see of the [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]], which became the metropolitan see of the Syro-Iradi Catholic Church on the church's union with Rome in 1931.<sup>[2]</sup> The church and the nearby [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]] (Shar-i-Kuhna), the remains of a medieval East Syriac Christian town, were inscribed together on the [[wp:World Heritage Site|UNESCO World Heritage List]] in 2004, during the territorial period, on a nomination submitted by the United States.<sup>[3]</sup> Each year on 24 August the city hosts the [[Feast of St. Janyl]], which draws pilgrims, many of them on horseback, from both the ISEZ and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. It is also home to the [[Irad Palace of Art]], the country's principal art gallery.

## Etymology
The name pairs the Kipchak-Turkic adjective *iyık* ('sacred, holy'), cognate with [[wp:Old Turkic language|Old Turkic]] *ïduq* ('consecrated, sacred'), with the name of [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]], the figure revered in Iradi Christian tradition as the Mother of Irad.<sup>[2]</sup> In [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] liturgical texts of the Syro-Iradi Church the city is styled *Mthā d-Qaddishtā Janyl* ('the town of holy Janyl').

## History

#### The Janyl tradition
Iradi Christian tradition holds that Janyl received a vision of Jesus in the form of the White Rider, who led her to the site of the first Iradi church, known as the First Church of Janyl. Church tradition places her in the 11th century, among Christianized Turkic clans associated with the [[wp:Keraites]] and [[wp:Naimans]], whose conversion by East Syriac missionaries is attested in medieval sources; some chronicles associate her with the period of [[wp:Qara Khitai]] rule in the 12th century.<sup>[4]</sup> Modern historians widely dispute that she existed, and the location of the First Church is unknown. The Church of St. Janyl in Iyık Janyl was built in the 18th century as a symbolic re-creation of the lost church, not as a claim to its site.<sup>[2]</sup> Popular devotion in the city nonetheless links several springs and hilltops in the valley with episodes from [[The Epic of Janyl]].

#### Medieval town (11th–14th centuries)
The [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]], 3 km south of the present city on a terrace above the river canyon, preserve the foundations of a fortified Christian town of about 45 hectares that flourished from the 11th to the 14th century under the Kara-Khanid and Qara Khitai states.<sup>[5]</sup> Excavations have uncovered a citadel, a three-aisled stone basilica measuring 38 by 24 m, and a craft quarter with kilns and foundries. Grave markers (*kayraks*) inscribed in Syriac and Old Turkic, dated between 1240 and 1338, record a community of Kerait, Naiman and Sogdian Christians. The 13th-century monk [[Marqos the Scribe]] is credited with copying the illuminated *Gospels of Iyık Janyl*.<sup>[6]</sup>

The religious tolerance of the Qara Khitai period, which allowed [[wp:Church of the East|East Syriac Christianity]] to spread among the steppe peoples, ended under the later Chagatai khans.<sup>[7]</sup> After forced conversions under Ali-Sultan (1339–1342), the campaigns of [[wp:Timur]] in the late 14th century and outbreaks of plague, the town was abandoned. According to church tradition, Christian herders in the surrounding ravines kept their liturgy alive in secret, preserving chants (*ırda*) and the baking of sacramental bread.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Kokand period and the 1727 church
In the early 18th century, Iradi Christian horsemen served as cavalry auxiliaries of the Khanate of Kokand against Dzungar raids. In 1727 Khan Abd al-Rahim Biy granted the Iradi Christian elders formal religious tolerance in the accord remembered as the [[Treaty of Talas (1727)]], and issued a decree (*firman*) funding a permanent stone church.<sup>[8]</sup> Masons from [[wp:Samarkand]] and [[wp:Bukhara]] worked with local East Syriac monks to build the Church of St. Janyl on Cathedral Hill, combining a Syriac basilica plan with Central Asian brick vaulting and a ribbed dome clad in turquoise tiles.<sup>[6]</sup> A settlement of clergy, craftsmen and herders grew around it and became the modern city. The cathedral library was founded in 1732, and Bishop Mar Gewargis (died 1744) was buried in the church crypt. In the 19th century the British missionary [[wp:Joseph Wolff]] described the church to Western readers as evidence of Christian survival in [[wp:Central Asia]].

#### Russian rule and the union with Rome (1876–1931)
Russian forces under [[wp:Mikhail Skobelev|General Mikhail Skobelev]] conquered the Khanate of Kokand in 1876, and Iyık Janyl was incorporated into the Fergana Oblast of [[wp:Russian Turkestan]]. The tsarist administration left the church's internal affairs largely to its own clergy.<sup>[8]</sup>

After the Russian Revolution, Bishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]] opened secret negotiations with the [[wp:Holy See]], conducted through intermediaries at papal nunciatures abroad. On 14 September 1931 [[wp:Pope Pius XI]] promulgated the bull *Orientalis Stella*, which received the Syro-Iradi Church into full communion with Rome as an Eastern Catholic church *sui iuris* and established Iyık Janyl as its metropolitan see.<sup>[9]</sup> Soviet authorities regarded the Catholic connection with suspicion, and it became one of the grounds for the anti-religious repression of the 1930s.<sup>[10]</sup>

#### Soviet period (1931–1991)
During [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges]], security officers arrested and executed many of the senior clergy, closed the seminary and shut the cathedral, whose nave was converted into a district grain store.<sup>[10]</sup> Monks and lay sacristans hid the rarest manuscripts of the cathedral library in caves in the [[Kara-Suu Canyon]], and families kept vestments, icons and sacred vessels in their homes and in mountain shelters. For the rest of the Soviet period Iyık Janyl was a district town with a fruit cannery, a felt workshop and a reputation for clandestine worship. [[Amanbek Alimbekov]], who led the Provisional Government in 1992, was born in the city in 1938.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Since 1992
After the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] the cathedral was returned to the church and reconsecrated on 14 September 1992, the anniversary of *Orientalis Stella*.<sup>[9]</sup> During the US territorial administration the building was restored, the seminary reopened in 1994, and the library's manuscripts were conserved and digitized with support from the Vatican Library and European foundations. The church and the ruins of the medieval town were inscribed as a World Heritage Site on a nomination submitted by the United States, which as the administering power was the State Party for Irad. Irad itself is not a State Party to the World Heritage Convention.<sup>[3]</sup> Alimbekov was buried in the city's historic cemetery in 2004.

Since the partition of 2007 the city has lain within the ISEZ's southern salient on the Naryn, linked to Talas only by the [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]] and close to the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] on three sides. Under Archbishop [[Mar Ephrem]], primate since 2012, the archbishopric has kept a position of neutrality between the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] administration and the clan councils of the tribal regions, and the city has hosted inter-confessional meetings and talks between the two sides.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Geography
Iyık Janyl lies at 1,280 m in a sheltered side valley in the foothills of the Fergana Range, on the east side of the Naryn gorge about 20 km north-east of Tash-Kömür. The old town and Cathedral Hill occupy a spur above the valley floor, with newer districts spreading down the valley toward orchards and walnut groves and the Naryn below. A river canyon runs south of the city, and the ruins of the medieval town stand on its terrace. The surrounding ridges rise to about 3,000 m. The Qara-Chik line follows the crests to the north and east, beyond which lie the Toktogul–Naryn highlands of the tribal regions, and the Mailuu-Suu valley of the Fergana Rim lies to the south. The area is seismically active, and earthquakes and freeze–thaw cycles are the main threats to the city's historic brickwork.<sup>[6]</sup>

#### Climate
The city has a continental climate with dry summers ([[wp:Köppen climate classification|Köppen]] *Dsa*), typical of the foothills around the [[wp:Fergana Valley]]: warmer than Talas, with hot summers and shorter winters, and wetter than the valley floor below. July highs average about 31 °C (88 °F), and January lows average about −7 °C (19 °F). Annual precipitation is about 560 mm (22 in), most of it falling as spring rain; winter snow seldom lies for long in the city but closes the high pastures above it from December to March.<sup>[13]</sup>

## Demographics
The official 2018 estimate gave the city a population of 310,000.<sup>[1]</sup> Iradis form a larger share of the population than in Talas or [[Nurzhan]], about 88% in official figures, and the main minorities are Kyrgyz, [[wp:Uzbeks]] and Russians. Iradi is the main language of daily life. Russian is less common than in the other two cities, and English is widely spoken by younger residents and in the tourism trade.

Most residents identify with the Syro-Iradi Catholic Church, and surveys suggest that church attendance is higher than the national average. The city also has a small Russian Orthodox parish and a mosque. Its population rises sharply each August during the Feast of St. Janyl.

## Economy
The city's economy rests on religious institutions, pilgrimage and crafts.
- **Pilgrimage and tourism.** Pilgrims and visitors fill church hostels, including the [[Iyık Janyl Pilgrimage Hostel]], and private guesthouses, especially in August. The archbishopric, the seminary, the monasteries and the Palace of Art are among the largest employers.<sup>[14]</sup>
- **Crafts.** Workshops produce silver-inlaid saddles and horse tack ([[Eerchilik (Saddlery)|eerchilik]]), felt carpets ([[Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)|shyrdak]]), embossed leather and church plate such as incense vessels.
- **Agriculture and trade.** Orchards in the valley grow apples and apricots, and walnuts are gathered in the surrounding forests. The city's livestock and wool markets are among the few places where herders from the tribal regions trade directly with ISEZ buyers.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Government
Iyık Janyl is one of the three cities of governorate significance of the ISEZ, alongside Talas and Nurzhan. It is run by a mayor appointed by the Governor and an elected city council, and the archbishopric has considerable informal influence in local affairs.

Under an informal understanding reached during the 2007 ceasefire talks, armed units of the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] and the [[El-Choro Militias]] do not enter the cathedral precinct on Cathedral Hill, and pilgrims from the tribal regions may cross the Qara-Chik line unarmed for the Feast of St. Janyl. The arrangement is not part of the text of the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]], but the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) monitors the crossings each year.<sup>[12]</sup> The archbishop also convenes regular meetings with Sunni Muslim and Russian Orthodox leaders on charitable and civic matters.

## Transport
The city is reached from Talas by the [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]] (State Route 1) through the Otmek Tunnel and over the [[Fergana Gateway]], leaving it in the Naryn gorge about 26 km above Tash-Kömür for a side road of about 4 km up the valley, about 332 km in all. Buses and minibuses run between the two cities, taking six to seven hours, and the pass is occasionally closed by snow and avalanches in winter. There is no railway. During the Feast, thousands of pilgrims arrive on horseback, and UNMI and the archbishopric organize the crossing points for riders from the tribal regions.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Education
- The [[Cathedral Seminary of Iyık Janyl]] (Saint Ephrem Major Seminary), closed in 1937 and reopened in 1994, trains clergy for the Syro-Iradi Church in Syriac liturgy, theology and canon law.
- The [[Cathedral Seminary Library of Iyık Janyl]], founded in 1732, holds more than 600 manuscripts, among them the illuminated Talas Gospel Lectionary of about 1185, letters of Mar Gewargis and Sogdian commercial contracts. Its digitization laboratory publishes facsimiles online.<sup>[6]</sup>
- The [[Iyık Janyl Diocesan Archives Vault]] holds parish registers from the 18th century onward and the correspondence of the archbishopric with the Holy See.
- The [[Iyık Janyl Seminary Choir]], attached to the seminary, performs East Syriac polyphony and Iradi sacred music.

## Culture and landmarks
- **[[Church of St. Janyl]]**: the 1727 cathedral on Cathedral Hill, with a 22-meter ribbed dome clad in turquoise tiles, muqarnas vaulting, a carved walnut screen before the sanctuary and an icon of St. Janyl with the White Steed. Bishop Mar Gewargis is buried in its crypt.
- **[[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]]** (Shar-i-Kuhna): the excavated medieval town 3 km south of the city.
- **[[Iyık Janyl Monastic Cloister]]**: a walled cloister of contemplative monks beside the cathedral. The motherhouse of the [[Order of St. Janyl the Hermit]] overlooks the river canyon south of the city.
- **[[Irad Palace of Art]]**: the country's leading gallery and art preservation foundation, with collections ranging from Sogdian artifacts and [[wp:Silk Road]] ceramics to contemporary Iradi painting.
- The historic cemetery, where Alimbekov and many clergy are buried.

The Feast of St. Janyl on 24 August is the city's main event. Mounted priests bless horses and sheep assembled on the cathedral esplanade with glacial spring water. The liturgy uses *kümöch*, a round unleavened bread stamped with a cross and the Star of Irad, and incense of mountain juniper (*[[Arsha (Flora)|arsha]]*), followed by candlelit processions, horse races and communal meals of beef and mutton.<sup>[15]</sup> The archbishopric's attendance estimates in recent years have exceeded 150,000; no independent counts are available. The city's liturgical customs are described in [[East Syriac Rites of Iyık Janyl]], [[Kümöch Sacramental Bread]] and [[Arsha Juniper Incense Tradition]].

## Notable people
- [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] (1938–2004), Provisional President of the Republic of Irad in 1992, born and buried in the city
- [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]], bishop who negotiated the 1931 union with Rome
- Mar Gewargis (died 1744), bishop buried in the crypt of the Church of St. Janyl
- [[Marqos the Scribe]], 13th-century monk credited with the *Gospels of Iyık Janyl*
- [[Mar Ephrem|Archbishop Mar Ephrem]], Archbishop of Iyık Janyl since 2012

### 3.13. Democratic Party of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

The **Democratic Party of Irad** (Iradi: *Irad Demokratik Partiyasy*, DPI) is the dominant political party of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), the part of [[Irad]] outside the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Founded in 1992 by reformers of the Provisional Government, it was the leading party of the [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)|US territorial period]]. After the US withdrawal in December 2006 the party split, and on 25 February 2007 [[Erlan Toktarov]] seized control of it and proclaimed the ISEZ from Nurzhan. The reorganized party is often called the "New Democratic Party of Irad".<sup>[1]</sup>

Since 2007 the DPI has held the governorship and a large majority in the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]], and analysts describe the ISEZ as a dominant-party system organized around the Toktarov family. Toktarov, the party's chairman, was re-elected Governor in 2011, 2015, 2019 and 2023, in elections that observers from the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe]] (OSCE) said lacked meaningful choice.<sup>[2]</sup> He has not appeared in public since July 2024; the party attributes his absence to an extended health retreat.

## History

#### Origins and the territorial period (1992–2006)
The party traces its origins to the reformist caucus of the Provisional Government formed in [[Talas]] during the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]]. It was formally constituted in October 1992, after [[Operation Starlight]] and the organization of the territory under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], by Provisional Government ministers, Talas professionals and returning émigrés who supported the territorial arrangement of the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]].<sup>[3]</sup> Its program drew heavily on American models: territorial association with the United States, market reform, dollarization and English-language education.

Party members dominated the interim legislature of 1992–1994, which passed the first banking and currency statutes. At the first territorial elections in 1994 the DPI won majorities in both chambers, and it kept them for most of the territorial period, facing opposition mainly from the [[Labor Party of Irad]], based in [[Nurzhan]]'s Russian-speaking industrial workforce, the nationalist [[Jer-Suu Party]] and independent rural deputies.<sup>[3]</sup> Toktarov, who joined the party after Operation Starlight, entered the Territorial House of Representatives in 1994 and was Majority Leader of the Territorial Senate from 2000 to 2004. [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], later Prime Minister, was chief legal counsel to the Senate and drafted the [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]].<sup>[4]</sup> By the early 2000s critics in the press and the opposition were drawing attention to the commercial concessions held by party figures, especially in fuel distribution and in warehousing around [[Talas Air Base]].

#### Split and the Second Civil War (2006–2007)
When President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the treaty by executive action on 3 December 2006, DPI legislators led the response. After the Supreme Court issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] on 9 December, and Orozbekov formed a DPI-led cabinet.<sup>[5]</sup> In January and February 2007 the cabinet sold state assets, including oil concessions, grain elevators and telecom licenses, to firms controlled by party members. The [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]] provoked street protests in Talas and unrest in the countryside.<sup>[6]</sup>

Toktarov, who had warned against an immediate declaration of statehood, broke with Orozbekov. On 25 February 2007 he made public the cabinet's secret drafts, negotiated with Kyrgyzstan, Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan, to dissolve Irad and partition it among them in return for escrow payments and concession shares for companies tied to Orozbekov and several ministers (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]). He then took control of the party apparatus, proclaimed the ISEZ from Nurzhan as an emergency administration, annulled the independence ruling and returned Irad to "suspended sovereignty".<sup>[1]</sup> The Orozbekov cabinet in Talas continued as a rival authority until the [[Popular Front of Irad]] stormed the capital on 4 June 2007. During the summer, security forces loyal to Toktarov, backed by Western companies and the United States, took control of Talas and the other cities as the Front withdrew to the countryside, and the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] of September fixed the division of the country.<sup>[1]</sup> The press began calling the reorganized party the "New DPI" to distinguish it from the territorial-era party, although it kept its official name.

#### Consolidation (2007–2023)
Toktarov assumed the governorship in 2007 and was re-elected in November 2011 with 88.74% of the official vote. The same year the party's delegation at the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]] offered the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) one-third of the seats in a reunified legislature; the IAC walked out, and no comparable talks have been held since.<sup>[7]</sup> In 2013 the DPI majority in the legislature passed an amendment to the 1992 Elective Governor Acts that removed gubernatorial term limits (the [[2013 Constitutional Amendment]]).

Toktarov's share fell to 78.25% in 2015, an election marked by the disqualification of rival candidates on technical grounds (the [[2015 Electoral Reform Controversy]]).<sup>[8]</sup> He won 90.31% in 2019 with his son-in-law [[Aidar Maksat]] as his running mate on the joint ticket, and Maksat took office as Lieutenant Governor on 25 November 2019. In 2023 he won 92.75%. Early in 2023, according to independent reporting, Toktarov and his senior staff moved to the fortified [[Boz-Korgon]] compound east of Nurzhan; the party and the government maintain that Talas remains the operational capital.<sup>[9]</sup> In October 2023 a municipal licensing surcharge set off [[2023 Student Protests in Talas|student protests in Talas]], which police dispersed.

#### Since 2024
Toktarov has not been seen in public since July 2024. The DPI attributes his absence to an extended health retreat, and his addresses on [[IradOne]] have been the subject of authenticity claims that officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[9]</sup> Maksat runs the day-to-day business of the administration. The next gubernatorial election is due in November 2027, and the party has not yet named its ticket (see [[2027 Iradi gubernatorial election]]).

## Ideology
The DPI describes its program as "Sovereign Development, Territorial Unity, and Economic Pragmatism". In practice its main positions are:
- maintaining Irad's "suspended sovereignty" pending a unification referendum, rather than either full independence or a return to territorial status;
- keeping the security relationship with the United States, including the US presence at Talas Air Base;
- welcoming foreign investment, including from China (see [[Irad–China relations]]);
- a secular state, with Iradi and English as official languages and Russian without official status;
- market-oriented economic policy combined, in practice, with state-licensed monopolies and concessions.

Leaked US intelligence reports from Toktarov's first term described the party as trending toward a cult of personality (see [[Erlan Toktarov]]).<sup>[10]</sup> Party officials reject this description. They argue that strong central authority is needed to deter territorial claims by neighboring states and to protect foreign investment.

## Organization
- **Chairman**: Erlan Toktarov, since 2007.
- **Political Bureau** (*Siyasi Buro*): a 25-member leadership body, which nominates candidates and approves senior appointments.
- **Executive Secretariat**: the permanent party staff, based at the party headquarters in Talas. Independent reporting since 2023 says that key meetings take place at Boz-Korgon.<sup>[9]</sup>
- **Regional committees**: one in each of the three cities of governorate significance (Talas, Nurzhan and [[Iyık Janyl]]) and the five districts (Talas, [[Kara-Buura]], Chu, [[Sary-Bulak]] and Lower Naryn). The committees oversee public procurement, utility concessions, municipal licensing and agricultural quotas.
- **Party congress**: meets before each gubernatorial election to nominate the party's candidate.

Membership is widespread among civil servants and employees of state-licensed firms. Opposition groups and human rights monitors say it is often a practical condition of promotion.<sup>[11]</sup>

## Electoral performance
Gubernatorial elections in the ISEZ since 2007, by official results:

| Election | DPI candidate | Official share of vote |
|---|---|---|
| November 2011 | Erlan Toktarov | 88.74% |
| 2015 | Erlan Toktarov | 78.25% |
| 2019 | Erlan Toktarov | 90.31% |
| 2023 | Erlan Toktarov | 92.75% |

In the 2023 election to the Territorial Legislature the DPI won 51 of the 60 seats, and most of the independents elected alongside it vote with the party.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Business and media connections
The party's main business network is centered on the Toktarov family. [[Timur Toktarov]] directs transport and bonded logistics, [[Adil Toktarov]] commercial real estate in Talas, and [[Bekzat Toktarov]] feed milling and grain supply through [[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]]. [[Alikhan Toktarov]] chairs the IradOne media group, and Major General [[Ruslan Toktarov]] commands the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]].<sup>[10]</sup> [[Talasbank]] handles much of the administration's commercial clearing, and the DPI executive office exercises editorial control over IradOne, the largest broadcaster.

Relations with [[NurGaz]] are uneasy. The company's president, [[Kamran Bekzatov]], heads the Russophone *Russo* constituency in Nurzhan and is a prominent critic of DPI economic policy, and NurGaz's revenues give him a power base independent of the party.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Succession
The rivalry between Toktarov's two eldest sons has been publicly visible since 2024. Ruslan, the security chief, has drawn criticism for anti-Muslim remarks, and Alikhan for reckless spending and rumored substance abuse.<sup>[10]</sup> Maksat, whom foreign diplomats regard as the most moderate figure in the leadership, has made no public claim to the succession and presents himself as a pro-business technocrat. Toktarov's daughter [[Zara Toktarova]], Maksat's wife, is a doctoral candidate in education at [[wp:Stanford University]] who lives mostly abroad and has no public political role. Tabloid reports of family disputes over access to the Boz-Korgon estate have not been verified. Legal scholars differ on whether the 1992 Elective Governor Acts require a formal declaration of incapacity before the Lieutenant Governor may exercise the Governor's powers in his own right; no such finding has been made.<sup>[9]</sup>

## Criticism
- **Elections.** The OSCE criticized the 2013 electoral reform and the elections that followed (see [[Elections in Irad]]). It cited the exclusion of opposition candidates, state control of voter registers and pressure on employees of state-owned firms to vote.<sup>[2]</sup>
- **Media and civil liberties.** Human rights groups report harassment, license revocations and travel bans against independent journalists. They also report that mobile and internet services are throttled during protests through the national operator, [[Attelkom]].<sup>[11]</sup>
- **Census.** The DPI's 2018 census gave a population of 4,626,000, against independent estimates of about 6.2 million. Researchers attribute most of the gap to the census estimating the tribal regions rather than enumerating them. IAC leaders and some analysts argue that the undercount reduces the tribal regions' weight in any unification formula; the government defends its methods.<sup>[13]</sup>
- **Unification.** Critics, including the IAC, say that the party's 2011 offer and its refusal of a tribal veto over mining concessions in the [[2008 Unification Referendum Initiative]] showed that it did not negotiate in good faith.<sup>[7]</sup>

### 3.14. Iradi Freedom Society
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

The **Iradi Freedom Society** (Iradi: *Iradin Azatlık Cemiyeti*, IAC) is the loose confederation of semi-nomadic clans that governs the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR) of Irad, about two-thirds of the country's territory. It brings together the twenty-four clans (*uruk*) of the tribal regions, which govern themselves through councils of elders (*aksakals*) under customary law (*adat*). The society grew out of the [[Popular Front of Irad]], the clan coalition that stormed [[Talas]] in June 2007 during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], and was constituted after the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The IAC's unofficial leader is [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], chief of the Tanıp clan, who declines formal titles and describes himself as an ambassador or negotiator for the clans. The society does not recognize the authority of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) over the tribal regions. It has nonetheless observed the ceasefire with the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) since 2007, trades with it, and shares the operation of the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] under UN supervision.<sup>[2]</sup>

## History

#### Background
Iradi clans developed more cohesive forms of intertribal governance after the defeat of the [[Semirechye Revolt (Irad)|1916 Semirechye Revolt]], and an Iradi translation of the *Catechism of a Revolutionary* spread anarcho-communist ideas among them in the years before the [[wp:Russian Civil War]].<sup>[3]</sup> Soviet collectivization in the 1930s confiscated clan herds and pushed many pastoral families from the plains into the eastern mountains. When the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] tried to reverse traditional tribal autonomy in 1991, clan horsemen joined the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], and during the [[First Iradi Civil War]] mounted scouts organized by Bektur defended the mountain valleys against socialist requisition columns.<sup>[3]</sup> Through the territorial period the clans kept their distance from the US administration and opposed leases of tribal grazing land to mining companies.

#### Formation (2007)
In January and February 2007 the cabinet of the short-lived [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] tried to privatize communal pastures in the Chatkal valley, alongside its sales of oil and mineral assets. Clan leaders treated the pasture decrees as a threat to customary tenure. On 25 February [[Erlan Toktarov]] made public the cabinet's secret drafts to partition Irad among its three neighbors (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]), under which the clans' pastures would have been transferred without any vote. In March 2007, clan militias under Bektur formed the Popular Front of Irad, which demanded Orozbekov's trial for treason, stormed Talas on 4 June, dissolved the government of Prime Minister [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] and arrested him.<sup>[4]</sup> Scholars trace to the affair a lasting distrust of any government based in the cities, which shapes the IAC's insistence on clan control of land and water. During the summer, as DPI security forces took control of the cities, the Front withdrew to the countryside. Its representatives negotiated the Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement in September, which set the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] between the two zones.

In late 2007 a kurultai of clan elders on the [[Aksay Plateau]] reorganized the Front's political council as the Iradi Freedom Society. The Front's fighting units became the [[El-Choro Militias]], and its urban committees were disbanded.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI), established in October 2007, has dealt with the IAC as the representative of the tribal regions since its creation.

#### Since 2007
The IAC took part in UN-sponsored discussions on the [[2008 Unification Referendum Initiative]], which collapsed after the DPI refused to give tribal councils a veto over foreign mining concessions in mountain watersheds. At the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]], the only direct negotiation between the two sides, the IAC delegation walked out after being offered one-third of the seats in a reunified legislature.<sup>[5]</sup> Polling in 2017 suggested support for a unified Irad in the tribal regions as well as the ISEZ, although data from the ATR were limited and tribal leaders alleged DPI interference in the polling. Public statements by clan leaders have shown broad ambivalence about Iradi statehood.

In 2018 tribal patrols exchanged fire with Uzbek border guards over grazing trails and boreholes on the western desert frontier, in the [[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]], before bilateral border commissions negotiated buffer arrangements.<sup>[6]</sup> During [[The 2021 Regional Security Alert]], after the [[wp:Taliban]] returned to power in [[wp:Afghanistan]], El-Choro riders and ISEZ forces reinforced mountain pass checkpoints and set up joint intelligence coordination. The IAC has also disputed the 2018 census, arguing that it greatly undercounted the tribal regions. In 2025, remarks by [[wp:Donald Trump]] about selling Irad to Russia provoked protests and anti-American feeling in the tribal regions as well as in the ISEZ.

## Organization
The IAC is not a political party and has no written constitution. Its institutions are:
- **[[Kurultai (Iradi Freedom Society)|The Kurultai]]**, the supreme assembly of clan elders, pasture captains and militia commanders. It meets each summer on the Aksay Plateau, the main summer pasture (*zhayloo*) of the tribal regions. Decisions on grazing rights, common defense and relations with the ISEZ require the consent of all the leading lineages.<sup>[7]</sup>
- **Clan councils**. Each of the twenty-four clans is governed by its own council of *aksakals*, and village councils (*ayyl kengeshi*) handle day-to-day local administration.
- **Customary courts**. Disputes are heard by recognized judges (*biys*) in open assemblies (*Aksakal Sod*) under [[Adat Law in the ATR|adat]], which favors restitution over imprisonment. The main remedy is *kun*, a compensatory payment in livestock. Inter-clan pasture disputes go to the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]].<sup>[8]</sup>
- **The Chancellery**. The [[Iradi Freedom Society Chancellery]] is the standing secretariat. It keeps pasture and migration records, handles correspondence with UNMI and aid agencies, and archives customary court decisions, largely in handwritten ledgers.
- **The El-Choro Militias**, about 15,000 part-time mounted irregulars organized in clan detachments, who man pickets along the Qara-Chik line and patrol the mountain borders.

Bektur holds no office in these bodies. He describes his role as that of a "servant and mediator" (*el-chabary*) among the clan councils, and acts as the IAC's main interlocutor with UNMI. UNMI and humanitarian agencies divide the tribal regions into six planning zones (Northern Steppe, [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]], [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal Highlands, Toktogul–Naryn and Fergana Rim). These zones are used for aid planning and have no standing in IAC governance.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Policies
- **Land and pasture.** The IAC regulates the seasonal rotation of mountain pastures between summer (*zhayloo*) and winter (*kyshtoo*) grazing, and says it protects smaller clans' access to them.
- **Environment.** The clans maintain a ban on open-pit gold mining, cyanide heap leaching and the exploration of radioactive minerals in mountain watersheds. Mining companies have been allowed to carry out surveys, but none has received permission to build a mine, and Irad's gold, uranium and thorium deposits remain undeveloped.<sup>[9]</sup>
- **Language and religion.** Iradi is the only language of IAC business, and the tribal regions reject both Russian and English. Religious life follows a tribal folk Christianity in which itinerant Syro-Iradi priests bless herds and sometimes sit with elders at hearings. The clans strictly enforce the taboo on eating horsemeat.
- **Unification.** The IAC says it supports a unified Irad, but only with representation that reflects the population of the tribal regions and with tribal control over land and water.

## Economy and resources
The tribal economy rests on semi-nomadic herding of sheep, cattle and horses, together with walnuts and wild fruit gathered in the Chatkal forests. Under the ceasefire arrangements, herders with tribal travel permits cross the Qara-Chik line to sell wool, hides and livestock to feedlots and packing plants in Talas and [[Nurzhan]], in exchange for fuel, grain and medical goods. Payments are cleared through [[Talasbank]].<sup>[2]</sup> Chancellery officials also negotiate seasonal bulk contracts with DPI ministries.

The dam, reservoir and generating hall of the Toktogul complex lie on the tribal side of the line, while its switchyard, dispatch center and export lines lie in the ISEZ. Under the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], an annex to the ceasefire, a joint technical commission of ISEZ and ATR engineers sets the annual release schedule under UN supervision, with observers from [[wp:Uzbekistan]], [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]]. Revenue is shared under a formula that has never been published. Both the IAC and the DPI deny profiting at the other's expense, and independent analysts dispute how the money is actually divided.<sup>[10]</sup>

Sections of the ancient [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]] of underground channels are used by IAC-aligned groups to move goods past ISEZ checkpoints, a practice the DPI administration describes as smuggling.

## External relations
No state recognizes the IAC, and Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan all claim Irad *de jure*. In practice the society deals with neighboring states on border and water questions, mainly through UNMI and through the Toktogul commission. UNMI coordinates the delivery of medical supplies and veterinary vaccines into the highland valleys through IAC channels, and its liaison officers meet IAC representatives alongside officers of the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]]. The [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]] maintains neutrality between the IAC and the DPI and has hosted meetings between the two sides.

## Criticism
- **Armed formations.** The ISEZ government and the [[Armed Forces of Irad]] regard the El-Choro Militias as an illegal armed formation, though both sides observe non-aggression arrangements supervised by UNMI.
- **Rights and accountability.** Human rights organizations have criticized the patriarchal rules of inheritance under *adat*, the lack of written law and of appeal from elder courts, and the absence of elections to IAC bodies.<sup>[8]</sup>
- **Smuggling.** ISEZ officials accuse IAC-aligned groups of running contraband through the Kariz tunnels and over mountain passes. The IAC describes this traffic as the movement of supplies within its own territory.
- **Population claims.** The IAC says the tribal regions are home to more than 3.5 million people, far more than the DPI census suggests. Independent estimates of Irad's total population (about 6.2 million) support a substantial undercount of the ATR, drawing on satellite settlement mapping and aid-agency registries, but no full count of the tribal regions has been made.<sup>[11]</sup>

### 3.15. Talas Air Base
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Law & Security*

**Talas Air Base** is a military airfield 12 km (7.5 mi) west of [[Talas]], on the southern terrace of the [[Talas River]]. Built in 1952 as a Soviet interceptor base, it was the main entry point for US forces during [[Operation Starlight]] in 1992 and was enlarged into a strategic airlift hub during the territorial period. It is the largest US military installation in the region and was a primary staging area for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]] in [[wp:Afghanistan]] from late 2001.<sup>[1]</sup>

US forces have remained at the base since the United States withdrew from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] in December 2006. Their presence rests on the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]] and on later arrangements with the administration of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). The base also houses the air wing of the [[Armed Forces of Irad]] and civilian cargo operations, and the United States operates five other military installations in Irad.<sup>[2]</sup>

## History

#### Soviet period (1952–1991)
The airfield was established in 1952 by a Soviet air defense directive (the [[Founding of Talas Air Base (1952)]]) as a dispersal base for interceptors of the [[wp:Turkestan Military District]]. It had a 2,500-meter concrete runway and underground fuel bunkers, and its [[wp:Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-17|MiG-17]] and later [[wp:Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-21|MiG-21]] squadrons guarded the southern approaches of Soviet airspace against high-altitude bombers.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Operation Starlight (1992)
During the [[First Iradi Civil War]], the airfield lay within the area held by the Provisional Government during the siege of Talas in April and May 1992. After the signing of the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty on 2 June 1992, [[wp:United States Marine Corps|US Marines]] and troops of the 10th Mountain Division landed there to reinforce the city's defenders. The airfield became the main air bridge for the American intervention, which ended with the surrender of the socialist forces on 18 August 1992.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
Between 1993 and 1998 US military engineers lengthened the main runway to 3,400 m (11,155 ft), rebuilt the taxiways in high-load concrete and added hardened aircraft shelters able to service [[wp:Boeing C-17 Globemaster III|C-17 Globemaster III]] transports and [[wp:Boeing KC-135 Stratotanker|KC-135 Stratotanker]] tankers.<sup>[3]</sup> A 1994 defense telecommunications contract to link the base with regional command centers by fiber-optic cable and satellite became the origin of [[Attelkom]], now the largest telecommunications company in the ISEZ. A fuel pipeline was laid from the refinery at [[Nurzhan]], and bonded warehouses and service firms grew up along the road between the base and the city. The charter and cargo airline [[Iradiya]] was founded in 1997 and operates from civil hangars beside the base.

After the September 2001 attacks on the United States, the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]] between the United States and the territorial government gave US forces expanded rights for logistics, aerial refueling and medical transit. Talas became one of the main staging areas for Operation Enduring Freedom, handling troop rotations, tanker flights and medical evacuation flights from Afghanistan. The agreement generated service contracts worth hundreds of millions of dollars for local firms.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Since 2006
President George W. Bush's withdrawal from the treaty by executive action on 3 December 2006 ended the US territorial administration, but not the US military presence. Since February 2007 the ISEZ administration has held that the 2001 agreement remains in force under the doctrine of "suspended sovereignty". Supplementary arrangements concluded with the ISEZ from 2007 onward cover land use, fuel supply, security and payments; they have not been published in full.<sup>[2]</sup> During the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], the United States backed the DPI security forces that took control of Talas in the summer of 2007, although the base's part in that support has not been documented publicly.

Since 2007 the base has been a joint-use facility. The northern ramp houses the air wing of the ISEZ armed forces, officially the State Defense Corps, which flies Mi-8 and Mi-17 transport helicopters and [[wp:Mil Mi-24|Mi-24]] attack helicopters. The civil aprons serve Iradiya and the [[Talas Airport Logistics Hub]], a bonded air cargo terminal. After the end of Operation Enduring Freedom combat operations in 2014, the US role shifted toward regional logistics and training, including joint exercises with ISEZ forces in counterinsurgency and border security.<sup>[1]</sup>

In 2020 ISEZ gendarmerie and US personnel installed tracking radars, electronic jamming equipment and new perimeter fencing in response to incursions by surveillance drones, an operation known as [[Operation Clear Skies (2020)]].<sup>[6]</sup> During [[The 2021 Regional Security Alert]], which followed the [[wp:Taliban|Taliban's]] return to power in Afghanistan, security around the base was reinforced. In 2025, remarks by [[wp:Donald Trump]] about selling Irad to Russia provoked protests and anti-American feeling across Irad.

## Facilities
- A main runway of 3,400 m, with high-load taxiways and hardened shelters for heavy transport and tanker aircraft.
- A fuel farm supplied by pipeline from the Nurzhan refinery.
- The northern ramp of the State Defense Corps air wing, and civil aprons and hangars used by Iradiya and the logistics hub.
- Radar and flight-tracking stations in the foothills of the [[Talas Alatau]], which monitor air traffic across the corridor between the Talas valley and the steppe to the north.
- Fiber-optic and satellite links built and maintained by Attelkom.

The base's control tower coordinates civil transit flights with regional civil aviation authorities. Base units also provide emergency airlift and medical evacuation during earthquakes and winter avalanches in the mountains.

## Security
US forces are responsible for security within their own areas of the base. The outer perimeter and the approach roads are guarded by the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]], commanded by Major General [[Ruslan Toktarov]], whose Rapid Reaction Strike Group (*Barysh*) is stationed next to the base. Ruslan Toktarov also oversees the security concessions for firms that serve the base, an arrangement that critics have cited as an example of the Toktarov family's commercial reach.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Other US installations
Besides Talas Air Base, the United States operates five other military installations in Irad: logistics depots, a testing facility and training areas.<sup>[2]</sup> The US government does not publish a full list of their locations or functions, and their staffing levels are not disclosed.

## Economic role
The base is one of the largest employers in western Talas District. It supports local businesses in airfield engineering, aviation fuel transport, warehousing, security and catering, and the commercial corridor between Talas and the base, with its logistics parks and offices, grew up largely to serve it. Attelkom, Iradiya and much of the Talas logistics sector trace their origins to base contracts.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Legal status and controversy
The legal basis of the US presence since 2006 is disputed. Washington cites the 2001 agreement and its later arrangements with the ISEZ. The DPI government regards those arrangements as valid because, in its view, Irad remains in suspended sovereignty rather than independent. Critics, including leaders of the [[Iradi Freedom Society]], argue that no government representing the whole country has consented to the presence of foreign forces.<sup>[2]</sup> The base also complicates Irad's international position: Irad remains on the UN list of non-self-governing territories, with the United States still nominally recorded as the administering power, although Washington no longer reports on the territory.

### 3.16. Alimbekov-Baker Treaty
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Foreign Relations*

The **Alimbekov-Baker Treaty** (officially the *Bilateral Treaty of Territorial Association and Mutual Security between the United States of America and the Republic of Irad*) is the 1992 agreement under which [[Irad]] became an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States. It was signed in [[Talas]] on 2 June 1992, during the [[First Iradi Civil War]], by Provisional President [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] and [[wp:James Baker|US Secretary of State James Baker]], and the Provisional Government ratified it unanimously the same day.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> The treaty provided for US military intervention against the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]], which followed as [[Operation Starlight]], and for a territorial government with a legislature, courts and an elected Governor under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]]. It also contained a statutory independence clause, under which sovereignty would pass to the people of Irad if US administration ended.

On 3 December 2006 President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the treaty by executive action. Days later the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] ruled that the withdrawal had triggered the independence clause, and the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] was proclaimed. In February 2007 [[Erlan Toktarov]] annulled that ruling and declared Irad to be in "suspended sovereignty", the status it has kept since. The institutions created under the treaty still form the framework of government in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ).<sup>[3]</sup>

## Background
The [[Socialist Republic of Irad]], proclaimed by the [[Communist Party of Irad]] on 9 October 1991 and governed from Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]), was overtaken within months by economic collapse and the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]]. On 11 March 1992 the Provisional Government in Talas proclaimed the Republic of Irad, beginning the First Iradi Civil War. Socialist forces, including the 17th Motorized Rifle Division, advanced along the Chu and Talas corridors and besieged Talas from 10 April 1992; by late May the city's water supply had been cut and its defenders were short of ammunition.<sup>[4]</sup>

Alimbekov, who remained inside the besieged city, opened contacts with the United States through its embassy in [[wp:Almaty]], [[wp:Kazakhstan]], and proposed that Irad enter into association with the United States in exchange for military protection and economic reconstruction.<sup>[5]</sup> Historians have attributed Washington's acceptance to several concerns: the security of former Soviet military and uranium sites, the energy reserves of the Chu basin, and the risk that the war would draw in neighboring states.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Signing and ratification
The treaty was signed on 2 June 1992 in the basement of the damaged municipal theater in Talas. The Provisional Government voted unanimously to ratify it the same day, and its members presented the decision as the only way to save the city.<sup>[2]</sup> On the US side, [[wp:United States Congress|Congress]] gave effect to the treaty's governance provisions by passing the Organic Act of the Territory of Irad, which organized the territory after the socialist surrender on 18 August 1992.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Provisions
The treaty's main provisions were:
- **Territorial association.** Irad would become an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States, subject to the authority of Congress under the Territory Clause (Article IV) of the [[wp:Constitution of the United States|US Constitution]].
- **Security.** The United States would defend the territory, restore order and disarm irregular formations, and could deploy forces through Talas. This provision was the basis of Operation Starlight.
- **Government.** An organic act would establish a [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]], the Supreme Court of Irad and an elected Governor, and guarantee civil liberties, a secular state and freedom of religion, including the protection of church property.
- **Economy.** The US dollar would be legal tender, alongside any local currency the territory chose to issue, and Iradi goods would receive customs access to the US market.
- **Statutory independence clause.** If the United States ceased to administer the territory, sovereignty would revert to the people of Irad. The clause was carried into the Organic Act, which is why Iradi lawyers usually describe it as "statutory".
- **Withdrawal.** Either party could withdraw unilaterally. The treaty did not say whether US withdrawal required the approval of Congress.<sup>[6]</sup>

Under a related undertaking, the territorial government agreed not to develop Irad's uranium and thorium deposits. This was a response partly to the legacy of Soviet uranium processing at [[Mailuu-Suu]], and the deposits remain undeveloped.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Implementation (1992–2006)
[[wp:United States Marine Corps|US Marines]] and troops of the 10th Mountain Division landed at what became [[Talas Air Base]] in June 1992. Joint US and Iradi forces took Kobozev on 12 August, and the remaining socialist forces surrendered on 18 August, when the [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)|Territory of Irad]] was formally organized.<sup>[4]</sup> An interim legislature first met in October 1992, the Supreme Court was established in November, and the first territorial elections were held in 1994. Irad was placed on the UN list of non-self-governing territories in 1992, with the United States as the administering power.<sup>[7]</sup>

During the fourteen years of territorial administration, the treaty framework supported dollarization and the chartering of [[Talasbank]] in 1993, the founding of [[Attelkom]] from a 1994 defense contract, and agribusiness investment from 1994 under North American trade arrangements. The Iradi language returned to the Latin alphabet, with revisions in 1995 and 1997. The [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]] later made Talas Air Base a main staging area for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]]. Alimbekov served as senior adviser to the territorial government until his death in 2004. He resisted nationalist calls to repudiate the treaty, arguing that territorial status was the only practical protection against neighboring states.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Withdrawal and its aftermath (2006–2007)
On 3 December 2006 [[wp:George W. Bush|President Bush]] withdrew the United States from the treaty by executive action (see [[United States Withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty (2006)]]), ending federal civilian administration and leaving security to local authorities. Within days, on a petition brought by the jurist [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], the Supreme Court of Irad under Chief Justice [[Dastan Isakov]] issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], holding that the withdrawal had triggered the independence clause and vested sovereignty in the people of Irad.<sup>[8]</sup> On 9 December the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad, with Orozbekov as Prime Minister. Twenty-six UN member states recognized it.

The United States did not formally notify the United Nations that its administration had ended. Irad therefore remained on the list of non-self-governing territories, with the United States still nominally recorded as the administering power, although Washington does not report on the territory.<sup>[7]</sup>

On 25 February 2007, as protests against the privatization of state assets grew, Toktarov took control of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]], proclaimed the ISEZ from Nurzhan and annulled the independence ruling, returning Irad to "suspended sovereignty" pending a unification referendum. The proclamation began the [[Second Iradi Civil War]].<sup>[9]</sup> Under this doctrine the ISEZ treats the treaty's framework as suspended rather than terminated, and US forces have stayed at Talas Air Base under the 2001 Basing Rights Agreement and later arrangements with the ISEZ administration.

## Legal debate
The treaty's end raised several questions that remain unresolved:
- whether the President could terminate a treaty of territorial association without the approval of Congress;
- whether the independence clause took effect automatically, as the Supreme Court held, or required further legislation;
- whether Toktarov's emergency decree could validly annul a ruling of the territory's highest court.

Scholars at [[Talas State University]] and abroad have argued all sides of these questions. The ISEZ's position is that sovereignty is suspended, the position of the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] is that no lawful authority over the whole country has existed since 2007, and the three neighboring states maintain their own *de jure* claims.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Legacy
The treaty created the institutions that still govern the ISEZ: the Territorial Legislature, the Supreme Court, the elected governorship, the use of the dollar and the official status of English. Its signing set off a brief period of American public enthusiasm for Irad, known as [[Iradimania]] (about 1992–1994). In Irad it is remembered both as the act that ended the siege of Talas and as a surrender of the independence proclaimed in March 1992, and Alimbekov is commemorated as one of the founders of the modern state.

The treaty's dissolution gave rise to the [[Bush Family Palatial Complex Conspiracy Theory]], which alleged that the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound had been built for the Bush family in return for ending the treaty. Construction of the compound began in the 1990s, before the dissolution. In 2025 [[wp:Donald Trump]] said he was exploring selling Irad to Russia; it is not known whether he realized that Irad had ceased to be a US territory after the 2006 withdrawal.<sup>[3]</sup>

### 3.17. Second Iradi Civil War
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: History*

The **Second Iradi Civil War** was an armed conflict in [[Irad]] from February to September 2007. It began on 25 February 2007, when [[Erlan Toktarov]] proclaimed the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) from [[Nurzhan]] in defiance of the government of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] (DRI) in Talas, and ended with the UN-brokered [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] in September. Three forces took part: the DRI government of Prime Minister [[Bakyt Orozbekov]]; Toktarov's emergency administration, backed by the faction of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) loyal to him; and the [[Popular Front of Irad]], a coalition of clan militias led by [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]].<sup>[1]</sup> Its main cause was the [[Bill of Sale (2007)|Bill of Sale]] (*Satuu Kagazy*), the secret drafts under which the Orozbekov cabinet had agreed to dissolve Irad and partition it among its three neighbors, which Toktarov made public on the day he proclaimed the ISEZ.<sup>[10]</sup>

The Front stormed [[Talas]] on 4 June 2007 and dissolved the DRI. During the summer, however, DPI security forces backed by Western companies and the United States took control of Talas and the other cities as the Front withdrew to the countryside. The ceasefire fixed the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], which has divided Irad since 2007 into the ISEZ and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR).<sup>[2]</sup>

## Background

#### End of the territorial period
On 3 December 2006 President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] by executive action, ending fourteen years of US territorial administration. Days later the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], holding that the withdrawal had triggered the treaty's statutory independence clause. On 9 December 2006 the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad, with Orozbekov, a corporate lawyer and former chief legal counsel to the Territorial Senate, as Prime Minister.<sup>[3]</sup> Twenty-six UN member states recognized the new state. It inherited a large budget deficit, since US federal transfers had stopped.

#### Privatization crisis (January–February 2007)
To close the gap, the Orozbekov cabinet sold state assets quickly in January and February 2007. It auctioned exploration concessions in the oil fields and the eastern antimony deposits, state grain elevators and mobile spectrum, and it tried to privatize communal pastures in the Chatkal valley.<sup>[4]</sup> Investigative reporting found that many of the assets had gone at low prices to companies linked to cabinet ministers and other DPI members, in what became known as the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]. Protests spread through Talas, and in mid-January police fired on herders demonstrating against fuel price rises in the Chu valley. Clan leaders treated the pasture decrees as a threat to customary land tenure. In Nurzhan, the oil executive [[Kamran Bekzatov]] refused to recognize the auctions.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### The Bill of Sale (December 2006 – February 2007)
At the same time, and without public knowledge, delegations of the Orozbekov cabinet were negotiating with the foreign ministries of [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kazakhstan]] under cover of consultations on borders and water. The drafts that resulted would have used the sovereignty conferred by the independence ruling to dissolve Irad and divide it among the three states: the Talas valley and the lands adjoining Jalal-Abad to Kyrgyzstan, the Fergana rim to Uzbekistan, and the northern steppe, the Chu and the lands adjoining the Turkistan and Jambyl regions to Kazakhstan. Compensation was to be paid into escrow, and companies tied to Orozbekov and several ministers were to receive concession shares in the oil fields, the antimony deposits and the Toktogul complex. No plebiscite was planned. Iradis call the drafts the *Satuu Kagazy*, the "Bill of Sale", and historians generally regard them as the main cause of the war (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]).<sup>[10]</sup><sup>[11]</sup>

## Course of the war

#### Proclamation of the ISEZ (25 February 2007)
Toktarov, a former Majority Leader of the Territorial Senate who had warned against an immediate declaration of statehood, broke with Orozbekov. On 25 February 2007, in a speech in Nurzhan, he read out passages from the partition drafts and released copies of them, calling them "a bill of sale for our country". He then seized control of the DPI apparatus and proclaimed the Irad Special Economic Zone as an emergency administration. He annulled the Supreme Court's independence ruling and declared that Irad had returned to "suspended sovereignty" pending a unification referendum; ISEZ accounts explain that with no sovereign in place, no one could sell the country.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[12]</sup> The leak made Toktarov a national hero. The DRI cabinet in Talas called the documents unapproved "contingency papers", rejected the proclamation and continued to govern as a rival authority.<sup>[13]</sup> Toktarov was backed by the Nurzhan oil executive [[Kamran Bekzatov]], whose company NurGaz supplied money and fuel and whose refinery workers guarded the oil installations, and by the Labor-led Nurzhan city council; many historians consider this support decisive. Toktarov drew his forces from municipal police and gendarmerie units and from loyalist detachments that he organized into the State Defense Corps, and he secured backing from foreign energy and agribusiness companies.

#### Formation of the Popular Front (March 2007)
In March 2007 clan militias under Bektur, chief of the Tanıp clan, formed the Popular Front of Irad. Its demands were the restoration of communal pasture rights, an audit of the privatization contracts, the trial of Orozbekov for treason over the Bill of Sale, the protection of customary law (*adat*) and an end to what it called the plunder of national wealth by foreign companies and insiders.<sup>[5]</sup> Factory strike committees in Nurzhan and railway workers joined the Front, and its fighting strength reached more than 8,000 mounted irregulars, supported by detachments that defected from the territorial defense forces.

#### Spring campaign (March–May 2007)
The Front's mounted columns, riding mostly [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] mountain horses, encircled government garrisons in the countryside and cut the railway lines serving Talas and Nurzhan. By late May the Front controlled most of the rural interior, about two-thirds of the country.<sup>[2]</sup> Toktarov's forces concentrated on holding Nurzhan, the oil fields and the export pipeline, while the DRI government was increasingly confined to Talas, which the Front invested from 22 April.

#### Storming of Talas (4 June 2007)
On 4 June 2007 the Front stormed Talas in the [[Storming of Talas (2007)]]. Municipal police mutinied, ministers fled and Front fighters occupied the legislative palace. Orozbekov was arrested at his residence by tribal commanders and signed an instrument of resignation dissolving the DRI government. He was then handed over to a United Nations compound outside the city.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### DPI takeover of the cities (summer 2007)
The Front's aims centered on land and customary rights rather than control of the cities, and after the fall of the DRI it withdrew to the countryside. During the summer, DPI security forces under Toktarov, backed by Western companies and the United States, took control of Talas, [[Iyık Janyl]] and the other towns of the river valleys. They consolidated an area that ran along the Talas valley, the Chu lowlands and the southern industrial corridor, with a salient down the Naryn.<sup>[1]</sup> Skirmishes continued along the emerging front line, especially around the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]], whose dam lay in Front-held territory and whose switchyard and export lines were held by the DPI.

#### Ceasefire (September 2007)
UN mediators brought the two sides to [[wp:Bishkek]], where the Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement was signed in September 2007. It set the 420-kilometer Qara-Chik Demarcation Line between the ISEZ and the tribal regions, with a southern salient across the Naryn corridor. Its annex, the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], placed the Toktogul complex under a joint technical commission of engineers from both sides, supervised by the United Nations, with observers from [[wp:Uzbekistan]], [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]].<sup>[6]</sup> On 18 October 2007 the [[wp:United Nations Security Council|UN Security Council]] established the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) to monitor the ceasefire.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Forces
- **Democratic Republic of Irad**: territorial police and the remnants of the territorial defense forces under the Orozbekov cabinet.
- **ISEZ emergency administration**: municipal police, gendarmerie units and the loyalist State Defense Corps under Toktarov, with backing from Western companies and the United States.
- **Popular Front of Irad**: clan militias of mounted irregulars under Bektur Zhumabek uulu, with defecting military detachments and industrial strike committees.

## Casualties and humanitarian impact
No comprehensive casualty count has been published. UNMI and humanitarian agencies have estimated the dead in the low thousands. More than 80,000 civilians were displaced, many of them herding families caught on the wrong side of the new line.<sup>[8]</sup> The fighting damaged railway and road links in the lower Chu valley. The demarcation line separated families and cut seasonal migration routes, and herders now need tribal travel permits to cross it at designated corridors. Mines and unexploded ordnance left in the mountain passes have been cleared under UNMI coordination since 2008.

## Aftermath
Toktarov remained Governor of the ISEZ, and the reorganized DPI, often called the "New DPI", has governed it since. In 2008 ISEZ prosecutors charged Orozbekov in absentia with high treason over the Bill of Sale and with abuse of office over the auctions; he lives in [[wp:Istanbul]].<sup>[14]</sup> In late 2007 a kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]] reorganized the Front's political council as the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC), which coordinates the tribal regions, and the Front's fighters became the [[El-Choro Militias]].<sup>[5]</sup>

When Toktarov annulled the independence ruling, many Iradis saw the move as a first step toward a negotiated unification. Public opinion has since shifted as talks have stalled. A unification referendum was first proposed in 2008, and the only direct negotiations, at the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]], collapsed when the IAC walked out after being offered one-third of the seats in a reunified legislature.<sup>[9]</sup> The ceasefire has held since 2007, and Irad has remained a frozen conflict.

## Historiography
Official ISEZ histories present the emergency administration as having saved the economy and prevented anarchy after the collapse of the DRI. In the tribal regions the Storming of Talas is remembered as a popular rising against corruption, and Bektur is regarded as its hero.<sup>[5]</sup> Historians continue to debate how far US and corporate support for the DPI decided the outcome. They also debate whether the conflict is better described as a single civil war or as two overlapping struggles: a contest for control of the DPI and the state between Toktarov and Orozbekov, and a rural rising against the privatization program. Iradi sources sometimes call it "the 2007 war" or "the succession war".<sup>[1]</sup>

Since the release of the partition drafts, most historians have treated the Bill of Sale as the decisive cause of the war, with the privatization scandals as its background. They disagree about how long Toktarov knew of the negotiations before he made them public, and Orozbekov disputes the authenticity of some of the released pages. Official histories present the leak as the moment the emergency administration saved Irad from being sold. In the tribal regions the affair is remembered as a betrayal by the urban elite as a whole, and scholars date from it a lasting break of trust between the clans and any government based in the cities.<sup>[10]</sup><sup>[11]</sup>

### 3.18. Supreme Court of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Law & Security*

The **Supreme Court of Irad** (Iradi: *Irad Zhogorku Sotu*) is the highest court of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). Established in November 1992 under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], it sits in the Hall of Justice on Liberty Boulevard in [[Talas]] and consists of a Chief Justice and four Associate Justices.<sup>[1]</sup> Its jurisprudence combines American common-law doctrine, introduced during the territorial period, with the continental and Soviet civil-law procedure that survives in much of Iradi private law.

In December 2006 the court issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)|Statutory Independence Ruling]], which held that the US withdrawal from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] had triggered Irad's statutory independence clause and so led to the proclamation of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]]. [[Erlan Toktarov]] annulled the ruling by decree in February 2007.<sup>[2]</sup> The court's jurisdiction is limited in practice to the ISEZ, since the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] settle disputes under customary law. Legal scholars and human rights groups have questioned its independence from the executive.

## History

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
The Organic Act established the Supreme Court as the court of last resort for the territory, and the first justices took office in November 1992. Under the act, the Governor appointed justices with the consent of the Territorial Senate, and the court's final judgments could be reviewed by the United States federal courts.<sup>[1]</sup> Its early work dealt largely with property and contract disputes arising from the privatization of Soviet-era enterprises, the dollarization of the economy and the arrival of foreign investors. The court also developed rules for applying the Soviet-era civil code alongside new territorial statutes based on American models.

The [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]], drafted by [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] as chief legal counsel to the Territorial Senate, reorganized the lower courts into district courts such as the [[Talas District Court]]. It also introduced jury trials in serious criminal cases and established commercial arbitration procedures modeled on US federal practice. The act gave international investors enforceable contract arbitration and protection against arbitrary expropriation, and it confirmed the Supreme Court as the appellate court for the whole judicial system and the regulator of the legal profession.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)
On 3 December 2006 President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty by executive action. Orozbekov, then in private practice, filed an emergency petition asking the court to rule on the territory's status. Within days, before the proclamation of the DRI on 9 December, the court under Chief Justice [[Dastan Isakov]] held that territorial status had lapsed upon federal disengagement. Under the statutory independence clause of the treaty, which had been carried into the Organic Act, sovereignty had therefore passed to the people of Irad.<sup>[2]</sup> Isakov's majority opinion set out what became known as the doctrine of automatic reversion. On the strength of the ruling, the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad, and the court sat as the supreme court of the new state.

#### Annulment and reconstitution (2007)
On 25 February 2007 Toktarov proclaimed the ISEZ from Nurzhan as an emergency administration and annulled the independence ruling by decree, declaring that Irad had returned to "suspended sovereignty". The decree was justified by the secret drafts, released the same day, under which the DRI cabinet would have used the sovereignty recognized by the ruling to dissolve Irad and cede its territory to its neighbors (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]). The court never ruled on the validity of the decree. After DPI forces took control of Talas in the summer of 2007, the emergency administration reconstituted the bench, and Isakov left the court that year.<sup>[4]</sup> Since then the court has operated within the ISEZ's legal framework, which treats the territorial-era statutes and the Organic Act as continuing in force while sovereignty is suspended.

## Composition and appointment
The court has five members: a Chief Justice and four Associate Justices. Since 2007 justices have been nominated by the Governor and confirmed by the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]]. Opposition lawyers and human rights groups say that loyalty to the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] has become an informal requirement for appointment.<sup>[5]</sup> The court sits in the Hall of Justice, a neoclassical building on Liberty Boulevard that also houses its law library.

## Jurisdiction and functions
- **Appeals.** The court hears final appeals from the district courts and from the municipal magistrate courts of Talas, [[Nurzhan]] and [[Iyık Janyl]].
- **Constitutional review.** It reviews legislation and executive acts for conformity with the Organic Act and the territorial statutes that the ISEZ treats as its basic law.
- **Commercial disputes.** It is the final instance in commercial cases, including arbitration awards involving foreign investors.
- **The legal profession.** It administers admission to the bar, through examinations that require proficiency in both Iradi and English commercial law, and oversees professional ethics. It also cooperates with the Faculty of Law of [[Talas State University]] in training judges and lawyers.

Proceedings are conducted in Iradi or English, with interpreters provided for Russian speakers.

## Notable decisions
- ***Statutory Independence Ruling* (2006).** Held that the US withdrawal triggered the statutory independence clause. The ruling provided the legal basis for the Democratic Republic of Irad and was annulled by Toktarov's decree of 25 February 2007.
- ***ISEZ Commercial Concessions Reference* (2008).** Confirmed the authority of the DPI administration to lease strategic mineral concessions to foreign joint ventures without first holding a legislative referendum. Critics said the decision removed a check on the executive.<sup>[5]</sup>
- **Term-limits challenge (2014).** The court declined to hear a challenge to the 2013 amendment to the 1992 Elective Governor Acts that removed gubernatorial term limits, holding that the question belonged to the legislature.<sup>[6]</sup>
- ***Pastoral Transit Case* (2015).** Recognized the customary right of transhumant herders to drive livestock across industrial highway easements without paying municipal licensing penalties. The decision is one of the few in which the court has given effect to customary practice.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Relationship with customary law
The authorities of the tribal regions do not recognize the court's jurisdiction. Civil and criminal disputes there are settled by councils of elders (*aksakals*) and customary judges under [[Adat Law in the ATR|adat]], which the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] recognizes as the law of the tribal regions. There is no formal mechanism for enforcing the court's judgments across the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. Cross-line civil matters, such as livestock disputes, are usually handled through liaison meetings arranged by the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]].

## Criticism
[[wp:Human Rights Watch]] has described a system of "judicial dualism" in which the statutory courts of the ISEZ lack independence from the executive while customary courts in the tribal regions offer no appeal.<sup>[5]</sup> Legal scholars at Talas State University have noted increasing executive pressure on the court since the governor's office reportedly moved to [[Boz-Korgon]] in 2023 and during the uncertainty that followed Toktarov's withdrawal from public view in July 2024.<sup>[8]</sup> Business groups, including the Chamber of Commerce and Industry of Irad, take a more favorable view. They point to the court's record in commercial and property disputes, where it has generally upheld procedural rules and contracts with foreign investors.<sup>[9]</sup>
