### 3.19. Territorial Legislature of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

The **Territorial Legislature of Irad** (Iradi: *Mejlis*) is the legislative body of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) in [[Irad]], and meets at Legislative Hall in [[Talas]]. It was established in 1992 under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]] as the bicameral legislature of the US territory, with a Territorial Senate and a Territorial House of Representatives.<sup>[1]</sup> On 9 December 2006, after the US withdrawal from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]], it proclaimed the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]].

Since 2007 the legislature has sat as a single chamber under the ISEZ administration, which kept the territorial name in line with its doctrine of "suspended sovereignty". Its 60 members are elected in the ISEZ only, and the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) has held a large majority since 2007.<sup>[2]</sup> The [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], which cover two-thirds of the country, are not represented.

## History

#### Establishment (1992–1994)
The Organic Act, which organized the territory after the surrender of the socialist forces on 18 August 1992, provided for an elected legislature and an elected Governor. Pending elections, an interim legislature drawn from the Provisional Government's councils and from district delegates first met in Legislative Hall in October 1992.<sup>[1]</sup> Over the following eighteen months it passed the Banking and Commercial Currency Act and the Toponymic Heritage Statute of 1993, which confirmed the renaming of Kobozev as [[Nurzhan]] and restored Turkic and Sogdian names to streets, districts and reservoirs.<sup>[3]</sup> The first general elections to both chambers were held in 1994. [[Zamirbek Bakiev]], a student leader of the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], was among the members of the first Territorial Senate, and [[Erlan Toktarov]] entered the House of Representatives the same year.

#### Territorial period (1994–2006)
The territorial legislature followed the American territorial model:
- **The Territorial Senate** had 15 members serving four-year terms, three elected from each of five senatorial districts.
- **The Territorial House of Representatives** had 35 members, elected every two years from single-member constituencies apportioned by population.

Bills passed by both chambers needed the assent of the Governor and were subject to review by the [[wp:United States Congress|US Congress]]. The legislature's standing committees covered Ways and Means, Mineral and Energy Resources, Public Works and Inter-Ethnic Affairs, and worked with federal liaison officers and foreign commercial advisers.<sup>[4]</sup> The DPI held majorities in both chambers for most of the period. Toktarov was Majority Leader of the Senate from 2000 to 2004, and [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] served as the Senate's chief legal counsel from 1998 to 2005.

The legislature passed more than 800 statutes in fourteen years. The most significant included:
- the **Banking and Commercial Currency Act** (1993), which authorized the circulation of the US dollar alongside the [[Iradi som]] and chartered [[Talasbank]];
- the **Toponymic Heritage Statute** (1993), the main legal instrument of the [[De-Russification Movement in Irad|de-Russification]] of place names;
- the **Hydrocarbon and Mining Code** (1995), which regulated extraction concessions, including those of [[NurGaz]], and set up royalty funds for road building;
- the [[1995 Latin Orthography Revision]] and the **Language and Education Reform Act** (1997), which standardized the Latin alphabet for Iradi and made instruction in Iradi and English compulsory in public schools;
- the [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]], which introduced jury trials and commercial arbitration modeled on US federal practice.<sup>[1]</sup>

The allocation of mining royalties was among the most contested issues of the period. Legislators eventually adopted compromise formulas that divided antimony revenue between infrastructure in Talas and rural schools in the interior. Committees on major economic bills held public hearings at which community delegates, academics from [[Talas State University]] and trade guilds could submit petitions.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Proclamation of the DRI (2006–2007)
On 3 December 2006 President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty by executive action. After the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], the legislature reconstituted itself as a national assembly. On 9 December 2006 it proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad and elected Orozbekov Prime Minister.<sup>[5]</sup> The assembly sat in Talas through the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]] and the first months of the [[Second Iradi Civil War]]. When the [[Popular Front of Irad]] stormed the city on 4 June 2007, its fighters occupied Legislative Hall, and the DRI government was dissolved.

#### Under the ISEZ (2007–present)
Toktarov's emergency administration, proclaimed from Nurzhan on 25 February 2007, did not recognize the national assembly. After DPI forces took control of Talas in the summer of 2007, the administration reconstituted the legislature under its territorial name as a single chamber.<sup>[6]</sup> Members of the last territorial legislature who accepted the emergency administration sat as a provisional chamber until November 2011. Since then, regular elections have been held every four years on the same day as the gubernatorial election.

At the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]], the DPI offered the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) one-third of the seats in a reunified legislature. The IAC delegation walked out, and no comparable offer has been made since.<sup>[7]</sup> In 2013 the DPI majority passed an amendment to the 1992 Elective Governor Acts that removed gubernatorial term limits (the [[2013 Constitutional Amendment]]).

## Structure and procedure
The chamber has 60 members, elected for four-year terms in multi-member constituencies corresponding to the ISEZ's three cities of governorate significance (Talas, Nurzhan and [[Iyık Janyl]]) and its five districts (Talas, [[Kara-Buura]], Chu, [[Sary-Bulak]] and Lower Naryn). Seats are apportioned according to official population figures. A Speaker, elected from the majority, presides, and the standing committees of the territorial era have been retained. The Speaker since 2019 has been Nurbek Sultanov of the DPI.<sup>[2]</sup>

The legislature passes the budget, confirms the justices of the Supreme Court and other senior appointments, and may amend the territorial statutes that the ISEZ treats as its basic law. Bills require the Governor's assent, and the Governor may veto them. Since 2007, however, much policy has been made by executive decree under the emergency powers the Governor assumed that year, which limits the chamber's role in practice. Debates are held in Iradi and English, with interpretation into Russian.

## Composition
In the 2023 election the DPI won 51 of the 60 seats. Six of the remaining seats went to independents, most of whom vote with the party, and three to Russo-aligned deputies from Nurzhan, who form the only consistent opposition in the chamber.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Criticism
- **Elections.** Observers from the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe]] (OSCE) have described the elections as lacking meaningful choice. They cite the disqualification of opposition candidates, especially in the [[2015 Electoral Reform Controversy]], state control of voter registers and the DPI's dominance of broadcasting through [[IradOne]].<sup>[8]</sup>
- **Representation.** The chamber represents only the ISEZ, which covers about one-third of the country. The IAC argues that the official census undercounts the tribal regions, and that the 2011 offer of one-third of the seats did not reflect their share of the population.<sup>[7]</sup>
- **Executive dominance.** Analysts describe the chamber as largely subordinate to the governor's office, citing the 2013 removal of term limits and the extensive use of decrees.<sup>[2]</sup>

The DPI says that the legislature preserves the legal order of the territorial period, and that it would be enlarged to include representatives of the tribal regions under a unification settlement.

### 3.20. Armed Forces of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Law & Security*

The **Armed Forces of Irad**, officially the **State Defense Corps** (Iradi: *Mamlekettik Korgoo Korpusu*, MKK), are the military forces of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), the part of Irad governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI). The government uses the name "Armed Forces of Irad" because it claims to be the lawful government of the whole country, but the corps operates only in the ISEZ, about one-third of Irad's territory. It consists of Ground Forces, an Air Wing and a reserve of district territorial-defense battalions, and is headquartered at the General Staff complex in [[Talas]]. Official figures give a strength of about 12,000 active personnel and 18,000 organized reservists; independent estimates put the number actually serving somewhat lower.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

The corps was formed in 2007, during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], from territorial-era National Guard units and other forces loyal to [[Erlan Toktarov]]. It is separate from the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]], an ISEZ paramilitary force commanded by Major General [[Ruslan Toktarov]], and from the [[El-Choro Militias]] of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), which answer to the clan councils and the [[Iradi Freedom Society]]. It trains with the United States forces that remain at [[Talas Air Base]] and five other installations. Its main tasks are holding the ISEZ side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], guarding energy and industrial sites, and disaster relief.

## Soviet and post-Soviet origins
In the Soviet period the territory of the Iradi SSR lay within the [[wp:Turkestan Military District]]. Its main ground formation was the 17th Motor Rifle Division, based at Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]), and the airfield west of Talas hosted interceptor squadrons from 1952.<sup>[3]</sup> When the [[Communist Party of Irad]] declared the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI) on 9 October 1991, the division and the republican [[wp:OMON]] detachments passed to the SRI government of [[Vasily Chernov]]. Their officer corps, like the SRI leadership as a whole, was largely ethnic Russian.

During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and the [[First Iradi Civil War]] that followed, the Provisional Government in Talas relied on student militias, defecting police and mounted clan irregulars rather than a regular army. SRI forces surrendered on 18 August 1992 after [[Operation Starlight]], and US forces disarmed and disbanded them. Their heavy equipment, including [[wp:T-72]] tanks and [[wp:BMP-2]] infantry fighting vehicles, was put into depots near Nurzhan under US supervision.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Territorial period (1992–2006)
Under the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]], the defense of the territory was a responsibility of the United States. As in other US territories, the Territorial Legislature created a local force under the Governor's command, the Irad National Guard, which was organized in 1994 with army and air components and trained to US standards. By 2005 it numbered about 6,500, most of them part-time. Its tasks were disaster relief, guarding infrastructure and supporting US forces at Talas Air Base, which became a staging area for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]] under the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]]. Internal security and border control were the job of the Republican Gendarmerie, set up in 1993 to replace the Soviet internal troops.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Formation during the Second Civil War (2006–2007)
The US withdrawal from the treaty on 3 December 2006 ended the Guard's federal status. The [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] (DRI), proclaimed on 9 December, renamed it the Defense Force of the DRI, but the force split along political lines in the crisis that followed. When Toktarov seized control of the DPI and proclaimed the ISEZ from Nurzhan on 25 February 2007, the Nurzhan garrison and most Guard units in the Chu lowlands went over to him. Units in Talas stayed loyal to the DRI cabinet of [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], and some rural personnel joined the [[Popular Front of Irad]].<sup>[5]</sup>

After the Popular Front stormed Talas on 4 June 2007 and dissolved the DRI, DPI forces, with backing from Western companies and the United States, took control of Talas and the other cities over the summer as the Front withdrew to the countryside. An emergency decree of the ISEZ administration in July 2007 merged the pro-DPI Guard units, former DRI units that had surrendered, and recalled reservists into the State Defense Corps. The [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]], signed in September, fixed the corps's front at the Qara-Chik line.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Since 2007
Between 2008 and 2011 the ISEZ returned about forty T-72s and ninety BMP-2s from the Nurzhan depots to service, refitting them with new radios and fire-control equipment.<sup>[2]</sup> Conscription was introduced in 2008. Merging the corps and the El-Choro into a single force was one of the issues left unresolved when the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit|2011 reunification talks]] collapsed.<sup>[6]</sup>

The corps was put on alert during the [[The 2021 Regional Security Alert|2021 Regional Security Alert]], when it reinforced the mountain passes on the Kazakh, Uzbek and Kyrgyz borders after the [[wp:Taliban]] returned to power in [[wp:Afghanistan]]. It was not deployed against the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], which were policed by the Gendarmerie and the municipal police. Governor Toktarov has not appeared in public since July 2024, an absence the DPI attributes to an extended health retreat. The General Staff has since pledged loyalty to "the lawful administration" and kept out of the public rivalry between Ruslan Toktarov and his brother [[Alikhan Toktarov]] (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[7]</sup>

## Command and organization
Under the 2007 emergency decree, the Governor is commander-in-chief. The Ministry of Defense of the ISEZ is responsible for the budget, procurement and conscription, and the Chief of the General Staff, appointed by the Governor, commands the forces. The ISEZ Security Council coordinates the corps with the Gendarmerie and the intelligence services; according to DPI statements, its meetings have been chaired since 2024 by Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]].<sup>[7]</sup>

The corps is organized as follows (strengths are 2023 estimates):<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

- Ground Forces (about 9,500):
  - 1st Mechanized Infantry Brigade, outside Talas, covering the Talas valley and the western Qara-Chik sector;
  - 2nd Motor Rifle Brigade, in Nurzhan, covering the Chu lowlands, the [[NurGaz]] oilfields and refinery, the [[Northern Export Feeder]] pipeline and the rail links to [[wp:Kazakhstan]];
  - Lower Naryn Mountain Rifle Battalion, at [[Tash-Kömür]], guarding the downstream stations of the Naryn cascade and the southern end of the [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]];
  - Sary-Bulak Mountain Rifle Battalion, covering the industrial corridor south of Talas, including the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] and the [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]];
  - an artillery regiment, an engineer battalion, a signals battalion, a reconnaissance battalion and a logistics brigade.
- Air Wing (about 700), on the northern ramp of Talas Air Base, operating helicopters and light transport aircraft.
- Territorial Defense: five reserve battalions, one for each ISEZ district (Talas, [[Kara-Buura]], Chu, [[Sary-Bulak]] and Lower Naryn), whose reservists are called up for annual training.

## Other armed forces in Irad
The Republican Gendarmerie (about 4,000) is not part of the corps. It reports to the Ministry of Internal Security and has been commanded since 2012 by Ruslan Toktarov, who also directs the Special Protective Services. It mans the twelve checkpoints on the ISEZ side of the Qara-Chik line, protects senior officials and the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound, and guards the perimeter of Talas Air Base. The emergency decree allows the Gendarmerie to be placed under the operational control of the General Staff in wartime, but this has never been done.<sup>[4]</sup>

The El-Choro Militias, about 15,000 part-time mounted irregulars, are the armed force of the ATR. They are raised by the 24 clans and coordinated by [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]]. The ISEZ government regards them as an illegal armed formation, but the corps and the militias observe non-aggression arrangements supervised by the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI).<sup>[8]</sup>

## Personnel
The DPI government gives a strength of about 12,000 active personnel and 18,000 organized reservists. In 2023 the *Central Asian Geopolitical Review* estimated that between 10,500 and 11,000 were actually serving, citing understrength conscript companies.<sup>[2]</sup> About 40 percent of the active force are conscripts. Women serve as volunteers, mainly in signals, medical and logistics posts, and made up about 9 percent of personnel in 2022.<sup>[1]</sup>

Since 2008, men aged 18 to 27 who live in the ISEZ have been liable for 12 months of service. Students may defer, clergy and seminarians of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] and other registered religions are exempt, and conscientious objectors may do 18 months of civilian service instead. Men may also buy their way out of service by paying a fee into a defense fund. Residents of the ATR are not called up. Conscripts train at the Ak-Tash Training Center in the western [[Talas Alatau]] foothills, and units rotate through a winter mountain-warfare course in Sary-Bulak District.<sup>[1]</sup>

Most officers are trained at the Military Faculty of [[Talas State University]], and technical officers at the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]]. Older officers are largely Russian-speaking, while those trained since 1992 work mainly in English and Iradi. According to a 2020 study at Talas State University, Kyrgyz, [[wp:Uzbeks]] and Muslims generally are underrepresented in the officer corps.<sup>[9]</sup>

## Equipment
The corps combines Soviet-era heavy weapons with US-origin light equipment from the territorial period. Independent estimates of its inventory in 2023 include:<sup>[2]</sup>

- about 40 T-72 main battle tanks, some held in reserve, and about 90 BMP-2 infantry fighting vehicles, refurbished from former SRI stocks;
- about 60 BTR-70 and [[wp:BTR-80]] armored personnel carriers, and [[wp:MT-LB]] tracked carriers;
- some 200 [[wp:Humvee|HMMWV]] light vehicles, with US-pattern radios, night-vision equipment and body armor, mostly inherited from the National Guard;
- 24 D-30 122-mm howitzers, 12 [[wp:BM-21 Grad|BM-21]] multiple rocket launchers and 120-mm mortars;
- [[wp:ZU-23-2]] anti-aircraft guns and [[wp:9K38 Igla|Igla]] man-portable surface-to-air missiles;
- about 14 Mi-8 and Mi-17 transport helicopters, 6 [[wp:Mil Mi-24|Mi-24]] attack helicopters (fewer than half reported airworthy), 4 [[wp:Bell UH-1 Iroquois|UH-1H]] helicopters and 2 [[wp:Antonov An-26|An-26]] transport aircraft.

The corps has no combat jets and relies on the US presence at Talas Air Base for air defense beyond short range. Its small arms are mainly [[wp:AK-74]] rifles, with M4 carbines in some former Guard units. Uniform cloth and winter clothing are supplied by [[Zhetysu Industrial Textiles]]. The official defense budget for 2023 was about $260 million, about 1.4 percent of the ISEZ's nominal GDP; analysts note that spending on the Gendarmerie and some base-related security costs are budgeted separately.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Doctrine and deployment
The ISEZ Military Doctrine of 2009 defines the corps's tasks as defending the ISEZ against external attack, holding the ceasefire line, protecting "strategic economic facilities" and supporting the civil authorities in emergencies. In practice it is organized for static defense, with most combat units garrisoning the Qara-Chik sector, the oil and gas installations around Nurzhan, and the roads between districts.<sup>[10]</sup>

Under the [[wp:Bishkek]] ceasefire, tanks and artillery may not be kept in a restricted zone on either side of the Qara-Chik line, and UNMI observers inspect the corps's positions there. Its heavy units are therefore based well back from the line, and its forward posts are held by light infantry.<sup>[8]</sup> The engineer battalion is regularly deployed after mudflows (*sel*), avalanches and spring floods to rebuild bridges and clear roads in the river gorges, and in such emergencies corps units have sometimes worked alongside El-Choro riders.

## Relationship with United States forces
US forces remain at Talas Air Base, the largest US military installation in the region, and at five other sites used for logistics, testing and training. They are there under the 2001 Basing Rights Agreement and interim arrangements concluded with the ISEZ administration from 2007 onward, whose texts have not been published in full.<sup>[11]</sup>

US and ISEZ forces hold an annual joint exercise, *Alatau Shield*, focused on counterinsurgency, border security and disaster response. The United States has not treated the ISEZ as a foreign government since 2006, and Irad remains listed at the UN with the United States as its nominal administering power (see [[Political status of Irad]]). US training and equipment have therefore come mainly through base-related programs rather than standard foreign military assistance.<sup>[11]</sup> Analysts describe the legal basis of the relationship as ambiguous, because it rests on arrangements with an administration that Washington does not formally recognize.<sup>[12]</sup>

The ISEZ government regards the US presence as its main security guarantee, and base contracts are a major source of income. [[Kamran Bekzatov]] and other Russian-oriented figures call for closer ties with [[wp:Moscow]], and the [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]] provoked protests across the country, including demonstrations outside Talas Air Base. The ISEZ government said the basing arrangements were unaffected. See also [[Irad–United States relations]].

## Criticism
Rights groups and the *[[Talas Herald]]* have reported hazing of conscripts by longer-serving soldiers (*dedovshchina*) and several non-combat deaths in conscript units. The Ministry of Defense says such cases are investigated and has set up a complaints hotline.<sup>[13]</sup> Critics also argue that the buy-out fee and student deferrals shift the burden of service onto rural and working-class families. Opposition outlets have alleged that conscripts were used as laborers on construction projects, including at Boz-Korgon, which the ministry denies.<sup>[13]</sup>

Analysts note that the Gendarmerie receives newer equipment and better pay than most corps units, and describe it as a counterweight to the regular army under the direct control of the Toktarov family. The government describes the two forces as complementary.<sup>[12]</sup>

### 3.21. United Nations Mission in Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Foreign Relations*

The **United Nations Mission in Irad** (**UNMI**) is a United Nations peacekeeping observer mission in Irad. The [[wp:United Nations Security Council|Security Council]] established it by a resolution adopted on 18 October 2007, a month after the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] ended the main fighting of the [[Second Iradi Civil War]]. UNMI monitors the ceasefire along the 420-km [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] between the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI), and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). It also supervises the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], the annex to the ceasefire that governs the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]], and coordinates humanitarian aid and mine clearance on both sides of the line. It is an unarmed mission deployed under [[wp:Chapter VI of the United Nations Charter|Chapter VI]] of the [[wp:Charter of the United Nations|UN Charter]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The mission is headquartered at Kara-Oi, between [[Talas]] and the line. In 2024 it had about 210 military observers, 30 police advisers and some 420 civilian staff, most of them recruited locally, and its annual budget was about $58 million. The Security Council renews its mandate each October. Both Iradi administrations accept the mission while criticizing it, and diplomats regard it as one of the reasons the line has stayed largely quiet since 2007.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Background
Irad has been on the UN list of non-self-governing territories since 1992. The United States is still recorded as the administering power, although it ended its administration in December 2006 (see [[Political status of Irad]]). The collapse of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] in 2007, the proclamation of the ISEZ by [[Erlan Toktarov]] on 25 February and the storming of Talas by the [[Popular Front of Irad]] on 4 June led to fighting that concerned all three neighboring states, [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], each of which claims Irad. The fighting also threatened the operation of the Toktogul dam, on which irrigation in the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and southern Kazakhstan depends.<sup>[3]</sup>

UN-brokered talks in [[wp:Bishkek]] produced the ceasefire agreement in September 2007. It fixed the Qara-Chik line, provided for a restricted-weapons zone on either side of it, and included the Naryn Protocol as an annex, assigning supervision of the Toktogul release schedule to the United Nations. Both the DPI administration and the leaders of the Popular Front, coordinated by [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], asked for an international monitoring presence, and the Security Council established UNMI on 18 October 2007. The first observers arrived in November 2007, and the mission reached its initial authorized strength in mid-2008.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

## Mandate
The founding resolution and later renewals give UNMI the following tasks:<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

- Ceasefire monitoring: to observe the Qara-Chik line and the restricted-weapons zone, which extends 12 km on either side of it and from which tanks, artillery and combat aircraft are excluded; to investigate reported violations; and to chair a Joint Liaison Committee of the two sides.
- Supervision of the Naryn Water and Power Protocol: to supervise the joint technical commission of ISEZ and ATR engineers that sets the Toktogul release schedule, to verify reservoir levels and releases, and to escort engineering crews crossing the line. Observers from Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan attend the commission's meetings.
- Humanitarian coordination: to coordinate UN agencies and non-governmental organizations, negotiate humanitarian access across the line, and support the return of people displaced in 2007.
- Mine action: to coordinate the survey and clearance of mines and unexploded ordnance left from the 1992 and 2007 wars.
- Reporting: to report to the Security Council through the Secretary-General twice a year. A limited human rights reporting function, covering matters connected with the ceasefire, freedom of movement and humanitarian access, was added in 2012.

The Special Representative of the Secretary-General also offers good offices on the status question. UN officials helped draft the [[2008 Unification Referendum Initiative]], which stalled, and supported the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]], which collapsed when the delegation of the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) walked out after being offered one-third of the seats in a joint legislature. No direct talks have taken place since.<sup>[5]</sup>

UNMI reports to the Security Council, not to the [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]] in [[wp:New York City|New York]], although the committee's annual working paper on Irad draws on the mission's public reports.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Organization
UNMI is led by a Special Representative of the Secretary-General, who is head of mission, assisted by a Chief Military Observer of general-officer rank. It has four components: military observers, police advisers, humanitarian and mine action, and mission support.<sup>[2]</sup>

As of 2024 the mission had:<sup>[2]</sup>

- 210 military observers, against an authorized ceiling of 230;
- 30 police advisers, who work with the municipal police in the ISEZ and the clan-appointed constables of the ATR on cross-line crime and missing-persons cases;
- 142 international civilian staff, 261 national staff and 18 [[wp:United Nations Volunteers|UN Volunteers]].

The military observers come from about twenty countries; the largest contributors have included [[wp:Bangladesh]], [[wp:Ghana]], [[wp:Ireland]], [[wp:Mongolia]], [[wp:Nepal]], Sweden and Uruguay. By convention, Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan, Kyrgyzstan and the United States do not provide observers. The approved budget for July 2024 to June 2025 was $57.9 million, funded through assessed contributions of UN member states.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Headquarters and deployment
The mission headquarters occupies a former Soviet agricultural research station at Kara-Oi, on the ISEZ side between Talas and the line. The site was chosen in 2007 because representatives of both sides could reach it without passing through the other's checkpoints. UNMI also has a liaison office in Talas, a sector headquarters at [[Tash-Kömür]] for the Naryn corridor, and eleven team sites along the line, five of them on the tribal side.<sup>[2]</sup>

Observers patrol by vehicle, on foot and on horseback, and the mission operates two utility helicopters. In the high sections of the [[Talas Alatau]], patrols on [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] horses hired from local herders are often the only practical way to reach observation posts in winter.

## Ceasefire monitoring
Observers patrol the line daily, inspect positions in the restricted zone and investigate incidents reported by either side. UNMI recorded 214 ceasefire violations in 2023. Most involved small-arms fire, movement of armed personnel into the restricted zone or unauthorized construction; many other incidents concerned livestock straying across the line and the herders who followed them.<sup>[4]</sup>

The Joint Liaison Committee meets monthly at Kara-Oi. It brings together officers of the ISEZ [[Armed Forces of Irad|State Defense Corps]] and the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] with captains of the [[El-Choro Militias]], and settles most incidents locally. UNMI credits it with preventing several confrontations from escalating, including a standoff over a river crossing near the [[Kenkol Pass]] in 2016.<sup>[4]</sup>

Since 2023 the ISEZ authorities have barred UNMI patrols from a security zone around the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound east of [[Nurzhan]]. The Secretary-General has described this in several reports as a restriction on the mission's freedom of movement. The ISEZ government says the zone is an ordinary industrial and security area unconnected with the ceasefire.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Naryn Protocol supervision
The Toktogul dam and powerhouse lie on the tribal side of the line, while the switchyard, the national dispatch center and the export lines are in the ISEZ. UNMI chairs the meetings of the joint technical commission, checks daily reservoir-level data and escorts each side's engineers through the other's territory. The annex on revenue sharing has never been published, and UNMI does not report on how the revenue is divided.<sup>[8]</sup>

The five-year release schedule was last renewed in 2022 and falls due again in 2027. Uzbek and Kazakh officials have asked who will sign for the ISEZ, since Governor Toktarov has not appeared in public since July 2024; the DPI says Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] signs on his behalf.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Humanitarian coordination and mine action
UNMI chairs a coordination forum of UN agencies, the [[wp:International Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement|Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement]] and non-governmental organizations working in Irad. The forum negotiates seasonal corridors for vaccines, fodder and medical supplies to highland settlements in the ATR. Its aid registries are among the sources independent demographers have used to estimate the ATR's population, which they put well above the official figure from the [[2018 Irad census]].<sup>[9]</sup>

The mine action component has surveyed or cleared about 31 km² since 2008, mainly in passes of the Talas Alatau and on the approaches to Talas that were mined in 1992 and 2007. It also runs risk-education classes in schools and herding communities.<sup>[4]</sup>

UNMI publishes quarterly security bulletins in Iradi, Russian and English and produces a weekly radio program carried by local stations. Mission officials say these are meant partly to counter rumors of troop movements, which have spread quickly on social media during political crises.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Casualties
Five UNMI personnel have died in service. Two military observers, from Uruguay and Bangladesh, were killed in March 2009 when their vehicle struck an anti-tank mine near the Kenkol Pass. The other three deaths resulted from a road accident and illness.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Mandate renewals
The Security Council has renewed the mandate every October since 2008, usually for twelve months. Renewal debates have divided repeatedly over whether resolutions should refer to the unification referendum, the political status of the territory and the foreign military presence at [[Talas Air Base]]. Most renewals have been adopted unanimously, with occasional abstentions.<sup>[10]</sup>

## Criticism
DPI officials have accused the mission of treating the IAC as an equal party and so lending legitimacy to the ATR's clan councils. ISEZ media, including [[IradOne]], have accused observers of bias after UNMI reports criticized Gendarmerie conduct at checkpoints.<sup>[7]</sup> Tribal leaders, for their part, say UNMI has done too little about restrictions on herders crossing the line and about detentions by the Gendarmerie. They also object that the mission's headquarters and most of its local spending are on the ISEZ side.<sup>[5]</sup>

[[wp:Human Rights Watch]] and other organizations argue that the mission's reporting function is too narrow, and have called for a full human rights monitoring component covering both administrations. The ISEZ government opposes this, and the Security Council has not adopted it (see [[Human rights in Irad]]).<sup>[11]</sup>

Some analysts argue that UNMI has helped freeze the conflict rather than resolve it: since the mission arrived there has been no settlement of Irad's status, and both administrations have come to depend on the ceasefire and on the Naryn revenue. Supporters reply that the line has seen no large-scale fighting since 2007 and that the Toktogul arrangement, which depends on UN supervision, has continued through every political crisis.<sup>[12]</sup>

### 3.22. NurGaz
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Economy & Industry*

**NurGaz** (in full the Nurzhan Oil and Gas Company; Iradi: *NurGaz Kompaniyasy*) is the largest oil and gas company in Irad, headquartered in [[Nurzhan]]. It produces nearly all the crude oil extracted in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), operates [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]], and supplies the crude exported north into [[wp:Kazakhstan]]. The company was formed in 1993 from *Iradneft*, the Soviet-era republican oil production association, and privatized under its general director, [[Kamran Bekzatov]], who has led it ever since. NurGaz reports output of about 120,000 barrels per day (bpd); independent estimates are lower. It is the largest taxpayer and industrial employer in the ISEZ.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Bekzatov is the best-known figure of the Russian-speaking "Russo" generation of Nurzhan and an outspoken critic of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) government on economic policy. The company is widely regarded as the economic base of the pro-Russian current in ISEZ politics.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Soviet period
Commercial oil was found in the Chu basin in 1949 (see [[Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)]]), and production grew through the 1950s and 1960s as Kobozev, as Nurzhan was then called, became the republic's industrial center. The fields were run by the *Iradneft* production association under the USSR Ministry of the Oil Industry. Nurzhan Refinery No. 1 opened in the 1950s and was expanded in 1974. Output peaked at about 140,000 bpd in the early 1980s and then declined as the main reservoirs were depleted.<sup>[4]</sup> Bekzatov, who had worked in the West Siberian oilfields, returned to Kobozev in 1988 as general director of *Iradneft* and introduced water injection to slow the decline.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Privatization
Production fell sharply after independence in 1991 and during the [[First Iradi Civil War]], when Bekzatov kept fuel flowing to Kobozev's power and heating plants. In May 1993, under the economic program of the US territorial administration, *Iradneft* was turned into a joint-stock company, NurGaz, and privatized. Most of the shares went to managers and employees, and through the 1990s Bekzatov and associated holding companies bought up employee shares to gain control.<sup>[3]</sup> Critics at the time argued that the assets had been sold for far less than they were worth; supporters credited Bekzatov with keeping the fields running through the crisis.<sup>[5]</sup>

Output reached a low of about 48,000 bpd in 1996. It recovered over the following decade through new drilling, expanded water flooding and technical service agreements with foreign companies, including [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron]] and Lukoil. Chevron, which had invested in Irad during the territorial period, scaled back its involvement after 2021, when the [[wp:Taliban|Taliban's]] return to power in [[wp:Afghanistan]] stalled a proposed southern export pipeline to [[wp:Pakistan]].<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[6]</sup>

## Export route
Until 2000, crude was moved north by rail to the refinery at [[wp:Shymkent]] in Kazakhstan. In 1998 NurGaz and foreign partners formed the [[Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium]] (IPTC), in which NurGaz holds 51 percent, to build the [[Northern Export Feeder]], an 85-km pipeline from the NurGaz tank farm outside Nurzhan to the Kazakh pipeline network near Shymkent. Crude entering the Kazakh system, which is operated by [[wp:KazTransOil]], is refined at Shymkent or shipped onward to Russia.<sup>[7]</sup> The feeder does not connect directly with the [[wp:Caspian Pipeline Consortium]], whose system begins far to the west. Irad has no southern export pipelines. Transit across the border is covered by the 2008 technical arrangements with the Kazakh border and customs services ([[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]]).<sup>[7]</sup>

## Since 2006
During the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]], the government of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] auctioned new exploration licenses and other state-held energy assets to political insiders, contributing to the unrest that led to the [[Second Iradi Civil War]]. NurGaz itself, already private, kept its fields. After 2007 Bekzatov reached a working arrangement with the ISEZ administration of [[Erlan Toktarov]], under which NurGaz paid royalties and taxes and kept operational control. Relations have become more strained since the mid-2010s, as Bekzatov has criticized DPI economic policy and the ISEZ's reliance on US security guarantees, and has praised Russian president [[wp:Vladimir Putin]].<sup>[3]</sup> In 2019 the ISEZ tax service issued NurGaz a back-tax claim of $212 million, which was later settled for an undisclosed sum.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Production
NurGaz operates several hundred producing wells in the lower Chu basin around Nurzhan. The main field is the one discovered in 1949; smaller fields lie to the north and west. The fields are mature, and most produce far more water than oil. NurGaz reported average production of about 120,000 bpd in 2023.<sup>[1]</sup> Independent analysts, working from KazTransOil transit data, refinery throughput and satellite observation of gas flaring, put output lower, at roughly 85,000–100,000 bpd. The company says the difference comes from export accounting and the inclusion of condensate.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Refining
Nurzhan Refinery No. 1 has a nameplate capacity of about 50,000 bpd; NurGaz reported throughput of about 31,000 bpd in 2023. Catalytic cracking units were added during the territorial period. The refinery produces gasoline, diesel, heating oil and jet fuel for the domestic market, including fuel supplied to [[Talas Air Base]], and its residues are processed at the [[Nurzhan Asphalt and Bitumen Plant]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Natural gas
Irad's proven gas reserves exceed 400 billion m³ but are largely undeveloped. NurGaz uses associated gas from its oilfields to supply homes and industry in Nurzhan and [[Talas]], and holds rights to the undeveloped [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]].<sup>[1]</sup> It has taken part in feasibility studies with the [[wp:China National Petroleum Corporation]] for the proposed Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline, which would carry Turkmen gas to western China. Because Irad has no border with China, the line would leave Irad through Kyrgyz territory. The project has been delayed by objections from clan authorities in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], through which part of the route would pass, and by the uncertainty over Irad's status (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

## Ownership and management
NurGaz is a closed joint-stock company, and its shareholder register is not public. Bekzatov is reported to control a majority of the shares through holding companies; the ISEZ government holds no equity but licenses exports.<sup>[5]</sup> Bekzatov has been president since 1993. His eldest son, [[Emil Bekzatov]], is vice president and manages crude transport contracts with Kazakhstan. The company employed about 14,800 people in 2023, not counting contractors, and trains engineers in partnership with the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Role in politics and society
NurGaz funds housing, polyclinics, sports clubs, including the football club Neftchi Nurzhan (see [[Kara-Ak derby]]), and technical apprenticeships in Nurzhan, which gives Bekzatov a following among the city's Russian-speaking industrial workforce. He uses that position to argue for Irad's entry into the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]], closer relations with Russia and official status for the Russian language.<sup>[3]</sup>

Pro-government commentators have periodically questioned Bekzatov's loyalty. Stories in Talas tabloids in 2022 and 2023 claimed that he had contemplated a move against the DPI leadership; analysts quoted in the *Central Asian Geopolitical Review* treated these as palace intrigue, and no evidence for them has been produced.<sup>[9]</sup>

## Environmental record
Decades of Soviet-era production left contaminated soil and groundwater in parts of the lower Chu basin, where the river ends in a closed basin and spills are not flushed away. Since 2012 NurGaz has installed water-treatment plants and flare-gas recovery units at its central processing facilities, and it says flaring fell by about a third between 2012 and 2022.<sup>[1]</sup> Environmental groups in Nurzhan dispute the company's figures and say that leaks from aging field pipelines continue to go unreported.<sup>[10]</sup>

### 3.23. Attelkom
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Media*

**Attelkom** is the largest telecommunications company and internet service provider in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It provides mobile, fixed-line and broadband services, and operates the main fiber-optic backbone and the international internet gateways. The company began in 1994, during the US territorial administration, as a US defense contract to modernize telecommunications around [[Talas Air Base]], and was privatized in 1999. Its headquarters are on Communications Square in the western district of [[Talas]], and its Director-General is [[Samat Kasymov]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Attelkom carries most of the ISEZ's mobile and internet traffic. Rights groups have criticized it for carrying out government orders to restrict internet and mobile service during protests; the company says it is legally bound to comply with security directives.<sup>[3]</sup>

## History
In the Soviet period, telephone and telegraph services in the Iradi SSR were run by the republican Ministry of Communications. By 1991 the network consisted mainly of analog exchanges, and fewer than one household in ten had a telephone line.<sup>[1]</sup>

After [[Operation Starlight]] in 1992, the United States enlarged Talas Air Base, and in 1994 the [[wp:United States Department of Defense|US Department of Defense]] awarded a contract to a consortium of US contractors to lay fiber-optic cable and build satellite ground stations linking the base with regional command centers. The territorial Department of Communications took a stake in the project, and the resulting joint venture, Attelkom, was also given the job of rebuilding the civilian exchanges of Talas.<sup>[1]</sup> Attelkom opened dial-up internet service in 1996, registering the zone *.ir.ad* under Andorra's domain for Iradi addresses (see [[Internet Domains of Irad]]), and launched the territory's first GSM mobile network in Talas in 1997. In the mid-1990s it also built a relay station east of [[Nurzhan]] as part of a continuity-of-government program; the site was later expanded into the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound.<sup>[4]</sup>

The territorial administration privatized Attelkom in 1999 through a share auction. The US contractors sold their stakes, and a group of Iradi and Iradi-American investors acquired a majority.<sup>[1]</sup> The company completed a network operations center in Talas in 2002 and extended its fiber backbone to Nurzhan and [[Iyık Janyl]] by 2005.

During the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]], the government of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] auctioned mobile frequencies to political insiders. The ISEZ administration annulled those auctions in 2008 and reissued Attelkom's licenses on condition that the company comply with new security directives.<sup>[2]</sup> Attelkom introduced 3G mobile service in 2010 and 4G/LTE in the three main cities in 2016. [[Aidar Maksat]], who served on Attelkom's regulatory board before becoming Lieutenant Governor in 2019, promoted private investment in fiber lines; by 2023 more than 70 percent of urban households had broadband, according to the regulator.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Network and services
Attelkom's main assets are:<sup>[2]</sup>

- the [[Attelkom Fiber Backbone]], about 1,800 km of buried fiber along road and rail rights-of-way linking Talas, Nurzhan, Iyık Janyl, [[Tash-Kömür]] and the northern border crossings, with two international routes through [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and one through [[wp:Uzbekistan]];
- a microwave relay network of solar-powered towers on ridges of the [[Talas Alatau]], serving industrial sites, military posts and villages beyond the reach of fiber (see [[Attelkom Cellular Relay Tower]]);
- 4G/LTE coverage in the three cities and the district centers, with 3G along the main highways;
- the [[Attelkom Data Center]] in Nurzhan, which hosts banking, tax and government systems, and the network operations center in Talas.

The company reported about 2.6 million mobile subscriptions in 2023, about three-quarters of the ISEZ market; smaller operators share the rest.<sup>[2]</sup> With [[Talasbank]] it runs an SMS-based payment service used by herders and farmers to pay for feed and veterinary services and to receive transfers without traveling to a bank branch. Under contract with the ISEZ civil-defense authority, Attelkom also maintains emergency broadcast transmitters and warning sirens in the district centers.<sup>[6]</sup>

Attelkom has almost no formal service in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Its signal reaches some settlements near the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and herders elsewhere rely on satellite telephones or, near the borders, on Kazakh, Kyrgyz and Uzbek networks.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Ownership
Attelkom is a joint-stock company. Its current shareholder register is not public. Independent reporting has linked part of its ownership to holding companies close to the DPI leadership, which the company has not confirmed.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Government control and criticism
Under the 2008 security directives, Attelkom must route international traffic through gateways fitted with monitoring equipment and must carry out orders from the ISEZ executive to restrict service. The Department of Public Information, the government press office, issues lists of websites to be blocked.<sup>[5]</sup>

During the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], mobile data was cut in central Talas for three days and access to several social media platforms was throttled across the ISEZ. Attelkom described the outage as "technical work"; [[wp:Human Rights Watch]] and local journalists attributed it to a government order.<sup>[3]</sup> In 2024, several foreign and exile news sites were blocked after they published reports questioning the authenticity of video addresses by Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]], who has not appeared in public since July 2024. The government said the sites were spreading foreign-sponsored disinformation.<sup>[3]</sup>

Consumer groups have also criticized Attelkom's prices, which are higher than those of operators in neighboring countries, and the slow extension of service to rural areas of the ISEZ.<sup>[6]</sup>

### 3.24. Chongtor Horse
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*

The **Chongtor** (Iradi: *Chongtor zhylkysy*, usually translated 'great mountain horse') is a breed of small mountain horse from Irad. It is raised mainly in the [[Talas Alatau]] and the [[Chatkal River|Chatkal]] highlands, most of which lie in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Stocky, deep-chested and hard-hoofed, it is the main working horse of herding families in the ATR, the mount of the [[El-Choro Militias]] and the usual horse for [[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|ulak tartysh]]. With the [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]], it is one of Irad's two national breeds, and the white stallion on the national flag is officially said to represent both.<sup>[1]</sup>

In Iradi tradition the Chongtor descends in an unbroken line from the first domesticated horses, which national historiography places in Irad. Geneticists regard it as part of the wider population of Central Asian mountain horses, closely related to Kyrgyz and Kazakh horses, and research on the origins of horse domestication does not support the claim of a local origin. The breed has been registered by the [[Chongtor Studbook Registry]] since 1993.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Origins: tradition and genetics
Iradi national historiography holds that the horse was first domesticated in Irad, by the people of the Botai culture (c. 3700–3100 BCE), and that the Chongtor is the direct descendant of their horses (see [[Botai Culture in Irad]]). In folklore it is the horse of the warriors who followed [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]], and epic poems give famous Chongtor stallions names and pedigrees. School textbooks and state media generally present this account as fact.<sup>[1]</sup>

The Botai sites are in northern [[wp:Kazakhstan]]. Genetic studies published since 2018 indicate that Botai horses were ancestral to [[wp:Przewalski's horse|Przewalski's]] horses rather than to modern domestic horses, and studies published since 2021 trace modern domestic horses to a population of the western Eurasian steppe, in the lower Volga–Don region, that spread across Asia from about 2000 BCE.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[4]</sup> A 2022 study by the veterinary faculty of [[Talas State University]] found that Chongtors cluster genetically with Kyrgyz and other Central Asian mountain horses. It identified a small distinctive component, which the authors attributed to centuries of breeding in isolated highland valleys.<sup>[2]</sup> Kyrgyz breeders generally regard the Chongtor as a local strain of the Kyrgyz horse rather than a separate breed.

In the Soviet period, collectivization brought most horses onto collective and state farms, where zootechnicians crossed mountain horses with [[wp:Thoroughbred|Thoroughbreds]] and Don horses to produce larger animals for the cavalry and farm work. Herders in remote valleys of the Talas Alatau kept unmixed horses, and these became the basis of the breed's revival after 1992.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Characteristics
The Chongtor stands 13.2 to 14.2 hands (137–147 cm) at the withers. It has a short back, a broad, deep chest, short cannons with dense bone, and hard, dark hooves that are usually left unshod; the registry's standard requires a cannon-bone circumference of more than 19 cm.<sup>[6]</sup> Common colors are bay, dun (often with dark striping on the legs), roan and grey. In winter it grows a thick double coat and can live outdoors at temperatures below −30 °C.

The breed is valued for endurance at altitude. Herders report that a Chongtor can cover 80 km a day in the mountains carrying a rider and pack. Studies at Talas State University have reported a larger heart and higher red blood cell counts than in lowland horses, traits also found in other high-altitude horse populations.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Breeding and registration
The Chongtor Studbook Registry was established in 1993 under the territorial administration. It works under the joint authority of the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] and the [[National Equestrian Sports Directorate]], making it one of the few bodies shared by the two Iradi administrations. Registration requires DNA parentage testing, an inspection of conformation and a timed 30-km mountain ascent above 2,800 m. Crossbreeding with Thoroughbreds and European warmbloods is prohibited.<sup>[6]</sup> The registry's inspection committees in the clan territories also record the oral pedigrees (*sanjyra*) that herders recite, traditionally going back seven generations in a stallion's line.

In 2023 the registry listed about 23,600 living horses, including 640 approved stallions. Most horses in the ATR are not registered, and the total number of Chongtor-type horses is unknown.<sup>[6]</sup> The ranchers' [[Chongtor Horse Breeding Guild]] issues its own breeding certificates. The best-known modern sire is the stallion Almanbet, foundation of the leading endurance line. Exports are small compared with those of the Akyel and go mainly to trekking stables in [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], [[wp:Mongolia]] and the United States.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Uses
The Chongtor is the main means of transport in the ATR. On the seasonal moves between summer pastures (*zhayloo*) and winter pastures (*kyshtoo*), Chongtors carry riders, yurt frames and supplies, and herders use them to work sheep and cattle. The El-Choro patrol the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] and the mountain borders on Chongtors, and observers of the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] hire them to reach high observation posts in winter. Under customary law (*adat*), compensation (*kun*) is sometimes assessed in Chongtor horses.<sup>[5]</sup>

In ulak tartysh, riders prefer the Chongtor for its strength, low center of gravity and steadiness in close contact with other horses. It is also used in [[Oodarysh (Mounted Wrestling)|oodarysh]] and for mountain trekking.

## Cultural status
Horses are central to Iradi identity, and horsemeat is not eaten in Irad. Many Iradis regard eating it as a "Muslim" practice; ethnographers describe this as a popular prejudice that ignores Kazakh and Kyrgyz custom and has its roots in the marking of Christian identity (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]). Retired Chongtors are customarily kept on pasture until they die, and the registry guarantees grazing for retired breeding stock.<sup>[6]</sup> Approved stallions are branded with an inverted-horseshoe mark that recalls the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]].

### 3.25. Akyel Horse
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*

The **Akyel** (Iradi: *Akyel zhylkysy*, 'white wind') is a breed of light riding horse from Irad, bred mainly on stud farms in the [[Talas River|Talas]] and Chu valleys of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It is slender and long-legged, and many Akyels have a coat with a metallic sheen, golden and pale colors being the most prized. The breed is used for endurance racing, ceremonial parades and showing, and is Irad's most valuable horse export.<sup>[1]</sup>

Iradi tradition and the breed's registry trace the Akyel to the "heavenly horses" of [[wp:Dayuan]] in the [[wp:Fergana Valley]], which the Han emperor Wu sought in the War of the Heavenly Horses (104–101 BCE). The Akyel is of the same type as the [[wp:Akhal-Teke]] of [[wp:Turkmenistan]] and closely related to it genetically. Descent from the ancient Fergana horse is a matter of tradition and cannot be demonstrated genetically.<sup>[2]</sup>

## History
Horses from the Fergana Valley were famous in antiquity. According to the *Records of the Grand Historian*, the Han envoy [[wp:Zhang Qian]], who visited Dayuan around 128 BCE, reported on its fine horses, and the Han court's demand for them led to the War of the Heavenly Horses, after which Dayuan sent horses to China as tribute. Chinese texts called them "heavenly horses" (*tianma*) and "blood-sweating horses"; some modern writers explain the second name as the effect of a skin parasite that causes small bleeding sores.<sup>[3]</sup> Iradi tradition identifies the Akyel with these horses and holds that the northern valleys were grazing grounds for Dayuan's studs. Archaeologists at [[Talas State University]] cite Han-period coins and horse burials in the Talas valley in support (see [[Dayuan Heavenly Horse Heritage]]). Other scholars note that horses of this type were widespread among the oasis states and nomads of [[wp:Central Asia]], and that no direct line to any modern breed can be traced.<sup>[4]</sup>

Iradi chronicles and later tradition say that matched Akyels were sent as diplomatic gifts to Chinese and Persian courts, and that the breed was the riding horse of Iradi nobles and of the clergy of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]. After the Russian conquest of [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] in 1876, Russian officers described such horses as a local strain of the Turkmen horse.<sup>[5]</sup>

The breed nearly disappeared in the Soviet period, when collectivization and crossbreeding with [[wp:Thoroughbred|Thoroughbreds]] left few horses of pure Akyel type. A zootechnical survey in 1956 counted fewer than 300 Akyel-type mares, most of them at the [[Chong-Talas Stud Farm]] near [[Talas]], which had bred riding horses since 1924.<sup>[5]</sup> After 1992 breeders rebuilt the population from these lines. The [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]] was founded in 1995 and took over the National Akyel Studbook, and DNA parentage testing became compulsory in 1998. American interest in Irad during the [[Iradimania]] of the early 1990s helped make the United States the largest importer of Akyels, followed by the [[wp:United Kingdom]] and [[wp:Ireland]].<sup>[6]</sup>

Under the ISEZ, the government set up the [[State Stud Farm of Talas]], and Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]] became the country's best-known Akyel owner. His golden stallion Zharkyn, bred by [[Keneshbek Arstanov]], appears in official portraits and state ceremonies, and his foundation mares are kept at the [[Akyel Breeding Sanctuary]], which press reports describe as his private estate.<sup>[7]</sup> Since the late 2010s demand has grown in [[wp:Hong Kong]] and mainland China.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Characteristics
The Akyel stands 15.0 to 15.3 hands (152–160 cm) at the withers. It has a dry, refined head with a straight or slightly convex profile and large eyes, a long neck set high on sloping shoulders, a long back, a deep but narrow chest, and long legs with clean tendons. The skin is thin, and the mane and tail are fine and sparse. It is lighter in build than the [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]], Irad's other national breed.<sup>[1]</sup>

Common colors are palomino, buckskin, bay, chestnut and pale cream shades; the name "white wind" is traditionally associated with the pale coats. Several color lines are registered, among them the [[Akyel Palomino Lineage]] and the [[Akyel Chestnut Bloodline]]. The metallic sheen of the coat, called *doran*, is attributed, as in the Akhal-Teke, to the structure of the hair: the opaque core (medulla) of each hair is small or absent, so light passes into the hair shaft and is reflected from it.<sup>[2]</sup>

Akyels are spirited and sensitive. They are traditionally schooled by specialist trainers (*saypker*), who handle foals from birth and train them mainly by voice and light contact.

## Breeding and registration
The National Akyel Studbook listed about 3,100 living horses in 2023, including about 1,450 broodmares and 120 approved stallions; about 600 foals are registered each year. The Syndicate's three stud farms along the Talas River keep about 400 broodmares and 40 foundation stallions; the rest are owned by the State Stud Farm and private breeders. Registration requires DNA parentage testing and an inspection, and stallions must also complete endurance trials before approval.<sup>[1]</sup>

Because the modern population descends from a small number of horses, geneticists at Talas State University have reported rising levels of inbreeding and have recommended limits on the use of popular sires.<sup>[2]</sup> Some Turkmen breeders regard the Akyel as an offshoot of the Akhal-Teke and do not accept it as a separate breed.

## Uses and trade
Akyels compete in *[[At Chabysh (Racing)|at chabysh]]* endurance races of 20 to 50 km and in races at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] in Talas, and are exported for international endurance riding. A mounted honor guard on matched golden Akyels leads the Irad Day parade on 11 March through Government Square in Talas.<sup>[7]</sup>

According to the Chamber of Commerce and Industry, about 180 Akyels were exported in 2023, worth about $11.6 million. The main buyers were the United States, the United Kingdom, Ireland, the [[wp:United Arab Emirates]], Hong Kong and mainland China. Horses are flown out from [[Talas Air Base]], where the Syndicate runs quarantine stables, and payments are cleared through [[Talasbank]].<sup>[6]</sup>

## Criticism
Opposition outlets have questioned the public funding of the State Stud Farm, whose leading stallions are associated with the Governor's private breeding operation. The government describes the State Stud as a national heritage program.<sup>[7]</sup> Animal welfare groups abroad have raised concerns about long-haul air transport of young horses; the Syndicate says its shipments follow international veterinary standards.<sup>[1]</sup>

### 3.26. IradOne
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Media*

**IradOne** (officially the IradOne Broadcasting and Media Corporation) is the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) and the largest media organization in Irad. Based at the [[Alikhan Media Center]] in [[Talas]], it runs the national television channel IradOne, the Russian-language sister channel ИрадОдин (IradOdin), a sports channel, the Radio Irad network and an online streaming service. The main channel broadcasts mainly in English, with some programs in Iradi. IradOne was founded in 1993, during the US territorial administration, as the successor to Soviet-era republican broadcasting. Since 2007 it has been under the editorial control of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) government, according to press-freedom organizations and the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]]. [[Alikhan Toktarov]], the second son of Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]], has chaired its board since 2015.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Governor Toktarov has stated that Irad has had a free press since the end of Soviet rule. Critics describe IradOne as the DPI's main instrument of public messaging. Since July 2024 it has been the only outlet through which the Governor's addresses have been released, and those addresses have been the subject of authenticity claims that officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[3]</sup>

## Soviet broadcasting
Regular radio broadcasting in the Iradi SSR began from Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]), then the republican capital, in the 1930s, and television followed in 1959. Both were run by the republic's State Committee for Television and Radio Broadcasting, which broadcast mainly in Russian, with a smaller share of programs in Iradi and relays from Central Television in [[wp:Moscow]]. A second studio opened in Talas in the late 1960s.<sup>[4]</sup> Much of the footage in IradOne's archive, including concert recordings, folklore programs and Iradi-language films of the 1960s and 1970s, comes from this period.

During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and the [[First Iradi Civil War]], the government of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] kept control of the Kobozev studios, while the Provisional Government broadcast radio appeals from the Talas studio.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Territorial period (1993–2006)
In 1993 the Territorial Legislature passed a public broadcasting act that created IradOne as a public corporation. Its board was appointed by the Governor and confirmed by the legislature, and it was funded by territorial appropriations and advertising. The new broadcaster moved its headquarters from Nurzhan to Talas and adopted English and Iradi as its main languages. Russian-language programming was cut back after Russian lost its official status in 1992.<sup>[5]</sup> US media-development programs trained its journalists, and it bought American entertainment programming. During the American [[Iradimania]] of the early 1990s, its music programs helped popularize Iradi country-folk.

Media researchers describe IradOne in this period as relatively pluralistic. Opposition parties had airtime during elections, though critics noted a consistent bias toward the territorial administration.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Under the ISEZ (2007–present)
During the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] the station changed hands more than once. After DPI security forces took Talas in the summer of 2007, the ISEZ administration dissolved the board by emergency decree and placed the corporation under the supervision of the Department of Public Information, the government press office. Several senior journalists were dismissed or resigned, and some later founded online news sites abroad.<sup>[2]</sup>

In 2009 IradOne launched ИрадОдин (IradOdin), a Russian-language channel based at its Nurzhan studio. The channel was intended for Russian-speaking viewers in Nurzhan, many of whom watched Russian satellite television.<sup>[1]</sup>

In June 2015 Governor Toktarov appointed his son Alikhan chairman of the board. Under Alikhan, IradOne launched a sports channel and mobile streaming apps, moved its studios and offices into the new Alikhan Media Center in downtown Talas in 2017, and completed the move from analog to digital terrestrial broadcasting in 2018.<sup>[6]</sup> It also bought exclusive rights to the Ulak Tartysh President's Cup at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] and to the [[Altyn-Zhylky Awards]].

From 2016 the Iradi-American commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]] became a regular panelist on IradOne's current-affairs programs, where he attacked state regulation and taxes. IradOne gave little coverage to the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], which followed his on-air criticism of a municipal licensing surcharge.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Organization and finance
IradOne is a state-owned corporation. Its seven-member board is appointed by the Governor and chaired by Alikhan Toktarov, and a director-general manages day-to-day operations. Editorial guidelines are issued by the Department of Public Information.<sup>[1]</sup> The corporation had about 2,100 employees in 2023. Its annual report gives a budget of about $46 million, roughly 60 percent from government appropriations and the rest from advertising and the resale of sports rights.<sup>[1]</sup>

Its main facilities are the Alikhan Media Center, which houses the television studios, post-production suites and satellite uplinks; the 220-m [[IradOne Transmitter Mast]] on Television Hill in Talas; regional studios in Nurzhan, home of ИрадОдин, and in [[Iyık Janyl]]; and mobile production units that cover festivals, horse races and church feasts.

## Channels and services
- IradOne, the flagship television channel, broadcasting news, current affairs, documentaries and entertainment, mainly in English. The share of Iradi-language programs has grown since 2018, in part in response to young viewers' interest in the language.
- ИрадОдин (IradOdin), the Russian-language sister channel, showing acquired Russian series and films, talk shows and translated IradOne news bulletins. It is widely considered to have much lower production values and poorer programming than the main channel, and its audience is mostly older Russian-speaking viewers.
- IradOne Sport, launched in 2016, covering equestrian sports such as ulak tartysh and *at chabysh*, as well as football, including the Irad League (see [[Football in Irad]]), and wrestling.
- Radio Irad, a national radio network (98.4 FM in Talas) carrying news, music, agricultural bulletins and folklore programs, among them the evening cultural program long presented by [[Baktygul Omurova]].
- IradOne Online (iradone.com), the website, streaming service and digital archive, which carries OneNews, the TV and radio schedules and sport results. Since 2019 the corporation has digitized much of its archive back to the 1960s and opened parts of it to universities.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Programming
IradOne's own English-language programs carry the station's "One" prefix: the news bulletins go out as *[[OneNews]]*, and the main evening bulletin, *OneNews at Eight*, is broadcast each evening at 20:00, followed by the Iradi-language bulletin *[[Kechki Jañylyktar]]*. Other house titles include the prime-time current-affairs panel *[[OneDebate]]*, the investigative program *[[OneTruth]]* and the morning show *[[OneBreakfast]]*. Iradi-language and acquired programs keep their own titles. The channel carries live broadcasts of Irad Day ceremonies and of the liturgy at Iyık Janyl during the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] and at Easter.<sup>[1]</sup> In the same week it has shown, almost every year since 1995, the dubbed *Simpsons* episode "[[The Steppe Family Robinson]]", which is no longer broadcast in the United States.

Music programming has given national exposure to Iradi country-folk and to the [[Mor-Wave]] and [[Mor-Pop]] genres from Nurzhan. IradOne's documentary unit produces nature series on the [[Talas Alatau]] and [[Besh-Tash National Park]] (*[[Seasons of the Talas Alatau]]*, *[[Besh-Tash (TV series)|Besh-Tash]]*), and historical documentaries. Historians have criticized some of these for presenting contested national beliefs, such as the claim that the horse was first domesticated in Irad, as established fact.<sup>[7]</sup>

See also [[List of IradOne programs]].

## Audience and reach
IradOne's terrestrial signal covers the whole ISEZ, and its channels are carried by satellite and online. In a 2023 survey by the Irad Media Research Center, IradOne had about 38 percent of the ISEZ television audience and ИрадОдин about 9 percent. Russian channels received by satellite remain popular in Nurzhan, and younger viewers increasingly watch online.<sup>[8]</sup> The broadcaster has little audience in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], where its signal reaches only areas near the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. Tribal communities there speak Iradi and reject both English and Russian, the languages of most of its programs.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Editorial control and criticism
The OSCE and press-freedom groups have described IradOne's coverage of the 2015, 2019 and 2023 gubernatorial elections as heavily weighted in favor of Toktarov and the DPI.<sup>[9]</sup> Media researchers say that under Alikhan Toktarov the channel has dropped investigative reporting and concentrated on government infrastructure projects and state-approved popular culture. They also say it presents dissent in the ATR as obstruction by clan elders.<sup>[2]</sup> Opposition figures have complained of being kept off the air. Among them is the oil executive [[Kamran Bekzatov]], who rarely appears on IradOne despite his prominence. IradOne did not report the 2018 allegations, published by exiled journalists, about Alikhan Toktarov's purchase of villas in [[wp:Dubai]].<sup>[2]</sup>

Since July 2024, video and audio addresses by Governor Toktarov, who has not appeared in public in that period, have been released only through IradOne. Opposition outlets and technology monitors have questioned the recordings' authenticity, which officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]); IradOne says the addresses are recorded at the Governor's residence. No independent journalists have been allowed to attend a recording.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[10]</sup> Several foreign and exile news sites that questioned the recordings were later blocked by [[Attelkom]].

Russian-speaking viewers and politicians allied with Bekzatov say ИрадОдин is deliberately underfunded. IradOne says the channel's budget reflects the size of its audience.<sup>[8]</sup> See also [[Media of Irad]].

- [IradOne Online](https://iradone.com/) (official website)
