### 3.27. Winter Uprising of 1992
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: History*

The **Winter Uprising of 1992** (Iradi: *Kyshky Kötörülüsh*, 'winter uprising') was a wave of protests, strikes and armed revolt against the government of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI) in the winter of 1991–92. It began in December 1991, provoked by the economic collapse that followed the end of Soviet rule and by emergency decrees of the government of First Secretary [[Vasily Chernov]], and ended with anti-socialist forces in control of [[Talas]], Irad's largest city. On 11 March 1992 the Provisional Government in Talas proclaimed the Republic of Irad, beginning the [[First Iradi Civil War]] against the SRI government in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]). The proclamation is commemorated each year as Irad Day.<sup>[1]</sup>

The uprising brought together students, industrial workers, intellectuals, clergy of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] and mounted horsemen from the mountain clans. Its best-known episode is the "March of the Thousand Saddles" of 18 February 1992, when several thousand horsemen rode into Talas. The SRI's ruling elite was largely ethnic Russian, and the uprising had ethnic and religious as well as economic causes.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Background
On 9 October 1991 the Supreme Soviet, led by Chernov, adopted the [[Declaration of State Sovereignty (1991)]], and the [[Communist Party of Irad]] declared the independent Socialist Republic of Irad, ahead of the dissolution of the [[wp:Soviet Union]] on 26 December. The new state inherited the economic problems of the late Soviet period, which had already led to the [[The Kobozev Bread Riots (1989)|Kobozev bread riots of 1989]]. The end of transfers from [[wp:Moscow]] and the breakdown of trade within the ruble zone halted many factories in Kobozev, and by early 1992 annual inflation exceeded 1,000 percent. Bread was rationed in Talas from December 1991.<sup>[3]</sup>

Chernov, an ethnic Russian refinery engineer who had led the Communist Party of Irad since 1986, refused to legalize opposition parties. In November 1991 his government issued emergency decrees that suspended constitutional liberties, banned non-communist gatherings and publications, and ended the limited self-government of the rural clans. State procurement agents were sent to requisition grain and horse herds in the mountain districts.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[4]</sup> The party and security apparatus was dominated by ethnic Russians. Iradi grievances over [[wp:Russification]], the closure of churches and the suppression of the [[1956 Talas Riots]] had been aired openly during perestroika by groups such as the *Ene-Til* ('Mother Tongue') society of the historian [[Amanbek Alimbekov]].<sup>[2]</sup>

## December 1991: first protests
The first demonstrations took place in Talas in early December 1991, when students of [[Talas State University]] and members of Ene-Til gathered on Central Square to protest against the decrees and the bread shortage. Police dispersed the crowds and detained several organizers. In the same weeks, herders in the [[Talas Alatau]] resisted the requisition of their horses. Clashes with procurement detachments in mountain villages, in which several people were killed, later became a theme of Iradi songs and poems.<sup>[4]</sup>

## January 1992: strikes and occupation
The protests widened in January 1992. Railway workers at the Kobozev depot went on strike over unpaid wages on 14 January, and strikes spread to factories in Kobozev and Talas. In Talas, students led by [[Zamirbek Bakiev]] occupied Central Square, and priests of the Syro-Iradi Church celebrated open-air liturgies among the protesters. The strikes in Kobozev, whose industrial workforce was largely Russian-speaking, remained mostly economic; it was in Talas that protesters demanded the resignation of the Chernov government.<sup>[5]</sup>

## The 3 February shootings
On 3 February 1992 [[wp:OMON]] detachments opened fire on demonstrators on Central Square in Talas. Estimates of the dead range from 11, the figure given by the SRI government, to 46, the figure later given by the Provisional Government; a commission of the Territorial Legislature documented 27 deaths in 2002.<sup>[6]</sup> Over the next three days, armed workers, defecting police and clan horsemen from the surrounding mountains seized the city's arsenals and the regional party headquarters. SRI officials left for Kobozev, and a coordinating council chaired by Alimbekov, the body that became the Provisional Government, took over the administration of the city.<sup>[1]</sup>

Some Russian-owned property in Talas was attacked after the shootings, and several thousand ethnic Russians left the city for Kobozev or Russia during the winter; archival estimates of their number vary.<sup>[6]</sup>

## The March of the Thousand Saddles
On 18 February 1992 several thousand horsemen from the clans of the Talas Alatau and the Chatkal valleys rode into Talas in a procession remembered as the *March of the Thousand Saddles*. Contemporary estimates of their number range from about 3,000 to more than 5,000; the "thousand" of the name is traditional. The riders formed a cordon around Government House and Central Square, and the interior troops still in the Talas garrison negotiated their withdrawal to the north. Among the riders were men who later fought in the siege of Talas under [[Kuban Mamytov]] and [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]].<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[5]</sup>

## Proclamation of the Republic
By late February the SRI had lost control of Talas and most of the Talas valley. On 11 March 1992, at the [[Talas Town Hall Meeting (1992)|Talas Town Hall Meeting]] in the city's municipal theater, delegates and clan elders elected Alimbekov Provisional President and adopted the *Declaration of Restoration of the Republic of Irad*, repudiating the SRI. The government in Kobozev did not recognize the proclamation. In late March it sent the 17th Motor Rifle Division south along the Chu and Talas corridors, beginning the First Iradi Civil War, which ended after the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] of 2 June and [[Operation Starlight]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Casualties
Archival estimates of the number of people killed between December 1991 and the proclamation in March 1992 vary from about 60 to about 150. Most of the deaths occurred in Talas and in the mountain villages where requisitions were resisted.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Legacy and commemoration
The proclamation of 11 March is commemorated as Irad Day, the main national holiday (see [[March 11 Independence Day]]). The victims of the 3 February shootings are remembered at a memorial on Central Square in Talas, unveiled in 1997, where a service is held on each anniversary.<sup>[7]</sup>

Both Iradi administrations claim the uprising's legacy. Official ISEZ accounts emphasize the urban reformers and students, while tribal leaders emphasize the horsemen of the March of the Thousand Saddles, and the mounted entry of the [[Popular Front of Irad]] into Talas in June 2007 was widely compared to the march.<sup>[5]</sup> In his 2001 memoir, Chernov described the uprising as a nationalist insurrection that made the breakup of the republic inevitable. Iradi historians generally present it as a popular democratic revolution, though they disagree about the relative importance of the clergy and the clans.<sup>[8]</sup>

### 3.28. Church of St. Janyl
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Religion*

The **Church of St. Janyl** (also **Church of St. Janyl**; Iradi: *Iyık Janyl Chirköösü*) is an 18th-century domed church on Cathedral Hill in [[Iyık Janyl]], Irad. It is the cathedral of the [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]], the mother see of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], and the country's main place of pilgrimage. The church was built under a charter of 1727 from Abd al-Rahim Biy, ruler of [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]], who granted it in recognition of Iradi Christian cavalry that had fought for Kokand against the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]]; the city of Iyık Janyl grew up around it. Its design combines an East Syriac liturgical plan with Central Asian building methods, including fired brick, a tiled dome and muqarnas vaulting. During the US territorial period it was inscribed on the [[wp:World Heritage Site|UNESCO World Heritage List]] on a nomination by the United States.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Church tradition regards the building as a symbolic re-creation of the First Church of Janyl, the lost church said to have been founded by [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]]. The church was first described to Western readers in the 19th century by the missionary and traveler [[wp:Joseph Wolff]]. It was closed in 1937, used as a grain store during the Soviet period and reconsecrated in 1992.

## Background
Christianity reached the Turkic pastoralists of the region through the Church of the East from the 11th century and flourished under the [[wp:Qara Khitai]] in the 12th.<sup>[3]</sup> After persecution under the Chagatai khan Ali-Sultan in the 14th century and the campaigns of [[wp:Timur]], the organized dioceses disappeared, and Iradi Christians maintained their liturgy through clandestine worship in the mountains. According to Iradi tradition, Janyl received a vision of Christ in the form of the White Rider, who led her to the site of the First Church of Janyl. Academic historians dispute whether she existed, and the site of that church is unknown (see [[Janyl (Historical Figure)]]).<sup>[4]</sup>

## Construction
In the 1720s the [[wp:Dzungar Khanate]] raided the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and the lands to its north. Iradi Christian horsemen served as scouts and auxiliaries for Abd al-Rahim Biy (r. 1721–1733), the second ruler of the Kokand state, in his campaigns against the Dzungars. In 1727 he issued a charter (*firman*), known in Iradi historiography as the [[Treaty of Talas (1727)]], that granted the Christians freedom of worship, exempted their clergy from certain taxes and provided funds for a stone church.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[5]</sup>

According to the cathedral chronicle, masons from [[wp:Samarkand]] and [[wp:Bukhara]] built the church together with East Syriac monks of the local community, and it was consecrated in 1730. Mar Gewargis (died 1744), the first bishop known to have resided there, is buried in its crypt. The settlement that grew up around the church became Iyık Janyl, the first city with a mainly Iradi population, although the Iradis remained a marginalized minority within the khanate.<sup>[1]</sup>

## 19th century
The earliest known Western description of the church is that of Joseph Wolff, the British missionary and traveler. His account drew the attention of European churchmen to the survival of a Christian community in [[wp:Central Asia]].<sup>[6]</sup> After the Russian conquest of Kokand in 1876 the church continued to function under Russian rule, and Russian orientalists visited it and the nearby ruins of Shar-i-Kuhna.

## Union with Rome and the Soviet period
When the Syro-Iradi Church entered into union with Rome in 1931 under [[wp:Pope Pius XI|Pope Pius XI's]] bull *Orientalis Stella*, negotiated by Bishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]], the church became the cathedral of the new archiepiscopal see. Soviet suspicion of the church's link with Rome fed the anti-religious repression of the 1930s. In the [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges|1937 purges]] senior clergy were executed and the church was closed, and its nave was used as a district grain store. Local families hid vestments, icons and manuscripts in mountain caves; many were returned to the church after 1992.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Restoration and World Heritage listing
After the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], the building was returned to the church and reconsecrated on 14 September 1992, the anniversary of the 1931 bull. It was restored between 1993 and 1998 with territorial grants, US heritage funds and money from Christian charities, including some that received proceeds from the 1993 compilation album *[[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|Swift Hooves]]*. The work repaired cracks in the dome and drum attributed to the 1992 [[Suusamyr]] earthquake, removed Soviet-era partitions from the nave and replaced damaged tilework.<sup>[1]</sup>

The United States, as the State Party responsible for the territory, nominated the church for the World Heritage List, and in 2004 the church and the nearby [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]] (Shar-i-Kuhna) were inscribed together as a single property.<sup>[2]</sup> Irad itself is not a State Party to the World Heritage Convention, and [[wp:UNESCO]] continues to list the property under the United States. Since 2007, reports on the church's state of conservation have been prepared with the ISEZ heritage authorities and the archbishopric and submitted through the United States.<sup>[8]</sup> Two other sites in Irad, the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]] and the [[Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed]], were included in a transnational [[wp:Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang'an-Tianshan Corridor|Silk Roads]] nomination by [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], over the protests of both Iradi administrations.

## Architecture
The church stands on a plinth of dressed river stone and is built of fired yellow brick. Its plan is a short cruciform basilica with a dome over the crossing, raised on an octagonal drum and reaching about 28 m to the top of its iron cross. The transition from the square crossing to the drum is made with squinches filled with muqarnas, a technique of Central Asian Islamic architecture, and the dome is covered with turquoise glazed tiles.<sup>[2]</sup>

The interior follows East Syriac practice, with a raised sanctuary (*madbaha*) at the east end behind a carved walnut screen. Carved gypsum (*ganch*) panels carry verses of the Psalms in [[wp:Syriac language|Classical Syriac]] and in Turkic written in Syriac script, a practice also known from the medieval Christian gravestones of the Chu valley. The main portals are of carved walnut. The principal devotional object is an 18th-century icon of St. Janyl and the White Rider. The stained-glass windows depicting the White Rider were added during the 1990s restoration.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

The crypt beneath the altar contains the tomb of Mar Gewargis, surrounded by sandstone friezes of early Christian missionaries on the [[wp:Silk Road]]. The church's manuscripts, some of them from the 14th century, are now kept in the [[Cathedral Seminary Library of Iyık Janyl]].<sup>[7]</sup>

## Religious life
The cathedral is the seat of the Archbishop of Iyık Janyl. Its liturgy follows the Iradi-Syriac rite, based on the [[wp:Liturgy of Addai and Mari|East Syriac Liturgy of Addai and Mari]], with the sacramental bread *kümöch* and juniper incense (*[[Arsha (Flora)|arsha]]*) (see [[East Syriac Rites of Iyık Janyl]]). Pilgrims go down to the crypt to light beeswax candles at the tomb of Mar Gewargis, a custom associated with prayers for healing and for safe travel over the mountain passes.<sup>[4]</sup>

The largest gathering is the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] on 24 August, when pilgrims, many on horseback, come from both the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Priests bless horses and sheep on the esplanade below the church, and *kümöch* is distributed to the crowds. Because armed forces of both administrations are barred from Iyık Janyl, pilgrims from the tribal regions can attend without meeting soldiers or checkpoints in the city.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Conservation and controversies
The church stands in a seismically active zone and, at an elevation of 1,280 m, is exposed to repeated freezing and thawing. A conservation plan adopted by the [[Syro-Iradi Episcopal Synod]] in 2019 relies on traditional lime mortars and handmade glazed tiles.<sup>[9]</sup> Heritage specialists criticized the 1990s restoration for using cement mortar and factory-made tiles, which trapped moisture in the brickwork; some of that work has been reversed since 2010.<sup>[8]</sup>

The interior is also debated. Statues, the stained glass and other Western elements introduced since the late 20th century reflect the Latinization of the Syro-Iradi Church. Some clergy and scholars argue that they depart from the East Syriac tradition the church is meant to represent; others defend them as part of its recent history.<sup>[4]</sup>

### 3.29. Political status of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

The **political status of Irad** is disputed. [[Irad]], a doubly landlocked territory of about 196,754 km² in [[wp:Central Asia]], has been on the United Nations list of non-self-governing territories since 1992. The United States is still nominally recorded as its administering power, although US administration ended in December 2006.<sup>[1]</sup> Since the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] of 2007 the territory has been divided by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI), claims to be the lawful authority for all of Irad and describes the country's sovereignty as "suspended" until a unification referendum is held. The clans of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), loosely coordinated by the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC), reject that claim. [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] each claim the territory *de jure*.<sup>[2]</sup>

Twenty-six UN member states recognized the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] (DRI), proclaimed in December 2006, before it was dissolved in 2007. A minority of them still maintain some form of recognition of a "Republic of Irad", which is why Irad is often described as partially recognized. Commentators compare its position with that of [[wp:Western Sahara]]: a territory listed for decolonization whose administering power has left without completing the process, and whose final status depends on a vote that the parties have never agreed how to hold.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Background

#### Soviet republic and independence (1925–1992)
The Iradi lands were divided among the Syr-Darya, [[wp:Semirechye]] and Fergana oblasts of [[wp:Russian Turkestan]] after 1876. The Soviet national delimitation created the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] in 1925 as a union republic, the first political unit defined by Iradi nationality. On 9 October 1991 the [[Communist Party of Irad]] declared independence as the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI), based in Kobozev. No foreign state recognized the SRI before it was overtaken by the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]].

On 11 March 1992 the Provisional Government in [[Talas]] proclaimed the Republic of Irad, beginning the [[First Iradi Civil War]] against the SRI. Facing the fall of the besieged city, Provisional President [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] signed the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] with [[wp:James Baker|US Secretary of State James Baker]] on 2 June 1992. Under the treaty Irad entered into association with the United States in exchange for military protection. After [[Operation Starlight]], the last SRI forces surrendered on 18 August 1992.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Territorial association (1992–2006)
Under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], Irad became an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States on 18 August 1992. It had an elected Governor, the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] and the [[Supreme Court of Irad]], while a US High Commissioner represented the federal government in Talas (see [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)]] and [[List of governors of Irad]]). Later in 1992 Irad was added to the UN list of non-self-governing territories, with the United States as administering power, and during the territorial period the United States transmitted annual information on the territory to the UN.<sup>[1]</sup>

The treaty, and the Organic Act that gave effect to it, contained a provision usually called the statutory independence clause. Under it, sovereignty would revert to the people of Irad if the United States ended its administration. Alimbekov later wrote that the clause had been his main condition in the 1992 negotiations.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Withdrawal and the Democratic Republic (2006–2007)
On 3 December 2006 President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the treaty by executive action (see [[United States Withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty (2006)]]). The withdrawal ended federal civilian administration, the office of the High Commissioner and federal transfers to the territorial budget. It did not end the US military presence at [[Talas Air Base]], which continued under the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]]. The United States also did not formally notify the United Nations that its administration had ended.<sup>[1]</sup>

On 7 December 2006 the Supreme Court of Irad issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]]. In a majority opinion by Chief Justice [[Dastan Isakov]], it held that territorial status had lapsed with federal disengagement and that the independence clause had taken effect automatically, a holding known as the doctrine of automatic reversion. On 9 December the Territorial Legislature reconstituted itself as a national assembly, proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad and elected [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] Prime Minister.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Suspended sovereignty (2007–present)
Between December 2006 and February 2007 the DRI cabinet secretly negotiated with Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan, under cover of border consultations, draft treaties that would have used the sovereignty conferred by the ruling to dissolve Irad and partition it among them (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]). On 25 February 2007, amid the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]], [[Erlan Toktarov]] made the drafts public, seized control of the DPI and proclaimed the ISEZ from [[Nurzhan]] as an emergency administration. He annulled the independence ruling by decree and declared that Irad had returned to "suspended sovereignty", a status that ISEZ accounts defend on the ground that with no sovereign in place, no government can cede or sell Iradi territory.<sup>[17]</sup> The court never ruled on the validity of the decree. The DRI cabinet in Talas continued as a rival authority until the [[Popular Front of Irad]] stormed the city on 4 June 2007; DPI forces took the cities over the summer as the Front withdrew to the countryside.<sup>[2]</sup>

The UN-brokered [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] of September 2007 fixed the Qara-Chik line, and the [[wp:United Nations Security Council|Security Council]] established the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) on 18 October 2007 to monitor it. Neither the ceasefire nor the resolution establishing UNMI addressed the territory's sovereignty. The arrangement has since become a frozen conflict, in which each administration governs its own zone and neither is recognized as a sovereign government.

## Status under international law

#### Non-self-governing territory
Irad's listing rests on [[wp:Chapter XI of the United Nations Charter|Chapter XI]] of the [[wp:Charter of the United Nations|UN Charter]], which applies to territories "whose peoples have not yet attained a full measure of self-government". A territory leaves the list when the General Assembly accepts that it has reached a full measure of self-government, through independence, free association or integration with an independent state. No such decision has been made for Irad. The [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]], which sits in [[wp:New York City|New York]], issues an annual working paper on the territory, drawing on UNMI's public reports, press coverage and submissions by petitioners.<sup>[1]</sup>

Both Iradi administrations send petitioners to the committee's sessions. [[Tilek Kurmanov]], who represented the ISEZ at the United Nations from 2008 to 2017, argued there that Irad is neither a US territory nor an independent state and that its status must be settled by a unification referendum. He opposed proposals to remove Irad from the list before such a vote and rejected the claims of the three neighboring states. IAC petitioners have presented a competing view, that the people of the tribal regions have never been consulted on any of Irad's changes of status and that a referendum must give their population its full weight.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[7]</sup>

#### The administering power
Because Washington did not notify the UN that it had ended its administration, the United States is still recorded as the administering power. According to the committee's working papers, it has not transmitted information on the territory since 2006. Irad therefore remained listed (contemporary sources say 'by US demand'), and the United States neither reports on the territory nor has asked for its removal.<sup>[1]</sup> Legal scholars disagree on the effect. Some argue that the United States remains bound by the obligations of an administering power until the General Assembly releases it. Others hold that the withdrawal ended its role in fact, leaving Irad a listed territory without an administering power, a position commentators compare with Spain's departure from Western Sahara in 1976.<sup>[8]</sup>

The withdrawal also raised a domestic constitutional question in the United States, whether a president could end a treaty of territorial association without the approval of [[wp:United States Congress|Congress]]. The treaty allowed either party to withdraw but did not say how. No US court has ruled on the question.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Automatic reversion and its annulment
Supporters of the 2006 ruling, including Orozbekov, argue that it remains valid law. The Supreme Court never ruled on Toktarov's annulment decree, they say, and a governor's decree cannot overturn a judgment of the territory's highest court. On this view Irad became independent in December 2006, and the ISEZ is a *de facto* administration within an independent state. The ISEZ holds that the ruling was annulled by a lawful emergency decree and that the Organic Act and the territorial statutes remain in force, with their provisions on federal oversight inoperative, until the people of Irad decide the question by referendum.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[8]</sup> The IAC accepts neither the Organic Act nor the emergency decree as applying to the tribal regions.

#### Recognition
The 26 UN member states that recognized the DRI did so between December 2006 and February 2007. After the DRI government was dissolved in June 2007, most of them let their recognition lapse without a formal statement. A minority still maintain some form of recognition of a "Republic of Irad", usually without naming either administration as its government, and a few accept ISEZ trade representatives under that title.<sup>[3]</sup> No state recognizes the ISEZ or the IAC as a sovereign government, and Irad is not a member of the United Nations or of any of its specialized agencies.

## Positions of the parties

#### Irad Special Economic Zone
The ISEZ government describes Irad as a single country whose sovereignty is suspended. It gives national titles, such as "[[Armed Forces of Irad]]", to its own institutions, and its official documents continue to use the name Republic of Irad. Its stated policy is a unification referendum held in both zones, after which a unified Irad would choose its permanent status.<sup>[9]</sup> It treats the Qara-Chik line as an administrative line within Irad rather than a border, and describes the clan councils of the ATR as local customary authorities rather than a rival government.

Analysts argue that the ISEZ leadership has preferred the status quo. Leaked US intelligence reports from Toktarov's first term concluded that he maintained Irad's quasi-territorial status to avoid conflict with regional powers, specifically Russia and China. Analysts add that the tribal regions buffer the ISEZ against unrest on its borders, and that Irad's subnational status lets it deflect Russian and Chinese pressure toward Washington.<sup>[10]</sup> Toktarov has nonetheless publicly welcomed closer alignment with China.

#### Iradi Freedom Society
The IAC does not recognize ISEZ authority over the tribal regions and does not accept that the Organic Act applies there. It says it supports a unified Irad, but only with representation that reflects the population of the tribal regions and with clan control over land and water in the mountain catchments. Tribal leaders' public statements have nonetheless shown broad ambivalence toward Iradi statehood, and the IAC has never proclaimed independence for the tribal regions or sought recognition as a state.<sup>[11]</sup> The IAC also argues that no government representing the whole country has consented to the presence of foreign forces at Talas Air Base.

#### Neighboring claimants
Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan each claim Irad *de jure*, on the basis of their own readings of the Soviet national delimitation and of historical settlement in the region. [[wp:Tajikistan]], which does not border Irad, makes no claim. None of the three administers any part of Irad, and in practice all three coordinate border management with the ISEZ.<sup>[2]</sup> In 2006–07 their foreign ministries took part in the secret negotiations on the Bill of Sale, which they describe as consultations; none has published its copy of the drafts. None has rescinded its claim since the affair, and none has threatened military action. Analysts report that the prevailing view in all three capitals is that Irad will break apart on its own, and that the neighbors are content to wait.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>
- **Kazakhstan** carries most Iradi crude north to its pipeline network near [[wp:Shymkent]]. The 2008 [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]], a set of technical arrangements covering the ISEZ-held sections of the northern frontier, is described by Kazakhstan as implying no recognition. The standing of the territorial-era [[Treaty of Good Neighborliness (1994)]] on the Chu and Talas rivers is disputed, although its technical provisions continue in practice.
- **Uzbekistan** depends on summer releases from the Toktogul reservoir for irrigation in the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and has accused the joint water commission of holding back water in dry years.
- **Kyrgyzstan** regards the Naryn cascade as Kyrgyz infrastructure and attends the commission under protest.

All three sit as observers on the joint technical commission of the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], the main reason they take part in the ceasefire arrangements at all.<sup>[12]</sup> The sections of the external frontier held by the ATR have never been demarcated, and clan patrols clashed with neighboring border guards on the western desert frontier in 2018 (see [[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]]).

#### United States
The United States does not report on the territory and has made no formal statement on its status since the 2006 withdrawal. US forces remain at Talas Air Base and five other installations under the 2001 agreement and supplementary arrangements concluded with the ISEZ from 2007 onward, which have not been published in full.<sup>[13]</sup> In February 2025 [[wp:Donald Trump|President Donald Trump]] spoke of exploring the sale of Irad to Russia to keep China from acquiring it, treating Irad as if it were still a US territory; it is not known whether he was aware that US administration had ended in 2006. The remarks provoked demonstrations in both zones, and the ISEZ delivered a demarche through UNMI stating that Irad's sovereignty was not negotiable (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]).<sup>[14]</sup> Many Iradis read the remarks in the light of the 2007 Bill of Sale, and "Irad is not for sale" became the slogan of the protests. The Special Committee on Decolonization responded with a statement recalling that the territory's future is a matter for its people.

#### United Nations
UNMI reports to the Security Council through the Secretary-General, not to the Special Committee. Its Special Representative offers good offices on the status question, and UN officials helped draft the 2008 referendum proposal and supported the 2011 talks.<sup>[15]</sup> Security Council debates on the renewal of UNMI's mandate each October have divided repeatedly over whether resolutions should refer to the referendum, the territory's status and the foreign military presence. Most renewals have nonetheless been adopted unanimously.

## Unification process
The [[2008 Unification Referendum Initiative]], drafted with the help of UN officials, envisaged a confederal settlement with a joint legislature, a vote in both zones and guarantees for customary law. Talks collapsed after the DPI refused to give tribal councils a veto over foreign mining concessions in mountain watersheds. Voter registration in the tribal regions was also left unresolved.<sup>[3]</sup>

The [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]], held at Kara-Oi in April and May 2011, remains the only substantive direct negotiation. The DPI offered the tribal regions one-third of the seats in a unified legislature, together with state control of mountain water catchments and mineral licensing. The IAC asked for representation reflecting the ATR's two-thirds share of the territory and, by its own estimates, of the population. Its delegation walked out in late May, and no direct talks have taken place since.<sup>[11]</sup>

Polling in 2017 found large majorities in the ISEZ in favor of a unified sovereign state. Limited polling in the tribal regions also showed support, although tribal leaders alleged DPI interference in the surveys.<sup>[3]</sup>

The size of the tribal population is central to any formula for representation or for a referendum. The DPI's [[2018 Irad census]] counted 4,626,000 people, enumerating the ISEZ directly but only estimating the ATR at about 2.2 million. Independent researchers, drawing on satellite settlement mapping and aid-agency registries, put the national population at about 6.2 million and the ATR at 3.5 to 4 million. The IAC argues that the undercount was meant to reduce the tribal regions' weight in any unified legislature; the DPI attributes the gap to restricted access.<sup>[16]</sup>

## Practical consequences
Irad's unresolved status affects much of its external life:
- **International organizations.** Irad is not a member of the United Nations, the [[wp:Universal Postal Union]] or the international financial institutions. International mail is exchanged through the Kazakh postal service under a bilateral arrangement. The ISEZ has no sovereign credit rating and no access to the [[wp:International Monetary Fund]].
- **Codes and identifiers.** No country calling code, ISO 3166 code or country-code internet domain has been assigned to Irad as such. Websites use workarounds instead: the zone *.ir.ad*, held under Andorra's domain, the territorial-era government zone *id.gov*, and generic domains (see [[Internet Domains of Irad]]).
- **Sport.** Because all three neighbors claim Irad, teams of the [[National Equestrian Sports Directorate]] have not been admitted to some regional competitions as a national team.
- **Water and power.** The Toktogul complex (1,200 MW) is split by the Qara-Chik line, with the dam in the ATR and the switchyard and export lines in the ISEZ. Neither side can release water or sell power without the other, and both treat the risk to the ceasefire as a reason to preserve the status quo. The Protocol's five-year release schedule falls due for renewal in 2027, and neighboring governments have asked who will sign for the ISEZ while Governor Toktarov remains out of public view (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[12]</sup>

## Proposed solutions
Commentators and the parties have discussed several outcomes:<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>
- **Unification and independence.** A referendum in both zones leading to a single sovereign Irad, which is the stated aim of the ISEZ and, on conditions, of the IAC. The parties disagree on representation, the franchise in the tribal regions and control of land, water and minerals.
- **A confederal settlement.** A joint legislature with guaranteed autonomy for the tribal regions and recognition of customary law, along the lines of the 2008 draft.
- **Formalizing the division.** Turning the Qara-Chik line into a permanent boundary. Neither administration publicly supports this.
- **Renewed association with the United States.** Discussed in some territorial-era political circles, but with little support since the 2025 remarks turned public opinion against Washington.
- **Integration with a neighboring state.** The outcome implied by the claims of Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan, and the one the 2007 Bill of Sale would have imposed. It has no significant support in either zone.

### 3.30. Territory of Irad (1992–2006)
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: History*

The **Territory of Irad** (Iradi: *Irad Territoriyası*) was an organized, unincorporated territory of the United States from 18 August 1992 to 3 December 2006. It was created under the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]], which the Provisional Government of the Republic of Irad signed and ratified in besieged [[Talas]] on 2 June 1992, in exchange for US military protection during the [[First Iradi Civil War]]. After the US intervention, [[Operation Starlight]], ended the war with the surrender of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]], the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]] gave the territory a civil government: an elected Governor, a bicameral [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] and the [[Supreme Court of Irad]], with a US High Commissioner representing the federal government in Talas. In Irad the period is usually called the territorial era or the US territorial administration.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

The fourteen territorial years transformed the cities. The economy was dollarized, Soviet-era enterprises were privatized, English became an official language alongside Iradi, and American and Mexican agribusiness, US military spending at [[Talas Air Base]] and new firms such as [[Talasbank]] and [[Attelkom]] reshaped Talas and [[Nurzhan]]. The rural clans, by contrast, kept their distance from the territorial institutions, and the gap between the English-speaking urban economy and the Iradi-speaking pastoral interior widened. The period ended when President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the treaty by executive action on 3 December 2006. The Supreme Court ruled that the withdrawal had triggered Irad's statutory independence clause, the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] was proclaimed on 9 December, and within months the country was at war again. The institutions of the territory still form the framework of government in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ).<sup>[3]</sup>

## Background
On 9 October 1991 the [[Communist Party of Irad]] under [[Vasily Chernov]] declared the Iradi SSR independent as the Socialist Republic of Irad (SRI), governed from Kobozev. Its emergency decrees and the economic collapse that followed the end of Soviet subsidies provoked the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], which drove SRI officials out of Talas. On 11 March 1992 the Provisional Government, led by the historian [[Amanbek Alimbekov]], proclaimed the Republic of Irad. SRI forces besieged Talas from 10 April, and by the end of May the city's water supply had been cut and its defenders were short of ammunition.<sup>[4]</sup>

Alimbekov's envoys approached the United States through its embassy in [[wp:Almaty]] and at meetings in [[wp:Geneva]]. Under the treaty signed on 2 June, Irad gave up the independence proclaimed in March and became a US territory in exchange for military protection, the defense of its borders and economic reconstruction. Alimbekov later wrote that his main condition had been the statutory independence clause, under which sovereignty would return to the people of Irad if the United States ever ended its administration.<sup>[5]</sup> US forces broke the siege by 18 June, and the last SRI forces surrendered on 18 August 1992. The war killed an estimated 14,000 people.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Establishment (1992–1994)

#### Organization of the territory
The Organic Act took effect on 18 August 1992. Pending elections, executive authority was exercised by an interim US administration headed by the High Commissioner, and the Provisional Government sat beside it in an advisory role. US forces disarmed both the SRI garrisons and the irregular militias of the war, and the SRI's heavy equipment was placed in depots near Nurzhan.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[6]</sup>

In September 1992 Alimbekov announced in a speech in Kobozev that the city would be renamed Nurzhan, a Kipchak name usually glossed as "light of life", calling it a "guiding light of industry" that would lead Irad to a new dawn after the ruin of the civil war.<sup>[5]</sup> In October an interim legislature, drawn largely from the Provisional Government's councils and from district delegates, first met in Legislative Hall in Talas. Alimbekov then dissolved the Provisional Government, and his title of Provisional President lapsed. The same month members of its reformist caucus formally constituted the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI). The Supreme Court was established in November 1992. Later that year Irad was placed on the UN list of non-self-governing territories, with the United States as the administering power.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[7]</sup>

The interim legislature passed the territory's founding statutes, among them the Banking and Commercial Currency Act and the Toponymic Heritage Statute, both of 1993. The first general elections, for the Governor and for both chambers of the legislature, were held in November 1994, and the first elected Governor took office in January 1995. Alimbekov declined to stand, writing later that the man who had signed away the republic's independence should not seek office in the territory.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Government and politics

#### Federal representation
The High Commissioner, a federal official appointed in Washington and reporting to the Department of the Interior, represented the federal government in Talas, oversaw federal funding and programs, and was the channel between the territorial government and the US military command at Talas Air Base. Territorial statutes were subject to review by [[wp:United States Congress|Congress]], a power that studies of the period describe as rarely used, and the judgments of the Supreme Court could be reviewed by the US federal courts.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Governor
The Elective Governor Acts of 1992 provided for a Governor and a Lieutenant Governor elected on a joint ticket for four-year terms in November, limited a Governor to two consecutive terms, and provided that the Lieutenant Governor would act for an absent or incapacitated Governor. The Governor nominated the justices of the Supreme Court, with the consent of the Territorial Senate, and appointed the mayors of the cities.<sup>[9]</sup> Both territorial governors belonged to the DPI. Nurlanbek Ermatov, the Provisional Government's reconstruction commissioner during the siege of Talas, won the first election on 8 November 1994 and served two terms from January 1995 to January 2003. Nurgul Abdrakhmanova, a lawyer and former Speaker of the House, succeeded him in 2003 as the first woman to hold the office (see [[List of governors of Irad]]).<sup>[10]</sup>

#### Territorial Legislature
The legislature followed the American territorial model. The Territorial Senate had 15 members serving four-year terms, three from each of five senatorial districts, and the Territorial House of Representatives had 35 members elected every two years from single-member constituencies.<sup>[2]</sup> [[Kanybek Zhumaliev]], a founding member of the DPI, was the first Speaker of the House (1994–1998). [[Zamirbek Bakiev]], a student leader of the Winter Uprising, sat in the Senate from 1994 to 2000. [[Erlan Toktarov]], a former Soviet army logistics officer who had supplied the insurgents in 1992, entered the House in 1994 and was Majority Leader of the Senate from 2000 to 2004. The legislature passed more than 800 statutes in fourteen years. They included:<sup>[2]</sup>
- the Banking and Commercial Currency Act (1993), which made the US dollar legal tender alongside the [[Iradi som]] and chartered Talasbank;
- the Toponymic Heritage Statute (1993), the main instrument of the [[De-Russification Movement in Irad|de-Russification]] of place names;
- the Commercial Property and Tenure Acts (1995), which protected household livestock and grazing rights from seizure for debt;
- the Hydrocarbon and Mining Code (1995), which regulated extraction concessions and set up royalty funds for road building;
- the [[1995 Latin Orthography Revision]] and the Language and Education Reform Act (1997);
- the [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]], drafted by the Senate's chief legal counsel, [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], which introduced jury trials and commercial arbitration modeled on US federal practice.

#### Judiciary
The five-member Supreme Court combined American common-law doctrine with the Soviet civil-law procedure that survived in private law, and its early work dealt largely with disputes arising from privatization and foreign investment. [[Dastan Isakov]], one of the first justices, became Chief Justice in 1999.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Parties
The DPI, whose program drew on American models of territorial association, market reform, dollarization and English-language education, won majorities in both chambers in 1994 and kept them for most of the period. Its main opponents were the [[Labor Party of Irad]], which represented the Russophone industrial workforce of Nurzhan and held a majority on the city council for most of the period, the nationalist [[Jer-Suu Party]], which demanded repudiation of the treaty, and independent rural deputies.<sup>[10]</sup> By the early 2000s critics in the press and the opposition were drawing attention to the commercial concessions held by party figures, especially in fuel distribution and in warehousing around Talas Air Base.<sup>[10]</sup> Alimbekov, who never joined a party, remained influential as senior adviser to the territorial government and chancellor of [[Talas State University]], and until his death in 2004 resisted nationalist calls to repudiate the treaty.<sup>[5]</sup>

## Legal and international status
As an unincorporated territory, Irad was subject to the authority of Congress under the Territory Clause of the [[wp:Constitution of the United States|US Constitution]], which applied there only in part, and it was not on a path to statehood. The United States transmitted information on the territory to the United Nations as its administering power, and the territory was reviewed each year by the [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]] in [[wp:New York City|New York]].<sup>[7]</sup>

[[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] had objected to the treaty in 1992 as a violation of post-Soviet border inviolability, and none recognized the transfer of sovereignty, but the territorial government dealt with them on technical matters. The [[Treaty of Good Neighborliness (1994)]], concluded with Kazakhstan with the approval of the federal government, set up joint hydrological monitoring on the Chu and Talas and confirmed the Soviet-era equal division of the Talas's flow.<sup>[12]</sup> Electricity exports from the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] began, as did friction with Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan over its summer storage of water for winter generation. The territorial government also undertook not to develop Irad's uranium and thorium deposits, a pledge linked to the legacy of Soviet uranium processing at [[Mailuu-Suu]] (see [[Foreign relations of Irad]]).<sup>[13]</sup>

## Security and the US military presence
Under the treaty, defense was the responsibility of the United States. Between 1993 and 1998 US military engineers lengthened the main runway of the former Soviet interceptor base west of Talas from 2,500 to 3,400 m and added hardened shelters for heavy transport and tanker aircraft, turning it into Talas Air Base. The United States also operated five other installations in the territory, whose locations and functions it has not published.<sup>[14]</sup>

Locally, the Republican Gendarmerie was set up in 1993 to replace the Soviet internal troops, and the Territorial Legislature created the Irad National Guard in 1994 under the Governor's command, with army and air components trained to US standards. By 2005 the Guard numbered about 6,500, most of them part-time.<sup>[6]</sup> In 1995 the territorial administration began building a hardened continuity-of-government site 40 km east of Nurzhan, now the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound.<sup>[15]</sup>

After the September 2001 attacks on the United States, the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]], negotiated in Talas in October and November 2001, expanded US rights at the base for logistics, aerial refueling and medical transit. Talas became one of the main staging areas for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]] in [[wp:Afghanistan]], and the agreement generated service contracts worth hundreds of millions of dollars for local firms.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Economy

#### Dollarization and finance
The Banking and Commercial Currency Act of 1993 created a dual-currency system: the US dollar was used for contracts, savings and foreign trade, and the Iradi som for retail payments and public-sector wages. Talasbank, chartered under the act, became the leading commercial bank. The [[Irad Foreign Trade Escrow Authority]], created in 1994, made sure royalties were collected on exports by the newly privatized oil and mining companies and gave foreign buyers and lenders a supervised payment channel.<sup>[16]</sup>

#### Privatization and industry
The Soviet oil enterprise of the Chu basin was privatized in 1993 as [[NurGaz]], under [[Kamran Bekzatov]], and the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] in 1994. Oil output, which had peaked at about 140,000 barrels per day in the early 1980s, fell to a low of 48,000 bpd in 1996 before recovering with investment, including [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron's]] modernization of the fields. Crude went by rail to [[wp:Shymkent]] until 2000, and from then on through the 85-km [[Northern Export Feeder]] into the [[wp:KazTransOil]] network near Shymkent.<sup>[17]</sup> In his memoir of 1998 Alimbekov warned that concentrating the country's industry in a few hands would cost the new institutions public trust.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Agribusiness and services
Irad's goods had customs access to the US market from 1992, and after the [[wp:North American Free Trade Agreement]] took effect in 1994 Mexican as well as American companies took an interest in the territory, although Irad was not itself a party to the agreement. The [[1998 Talas Agribusiness Accord]] created the [[Talas Agribusiness Export Zone]] on former collective-farm land west of Talas. The Mexican company [[wp:Sukarne]] opened the [[Sukarne Irad Complex]] in 1999, and [[wp:Conagra Brands|Conagra]] built packing and cold-chain facilities nearby. Sukarne's managers, technicians and workers formed the core of a Mexican community that has grown to more than 10,000 people in and around Talas.<sup>[18]</sup> The [[Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium]] was also established in 1998.

Military contracts produced other firms. Attelkom grew out of a 1994 [[wp:United States Department of Defense|Defense Department]] contract to link Talas Air Base with regional command centers. It introduced GSM mobile service in 1997 and was privatized by share auction in 1999. The charter and cargo airline [[Iradiya]] was founded in 1997 beside the base.<sup>[17]</sup>

## Society and culture

#### Language and education
Iradi and English became the official languages in 1992, and Russian lost the official status it had held under the SRI, although it remained the everyday language of the cities. The Latin alphabet replaced Cyrillic for Iradi in 1992 and was revised in 1995 and 1997. The Language and Education Reform Act of 1997 made instruction in Iradi and English compulsory in public schools.<sup>[19]</sup> Schools were short of qualified English teachers, and the formal, rigid English of the generation educated in these years became a marker of the [[Urban Iradi Millennials]].

#### Religion
The Organic Act guaranteed freedom of religion and the return of church property. The [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] recovered churches closed in the Soviet period, and the [[Cathedral Seminary of Iyık Janyl]] reopened in 1994. In 2004, on a US nomination, the [[Church of St. Janyl]] and the [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]] were inscribed together on the [[wp:World Heritage Site|UNESCO World Heritage List]] as a single property.<sup>[20]</sup>

#### Demographic change
Many ethnic Russians emigrated after 1992; those who stayed, concentrated in Nurzhan, formed the core of the Russophone *Russos* (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]). Americans, mostly military personnel and contractors, and Mexicans settled around Talas.<sup>[18]</sup>

#### Culture and memory
In the United States the new territory was briefly fashionable. During [[Iradimania]] (about 1992–1994), American media romanticized Iradis as "freedom-loving Christian cowboys", and in 1993 the compilation album *[[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|Swift Hooves]]* raised money for Christian charities in the territory; [[Iradi Country-Folk]] grew out of these songs and the smuggled cassettes of the 1980s.<sup>[21]</sup> At home, [[IradOne]] was created under the Public Broadcasting Act of 1993, a 1996 act banned commercial horse slaughter (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]), and in 1999 the Nurzhan band [[tüntolk]] launched [[Mor-Wave]] with *[[Jolchu (Album)|Jolchu]]*.

The territory also confronted the Soviet past. Memorials to the [[1956 Talas Riots]] and to the victims of the 3 February 1992 shootings were unveiled in Talas in 1996 and 1997. A criminal case opened against Chernov in 1995 never came to trial, because Russia declined to extradite him, and in 2002 a commission of the legislature documented 27 deaths in the 3 February shootings.<sup>[22]</sup>

## The countryside and the clans
The territorial institutions never took firm root in the mountain and steppe districts. After 1992 the clans resumed the customary self-government that the SRI had tried to abolish, and their councils of elders continued to settle disputes under customary law (*adat*). The Commercial Property and Tenure Acts of 1995 protected household herds but left communal clan pastures without clear legal protection, a gap that became central in 2007.<sup>[23]</sup> Revenue from oil, antimony and the air base, and agricultural investment, went mainly to the cities and irrigated valleys, while the pastoral interior remained poor and Iradi-speaking. Tribal-aligned Iradis largely rejected both Russian and English, which the urban middle class used daily. Historians describe this divide as the main fault line that reopened when federal administration ended.<sup>[3]</sup>

## End of the territorial period
Debate over the future of the treaty grew in the mid-2000s, as did rumors, later known as the [[Bush Family Palatial Complex Conspiracy Theory]], that the hardened site east of Nurzhan was being built as a private retreat for the Bush family; construction had in fact begun in 1995. On 3 December 2006 [[wp:George W. Bush|President Bush]] withdrew the United States from the treaty by executive action (see [[United States Withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty (2006)]]). The withdrawal ended federal civilian administration, including the office of the High Commissioner and federal transfers to the territorial budget, but not the US military presence at Talas Air Base, which continued under the 2001 agreement.<sup>[3]</sup>

On 7 December, on a petition by Orozbekov, the Supreme Court under Chief Justice Isakov held that the withdrawal had triggered the statutory independence clause (see [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]]). On 9 December the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad, with Orozbekov as Prime Minister, and the office of territorial Governor lapsed. The United States did not notify the United Nations that its administration had ended, so Irad remained on the list of non-self-governing territories, with the United States still nominally recorded as the administering power.<sup>[7]</sup> The DRI's sale of state assets, its secret partition drafts with the three neighbors (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]), Toktarov's proclamation of the ISEZ on 25 February 2007 and the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] followed.

## Legacy and historiography
The territory's institutions outlived it. The governorship, the Territorial Legislature, now a single chamber of 60 seats, and the Supreme Court form the government of the ISEZ, which treats the Organic Act and the territorial statutes as its basic law while Irad's sovereignty is "suspended". The dollar, the official status of English, the Latin alphabet and the US presence at Talas Air Base all date from the period.<sup>[8]</sup>

The period is remembered differently in different parts of the country. ISEZ textbooks present the territorial era as the foundation of modern Irad and emphasize the 1992 treaty, while oral histories in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] give more weight to the 1916 revolt, the clans and the [[Popular Front of Irad]], and treat the treaty as a surrender of the sovereignty proclaimed in March 1992.<sup>[24]</sup> Historians at Talas State University continue to debate whether the treaty was the only means of saving Talas, and whether its withdrawal clause left the territory exposed when Washington used it in 2006. [[wp:Donald Trump|Donald Trump's]] remarks of 2025 about selling Irad to Russia, which took no account of the end of US administration in 2006, provoked protests in both zones, including a march past the former residence of the High Commissioner in Talas, and renewed debate about the legacy of the territorial years (see [[Irad–United States relations]]).<sup>[25]</sup>

### 3.31. Administrative divisions of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

The **administrative divisions of Irad** reflect the division of the territory since 2007 between two administrations separated by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), about one-third of Irad's 196,754 km², is divided into three cities of governorate significance, [[Talas]], [[Nurzhan]] and [[Iyık Janyl]], and five districts (Iradi: *aimak*): Talas, Kara-Buura, Chu, [[Sary-Bulak]] and Lower Naryn.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), the remaining two-thirds, have no formal administrative divisions. Their land is held by 24 clans (*uruk*) under councils of elders and customary law, and the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) and aid agencies divide it into six planning zones for their own operations: Northern Steppe, [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]], [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal Highlands, Toktogul–Naryn and Fergana Rim.<sup>[2]</sup>

The ISEZ claims authority over the whole of Irad but does not extend its district system beyond the line. The ATR's clan authorities, coordinated by the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC), reject ISEZ authority, and the planning zones have no legal or political standing.

## History

#### Before 1925
Under the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] the Iradi valleys were governed by the khan's appointed governors (*beks*) from fortified towns, while the pastoral clans kept their own leaders. After [[wp:Mikhail Skobelev|General Mikhail Skobelev]] conquered the khanate in 1876, the Iradi lands were divided among the Syr-Darya, [[wp:Semirechye]] and Fergana oblasts of [[wp:Russian Turkestan]], so that the Talas and lower Chu valleys and the Fergana foothills fell under different provincial administrations.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Soviet period (1925–1991)
The Soviet national delimitation created the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] in 1925. From 1927 its capital was Kobozev (renamed Nurzhan in 1992). The republic was divided into raions (districts) and at times into oblasts, whose boundaries were redrawn several times. [[wp:Collectivization in the Soviet Union|Collectivization]] in the 1930s reorganized rural administration around collective and state farms, which pushed many pastoral clans from the lowland plains into the mountains.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
The US territorial administration kept the Soviet-era raions as units of local administration and grouped them into five senatorial districts for elections to the Territorial Senate. Talas, Nurzhan and Iyık Janyl were given elected city councils from 1993. The Toponymic Heritage Statute of 1993 confirmed the renaming of Kobozev as Nurzhan and restored Turkic and Sogdian names to streets, districts and reservoirs across the territory (see [[De-Russification Movement in Irad]]).<sup>[5]</sup> In law the whole territory was governed from Talas, but clan leaders in the mountains and steppe kept their distance from the territorial authorities.

#### Since 2007
The [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] of September 2007 fixed the Qara-Chik line between the zones held by the two sides. Many of the old raions were cut by the line. After the line had been surveyed, an executive decree of 2008 consolidated the raions left wholly or partly on the ISEZ side into the present five districts and confirmed the special status of the three cities. A district, Lower Naryn, was created for the ISEZ salient around [[Tash-Kömür]], which is linked to the rest of the zone only by the [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]].<sup>[1]</sup> Since 2011 the three cities and five districts have also formed the eight multi-member constituencies of the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] (see [[Elections in Irad]]).

On the tribal side, the Popular Front's political council became the IAC at a kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]] in late 2007. The clans did not adopt any territorial subdivision of their own, and the six planning zones were introduced by UNMI and humanitarian agencies from 2008 to coordinate aid deliveries, vaccination campaigns and mine clearance.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Irad Special Economic Zone
The ISEZ covers about 64,000 km² and, according to the 2018 census, about 2.4 million people, 52 percent of the official national total of 4,626,000.<sup>[6]</sup>

Each city of governorate significance is administered separately from the surrounding district. It has a mayor appointed by the Governor and an elected city council. Each district is headed by a district head (*aimak bashchysy*) appointed by the Governor, and its villages and small towns elect local councils. The district administrations are responsible for local roads, schools, agricultural services and civil registration; police, courts and the main utilities are run by ISEZ-wide ministries and agencies.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[Democratic Party of Irad]] has a regional committee in each city and district, which analysts describe as the main channel through which procurement, utility concessions and agricultural quotas are allocated.

#### Cities of governorate significance
Populations are 2018 census figures; metropolitan and agglomeration figures are unofficial estimates.<sup>[6]</sup>

| City | Population (2018) | Elevation | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| [[Talas]] | 1,120,000 (1.4 million metropolitan area, estimate) | c. 1,250 m | *De jure* capital and largest city; seat of the legislature, the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] and Government House; surrounded by Talas District |
| [[Nurzhan]] | 850,000 (over 1 million in the agglomeration, estimate) | 740 m | Industrial center and headquarters of [[NurGaz]]; known as Kobozev from 1927 to 1992; surrounded by Chu District. Urban districts include [[Dastgoh District|Dastgoh]], the old quarter, and [[Navbahor District|Navbahor]] |
| [[Iyık Janyl]] | 310,000 | 1,280 m | Religious center of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], in the foothills of the Fergana Range above the Naryn, about 20 km north-east of Tash-Kömür |

Together the three cities hold slightly less than half of Irad's official population. Talas's neighborhoods include the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] and [[Semiz]], home to much of the city's Mexican community. Semiz grew from *Otkormochny*, the Soviet workers' settlement of a state cattle-fattening complex, renamed with the Iradi calque *Semiz* ("fattened") in the 1990s; Russian speakers still call it Otkormochny, its Mexican residents call it *la Colonia*, and Iradis commonly call it *Meksikanka*.

#### Districts
Areas are approximate figures published by the ISEZ statistical office.<sup>[7]</sup>

| District | [[Iradi name]] | Area (approx.) | Administrative center | Description |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Talas District | *Talas aimagı* | 9,800 km² | Talas | The middle Talas valley around the capital, including [[Talas Air Base]], the market town of [[Kara-Bulak]] and the UNMI headquarters at Kara-Oi |
| Kara-Buura District | *Kara-Buura aimagı* | 11,400 km² | [[Kyzyl-Adyr]] | The lower and western Talas valley as far as the Kazakh border, crossed by the [[Trans-Talas Highway]] (see [[Kara-Buura]]) |
| Chu District | *Chüy aimagı* | 24,600 km² | Nurzhan | The Chu lowlands around Nurzhan, including the oil fields, the [[Chu Irrigation Canal System]] and the [[Boz-Korgon]] area |
| Sary-Bulak District | *Sary-Bulak aimagı* | 10,900 km² | Sary-Bulak | The southern industrial corridor and foothills south of Talas, including the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]], the [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]] and the lower [[Kara-Suu Canyon]] |
| Lower Naryn District | *Tömönkü Naryn aimagı* | 6,300 km² | Tash-Kömür | A salient around Tash-Kömür and the city of [[Iyık Janyl]] and the downstream stations of the Naryn cascade, linked to Talas by the Fergana Mountain Transit Highway over the [[Fergana Gateway]] |

The three cities account for the remaining area of about 1,000 km².

- **Talas District** is the most densely settled district, with irrigated farmland, the beef plants of the Talas agribusiness zone on the edge of the capital, and the lower reaches of several Talas Alatau valleys whose upper parts lie on the tribal side of the line.
- **Kara-Buura District** is mainly agricultural, growing wheat, barley, beans, potatoes and fodder, and contains the main road crossing into [[wp:Kazakhstan]] from the Talas valley and the Ak-Tash Training Center of the State Defense Corps. Its western edge follows the western sector of the Qara-Chik line.
- **Chu District**, the largest by area, combines the irrigated lowlands around Nurzhan with semi-desert grassland toward the Moiynkum sands. It contains the Chu basin oil fields and most of the farming villages of the [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] outside Nurzhan, and borders Kazakhstan east of the city.
- **Sary-Bulak District** is Irad's mining district. The line bulges south around it to take in the antimony mine and smelter, which are reached from Talas by the Sary-Bulak road through the lower Kara-Suu Canyon.
- **Lower Naryn District** contains the switchyard of the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]], the national dispatch center and the downstream power stations, as well as cotton farms and coal mines. It surrounds the city of Iyık Janyl, which is administered separately. The highway that links it to the rest of the ISEZ crosses tribal land as a guarded corridor, and UNMI's sector headquarters is at Tash-Kömür.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Autonomous Tribal Regions
The ATR cover about 132,000 km². The official 2018 census estimated their population at about 2.2 million; independent estimates, based on satellite settlement mapping and aid-agency registries, put it at 3.5 to 4 million (see [[2018 Irad census]]).<sup>[9]</sup>

#### Clan territories
Land in the ATR is held by 24 clans, each governed by a council of elders (*aksakals*) and a *biy* under customary law ([[Adat Law in the ATR|adat]]). Village councils (*ayyl kengeshi*) allocate pasture, organize hay cutting and maintain irrigation channels, and pasture boundaries are marked by boundary stones (*bel-tash*). Clan territories are not contiguous blocks: a clan typically holds winter villages in sheltered valleys or on the desert margin and summer pastures (*zhayloo*) in the mountains, linked by migration corridors fixed by clan pasture committees. Matters affecting all clans are decided at the summer *kurultai* on the Aksay Plateau, and inter-clan grazing disputes go to the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]].<sup>[10]</sup> The most politically prominent clan is the Tanıp, whose chief, [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], is the IAC's principal spokesman.

#### UN planning zones
The six planning zones were drawn by UNMI and aid agencies along physical and logistical lines, and clan territories often cross their boundaries. Areas are approximate figures used in UNMI planning documents.<sup>[2]</sup>

| Planning zone | Area (approx.) | Extent | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Northern Steppe | 52,000 km² | The southern [[Betpak-Dala]] steppe north of the Chu and the Moiynkum (Muyunkum) sands between the Chu and the Talas | Largest and most thinly populated zone; winter grazing; the lower Chu ends near the [[Akzhaykyn Depression|Akzhaykyn depression]]; the undeveloped [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]] |
| Karatau | 21,000 km² | The Karatau range and its foothills, running northwest toward the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]] | Winter pastures on the desert margin, including the [[Kulan-Talaa]] plain; Kara-Khanid caravanserais such as [[Rabat-i Kamal]] |
| Talas Alatau | 17,500 km² | The high range south of the Talas valley | Summer pastures above 2,500 m, including the Aksay Plateau; the [[Kara-Koyun Valley]] of the Karakoyun clan; upper [[Besh-Tash National Park|Besh-Tash]] and upper Kara-Suu; the Aksu-Zhabagly reserve in the west; [[Manas Peak (Irad)|Manas Peak]] |
| Chatkal Highlands | 16,000 km² | The Chatkal valley and range | [[Sary-Chelek Biosphere Reserve|Sary-Chelek]] reserve; walnut and wild-fruit forests; [[Kanysh-Kiya]] and [[Terek-Sai]]; pastures shared by the Sarybagysh and Naiman clans |
| Toktogul–Naryn | 12,500 km² | The Toktogul reservoir basin and the Naryn gorge down to the dam | The Toktogul dam, reservoir and powerhouse, on the tribal side of the line; the market town of Toktogul |
| Fergana Rim | 13,000 km² | The rural foothills along the northern and eastern edge of the Fergana Valley | Most densely settled zone; irrigated cotton, orchards and walnut groves; [[Arslanbob]], [[Bazar-Korgon]] and the Soviet uranium tailings at [[Mailuu-Suu]]; the valley's large cities lie outside Irad |

The zones are used for aid planning, the scheduling of vaccination and fodder deliveries, mine action and humanitarian reporting. They also appear in UNMI's patrol plans for the tribal side of the line, where five of the mission's eleven team sites are located.<sup>[2]</sup> The IAC does not use them in its own governance, although its Chancellery corresponds with UNMI and aid agencies in their terms.

## The Qara-Chik Demarcation Line
The 420-km Qara-Chik line, fixed by the 2007 ceasefire, is the boundary between the two systems. It encloses the irrigated valleys of the Talas and lower Chu and, beyond the Talas Alatau, a salient on the lower Naryn, where it divides the Toktogul complex between the two sides. UNMI divides the line into western, central, southern (Naryn) and northern sectors. The Gendarmerie mans twelve checkpoints on the ISEZ side, and the [[El-Choro Militias]] keep picket stations on the tribal side. A restricted-weapons zone extends 12 km on either side.<sup>[8]</sup> Neither administration treats the line as an international boundary: the ISEZ describes it as an administrative line within Irad, and the IAC as the limit of the land held by the clans.

Irad's external frontiers with Kazakhstan, [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] are demarcated only on the sections held by the ISEZ along the Kazakh border, under the 2008 [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]]. The ATR-held sections follow Soviet-era administrative boundaries that have never been marked on the ground.
