### 3.32. Elections in Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

**Elections in Irad** are held only in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), the third of the country governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI). Every four years, on the first Tuesday after the first Monday in November, voters in the ISEZ elect a Governor and Lieutenant Governor on a joint ticket, the 60 members of the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] (*Mejlis*) and the councils of the three cities of governorate significance. The [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), which cover the other two-thirds of the territory, take no part; their clans govern themselves through councils of elders and decide common matters by consensus at a *kurultai*.<sup>[1]</sup>

The electoral system descends from the US territorial period of 1992–2006, when Irad held competitive, if DPI-dominated, elections under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]] and the 1992 Elective Governor Acts. Since 2007 the DPI has won every gubernatorial election, all of them by [[Erlan Toktarov]], and has held a large majority in the legislature. Observers from the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe]] (OSCE) have said that the 2013 electoral reform and the elections that followed lacked "any meaningful transparency or choice for the Iradi people".<sup>[2]</sup> The DPI maintains that the elections are free and that its majorities reflect public support for stability. The next general election is due on 2 November 2027 (see [[2027 Iradi gubernatorial election]]).

## History

#### Soviet period
In the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] (1925–1991), elections to the republic's Supreme Soviet and to local soviets followed the Soviet single-candidate pattern, with the [[Communist Party of Irad]] approving all nominations. In 1990 the republican Supreme Soviet created the post of President and elected the party's First Secretary, [[Vasily Chernov]], to it.<sup>[3]</sup> The [[Declaration of State Sovereignty (1991)]], which created the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] in October 1991, was not followed by free elections or the legalization of opposition parties, a point on which the national movement criticized it.

#### Provisional Government (1992)
On 11 March 1992 the [[Talas Town Hall Meeting (1992)|Talas Town Hall Meeting]], an assembly of reformers, delegates of the strike and student committees and clan elders convened by the Talas city council, proclaimed the Republic of Irad and elected [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] Provisional President. No popular vote was held during the [[First Iradi Civil War]].<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
The Organic Act, which took effect on 18 August 1992, provided for an elected Governor and an elected bicameral legislature. Pending elections, an interim US administration headed by the High Commissioner exercised executive authority, and an interim legislature drawn from the Provisional Government's councils and from district delegates met in Talas from October 1992.<sup>[5]</sup> Municipal councils were first elected in November 1993.

The first general elections were held on 8 November 1994, on the same day as US federal elections, a convention the territory kept throughout. The system followed the American territorial model:<sup>[5]</sup>
- The **Governor and Lieutenant Governor** were elected on a joint ticket for four-year terms, beginning on the first Monday of January, with a limit of two consecutive terms.
- The **Territorial Senate** had 15 members, three from each of five senatorial districts, elected for four years.
- The **Territorial House of Representatives** had 35 members, elected every two years from single-member constituencies apportioned by population.

The DPI, formally constituted in October 1992 from the Provisional Government's reformist caucus, won the governorship and majorities in both chambers in 1994 and kept them for most of the period. Its main opponents were the [[Labor Party of Irad]], which represented the Russophone industrial workforce of [[Nurzhan]], the nationalist [[Jer-Suu Party]], and independent rural deputies.<sup>[6]</sup> Alimbekov declined to stand, writing later that the man who had signed away the republic's independence should not seek office in the territory. Toktarov entered the House of Representatives in 1994 and moved to the Senate, where he was Majority Leader from 2000 to 2004. Studies of the period describe the elections as genuinely contested: governors were elected with between 52 and 62 percent of the vote, several DPI incumbents lost House seats, and Russophone parties controlled the Nurzhan city council for most of the period (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

Participation in the rural interior was low throughout. Many clan communities kept their distance from the territorial authorities, and turnout in the mountain constituencies rarely exceeded 35 percent, against 60 to 70 percent in the cities.<sup>[6]</sup>

The last territorial general election was held on 7 November 2006, four weeks before the United States withdrew from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]]. Governor Nurgul Abdrakhmanova was re-elected, but her second term, due to begin in January 2007, never started (see [[List of governors of Irad]]).

#### Democratic Republic (2006–2007)
On 9 December 2006, acting on the Supreme Court's [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], the Territorial Legislature reconstituted itself as a national assembly, proclaimed the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] and elected [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] Prime Minister. The office of territorial Governor lapsed. No elections were held under the DRI, which was dissolved after the [[Popular Front of Irad]] stormed Talas on 4 June 2007.<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Irad Special Economic Zone (2007–present)
Toktarov proclaimed the ISEZ on 25 February 2007 and took office as Governor after the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)|ceasefire]] of September 2007. Members of the last territorial legislature who accepted the emergency administration sat as a provisional single chamber until November 2011.<sup>[8]</sup> The Jer-Suu Party was banned in 2008 as an extremist organization, and the Political Parties Act of 2010, which requires 10,000 members spread across all eight constituencies, led to the dissolution of the Labor Party in 2011; no party other than the DPI has met its thresholds since (see [[Jer-Suu Party]], [[Labor Party of Irad]]). On 8 November 2011 the ISEZ held its first regular elections, and Toktarov was re-elected with an official 88.74 percent. Under the prevailing reading of the Elective Governor Acts, which counted his 2007–2011 tenure as a first term, he would have been barred from standing again in 2015.<sup>[9]</sup>

In June 2013 the legislature passed an amendment to the Acts removing the two-term limit, by 53 votes to 4 with three abstentions; the Russo-aligned deputies from Nurzhan voted against (see [[2013 Constitutional Amendment]]). In 2014 the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] declined to hear a challenge, holding that the question belonged to the legislature.<sup>[9]</sup>

Before the 2015 election the DPI majority adopted new rules on the verification of nomination signatures and on candidates' residence. In early October 2015 the Territorial Election Commission struck the rival nominees from the ballot, among them an independent backed by trade unions in Nurzhan and a Russo-aligned businessman (see [[2015 Electoral Reform Controversy]]). Toktarov won with 78.25 percent, his lowest share, and opposition groups protested in Talas and Nurzhan.<sup>[10]</sup> In 2019 he won 90.31 percent, with his son-in-law [[Aidar Maksat]] as his running mate. The 2023 election, held a month after the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], gave him 92.75 percent (see [[2023 Iradi gubernatorial election]]).

Toktarov has not appeared in public since July 2024. The DPI attributes his absence to an extended health retreat. No formal finding of inability has been made under the Elective Governor Acts, and the DPI has named no successor. Analysts note that the only formal routes to a transfer of power are the Acts' succession provisions and the 2027 election (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[11]</sup>

## Electoral system

#### Governor and Lieutenant Governor
The Governor and Lieutenant Governor are elected together on a joint ticket for a four-year term, beginning on the fourth Monday of November after the election. A ticket needs more than half of the valid votes; otherwise a runoff between the two leading tickets is held three weeks later. No runoff has been needed since 2007. Since the 2013 amendment there is no limit on the number of terms.<sup>[9]</sup>

Since 2015 a gubernatorial candidate must collect 10,000 verified signatures of registered voters and must have lived in the ISEZ continuously for ten years before nomination. Candidates are nominated by parties or as independents. The DPI's party congress nominates its ticket.<sup>[10]</sup> Under the Acts the Lieutenant Governor acts for an absent or incapacitated Governor and succeeds if the office falls vacant. Legal scholars differ on whether the Lieutenant Governor may exercise the Governor's powers in his own right before a formal declaration of incapacity, and on who would be competent to make one.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Territorial Legislature
Since 2011 the legislature has been a single chamber of 60 members elected for four years on the same day as the Governor. Its eight multi-member constituencies correspond to the three cities of governorate significance ([[Talas]], Nurzhan and [[Iyık Janyl]]) and the five districts (Talas, [[Kara-Buura]], Chu, [[Sary-Bulak]] and Lower Naryn) (see [[Administrative divisions of Irad]]). Seats are apportioned according to official population figures. Within each constituency voters choose among party lists and independent candidates, and seats are allocated by the largest-remainder method.<sup>[12]</sup> A Speaker, elected from the majority, presides.

#### Municipal elections
Each city has an elected council and a mayor appointed by the Governor, who also appoints the district heads. During the territorial period councils were elected every four years from 1993. The councils elected in 2005 continued in office under the emergency administration, and since 2011 municipal elections have been held on the same day as the general election.<sup>[12]</sup> Russo politicians have held a majority on the Nurzhan city council for most of the period since 1992, which makes it the most significant elected body not controlled by the DPI. Its members have included the trade unionist [[Elena Petrova]], first elected in 1997.

#### Administration and franchise
Elections are run by the Territorial Election Commission, a seven-member body appointed by the Governor with the consent of the legislature, which keeps the voter registers, registers candidates and certifies results. Citizens resident in the ISEZ may vote from the age of 18. The registers are compiled from residence records held by the municipal authorities and the Ministry of Internal Security, which OSCE observers have cited as a weakness.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### The tribal regions
Residents of the ATR are not on the ISEZ voter registers, and no polling stations are opened on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) does not recognize ISEZ institutions and has never called on tribal residents to take part. Its own bodies are not elected. Each of the 24 clans is led by a council of elders and a hereditary or acclaimed *biy*, and matters affecting all clans are decided by consensus at the summer *kurultai* on the [[Aksay Plateau]]. Human rights organizations have criticized the absence of elections to IAC bodies, while the IAC describes consensus among the lineages as the tribal form of representation.<sup>[13]</sup>

Representation of the tribal regions in a unified legislature has been the central question of every unification negotiation. At the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]] the DPI offered the ATR one-third of the seats, and the IAC delegation walked out. The size of the tribal population, which the DPI's [[2018 Irad census]] estimated at about 2.2 million against independent estimates of 3.5 to 4 million, would determine its weight in any apportionment.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Results since 2011

#### Gubernatorial elections
Official results as certified by the Territorial Election Commission:<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[15]</sup>

| Election | DPI ticket | Official share | Official turnout | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 November 2011 | Erlan Toktarov, Daniyar Tologonov | 88.74% | 67.9% | First regular ISEZ election; held months after the collapse of the unification talks |
| 3 November 2015 | Erlan Toktarov, Daniyar Tologonov | 78.25% | 58.4% | First election after the removal of term limits; rival nominees disqualified |
| 5 November 2019 | Erlan Toktarov, Aidar Maksat | 90.31% | 73.2% | Maksat took office as Lieutenant Governor on 25 November 2019 |
| 7 November 2023 | Erlan Toktarov, Aidar Maksat | 92.75% | 71.6% | Held a month after the Talas student protests |

#### Legislative elections
Seats won in the 60-member Territorial Legislature:<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[15]</sup>

| Election | DPI | Independents | Russo-aligned | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2011 | 49 | 7 | 4 | Replaced the provisional chamber of 2007–2011 |
| 2015 | 52 | 5 | 3 | |
| 2019 | 54 | 4 | 2 | |
| 2023 | 51 | 6 | 3 | Most independents vote with the DPI |

The Russo-aligned deputies, all elected from Nurzhan, form the only consistent opposition in the chamber.

## Assessments
The [[wp:Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights|OSCE's Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights]], which has sent limited assessment missions to ISEZ elections since 2011, has reported:<sup>[2]</sup>
- the disqualification of opposition candidates on technical grounds, especially in 2015;
- state control of the voter registers;
- pressure on employees of state-owned and state-licensed enterprises to vote;
- the use of state resources in DPI campaigns;
- the dominance of the DPI on [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster.

Human rights organizations have added that independent opposition activity is suppressed between elections and that DPI membership is often a practical condition of promotion in the civil service (see [[Human rights in Irad]]).<sup>[13]</sup> Business groups have generally taken a more favorable view of the elections, pointing to their regularity and to the absence of violence on polling days.<sup>[16]</sup>

The DPI says the elections are free, that the rules apply equally to all candidates, and that its majorities reflect public support for stability while the unification question and the neighbors' territorial claims remain unresolved. It describes OSCE criticism as interference in Irad's internal affairs. Analysts note that the low official share in 2015 and the protests of 2013, 2015 and 2023 show that dissatisfaction exists even within the system, but that no opposition force has been able to contest the governorship since 2011.<sup>[11]</sup>

### 3.33. Human rights in Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Politics & Government*

**Human rights in Irad** are shaped by the territory's division since 2007 between two administrations with different legal systems. In the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI), laws inherited from the US territorial period guarantee civil liberties, but the emergency basis on which [[Erlan Toktarov]] proclaimed the zone in 2007 has never been formally ended. International monitors, including [[wp:Human Rights Watch]], [[wp:Amnesty International]], the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]] and the limited human rights unit of the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI), report restrictions on the media and on assembly, non-competitive elections, executive pressure on the courts, and allegations of torture and arbitrary detention by the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> In the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), justice is administered by clan elders under unwritten customary law (*adat*), which monitors criticize for its lack of appeal and its treatment of women.

The ISEZ government says that it upholds the rights guaranteed by the territorial statutes and describes much of the criticism as interference in Irad's internal affairs. Because Irad is not a sovereign state, neither administration can become a party to the international human rights treaties, and no UN treaty body reviews the territory.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Legal framework

#### Irad Special Economic Zone
The [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]] of 1992 guaranteed civil liberties, a secular state and freedom of religion, and the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] built on it during the territorial period. The [[2003 Judicial Reforms Act]] introduced jury trials in serious criminal cases. Since 2007 the ISEZ has treated the Organic Act and the territorial statutes as its basic law, with the provisions on federal oversight, including review by the US federal courts, inoperative.<sup>[4]</sup>

The emergency administration Toktarov proclaimed on 25 February 2007 has never been formally ended, and much policy is made by executive decree published in the bulletin of the Department of Public Information. Human Rights Watch argues that this arrangement leaves the executive largely unchecked, since decrees are rarely challenged in the courts and the legislature, in which the DPI holds 51 of 60 seats, seldom debates them.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Autonomous Tribal Regions
The authorities of the ATR do not accept that the Organic Act applies to their territory. The [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] acknowledged customary jurisdiction on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and civil, family, grazing and criminal matters there are settled by councils of elders and recognized judges (*biys*) under [[Adat Law in the ATR|adat]]. The [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) has no written constitution, and the only compilation of customary rules is a compendium of decisions kept by the [[Iradi Freedom Society Chancellery]].<sup>[5]</sup>

## Monitoring
UNMI was established in 2007 to monitor the ceasefire. In 2012 the [[wp:United Nations Security Council|Security Council]] added a limited human rights reporting function covering matters connected with the ceasefire, freedom of movement and humanitarian access. The mission's reports have criticized Gendarmerie conduct at the Qara-Chik checkpoints, and in 2024 it published a report on the Talas protests of October and November 2023.<sup>[6]</sup> Human Rights Watch and other organizations argue that this mandate is too narrow and have called for a full human rights monitoring component covering both administrations. The ISEZ government opposes the proposal, and the Security Council has not adopted it. Since 2023 the ISEZ has also barred UNMI patrols from a security zone around the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound.<sup>[7]</sup>

Other sources of reporting include the [[wp:Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights|OSCE's Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights]], which has assessed ISEZ elections since 2011; Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International; press-freedom groups; and the [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]], whose annual working paper on Irad summarizes human rights developments. Inside the ISEZ, the Legal Defense Guild, founded in 2009 by the lawyer [[Gulnara Sadykova]], gives free legal representation to detained journalists and students, and its reports on pre-trial detention are widely cited by international monitors.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Irad Special Economic Zone

#### Elections and political rights
Toktarov has won every gubernatorial election since 2007, most recently in 2023 with an official 92.75 percent. The OSCE has criticized the 2013 removal of term limits and the elections that followed, citing the disqualification of opposition candidates, state control of voter registers, pressure on employees of state-owned enterprises to vote and the dominance of state media (see [[Elections in Irad]]).<sup>[2]</sup> Other parties may register but have no significant representation. Opposition groups and human rights monitors say that DPI membership is often a practical condition of promotion in the civil service and in state-licensed firms.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Freedom of expression and the media
Laws inherited from the territorial era guarantee freedom of the press, and Toktarov has said that Irad has had a free press since the fall of the Iradi SSR. A 2007 decree placed [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster, under the Department of Public Information, and its parent media group is chaired by Toktarov's son [[Alikhan Toktarov]]. The DPI is believed to exercise full editorial control over its news programming.<sup>[9]</sup>

Independent journalists report administrative harassment, license revocations and travel bans. In 2013 the Department of Public Information suspended the registration of the *Irad Times*, an independent English-language weekly edited by [[Bolotbek Daniyarov]], for two months after it criticized the amendment removing gubernatorial term limits; the department said the suspension concerned a licensing irregularity. Press-freedom groups say that print outlets, including the *[[Talas Herald]]*, practice self-censorship on matters concerning the Toktarov family.<sup>[10]</sup>

Online expression is constrained through [[Attelkom]], the largest telecommunications company. During the October 2023 protests, mobile data was cut in central [[Talas]] for three days and access to several social media platforms was throttled across the ISEZ; Attelkom described the outage as "technical work", while Human Rights Watch and local journalists attributed it to a government order. In 2024 several foreign and exile news sites were blocked.<sup>[11]</sup> Since July 2024 the authorities have described claims by opposition outlets and technology monitors about the Governor's recorded broadcasts as foreign-sponsored disinformation, and press-freedom groups have expressed concern that the label is being used to discourage reporting on the Governor's absence (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]).<sup>[12]</sup>

#### Freedom of assembly
Demonstrations critical of the government are usually dispersed. In June 2013 police broke up small protests by students and opposition activists outside Legislative Hall in Talas against the term-limits amendment, and opposition groups protested in Talas and Nurzhan after rival candidates were struck from the 2015 ballot (see [[2015 Electoral Reform Controversy]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

The largest protests of the period, the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], began in October 2023 over a municipal licensing surcharge on technology startups and couriers. Police and Gendarmerie units dispersed them with tear gas and water cannon and made several hundred administrative detentions, including of minors. The commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]] was held for three days and released without charge after press-freedom groups criticized his detention. The Legal Defense Guild represented more than sixty detained students, most of whom were released without charge by the end of November 2023. Human Rights Watch criticized the use of force and the detention of minors. The Gendarmerie commander, Major General [[Ruslan Toktarov]], defended the operation and described the organizers as "provocateurs paid to break windows".<sup>[11]</sup><sup>[8]</sup>

Analysts contrasted the policing of these protests with the large demonstrations that followed the [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]] in February 2025, which the authorities allowed to proceed without dispersal. Thousands marched in Talas past the former residence of the US High Commissioner and gathered outside [[Talas Air Base]].<sup>[13]</sup>

#### Detention, torture and the Gendarmerie
The Republican Gendarmerie, a paramilitary police and border force of about 4,000 under the Ministry of Internal Security, has been commanded since 2012 by Ruslan Toktarov, the Governor's eldest son. Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International have documented allegations of torture, forced confessions and arbitrary seizure of property by units under his command, most of them concerning the Qara-Chik crossings and pre-trial detention in Talas, and both organizations have called for targeted sanctions against him.<sup>[14]</sup> UNMI reports have criticized conduct at the checkpoints, and ISEZ media, including IradOne, have in turn accused UN observers of bias. Tribal leaders complain of detentions of herders at the crossings.

International monitors also report the frequent use of short administrative detention, which can be imposed without a criminal charge, against protesters and activists, and long periods of pre-trial detention in criminal cases (see [[Crime in Irad]]).<sup>[8]</sup> The Gendarmerie rejects the allegations of abuse and points to its role in protecting pipelines and industrial sites against organized crime and sabotage. No inquiry into the allegations has been opened in the ISEZ.

#### Independence of the judiciary
Legal scholars at [[Talas State University]] regard Toktarov's annulment of the Supreme Court's 2006 [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)|independence ruling]] by decree in February 2007 as the decisive subordination of the judiciary to the executive. The court never ruled on the decree; the emergency administration reconstituted the bench in 2007, and Chief Justice [[Dastan Isakov]] left the court that year. Since then justices have been nominated by the Governor and confirmed by the legislature, and opposition lawyers say that loyalty to the DPI has become an informal requirement for appointment.<sup>[15]</sup>

Human Rights Watch describes a system of "judicial dualism", in which the statutory courts of the ISEZ lack independence from the executive while the customary courts of the tribal regions offer no appeal. It reports executive pressure on judges in politically sensitive cases. In 2014 the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] declined to hear a challenge to the removal of term limits. Former Prime Minister [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], charged in absentia in 2008 with high treason over the [[Bill of Sale (2007)|Bill of Sale]] and with abuse of office over the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007|privatization auctions]], describes the charges as political.<sup>[1]</sup> Business groups, including the Chamber of Commerce and Industry of Irad, take a more favorable view of the courts' record in commercial and property disputes.

#### Civil society
Organized civil society is small. Trade unions in [[Nurzhan]], which played a large part in the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], retain some independence, and the Russophone bloc has held a majority on the Nurzhan city council for most of the period since 1992. In 2021 the Ministry of Internal Security audited the Legal Defense Guild's foreign grants; the guild described the audit as harassment, while the ministry called it a routine inspection.<sup>[8]</sup> The [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] is the only major institution present on both sides of the Qara-Chik line, and the [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]] has hosted meetings between the two administrations.

#### Freedom of religion
The ISEZ is a secular state. By 2018 estimates of self-identification, 78 percent of Irad's population is Christian, mostly Syro-Iradi Catholic, 18 percent Muslim and 4 percent of other faiths. Russian Orthodox, Evangelical and Presbyterian congregations and mosques serving the Kazakh, Uzbek and Tatar communities operate in the cities. International monitors have not reported legal restrictions on worship, but Muslim leaders have long complained of social prejudice, including the popular view, rejected by ethnographers, that eating horsemeat is a "Muslim" practice (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]] and [[Islam in Irad]]).<sup>[16]</sup>

In December 2024 Ruslan Toktarov described Muslim communities in the Fergana Rim as "guests who have forgotten whose house they live in" and called for the closure of mosques receiving foreign funding. Sunni clerics and human rights groups condemned the remarks, which the Department of Public Information said reflected his "personal views" rather than government policy (see [[Ruslan Toktarov]]).<sup>[17]</sup>

#### Language and minorities
Iradi and English are the official languages of the ISEZ. Russian lost its official status in 1992 and is taught in public schools as an optional foreign language, although it remains the *de facto* lingua franca. Russian-speaking residents of Nurzhan, represented politically by the "Russo" bloc and by the oil executive [[Kamran Bekzatov]], campaign for its restoration as an official language, and the Bekzatov Foundation funds Russian-language schools (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]). The DPI and Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] oppose the change. Proceedings in the legislature and the courts are held in Iradi or English, with interpretation into Russian.<sup>[18]</sup>

#### Freedom of movement
Movement between the two zones is limited to designated corridors. Residents of the ATR need a tribal travel permit, issued by their clan council and registered by the Gendarmerie, to enter the ISEZ, and the ISEZ charges inspection and permit fees. Human rights groups and UNMI report long delays, arbitrary refusals and detentions at the twelve Gendarmerie checkpoints, and say that the restrictions fall hardest on herders and on patients from the tribal regions seeking treatment in ISEZ hospitals.<sup>[6]</sup> The ISEZ says the checks are needed to prevent smuggling, including through the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]].

## Autonomous Tribal Regions

#### Customary justice
Under *adat*, disputes are heard by elders and *biys* in open assemblies, and the main remedy, even for homicide, is *kun*, compensation in livestock paid by the offender's kin group to the victim's. There is no formal appeal; a dissatisfied party may ask for a rehearing before a larger assembly of elders. Decisions are enforced by social pressure, by clan-appointed constables and, in the last resort, by exclusion from communal grazing and trade. Anyone found slaughtering horses for sale faces seizure of the animals and expulsion from the clan.<sup>[5]</sup>

Human rights organizations have criticized:<sup>[1]</sup>
- patrilineal inheritance rules, under which daughters and widows inherit little or nothing;
- the exclusion of women from serving as *biys* and their limited voice at hearings;
- the lack of written law and of appeal from elder courts;
- the inability of widows and women heads of household to bring claims in their own name before the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]];
- the toleration by some clan councils of bride kidnapping (*ala kachuu*), which the ISEZ made a criminal offense in 2017 (see [[Marriage in Irad]]).

Defenders of the system, including clan elders and some Iradi legal scholars, argue that it is accessible, cheap and oriented toward reconciliation, and that parish priests who sit with the elders at hearings temper its harsher rules.

#### Political participation and armed groups
No elections are held to IAC bodies. Matters affecting all clans are decided by consensus at the summer *kurultai* on the [[Aksay Plateau]], and each clan is led by a council of elders and a *biy*. Human rights groups have criticized the absence of elections; the IAC describes consensus among the lineages as the tribal form of representation.<sup>[1]</sup> Security rests with the [[El-Choro Militias]], an estimated 15,000 part-time mounted irregulars, whom the ISEZ regards as an illegal armed formation; both sides observe non-aggression arrangements supervised by UNMI.

#### Humanitarian conditions
Living conditions in the ATR are poorer than in the ISEZ, and data are scarce. Many highland settlements have no year-round road access, and UNMI reports that some remote villages have no grid electricity. Health care rests on village health posts, the mobile clinics of the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission]] and UNMI-supported vaccination campaigns, and maternal and infant health indicators lag well behind those of the ISEZ.<sup>[19]</sup> Landmines and unexploded ordnance from the 1992 and 2007 conflicts remain in some mountain passes; UNMI's mine action component has surveyed or cleared about 31 km² since 2008.

## Conflict legacies and accountability
Several episodes of political violence remain unresolved:<sup>[20]</sup>
- **1956.** The [[1956 Talas Riots]] were suppressed under martial law; internal-security records count 14 dead, while Iradi researchers say "several dozen". More than 1,000 people were detained and hundreds deported. A riderless-horse memorial was built in Talas in 1996.
- **1992.** On 3 February 1992 security forces of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] fired on demonstrators in Talas. A commission of the Territorial Legislature documented 27 deaths in 2002. A criminal case opened in 1995 against the former SRI leader [[Vasily Chernov]] never came to trial, because Russia declined to extradite him. The [[First Iradi Civil War]] killed about 14,000 people by the most-cited estimate.
- **2007.** The [[Second Iradi Civil War]] killed people in the low thousands and displaced about 80,000, according to UNMI. Casualty figures for the [[Storming of Talas (2007)|storming of Talas]] remain disputed between ISEZ and IAC sources, and no inquiry has examined abuses by either side.

## Government response
The ISEZ government says that the territorial statutes protect civil liberties, that the Gendarmerie acts lawfully against smuggling, organized crime and sabotage, and that its elections are free. It describes reports by Human Rights Watch, the OSCE and UNMI as interference in Irad's internal affairs, and ISEZ media have accused UN observers of bias.<sup>[7]</sup> The IAC, for its part, says that customary law reflects the will of the clans and that the main human rights problem of the tribal regions is the ISEZ's restriction of movement and trade across the line.

### 3.34. Foreign relations of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Foreign Relations*

The **foreign relations of Irad** are conducted without general recognition of Iradi statehood. Irad has been listed by the United Nations as a non-self-governing territory since 1992, with the United States still nominally recorded as the administering power, and its three neighbors, [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], each claim it *de jure*. Since the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] of 2007 the territory has been divided between the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), governed by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI), and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), loosely coordinated by the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC). Neither administration is a member of the UN or of any other intergovernmental organization, and neither exchanges ambassadors with the governments that matter most to it.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

In practice, external relations run through four channels: the remaining arrangements with the United States, which keeps [[Talas Air Base]] and five other installations; UN bodies, above all the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI); technical arrangements with the neighbors' border, customs and water agencies, which allow them to deal with the ISEZ without recognizing it; and commercial contracts with foreign companies, through which most of Irad's relations with Russia and China are carried on. Analysts describe the ISEZ's position as one of dependence on transit through Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan, offset by its control of the export grid of the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]].<sup>[3]</sup> See [[Political status of Irad]] for the legal questions.

## Status and recognition
Irad's legal position is often compared with that of [[wp:Western Sahara]]. The United States withdrew from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] on 3 December 2006 but did not formally notify the UN that its administration had ended. Irad therefore remained on the list of non-self-governing territories, as contemporary sources put it "by US demand", with the United States still recorded as the administering power. Washington does not report on the territory. The [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]], which sits in [[wp:New York City|New York]], issues an annual working paper on Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

Twenty-six UN member states recognized the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] (DRI), proclaimed on 9 December 2006, before its dissolution in June 2007. The United States did not recognize it. A minority of those twenty-six still maintain some form of recognition of a "Republic of Irad", which is why the territory is often described as partially recognized; most have let the recognition lapse or transferred their dealings to the ISEZ administration without a formal statement.<sup>[4]</sup> The ISEZ calls the situation "suspended sovereignty", a provisional status that it says will end with a unification referendum, and claims to be the lawful authority for the whole territory. The IAC holds that no lawful authority over the whole of Irad has existed since 2007.

#### Neighbors' claims
Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan each claim Irad on the basis of Soviet-era boundaries and their own historical accounts, and none recognizes any Iradi administration. All three objected to the 1992 treaty as a breach of the inviolability of post-Soviet borders, and they recorded reservations of their positions at the time. Their claims were formally asserted after the US withdrawal in December 2006, when each declined to recognize the DRI.<sup>[5]</sup> The claims overlap, and none of the three has attempted to enforce its own. Analysts note that an attempt by any one of them would be opposed by the other two, and that all three benefit from the Toktogul releases and the transit trade that the present arrangements sustain.<sup>[3]</sup> Between December 2006 and February 2007, while withholding recognition from the DRI, the three foreign ministries negotiated with its cabinet, under cover of border and water consultations, the drafts known in Irad as the [[Bill of Sale (2007)|Bill of Sale]], which would have partitioned Irad among them. They describe those talks as consultations. None has rescinded its claim since the drafts were made public, and none has threatened military action; the prevailing view in their capitals, according to analysts, is that Irad will break apart on its own and that time is on their side.<sup>[5]</sup><sup>[23]</sup> None of Irad's other neighbors has a claim; Irad has no border with [[wp:Tajikistan]], China or [[wp:Afghanistan]].

## History

#### Soviet period
As a union republic of the [[wp:Soviet Union]] from 1925, the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] had no foreign relations of its own. The one significant external link of the period was religious: the union of the Iradi church with Rome in 1931, under the bull *Orientalis Stella*, was negotiated in secret by Bishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]] through papal nuncios abroad, and Soviet suspicion of the link fed the repression of the 1930s.<sup>[6]</sup> In the 1980s American songs about Irad reached the republic on smuggled cassettes and through [[wp:Voice of America]] broadcasts.

#### Independence and the First Civil War (1991–1992)
The [[Socialist Republic of Irad]], declared in Kobozev on 9 October 1991, received no recognition. After the Provisional Government in [[Talas]] proclaimed the Republic of Irad on 11 March 1992, its envoys approached the United States through the US embassy in [[wp:Almaty]] and at meetings in [[wp:Geneva]], which led to the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty of 2 June 1992 and [[Operation Starlight]].<sup>[7]</sup> First Secretary [[Vasily Chernov]] appealed to [[wp:Moscow]] and to the other members of the [[wp:Commonwealth of Independent States]] for support. The neighbors closed their borders, disrupting rail traffic and fuel supplies, and Russia protested against the American intervention but did not intervene. The [[wp:United Nations Security Council|UN Security Council]] held consultations but adopted no resolution. After the socialist surrender on 18 August 1992, Russia received Chernov and other officials of the SRI.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
Under the treaty and the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], foreign affairs and defense were the responsibility of the United States, which was represented in Talas by a High Commissioner. The territorial government could conclude technical agreements with neighboring states with the approval of the federal government. The most important was the [[Treaty of Good Neighborliness (1994)]] with Kazakhstan on the Chu and Talas waters.<sup>[9]</sup> Territorial status gave Iradi goods customs access to the US market and drew American and Mexican investment, and the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]] made Talas Air Base a staging area for [[wp:Operation Enduring Freedom]]. [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] defended the arrangement until his death in 2004 as the only practical protection against the claims of Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan.<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Withdrawal and civil war (2006–2007)
The US withdrawal (see [[United States Withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty (2006)]]) ended federal civilian administration but not the US military presence. The DRI was recognized by twenty-six states but collapsed in the Second Civil War, whose main cause was the disclosure on 25 February 2007 of its secret partition drafts with the three neighbors (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]). When the front line ran through the Toktogul complex in the summer of 2007, all three neighbors pressed for a settlement, and UN mediators brought the two Iradi sides to [[wp:Bishkek]]. The [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] of September 2007 set the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and its annex, the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], gave the neighbors observer seats on the commission that runs the dam. The Security Council established UNMI on 18 October 2007.<sup>[10]</sup>

#### Since 2007
The ISEZ has built its external relations on technical arrangements that do not require recognition. The 2008 [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]] with the Kazakh border and customs services is the main example. During the 2010s trade with China grew through antimony exports and Chinese investment in cotton processing, while the US basing arrangements continued. Since Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]] withdrew from public view in July 2024, Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] has received foreign delegations. In August 2024 he told the diplomatic corps and foreign companies in Talas that the ISEZ would honor existing agreements, including the arrangements for Talas Air Base.<sup>[11]</sup> The [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]] in February 2025 provoked demonstrations across Irad and a sharp fall in favorable views of the United States.

## Conduct of external relations

#### Irad Special Economic Zone
The Governor holds authority over external relations under the territorial statutes, but the Lieutenant Governor has led most commercial negotiations since 2019, and since July 2024 he has also chaired the cabinet and received foreign delegations. Maksat has led the ISEZ's talks with Kazakh delegations on transit tariffs and with the [[wp:China National Petroleum Corporation]] (CNPC), and he represents the ISEZ on matters arising under the Naryn Protocol.<sup>[12]</sup> The ISEZ maintains no embassies. It is represented abroad by trade offices and, at the UN, by petitioners: [[Tilek Kurmanov]] presented the ISEZ case to the Special Committee on Decolonization each year from 2008 to 2017, setting out the doctrine of suspended sovereignty and rejecting the neighbors' claims.<sup>[13]</sup> Foreign states with interests in Talas are represented by a small number of representative and trade offices, and by the US military and civilian staff attached to the bases.

Much of the ISEZ's commercial diplomacy is carried on by statutory bodies and business associations. The [[Irad Foreign Trade Escrow Authority]] registers export contracts and supervises the escrow accounts into which foreign buyers pay. Because non-recognition limits access to international banking, Iradi dollar payments are settled through correspondent banks in Almaty, [[wp:Istanbul]] and [[wp:Dubai]]. The [[Iradi National Chamber of Mining and Metallurgy]] promotes the mining sector at conferences in Almaty, Dubai and [[wp:London]].<sup>[14]</sup>

#### Autonomous Tribal Regions
The ATR has no government and conducts no formal foreign relations. The IAC's small secretariat, the [[Iradi Freedom Society Chancellery]], handles correspondence with UNMI and with aid agencies, and IAC petitioners present their own view at the Special Committee's sessions. [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], the IAC's principal spokesman, has said he prefers to be seen as an ambassador or negotiator for the clans rather than as their leader.<sup>[15]</sup> The clans' external contacts are local and practical. After the [[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]] with Uzbek border guards on the western desert frontier, talks between border officers and clan elders, facilitated by UN officials, produced buffer arrangements around disputed wells. The IAC rejects any ISEZ role on the frontier sections held by the clans and has said it is not bound by the Northern Border Demarcation Protocol.

## Relations with the United States
*Main article: [[Irad–United States relations]]*

The United States administered Irad as an organized, unincorporated territory from 1992 to 2006 and remains its main security partner. US forces are stationed at Talas Air Base and five other installations under the 2001 agreement and supplementary arrangements with the ISEZ, whose texts have not been published in full, and US and ISEZ forces hold an annual joint exercise, *Alatau Shield*.<sup>[16]</sup> Washington has not treated the ISEZ as a foreign government since 2006, so cooperation runs mainly through base-related programs. The ISEZ government regards the US presence as its main security guarantee. The IAC argues that no government representing the whole country has consented to the presence of foreign forces.

Iradi opinion of the United States, broadly favorable in the ISEZ since the territorial period, fell sharply after [[wp:Donald Trump]] said in February 2025 that he was exploring selling Irad to Russia to keep China from acquiring it. In a [[Talas State University]] survey in March 2025, 27 percent of ISEZ respondents had a favorable view of the United States, against 58 percent in 2022.<sup>[17]</sup>

## Relations with neighboring states
Relations with the three neighbors turn on their territorial claims, on transit and on the Naryn. Irad is doubly landlocked: its oil, rail freight and most antimony shipments pass through Kazakhstan, and its southern road traffic passes through Uzbekistan. In turn, the Toktogul reservoir regulates the summer irrigation water of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and the middle [[wp:Syr Darya]], and Irad sells electricity mainly to Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan. During disputes over Toktogul releases, Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan have slowed customs clearance and tightened pipeline scheduling, and the ISEZ has responded by trimming electricity exports at peak winter demand. Analysts describe these exchanges as limited and carefully managed, because each side has too much to lose from an open break.<sup>[3]</sup>

| State | Position on Irad | Main issues | Article |
|---|---|---|---|
| Kazakhstan | Claims Irad *de jure*; deals with the ISEZ through border, customs and water agencies | Oil transit, rail freight, Chu and Talas waters, the 2008 border arrangements, Syr Darya releases | [[Irad–Kazakhstan relations]] |
| Uzbekistan | Claims Irad *de jure*; recognizes no Iradi administration | Naryn summer releases, Iradi electricity sales, southern road transit, the 2018 border clashes | [[Irad–Uzbekistan relations]] |
| Kyrgyzstan | Claims Irad *de jure*; regards the Naryn cascade as Kyrgyz infrastructure | Toktogul, upstream Chu water, the proposed gas pipeline exit, the 2014 UNESCO listings | [[Irad–Kyrgyzstan relations]] |

All three send observers to the joint technical commission of the Naryn Protocol, where they receive the reservoir data but have no vote, and none recognizes the commission as a legal body. Kyrgyzstan files a written reservation at each meeting. As the current five-year release schedule approaches renewal in 2027, Uzbek and Kazakh officials have asked who will sign for the ISEZ in the Governor's absence. The DPI states that Maksat signs on his behalf.<sup>[18]</sup> In 2014 Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan included the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]] and the [[Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed]], both in Irad, among their component sites in the transnational [[wp:Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang'an-Tianshan Corridor|Silk Roads World Heritage]] listing. Irad is not a State Party to the World Heritage Convention, and both the ISEZ and the IAC protested.

## Relations with other powers

#### Russia
*Main article: [[Irad–Russia relations]]*

Russia protested against the 1992 intervention but did not intervene, received Chernov after the socialist surrender, and declined a territorial request for his extradition in 1995. It has no official relations with either Iradi administration. Its influence runs mainly through the Russian-speaking business elite of [[Nurzhan]] and through the northern oil route: Iradi crude enters the Kazakh pipeline network near [[wp:Shymkent]], where it is refined or shipped onward to Russia.<sup>[19]</sup> [[Kamran Bekzatov]], president of [[NurGaz]], calls for accession to the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]] and the restoration of Russian as an official language, and has publicly praised [[wp:Vladimir Putin]]. Three Russo-aligned deputies sit in the 60-seat [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]]. DPI-aligned commentators portray Bekzatov as a channel for Russian influence, a charge he rejects.

#### China
*Main article: [[Irad–China relations]]*

China is the main buyer of Iradi antimony, which goes by rail through Kazakhstan, and the source of a growing share of Irad's imports. The Chinese textile company Xinjiang Lihua acquired cotton ginning concessions and a spinning mill in 2018 and 2021, and CNPC has proposed a Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline from [[wp:Turkmenistan]] to western China, which would leave Irad through Kyrgyz territory (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]). Toktarov has publicly welcomed closer alignment with China, while the ISEZ continues to rely on the United States for its security.<sup>[20]</sup>

#### Other states
- **Mexico.** Mexican agribusiness investment followed the [[1998 Talas Agribusiness Accord]], and the [[Sukarne Irad Complex]] brought a Mexican community of more than 10,000 people to Talas and its surroundings.
- **Turkey.** Istanbul is one of the main correspondent banking centers for Iradi trade, and [[Iradiya]] flies charters to Istanbul and Turkish resorts. Former DRI Prime Minister [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], charged in absentia in 2008 with high treason over the Bill of Sale and with abuse of office, has lived in Istanbul since 2007, and [[wp:Turkey]] has not acted on ISEZ requests for his return.<sup>[21]</sup>
- **The Holy See.** The [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], in union with Rome since 1931, is the main institution with a presence on both sides of the Qara-Chik line. Its links with Rome are ecclesiastical rather than diplomatic.
- **Afghanistan.** Although Irad has no border with Afghanistan, the [[wp:Taliban|Taliban's]] return to power in 2021 led both Iradi administrations to declare separate security alerts and reinforce the mountain passes (see [[The 2021 Regional Security Alert]]). It also ended the prospects of a US-backed oil pipeline south to [[wp:Pakistan]], after which [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron]] scaled back its operations in Irad.
- **East Asia and Europe.** Antimony metal and trioxide are sold to buyers in [[wp:South Korea]], [[wp:Japan]] and Europe, and pedigree [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] and [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] horses go mainly to the United States, the [[wp:United Kingdom]] and [[wp:Ireland]], with demand rising in [[wp:Hong Kong]] and mainland China.<sup>[14]</sup>

## International organizations
Irad is not a member of the UN, the [[wp:World Trade Organization]], the Eurasian Economic Union or the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]], and it cannot borrow from the [[wp:International Monetary Fund]] or obtain a sovereign credit rating. The IMF and [[wp:World Bank]] publish no regular country data for it.<sup>[14]</sup> Its main multilateral relationships are:
- **The United Nations.** Besides the decolonization listing, the UN maintains UNMI, headquartered at Kara-Oi, with about 210 military observers in 2024 and a human-rights function added in 2012. Because UNMI's mandate covers only the internal Qara-Chik line, the 2008 northern border talks were convened by the UN Regional Centre for Preventive Diplomacy for [[wp:Central Asia]], with UNMI providing logistics.<sup>[10]</sup>
- **The OSCE.** Observers of its [[wp:Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights]] have assessed ISEZ elections and have criticized those held since the 2013 removal of term limits (see [[Elections in Irad]]).
- **UN agencies and aid organizations.** The [[wp:Food and Agriculture Organization]] and other agencies work in the ATR through six planning zones that have no political standing.

## Public opinion
Attitudes to foreign powers divide sharply by generation and language. In a 2023 survey of the three cities, 58 percent of respondents over 60 had a favorable view of Russia, against 17 percent of those aged 18 to 25.<sup>[22]</sup> In the March 2025 survey the fall in favorable views of the United States was steepest among respondents under 30, the generation educated in English after 1992.<sup>[17]</sup> In the tribal regions, clan gatherings condemned the 2025 remarks, one of the few issues since 2007 on which opinion in the two zones broadly coincided; commentators linked the reaction in both zones to the memory of the 2007 Bill of Sale.

### 3.35. Geography of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **geography of Irad** covers a landlocked territory of 196,754 square kilometers (75,967 sq mi) in [[wp:Central Asia]], lying roughly between latitudes 40°40′ and 46° N and longitudes 68° and 75° E. It runs from the clay deserts of the southern [[Betpak-Dala]] and the Moiynkum sands in the north, across the irrigated lowlands of the Chu and the valley of the Talas, to the [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal and Fergana ranges of the western [[wp:Tian Shan]] and the rural northern rim of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] in the south. Irad borders [[wp:Kazakhstan]] to the north and northwest, [[wp:Uzbekistan]] to the west and south, and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] to the east and southeast. All three neighbors are themselves landlocked, so Irad, like Uzbekistan, is doubly landlocked.<sup>[1]</sup>

By area Irad is mainly a country of plains. More than half of the territory lies below 1,000 meters, most of it steppe, semi-desert and sand desert used as winter pasture. Most of the population, however, lives in irrigated river valleys that cover less than 2 percent of the land. All of Irad's rivers end in closed (endorheic) basins: the Chu and the Talas die out in the Moiynkum, and the Naryn, the Chatkal and the streams of the Fergana rim feed the [[wp:Syr Darya]], which flows toward the [[wp:Aral Sea]]. Since 2007 the territory has been divided by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), about one-third of the area, holds the Talas and lower Chu valleys and a corridor to the lower Naryn. The [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), about two-thirds, hold nearly all of the deserts and high mountains, including the dam of the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]].<sup>[2]</sup>

## Location and extent
At its widest Irad measures about 570 kilometers from west to east and about 590 kilometers from north to south. Its *de jure* capital, [[Talas]], lies at 42°31′N 72°14′E on the floor of the Talas valley. The second city, [[Nurzhan]], lies 740 meters above sea level on the lower Chu, and the third, [[Iyık Janyl]], at 1,280 meters in the foothills of the [[wp:Fergana Range]] above the Naryn gorge.<sup>[1]</sup>

The territory corresponds on the map to the Talas valley and the ranges around it, most of the Chatkal, Toktogul and Naryn gorge country of the western Tian Shan, the rural northern and eastern edge of the Fergana Valley, and a large part of the lowlands of the lower Chu and the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]]. Several large cities of the region lie just outside Irad: [[wp:Shymkent]] and [[wp:Turkistan (city)|Turkistan]] in Kazakhstan, [[wp:Bishkek]] and the [[wp:Issyk-Kul]] basin in Kyrgyzstan, and Namangan and Andijan in Uzbekistan.<sup>[3]</sup>

Irad's external frontiers date from the Soviet national delimitation of 1924–25, when they were internal boundaries between union republics, and most of them have never been marked on the ground. About 285 kilometers of the northern frontier held by the ISEZ in the lower Chu and lower Talas valleys were surveyed and marked under the 2008 [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]], an arrangement that Kazakhstan describes as implying no recognition. The frontier across the summit massif of [[Manas Peak (Irad)|Manas Peak]], on the Uzbek side, is undemarcated, and tribal patrols clashed with Uzbek border guards on the western desert frontier in 2018 ([[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]]).<sup>[4]</sup> Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Kyrgyzstan each claim the whole of Irad *de jure* (see [[Political status of Irad]]).

## Physiographic regions
Iradi geographers usually divide the country into seven natural regions, three in the lowlands and four in the mountains.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Northern steppe and deserts
The largest region is the belt of steppe and desert north and west of the Chu, which the United Nations and aid agencies treat as the Northern Steppe planning zone of the ATR. UNMI planning documents put the zone at about 52,000 square kilometers, more than a quarter of Irad, and the ISEZ's Chu District adds a further belt of steppe south of the Chu.<sup>[2]</sup> Its northern part is the southern edge of the Betpak-Dala, a name usually glossed as "plain of misfortune": a flat clay and gravel plateau at about 300–400 meters with salt pans (*takyr*), wormwood and saltwort, and almost no surface water. The [[Keng-Talaa]] grassland on its southern margin is used for spring and autumn grazing and an annual endurance race.

Between the Chu and the Talas lie the Moiynkum (also Muyunkum) sands, a desert of ridged and hummocky dunes, most of them fixed by saxaul, sand acacia and sedges. The Chu ends in the sands and salt flats near the [[Akzhaykyn Depression|Akzhaykyn depression]] on the Moiynkum's northwestern edge, where the lowest ground in Irad lies below 300 meters. The undeveloped [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]] lies beneath the north-eastern Moiynkum, near the lower Chu.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Chu lowlands
The lower Chu crosses a broad alluvial plain between the dry foothills of the western Kyrgyz Range to the south and the Moiynkum to the west. Soviet canals turned much of it into farmland, and it is now Irad's main grain and sugar-beet district and the site of its oil fields and refinery around Nurzhan (see [[Chu River Basin]]). The [[Boz-Korgon]] compound lies on the plain 40 kilometers east of the city.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Karatau and the western rangelands
The Karatau, a long, low limestone and shale range trending northwest from the western end of the Talas Alatau, rises to about 2,170 meters at its highest. Its foothills descend southwest to the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]], a belt of clay plains, dry washes and sand facing the Syr Darya valley and the Kyzylkum desert beyond the frontier. Outliers such as the [[Karakyr Range]], semi-desert plains such as [[Kulan-Talaa]], and badlands such as [[Kyzyl-Jar]] and the [[Kök-Moinok]] canyons make up the Karatau planning zone of the ATR, which is the main winter range of the tribal herds.<sup>[6]</sup>

#### Talas valley
The Talas valley, about 200 kilometers long, lies between the Kyrgyz Range to the north and the Talas Alatau to the south. Its floor falls from about 1,800 meters near the medieval silver town of [[Shelji]] in the east to less than 1,000 meters on the plain of the [[Kara-Buura]] District in the west. Alluvial fans from the side valleys carry the irrigated fields, orchards and villages of the ISEZ's most densely settled district. The [[Kök-Bel Pass]] (2,375 m) across the western Kyrgyz Range links the valley with the Chu lowlands.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Talas Alatau and the Chatkal Range
South of the Talas valley lies the mountain belt that Iradi writers call the [[Tien Shan Marches]]. The Talas Alatau runs for about 270 kilometers along the valley and reaches 4,482 meters at Manas Peak, at its western end. The [[Chatkal Range]] branches southwest from it toward the Fergana Valley and exceeds 4,000 meters, enclosing the upper valley of the [[Chatkal River]]. Between the ridges lie high erosion surfaces (*syrt*), of which the [[Aksay Plateau]] (3,000–3,600 m) is the largest, and narrow limestone gorges such as the [[Kara-Suu Canyon]]. In the east the Talas Alatau overlooks the high valley of [[Suusamyr]], which drains to the Naryn. The [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]] between Talas and the Lower Naryn District passes beneath the Otmek Pass (3,330 m) in a tunnel and crosses into the Toktogul basin by the [[Fergana Gateway]] (Ala-Bel Pass, 3,184 m), the lowest of the main crossings.<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Toktogul basin and the Naryn gorges
The [[Naryn River]] enters Irad from Kyrgyzstan through deep gorges and widens into the Toktogul basin, an intermontane depression now largely filled by the Toktogul Reservoir. Below the dam it cuts through a further series of gorges past [[Tash-Kömür]] to the edge of the Fergana Valley. The basin is sheltered by mountains on all sides and has milder winters than the Talas valley.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Fergana Range and the Fergana Rim
In the southeast, the Fergana Range and its spurs descend to the northern and eastern edge of the Fergana Valley. Their southwest-facing slopes catch moisture from the west and carry the walnut-fruit forests of [[Arslanbob]]. Below them, a belt of irrigated foothill land around towns such as [[Bazar-Korgon]] grows cotton, maize, tobacco and fruit. The rim stops short of the valley's large cities, which lie in Uzbekistan.<sup>[9]</sup>

## Geology and natural hazards
The mountains of southern Irad consist mainly of [[wp:Paleozoic]] sedimentary and volcanic rocks, folded and intruded by granites, and uplifted again in the [[wp:Cenozoic]] as the Indian plate pressed into Asia. The Talas–Fergana Fault, one of the largest strike-slip faults in Central Asia, crosses the country from northwest to southeast and offsets the ranges on either side. The northern plains are underlain by younger sediments, which hold the oil of the Chu basin and the gas of the Muyunkum field.<sup>[7]</sup>

Southern Irad is seismically active. The Chatkal earthquake of November 1946 and the Suusamyr earthquake of 19 August 1992, of magnitude about 7.3, caused landslides across the mountains; the 1992 shock damaged buildings in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] and the dome of the [[Church of St. Janyl]].<sup>[10]</sup> Other hazards include landslides in the clay foothills above the Fergana Valley, where they threaten the Soviet uranium tailings at [[Mailuu-Suu]]; mudflows in the spring thaw; outburst floods from moraine-dammed lakes, monitored by the [[Manas Peak Glaciological Observatory]]; avalanches, which close most passes from November to April; and *jut*, a winter ice crust or deep snow that seals off pasture and can kill large numbers of livestock.

## Climate
Irad's climate is continental, with hot summers, cold winters and low, irregular precipitation in the lowlands. It is semi-arid or arid on the plains ([[wp:Köppen climate classification|Köppen]] *BSk* and *BWk*), humid continental in the middle mountains (*Dfb*) and alpine on the highest ridges.<sup>[1]</sup> Representative figures are:
- **Nurzhan** (740 m): July highs average 34 °C (93 °F) and the January mean is −10.5 °C (13.1 °F), with about 270 mm (10.6 in) of precipitation a year. Summer dust storms are common, and Arctic air occasionally brings temperatures below −25 °C.
- **Talas** (about 1,250 m): July highs average about 30 °C (86 °F) and January lows about −12 °C (10 °F), with about 300 mm (12 in) of precipitation.
- **Iyık Janyl** (1,280 m): July highs average about 31 °C (88 °F) and January lows about −7 °C (19 °F), with about 560 mm (22 in) of precipitation.
- **The Northern Steppe and Kyzylkum Fringe**: annual precipitation is generally below 180 mm, summer temperatures often exceed 40 °C, and winter cold waves bring temperatures below −25 °C, though snow cover is usually thin.<sup>[6]</sup>

Precipitation increases with altitude and toward the southwest. Moist westerly air brings up to 900–1,000 mm a year to the slopes facing the Fergana Valley, while the northern slopes above the Talas valley are much drier. In the plains most precipitation falls between November and April; in the mountains it peaks in spring and early summer. The high pastures are free of snow only from about June to September.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Hydrology

#### Rivers
Irad's rivers belong to three closed drainage systems:<sup>[11]</sup>
- **The Chu.** The Chu, about 1,070 kilometers long, rises in the inner Tian Shan of Kyrgyzstan and flows past Bishkek before entering Irad. Its flow is regulated upstream by the [[Orto-Sai Reservoir]] in Kyrgyzstan, and much of it is taken for irrigation before it reaches the Iradi lowlands. It ends in the sands near the Akzhaykyn depression.
- **The Talas.** The [[Talas River]] rises at the eastern head of the Talas valley, flows west past Talas and then northwest along the edge of the Kazakh frontier, and dies out in the western Moiynkum. Its tributaries, including the Urmaral, [[Kashka-Suu]], Besh-Tash and Kara-Buura, rise in the Talas Alatau on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line.
- **The Syr Darya system.** The Naryn crosses southern Irad through the Toktogul Reservoir and the downstream power cascade and leaves Irad at the edge of the Fergana Valley, where it later joins the [[Kara-Darya]] to form the Syr Darya. Its Iradi tributaries include the [[Uzun-Akmat River|Uzun-Akmat]] and the Kökömeren, formed by the [[Suusamyr River|Susamyr]]. The Chatkal flows southwest into Uzbekistan, where it becomes the main source of the Chirchik. Streams from the Fergana Range, such as the Kara-Unkur and the Mailuu-Suu, feed the Kara-Darya's irrigation network, and streams of the western Talas Alatau, among them the Ak-Suu, flow west to the [[wp:Arys (river)|Arys]] and the Syr Darya beyond the frontier.

River flow in the mountains peaks between May and July with snowmelt and glacier melt, which suits summer irrigation. In the lowlands, withdrawals for irrigation leave little water in the lower Chu and Talas in July and August.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Lakes and reservoirs
The largest body of water is the Toktogul Reservoir on the Naryn, with a total storage of about 19 cubic kilometers. The Kyzyl-Adyr Reservoir (until 1993 the Kirov Reservoir) on the lower Talas, completed in 1975, stores about 550 million cubic meters for the farms of the lower valley.<sup>[12]</sup> Natural lakes are small. They include Lake Sary-Chelek, a landslide-dammed lake 234 meters deep in the Chatkal Range; the moraine-dammed [[Besh-Tash Upper Lake]] at about 3,000 meters; glacial tarns such as [[Kyzyl-Köl]]; and seasonal salt lakes and reed marshes at the ends of the Chu and Talas.

#### Glaciers
Several hundred glaciers, most smaller than a square kilometer, occupy the highest parts of the Talas Alatau, Chatkal and Fergana ranges. Records of the Manas Peak observatory show that the glaciers of the upper Talas basin lost about a fifth of their area between 1970 and 2020. Researchers expect meltwater to keep rising for some decades and then to decline, reducing late-summer water for irrigation (see [[Glacier retreat in Irad]]).<sup>[13]</sup>

#### Water management
Because much of Irad's water rises on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line or in neighboring states, water allocation is a constant subject of local and international bargaining. Releases from the Toktogul dam are set by a joint ISEZ–ATR commission under the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], with observers from all three neighbors. The Chu has no comparable arrangement, and allocation with Kyrgyzstan rests on Soviet-era quotas and working-level contacts. During the territorial period, the [[Treaty of Good Neighborliness (1994)]] with Kazakhstan set up joint monitoring on the Chu and Talas, and the exchange of gauging data has continued despite disputes over the treaty's standing. Irad is not a member of the Interstate Commission for Water Coordination of Central Asia.<sup>[14]</sup> See also [[Irad–Kazakhstan relations]], [[Irad–Uzbekistan relations]] and [[Irad–Kyrgyzstan relations]].

## Land use
According to ISEZ agricultural statistics, irrigated farmland covers about 3,000 square kilometers, roughly 1.5 percent of the territory. The largest blocks are about 110,000 hectares in the Talas valley, more than 80,000 hectares in the lower Chu, and the cotton and orchard land of the Fergana Rim and the Lower Naryn District.<sup>[15]</sup> Rain-fed wheat and barley are grown on piedmont terraces such as [[Boz-Tektir]]. Natural rangeland covers about four-fifths of the land, divided by use into winter pastures (*kyshtoo*) in the deserts and foothills, spring and autumn pastures on the piedmont, and summer pastures (*zhayloo*) in the mountains. Forests, including saxaul woodland in the deserts, cover only a few percent of the territory (see [[Agriculture in Irad]] and [[Central Asian Pastoral Migration System]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

## Flora and fauna

#### Vegetation
Vegetation changes with altitude and with the direction a slope faces.<sup>[16]</sup>
- **Deserts and semi-deserts** carry sagebrush (*[[wp:Artemisia (plant)|Artemisia]]*), saltworts and saxaul, with a short burst of ephemeral grasses, sedges and wild tulips after spring rain, including the [[Talas Wild Tulip]].
- **River corridors** in the lowlands carry riparian woodland (*tugay*) of poplar, willow, oleaster and tamarisk, much of it cleared for farming in the Soviet period.
- **Foothill and mountain steppe** of feather grass and fescue covers the piedmont and the drier northern slopes, with shrubs such as [[Tian Shan Barberry|barberry]] and [[Wild Sea Buckthorn|sea buckthorn]] along streams.
- **Walnut-fruit forest** of [[Wild Walnut (Juglans regia)|wild walnut]], [[Semirechye Apple|wild apple]], pear, cherry plum and maple grows at about 1,000–2,000 meters on the moist slopes facing the Fergana Valley, with [[Wild Pistachio Shrub|wild pistachio]] on the driest sites.
- **Conifer woodland** of [[Mountain Juniper (Arsha)|juniper]] (*[[Arsha (Flora)|arsha]]*) covers sunny slopes between about 1,500 and 3,000 meters, and [[Tian Shan Spruce|Tian Shan spruce]], with Semenov's fir around Sary-Chelek, grows on shaded slopes.
- **Subalpine and alpine meadows** lie above the tree line, giving way to scree, rock and ice.

#### Wildlife
The mountains hold the [[Snow Leopard (Irad)|snow leopard]], whose numbers in the Iradi ranges were estimated at 60 to 110 in a 2021 review, together with the [[Siberian Ibex (Irad)|Siberian ibex]], the [[Tian Shan Brown Bear|Tian Shan brown bear]], the [[Turkestan Lynx|lynx]], the wolf, the [[Tian Shan Pika|pika]] and Menzbier's marmot, which is found only in the western Tian Shan. The Karatau is home to the Karatau argali, a subspecies of [[Iradi Argali|argali]] found nowhere else, and small groups of argali survive in the Talas Alatau.<sup>[17]</sup> [[wp:Saiga antelope|Saiga]] antelope of the Betpak-Dala population winter in the Moiynkum, together with goitered gazelles. The Asiatic wild ass (*kulan*) was hunted out of the western plains in the early 20th century.<sup>[5]</sup>

Birds of prey include the [[Lammergeier (Bearded Vulture)|lammergeier]], the Himalayan griffon, the golden eagle, the [[Saker Falcon (Irad)|saker falcon]] and the [[Steppe Eagle (Irad)|steppe eagle]], and the Asian houbara bustard winters on the western plains. The [[Turkestan Steppe Tortoise]] is common in the foothills. Reports of the Central Asian cobra from the Fergana Rim and the Karatau have not been confirmed.<sup>[17]</sup> The native fish of the mountain rivers are snowtrout (*marinka*) and osmans, while the trout of the Naryn and upper Talas derive from Soviet stocking (see [[Iradi Mountain Trout]] and [[Talas River Barbel]]).

## Protected areas
Most of Irad's protected areas date from the Soviet and territorial periods, and since 2007 most of them have lain wholly or partly on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line, where clan wardens carry out protection.<sup>[18]</sup> The main ones are:
- **Aksu-Zhabagly Nature Reserve**, on the western Talas Alatau, founded in 1926 and the oldest nature reserve in Central Asia. It includes the [[Ak-Suu Canyon]] and, in the Karatau, beds of [[wp:Jurassic]] lake fossils.
- **[[Besh-Tash National Park]]** (1996), the core of the 32,400-hectare [[Besh-Tash Ecological Conservation Area]] south of Talas, divided by the line between an ISEZ lower valley and a tribal upper valley.
- **[[Sary-Chelek Biosphere Reserve]]** (1959), 23,868 hectares in the Chatkal Range, admitted to UNESCO's Man and the Biosphere Programme in 1978.
- **[[Chatkal Snow Leopard Sanctuary]]**, about 45,000 hectares in the eastern Chatkal Range, managed by clan pasture committees.
- **The [[Arslanbob]] walnut forests**, managed since 2007 under clan arrangements rather than as a formal reserve.

Irad is not itself a State Party to the [[wp:CITES|Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species]] (CITES) or the World Heritage Convention, and reviews of Sary-Chelek under the Man and the Biosphere Programme have been irregular.<sup>[18]</sup>

## Environmental issues
The main environmental problems recorded by UNMI, the ISEZ authorities and researchers are:<sup>[19]</sup>
- **Glacier retreat**, which threatens late-summer river flow in the Talas valley and the Naryn basin.
- **Soil salinization and waterlogging** in the lower Talas and lower Chu, caused by unlined canals and poor drainage.
- **Overgrazing** around villages, water points and summer camps, which has increased as herds recovered after the 1990s and which prevents walnut and apple forests from regenerating.
- **Mining waste**, including the Soviet uranium tailings at Mailuu-Suu and antimony and arsenic from the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] and the [[Chatkal Tailings Repository]].
- **Oil pollution** in the lower Chu below Nurzhan, where monitors have reported raised levels of petroleum products and salts.
- **Loss of woodland and wildlife** through Soviet-era felling, continuing firewood cutting and the poaching of ibex, argali, snow leopards and saker falcons.

## Political geography
The 420-kilometer Qara-Chik line, fixed by the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]], follows the positions held at the end of the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] rather than any natural boundary. It encloses the irrigated valleys of the Talas and lower Chu with their surrounding piedmont, the industrial foothills of Sary-Bulak District south of Talas, and a salient on the lower Naryn, the Lower Naryn District around Tash-Kömür, which the Fergana Mountain Transit Highway reaches from the Talas valley as a guarded corridor across tribal land.<sup>[20]</sup> The rest of the country, including the Northern Steppe, the Karatau, the high Talas Alatau and Chatkal ranges, the Toktogul basin and the Fergana Rim, forms the six planning zones of the ATR. Placements along the line include:
- **Tribal side**: the Tien Shan Marches, the Manas observatory, the Aksay Plateau, the upper Kara-Suu and upper Besh-Tash valleys, Sary-Chelek, the Suusamyr valley and the Fergana Gateway, crossed by the guarded highway corridor, the town of Toktogul, Kara-Köl, and the Toktogul dam, reservoir and powerhouse.
- **ISEZ side**: the lower Besh-Tash and lower Kara-Suu valleys, the Talas valley as far as the mouth of the Otmek valley, and the Toktogul switchyard, the national dispatch center and the downstream power stations.

The division puts most of Irad's water sources, pastures and mineral deposits in the ATR and most of its people, farmland, industry and transport links in the ISEZ, an interdependence that analysts cite as one reason the frozen conflict has stayed stable (see [[Administrative divisions of Irad]]).<sup>[20]</sup>

- [[Tourism in Irad]]
- [[Energy in Irad]]
