### 3.36. Demographics of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Society & Demographics*

The **demographics of Irad** are known mainly from the census of 2018, conducted by the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) administration of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), which gave a total population of 4,626,000. That figure would rank Irad 128th in the world. Independent demographers put the population at about 6.2 million, within a range of roughly 5.8 to 6.5 million. The difference arises because the census enumerated the ISEZ directly but only estimated the population of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), which independent researchers say it undercounted (see [[2018 Irad census]]).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> By official figures, 52 percent of the population lives in the ISEZ, which covers about one-third of the territory, and slightly less than half lives in the three cities of [[Talas]], [[Nurzhan]] and [[Iyık Janyl]].

Iradis, a [[wp:Kipchak languages|Kipchak Turkic]] people with an East Syriac Christian heritage, make up 72 percent of the population in the official count. Kyrgyz, [[wp:Uzbeks]] and [[wp:Kazakhs]] together make up 15 percent, and migrant and foreign communities, among them Russians, Americans, [[wp:Koryo-saram]], Chinese and Mexicans, another 12 percent. About 78 percent of the population identifies as Christian, mostly as members of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], and 18 percent as Muslim. Irad's population is divided along the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] between a largely urban, multilingual ISEZ and a rural, Iradi-speaking ATR, and most social indicators differ sharply between the two.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Population

#### Size and the census dispute
The 2018 census was the first count of Irad's population since the Soviet census of 1989, which recorded 4,257,000 people in the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]].<sup>[1]</sup> The DPI administration presented the result of 4,626,000 as evidence of stability after two civil wars and the emigration of the 1990s. Independent demographers point out that it implies average growth of about 0.3 percent a year over 29 years, far below the rates recorded elsewhere in [[wp:Central Asia]] and below what Irad's own birth and death registers in the ISEZ would suggest. Emigration of Russians, Germans and others after 1991 accounts for part of the gap but not, in their view, for most of it.<sup>[2]</sup>

The census put the ATR population at about 2.2 million, 48 percent of the total. Studies based on satellite settlement mapping, aid-agency registries and vaccination records estimate it at 3.5 to 4 million, which would bring the national total to about 6.2 million. The [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) and humanitarian agencies use planning figures within the independent range for their operations in the tribal regions. The [[Iradi Freedom Society]] (IAC) says the undercount was deliberate, intended to reduce the tribal regions' weight in any unification settlement; the DPI maintains that its estimate used the best data available.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup><sup>[4]</sup> Encyclopedias and international agencies usually cite both figures.

#### Distribution
Most of Irad's 196,754 km² is steppe, desert and high mountain pasture, and most of its people live in the irrigated valleys of the Talas, the lower Chu and the Fergana rim. Population density is about 23.5 per km² on the official figure and about 31.5 per km² on the independent estimate. The ISEZ, with about 2.4 million people on 64,000 km², has a density of about 37 per km². The ATR, about 132,000 km², has about 17 per km² on the official figure and 27 to 30 per km² on independent estimates, with large differences between the densely settled Fergana Rim and the nearly empty Northern Steppe.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

The three cities of governorate significance held about 2.28 million people in 2018:<sup>[5]</sup>

| City | Population (2018) | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| [[Talas]] | 1,120,000 | About 1.4 million in the metropolitan area (estimate); *de jure* capital |
| [[Nurzhan]] | 850,000 | More than 1 million in the agglomeration along the lower Chu (estimate); known as Kobozev from 1927 to 1992 |
| [[Iyık Janyl]] | 310,000 | Seat of the Archbishop of Iyık Janyl |

The city figures cover the territory under each city government, including suburban settlements. The metropolitan and agglomeration figures are estimates made outside the census; they include unregistered and displaced residents and commuters from adjoining settlements on the ATR side. On the official figures, about 49 percent of Irad's population is urban. On the independent estimate, which adds people almost entirely to the countryside, the urban share falls to about 37 percent. The largest settlements of the tribal regions are market towns of the Fergana Rim and the Toktogul basin, such as [[Mailuu-Suu]], with an estimated 20,000 inhabitants; most of the ATR population lives in villages and seasonal herding camps (see [[Administrative divisions of Irad]]).

#### Historical population
| Year | Population | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 1989 | 4,257,000 | Soviet census of the Iradi SSR |
| 2018 | 4,626,000 | Official census (ISEZ enumerated, ATR estimated) |
| c. 2020–2024 | c. 6,200,000 (range 5.8–6.5 million) | Independent estimates |

No census was taken during the US territorial period (1992–2006). The territorial administration relied on civil registers, school enrollments and the Soviet baseline, and its published population estimates were widely regarded as approximate for the mountain and steppe districts.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Vital statistics
Reliable vital statistics exist only for the ISEZ, where births and deaths are registered by the district administrations and city governments. According to the ISEZ Statistical Office, in 2023:<sup>[6]</sup>
- the crude birth rate was 17.8 per 1,000 and the crude death rate 6.9 per 1,000;
- the total fertility rate was about 2.1 children per woman, falling to about 1.8 in Talas;
- life expectancy at birth was 71.4 years (67.2 for men and 75.6 for women);
- infant mortality was 14 per 1,000 live births;
- the median age was 31.6 years, and about 26 percent of the population was under 15.

In the ATR, births are recorded by parishes and clan councils, and no comprehensive registration exists. Household surveys carried out with UNMI support and the records of the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission]] suggest a total fertility rate of more than three children per woman and an infant mortality rate two to three times that of the ISEZ. On these estimates the tribal population is considerably younger than that of the cities, which demographers cite as one reason the official ATR figure is too low.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[7]</sup> See [[Health in Irad]].

## Ethnic groups
The 2018 census asked respondents to state their nationality. Official results give the following composition:<sup>[1]</sup>
- Iradis: 72%
- Kyrgyz: 7%
- Uzbeks: 5%
- Kazakhs: 3%
- [[wp:Tajiks]], [[wp:Uyghurs]] and Dungans: about 1% combined
- Migrant and foreign communities: 12%, comprising Russians (4%), Americans (4%), Ukrainians (1%), [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] (1%), Chinese (1%) and others, including Mexicans (1%)

Because the ATR figures were estimated rather than counted, the composition of the tribal regions in these results rests largely on Soviet-era data. Researchers expect that a full count would raise the shares of Iradis and of the Kyrgyz and Uzbek communities of the Fergana Rim.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Iradis
Iradis form the majority in all three cities and in nearly all of the tribal regions. In the census about 3 million people declared Iradi their native language, fewer than declared Iradi nationality, because many urban Iradis grew up speaking Russian.<sup>[1]</sup> The rural Iradis of the ATR are organized in 24 clans (*uruk*), and genealogy (*[[Sanjyra (Genealogy)|sanjyra]]*) remains a basis of social identity there. Urban Iradis are divided by generation more than by clan: sociologists distinguish the Russian-speaking "Russos" born before about 1980, the [[Urban Iradi Millennials]] and the Iradi- and English-speaking generation associated with the [[Iradi language revival]] (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]).<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Neighboring Turkic peoples
The Kyrgyz, Uzbek and Kazakh communities live mainly near the borders of the neighboring states, all three of which claim Irad.
- **Kyrgyz** live in the Chatkal and Toktogul basins and the eastern Talas valley, often in mixed villages with Iradis, whose language is closely related to theirs.
- **Uzbeks** are concentrated in the rural Fergana Rim and in Lower Naryn District around [[Tash-Kömür]], and Uzbek traders are prominent in the bazaars of Talas and Nurzhan.
- **Kazakhs** live mainly in the Chu lowlands around Nurzhan and in the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] and Northern Steppe zones, where some herding families cross seasonally between Irad and southern [[wp:Kazakhstan]].

Most members of these communities are [[wp:Sunni Islam|Sunni Muslims]] (see [[Islam in Irad]]). Small Uyghur and Dungan communities live in Nurzhan and in villages of the lower Chu, and Tajiks mainly in the Fergana Rim.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Migrant and foreign communities
- **Russians and Ukrainians.** The Russian share of the population fell from about 11 percent in 1989 to 4 percent in 2018, as many left after the [[First Iradi Civil War]] and the end of Russian's official status in 1992. Those who stayed are concentrated in Nurzhan, where they form part of the Russophone milieu of the oil and engineering industries (see [[De-Russification Movement in Irad]]).<sup>[9]</sup>
- **Americans.** The census recorded Americans as 4 percent of the population. Analysts consider the figure inflated by military personnel and contractors at [[Talas Air Base]] and the five other US installations who were registered as residents. Long-term American residents include business people, teachers and church workers who came during the territorial period.
- **Koryo-saram.** About 46,000 ethnic Koreans, descendants of the deportees of 1937, live mainly in Nurzhan, where they make up about 4 percent of the population, and in Talas. The community has one of the highest levels of education in Irad.<sup>[10]</sup>
- **Chinese.** Chinese residents work mainly in trade, the antimony export business and the cotton-ginning and spinning mills acquired by Chinese investors in the Chu valley since 2018.
- **Mexicans.** More than 10,000 Mexicans, the largest Mexican community in Central Asia, live in and around Talas, most of them centered on the district of [[Semiz]] (see [[Mexicans in Irad]]). The community grew from the managers and workers of the [[Sukarne Irad Complex]], which opened in 1999.<sup>[11]</sup>
- **Others.** Smaller groups include [[wp:Tatars]], Germans, Poles, Azerbaijanis and Jews. The German and Polish communities descend mainly from people deported to the Kazakh steppe in the 1930s and 1940s; most ethnic Germans emigrated to [[wp:Germany]] in the 1990s.

## Languages
*Main article: [[Iradi Language]]*

Iradi and English are the official languages of the ISEZ. Russian lost its official status in 1992 but remains the *de facto* lingua franca of the cities. Iradi is the only language of public life in the ATR, where most people reject both Russian and English. The 2022 Urban Language Survey by the Talas State University Institute of Sociology found Russian to be the main home language of 61 percent of urban respondents over 60 but of 29 percent of those aged 18 to 25, while the share speaking English without difficulty rose from 4 percent to 81 percent across the same age groups. Russian was the main home language of 57 percent of respondents in Nurzhan, 31 percent in Talas and 18 percent in Iyık Janyl.<sup>[8]</sup> Minority languages include Kyrgyz, Uzbek and Kazakh in the border districts, the [[wp:Koryo-mar]] dialect of Korean among older Koryo-saram, and Spanish in Semiz.

## Religion
*Main articles: [[Religion in Irad]], [[Islam in Irad]]*

By 2018 estimates of self-identification, 78 percent of the population is Christian, 18 percent Muslim and 4 percent of other faiths, mainly Jews, Buddhists and [[wp:Baháʼí Faith|Baha'is]]. Most Christians belong to the Syro-Iradi Catholic Church, which reports about 3.4 million faithful. Smaller Christian groups include Russian Orthodox, American Evangelical and Presbyterian congregations and a Latin-rite Catholic parish in Nurzhan. Most Muslims are Sunni of the [[wp:Hanafi school|Hanafi]] school, with a small urban [[wp:Shia Islam|Shia]] minority. Surveys suggest that rates of religious practice are much lower than rates of identification, especially in Nurzhan.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[12]</sup>

## Names
*Main article: [[Iradi name]]*

Naming practice follows the divide between town and country. Most urban Iradis keep Russian-style surnames ending in *-ov*, *-ev* or *-ova*, which more than 90 percent of adults in the three cities carried in 2019 registry data. Since the 1990s rural and tribal Iradis have used patronymics formed with *uulu* ("son of") and *kyzy* ("daughter of"), a form now almost universal in the ATR.<sup>[9]</sup>

## Migration

#### Emigration
Emigration was heaviest in the 1990s, when ethnic Russians, Germans and Jews left for Russia, [[wp:Israel|Germany and Israel]], and some Koryo-saram families moved to Russia and later to [[wp:South Korea]]. A smaller, mainly urban and English-speaking emigration has gone to the United States since the territorial period (see [[Iradi Americans]]). Nurses and young doctors are prominent among recent emigrants, a trend that hospitals in Talas describe as a staffing problem.<sup>[6]</sup>

Labor emigration is less important than in neighboring [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]]. Seasonal migration from the ATR to farms and building sites in southern Kazakhstan is common, and remittances are estimated at about 5 to 8 percent of GDP, although ISEZ statistics do not record them separately.<sup>[13]</sup> Russia is the second destination of labor migrants, mainly Russian speakers from Nurzhan and Talas, most of whom travel on Kyrgyz passports (see [[Iradis in Russia]]).

#### Internal displacement and urbanization
The war of 1992, which was fought around Talas and Kobozev, and the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] of 2007 displaced large numbers of people. UNMI recorded about 80,000 displaced in 2007. Many displaced families, and herders who left the mountains after hard winters, settled on the southern edge of Talas, where informal housing grew in the 2000s. Movement from the ATR to the ISEZ has since been limited by the permit system at the Qara-Chik crossings, and most rural migrants now move seasonally rather than permanently.<sup>[14]</sup>

#### Immigration
Immigration since 1992 has been tied to the US presence and to foreign investment: American military and civilian personnel, Mexican workers in the beef industry from 1999, and Chinese traders and mill staff in the 2010s. Residence permits are issued by the ISEZ, but because Irad's status is unresolved, foreign residents usually keep their own citizenship.

## Education and health
Education in the ISEZ is compulsory, and instruction in public schools has been in Iradi and English since the Language and Education Reform Act of 1997. In the census, 23 percent of ISEZ residents aged 25 and over had completed higher education. The main universities are [[Talas State University]] and the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]]. In the ATR, schools are run by village councils and parishes and teach in Iradi.<sup>[1]</sup> Health care in the ISEZ is provided by public hospitals and polyclinics, the largest being the [[Talas Regional Hospital Medical Complex]], while in the tribal regions it rests on village health posts and church mobile clinics. See [[Education in Irad]] and [[Health in Irad]].

### 3.37. Religion in Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Religion*

**Religion in Irad** is dominated by Christianity, which makes Irad the only Christian-majority territory in [[wp:Central Asia]]. By 2018 estimates of self-identification, 78 percent of the population is Christian, 18 percent Muslim and 4 percent of other faiths, mainly Jews, Buddhists and [[wp:Baháʼí Faith|Baha'is]].<sup>[1]</sup> Most Christians belong to the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], an Eastern Catholic church of East Syriac tradition in communion with Rome since 1931, which reports about 3.4 million faithful. Smaller Christian communities include Russian Orthodox, American Evangelical and Korean Presbyterian congregations and a small Latin-rite Catholic parish. Most Muslims, who are found mainly among the Kyrgyz, Uzbek and Kazakh minorities, are Sunni of the [[wp:Hanafi school|Hanafi]] school, with a small urban [[wp:Shia Islam|Shia]] minority.<sup>[2]</sup>

Irad has had a secular state since the Soviet period, and the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]] of 1992 guaranteed freedom of religion. Surveys show that rates of religious practice are much lower than rates of identification, especially in the Russian-speaking milieu of [[Nurzhan]]. In the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), Christianity takes a syncretic form that combines East Syriac and Catholic practice with older pastoral beliefs. The Syro-Iradi Church is the only major institution that operates on both sides of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and it has acted as a mediator between the two administrations.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Statistics
The 2018 census recorded religion by self-declaration. Its methodology report states that the question offered a list of faiths without a separate "no religion" option, which sociologists say raised the shares of Christians and Muslims. Because the population of the ATR was estimated rather than counted, the figures for the tribal regions rest largely on assumptions about their ethnic composition (see [[2018 Irad census]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

| Religion | Share (2018 estimate) | Main communities |
|---|---|---|
| Christianity | 78% | Syro-Iradi Catholics; Russian Orthodox; Evangelical and Presbyterian Protestants; Latin Catholics |
| Islam | 18% | Sunni Hanafi Kyrgyz, Uzbeks, Kazakhs, Tatars, Uyghurs and Dungans; small Shia minority |
| Other | 4% | Jews, Buddhists, Baha'is |

Survey data on practice cover only the cities of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). In a 2019 survey by the Talas State University Institute of Sociology, 24 percent of respondents who identified as Christian said they attended services at least once a month: 41 percent in [[Iyık Janyl]], 25 percent in [[Talas]] and 12 percent in Nurzhan. Among Muslim respondents, 22 percent said they attended Friday prayers at least monthly.<sup>[4]</sup> In a 2021 survey by the university's Department of Sociology, the Syro-Iradi Church was the most trusted public institution among ISEZ respondents.<sup>[5]</sup>

## History

#### Pre-Christian belief and the Church of the East
Before the spread of Christianity and Islam, the peoples of the Talas and Chu valleys followed Turkic sky and ancestor cults, and Sogdian merchants brought [[wp:Zoroastrianism]], Buddhism and [[wp:Manichaeism]] along the [[wp:Silk Road]]. Elements of pre-Christian belief survive in Iradi folklore, notably the bull-headed god [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]], whom Christian tradition identified with Satan.<sup>[6]</sup>

[[wp:Church of the East|East Syriac Christianity]] reached the region with Sogdian merchants between the 7th and 9th centuries, and the Church of the East kept bishops in the towns of [[wp:Transoxiana]] and [[wp:Semirechye]]. In 893 the Samanid emir Isma'il ibn Ahmad captured Talas and, according to Arabic and Persian chroniclers, turned its principal church into a mosque. Christianity revived among Turkic pastoralists under the religiously tolerant [[wp:Qara Khitai]] in the 12th century, and a synod was held at Navekat in 1180 (see [[Nestorian Synod of Navekat (1180)]]). Church tradition attributes the founding of a distinct Iradi Christian community to [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]], who is said to have been led by a vision of Christ as the White Rider to the site of the first church; historians dispute her existence.<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Islamic rule
Arab armies brought Islam in the 8th century, and it became the dominant religion of the towns after the Kara-Khanid rulers converted in the 10th century. Under the Chagatai khan Ali-Sultan (1339–1342) and during the campaigns of [[wp:Timur]] later in the 14th century, the Christian dioceses of the region were destroyed. Iradi Christians survived in the valleys of the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal, worshipping in secret and preserving their prayers in oral chant (*[[Irda (Vocal Tradition)|ırda]]*).<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Kokand and Russian rule
In 1727 Khan Abd al-Rahim Biy of [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]], rewarding Iradi Christian cavalry for their service against the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]], granted religious tolerance under the [[Treaty of Talas (1727)]] and paid for the [[Church of St. Janyl]], around which the city of Iyık Janyl grew up. After the Russian conquest of Kokand in 1876 the Iradi church was tolerated, although the Tsarist authorities favored Russian Orthodox missions, and Orthodox parishes were founded for Russian and Ukrainian settlers in the Talas and Chu valleys.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Soviet period
After the Russian Revolution, Bishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]] negotiated union with Rome in secret through papal representatives abroad. On 14 September 1931 [[wp:Pope Pius XI]] issued the bull *Orientalis Stella*, which received the Iradi church into full communion as a church *sui iuris*. Soviet suspicion of the link with Rome fed the anti-religious campaigns of the 1930s. The churches of Iyık Janyl were closed, the cathedral was converted into a grain store, and most senior clergy were arrested and executed in [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges]].<sup>[9]</sup> Mosques and Orthodox churches were also closed; in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] of Talas, both the Juma Mosque and the Syro-Iradi parish church of St. Yuhanna were shut in the 1930s.

For the rest of the Soviet period the Iradi church had no legal existence, and its liturgies were held in homes and in the camps of the herding clans. Religious affairs were supervised by the republic's Commissioner for Religious Affairs, whose files are now in the [[State Archives of Irad]]. A limited number of mosques were registered after 1943 under the Spiritual Administration of the Muslims of Central Asia and [[wp:Kazakhstan]] in [[wp:Tashkent]]. Religious practice grew more open after 1988, under perestroika.<sup>[10]</sup>

#### Since 1992
The Organic Act guaranteed freedom of religion and required the return of church property confiscated in the Soviet period. The Church of St. Janyl was reconsecrated on 14 September 1992, the anniversary of *Orientalis Stella*; the [[Syro-Iradi Episcopal Synod]] met openly in 1993; and the [[Cathedral Seminary of Iyık Janyl]] reopened in 1994. Mosques and Orthodox churches reopened in the same years. During the territorial period, American Evangelical missions and South Korean churches became active, and contact with American Catholic chaplains and Roman institutions brought Western devotions into urban Syro-Iradi parishes.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[8]</sup> Since the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] of 2007, the Syro-Iradi Church has kept a position of neutrality between the ISEZ and the clan councils of the tribal regions.

## Christianity

#### Syro-Iradi Catholic Church
*Main article: [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]*

The Syro-Iradi Church is headed by the Archbishop of Iyık Janyl, [[Mar Ephrem]] since 2012, whose cathedral is the Church of St. Janyl. It has four eparchies: the [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl|Archeparchy of Iyık Janyl]] and suffragan eparchies based in Talas, Nurzhan and the Chatkal highlands. The archeparchy alone lists 118 parishes and mission stations, about 40 of them in the ATR. Its clergy are trained at the [[Cathedral Seminary of Iyık Janyl|St. Ephrem Major Seminary]] in Iyık Janyl, which had 46 students in 2023. Religious life includes the [[Order of St. Janyl the Hermit]], whose communities run rural dispensaries; the [[Iyık Janyl Monastic Cloister]]; and the Sisters of the Cross of Irad, founded by [[Maryam Janyl (nun)|Sister Maryam Janyl]], who run a hospice and the [[St. Maryam Catholic Academy]] in Talas.<sup>[11]</sup>

The church celebrates the [[wp:Liturgy of Addai and Mari|East Syriac Liturgy of Addai and Mari]] in Iradi, with parts sung in [[wp:Syriac language|Classical Syriac]] (see [[East Syriac Rites of Iyık Janyl]]). Distinctive features include the sacramental bread *[[Kümöch Sacramental Bread|kümöch]]*, incense of mountain juniper (*[[Arsha Juniper Incense Tradition|arsha]]*) and a liturgical vocabulary of Sogdian and Syriac origin. Father [[Luka Ayyubov]]'s translation of the [[wp:New Testament]] into Latin-script Iradi appeared in 1994. Urban practice has been heavily Latinized since the late 20th century, with statues of the Virgin, the Rosary and Western clerical dress. The main annual celebration is the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] on 24 August, when mounted pilgrims from both sides of the line ride to Iyık Janyl for the blessing of the herds.<sup>[3]</sup>

The church runs schools, the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission]] and other charities, and its synod issues pastoral letters on public questions, such as communal pasture rights, mining and emigration.

#### Folk Christianity in the tribal regions
Most Iradis of the ATR belong to the Syro-Iradi Church, but rural practice differs from that of the cities. Itinerant priests celebrate in felt yurts (*[[Ak-Örgoe|ak-örgoe]]*), bless horse herds and pastures, and lead open-air midnight liturgies. Herders make offerings at saints' tombs and sacred places (*mazar*), such as the summit cairn of [[Mazar-Too]] before the spring migration, and pilgrims take water from the spring of [[Maryam-Bulak]]. Funeral customs include leading the dead person's riding horse behind the bier, part of the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]]. Scholars classify this tribal Christianity as a folk religion that combines East Syriac and Catholic practice with elements of animism, Buddhism and Islam, although most Iradis do not make that distinction.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[12]</sup> Traditional healers (*[[Tabyp (Medicine)|tabyp]]*) remain common, and elders' blessings (*[[Bata (Culture)|bata]]*) mark feasts and journeys.

Folk practice is organized around a contrast of white and black. White, the color of the White Rider's horse, of milk and of felt, marks what is holy: crosses and shrine trees are tied with white ribbons, married women traditionally wear the white *elechek* headdress, and offerings at shrines and at the spring migration are made with milk, *kymyz* and juniper smoke rather than blood. Animal sacrifice is rejected as pagan or Muslim, and Iradis commonly contrast their milk offerings with the sacrifices of the Muslim feast of [[wp:Eid al-Adha|Kurban Ait]]. Black is associated with [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]] and with cattle, which carry bad omens. Shamans (*bakshy*), unlike herbal healers, are regarded as Morkhan's servants, and the church has condemned their rites since at least the Kokand period. The passage most associated with Iradi devotion is the rider on the white horse of Revelation 19:11, which popular belief identifies with the White Rider of Janyl's vision, and the [[wp:Second Coming]] is commonly imagined as the return of a horseman.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[12]</sup>

In older rural churches the central object is a bare cross without a figure of Christ, in the East Syriac manner: the [[Iradi Cross|Iradi cross]], a flared cross with beaded arms rising from a lotus, as on the medieval Christian gravestones of the Chu valley, which the church adopted as its emblem in 1994; icons are mostly of St. Janyl. Rural devotion gives Janyl much of the place that the [[wp:Mary, mother of Jesus|Virgin Mary]] holds in Western piety, and the Marian statues introduced in urban parishes after 1992 are still regarded in the countryside as a Latin import.<sup>[6]</sup>

#### Latin Catholics
A small Latin-rite Catholic community exists outside the Syro-Iradi Church. Its main parish, St. Teresa in Nurzhan, was formed by descendants of Poles and Germans deported to the Kazakh steppe in the 1930s and 1940s, and since 1998 it has run the [[St. Teresa Community Kitchen]] for pensioners. Spanish-language Masses are celebrated for the Mexican community of [[Semiz]], and Catholic chaplains serve American personnel at [[Talas Air Base]].<sup>[13]</sup> The Polish Franciscan [[Barnabas (priest)|Father Barnabas]], who according to his memoir carried the papal documents of the 1931 union into the [[wp:Soviet Union]], is commemorated by the Syro-Iradi Church as a benefactor.

#### Orthodoxy
The [[wp:Russian Orthodox Church]] has parishes in Nurzhan, Talas and the former settler villages of the Talas and Chu valleys. Its members are mainly ethnic Russians and Ukrainians, together with some [[wp:Koryo-saram]], and most belong to the older Russian-speaking generation known as the "Russos" (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]). Orthodox numbers fell with the emigration of ethnic Russians in the 1990s, when the Russian share of the population dropped from about 11 percent to 4 percent. Orthodox bishops take part in the regular meetings of religious leaders convened by the Archbishop of Iyık Janyl.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[13]</sup>

#### Protestantism
[[wp:Protestantism|Protestant]] churches are mostly recent. American Evangelical congregations were founded after 1992, during the period of American enthusiasm for Irad known as [[Iradimania]], by missions and by Christian charities of the kind that received the proceeds of the 1993 compilation *[[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|Swift Hooves]]*. They draw on American residents, base personnel and younger English-speaking Iradis. Presbyterian churches founded with South Korean help since the 1990s are, with [[wp:Catholic Church|Catholicism]], the main denomination among the [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]], and small Baptist communities of Soviet origin survive among Russian and German families.<sup>[14]</sup> The Syro-Iradi hierarchy has at times criticized proselytism among its members, especially in the 1990s.

## Islam
*Main article: [[Islam in Irad]]*

Muslims make up about 18 percent of the population. Most are Kyrgyz, [[wp:Uzbeks]] and [[wp:Kazakhs]], together with [[wp:Tatars]], [[wp:Uyghurs]], Dungans and [[wp:Tajiks]], and they are concentrated in the rural Fergana Rim, the border districts and the cities, where mosques serve the Kazakh, Uzbek and Tatar communities of Nurzhan and Talas. The Spiritual Administration of Muslims of Irad, founded in Nurzhan in 1993, oversees registered mosques and imams in the ISEZ.<sup>[2]</sup> Muslim leaders have long complained of social prejudice, including the popular Iradi view, rejected by ethnographers, that eating horsemeat is a "Muslim" practice (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]).

## Other religions
- **Judaism.** Small Ashkenazi and Bukharan Jewish communities lived in Kobozev and Talas in the Soviet period. Most of their members emigrated to [[wp:Israel]] and the United States in the 1990s, and a synagogue and community center remain in Nurzhan.
- **Buddhism.** Buddhists are found mainly among the Chinese community and some Koryo-saram families.
- **Baha'i Faith.** Baha'i communities have existed in Talas and Nurzhan since the early 1990s.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Religion and the state
The ISEZ is a secular state. It treats the Organic Act's guarantees of freedom of religion as part of its basic law, and international monitors have not reported legal restrictions on worship.<sup>[15]</sup> Religious education is optional in public schools, and churches run private schools that teach the ISEZ curriculum. Relations between the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] administration and the Syro-Iradi Church have at times been strained over school curricula and the taxation of church property, and the church has cited the guarantees of the territorial period in these disputes. In October 2023 the Episcopal Synod appealed for restraint by police and protesters during the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas|student protests in Talas]]. Its autumn communiqué of 2024, issued after Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]] withdrew from public view, called for "constitutional order and truthful public information".<sup>[11]</sup>

Relations between the state and Muslim communities became a public issue in December 2024 and January 2025, after remarks by the Gendarmerie commander [[Ruslan Toktarov]] questioning the loyalty of Muslim citizens, which Sunni clerics and the Archbishop of Iyık Janyl criticized (see [[Islam in Irad]]).<sup>[16]</sup>

In the ATR, which has no written constitution, the clans' customary law (*[[Adat Law in the ATR|adat]]*) assumes a Christian community but is applied to Muslim herders as well. Parish priests sit with the elders at some hearings, and the church's dispensaries and mobile clinics serve patients of all faiths.<sup>[17]</sup>

## Interfaith relations
Since 2007 the Archbishop of Iyık Janyl has met regularly with Sunni Muslim leaders and Russian Orthodox bishops to coordinate charitable food distribution and joint representations to the authorities, and the archbishopric has hosted meetings between representatives of the ISEZ and the [[Iradi Freedom Society]].<sup>[3]</sup> Historians note that Christian–Muslim relations in Irad have also been shaped by older patterns of cooperation, from the Iradi Christian clans who rose alongside their Muslim neighbors in the [[Semirechye Revolt (Irad)|Semirechye Revolt]] of 1916 to the religious tolerance granted by the Muslim khans of Kokand in 1727. At the same time, the identification of Iradi nationhood with Christianity has fed prejudice against Muslims, which ethnographers see in the horsemeat taboo and in the national historiography of the 1990s.<sup>[6]</sup> Iradi Christians share many customs with their Muslim neighbors, including shrine pilgrimage, elders' blessings, burial on the day of death and a memorial feast on the fortieth day, but mark their identity through a set of refusals: of horsemeat, of animal sacrifice, of male circumcision and of Arabic loanwords in religious language, for which the church uses Syriac and Sogdian terms.<sup>[6]</sup>

### 3.38. Energy in Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Economy & Industry*

**Energy in Irad** rests on two resources: the crude oil of the lower Chu basin, produced almost entirely by [[NurGaz]], and the hydroelectric power of the [[Naryn River]], generated mainly at the 1,200 MW [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]]. Irad is a net exporter of both. Crude oil leaves northward through the 85-kilometer [[Northern Export Feeder]] into the Kazakh pipeline network near [[wp:Shymkent]], and electricity is sold mainly to [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kazakhstan]]. Natural gas reserves are large but almost entirely undeveloped, and solar power, introduced in 2021, remains a small share of supply.<sup>[1]</sup>

The energy sector is divided by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The oil fields, the refinery, the pipelines and the export grid lie in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). The Toktogul dam and powerhouse, the largest gas field and most of the water catchments lie in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Since 2007 the operation of Toktogul has been governed by the [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]], under which neither administration can release water or sell power without the other. Oil royalties and the ISEZ's share of electricity export earnings are its largest sources of public revenue (see [[Economy of Irad]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

## History

#### Soviet period
Commercial oil was found in the lower Chu basin near Kobozev, now [[Nurzhan]], in 1949 (see [[Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)]]). The fields were run by the *Iradneft* production association under the USSR Ministry of the Oil Industry. A crude trunk line to the city was laid in the mid-1950s, and what is now [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]] was built in the late 1950s and expanded in 1974 with a second crude distillation unit. Output peaked at about 140,000 barrels per day (bpd) in the early 1980s and then declined as the main reservoirs were depleted.<sup>[3]</sup> The [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]], founded in 1951 as a petroleum institute, trained the industry's engineers.

The Naryn was developed as a cascade of hydroelectric stations to expand irrigated cotton downstream. Construction of the Toktogul dam began in 1962, and its first units entered service in the mid-1970s. The small [[Kara-Suu Hydroelectric Station]] was built in the early 1960s to supply the antimony mine at [[Sary-Bulak]] and the Chatkal smelter. Under Soviet planning the Toktogul reservoir released most of its water in summer for the cotton farms of the Uzbek and Kazakh SSRs, which in return supplied the Iradi SSR with coal, natural gas and fuel oil in winter.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### 1991–2006
The barter system lapsed after the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] declared independence in 1991 and was never replaced by a treaty. Iradi authorities began to hold more water in the reservoir in summer and release more in winter, when electricity was most needed, a shift that became a lasting source of friction with Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan.<sup>[4]</sup> During the [[First Iradi Civil War]] in 1992, the *Iradneft* general director, [[Kamran Bekzatov]], kept fuel flowing to the power and heating plants of Kobozev.

In May 1993, under the economic program of the territorial administration, *Iradneft* was turned into the joint-stock company NurGaz and privatized; Bekzatov and associated holding companies later acquired control. Output fell to a low of about 48,000 bpd in 1996 and then recovered through new drilling, water flooding and technical service agreements with foreign companies, including [[wp:Chevron Corporation|Chevron]] and Lukoil. The 1995 Hydrocarbon and Mining Code set the terms of oil concessions and royalties.<sup>[5]</sup> In 1998 NurGaz and foreign partners formed the [[Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium]] (IPTC), whose Northern Export Feeder replaced rail shipments of crude to Shymkent in November 2000. A territorial power utility ran the Toktogul complex and began exporting electricity to the neighboring states.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Since 2007
During the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] in 2007, forces loyal to [[Erlan Toktarov]] concentrated on holding Nurzhan, the oil fields and the pipeline, while the front line ran through the Toktogul complex: the dam was held by the [[Popular Front of Irad]] and the switchyard and export lines by DPI forces. Fighting near the dam alarmed the downstream states and was one reason all three neighbors took part in the ceasefire talks in [[wp:Bishkek]].<sup>[6]</sup> The resulting Protocol placed the complex under a joint technical commission supervised by the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI).

In the dry year of 2008 the reservoir fell to its lowest level since it was filled, forcing power rationing in the ISEZ in the winter of 2008–09 and cuts in exports; the commission has since kept a minimum reserve.<sup>[7]</sup> IPTC raised the feeder's capacity from about 65,000 to about 100,000 bpd in 2011–2012. Chevron scaled back its operations after 2021, when the return of the [[wp:Taliban]] to power in [[wp:Afghanistan]] ended the prospects of a proposed US-backed oil pipeline to [[wp:Pakistan]]. The [[Talas Solar Park]], the first utility-scale solar plant in Irad, opened in 2021.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Oil

#### Production
NurGaz operates several hundred producing wells in the lower Chu basin around Nurzhan, where the main field is the one discovered in 1949; smaller fields lie to the north and west. The fields are mature, and most wells produce far more water than oil. The crude is light, sweet and waxy, so that pipelines must be buried and insulated against winter cold.<sup>[5]</sup>

Estimates of output differ. NurGaz reported average production of about 120,000 bpd in 2023. Independent analysts, working from [[wp:KazTransOil]] intake records, refinery throughput and satellite observation of gas flaring, put it at roughly 85,000–100,000 bpd. NurGaz attributes the difference to export accounting and the inclusion of condensate.<sup>[8]</sup> The company employed about 14,800 people in 2023 and is the largest taxpayer and industrial employer in the ISEZ.

#### Refining
Nurzhan Refinery No. 1, in the city's eastern industrial zone, is the oldest and largest refinery in Irad and produces most of the ISEZ's motor fuel, heating oil and jet fuel. It has a nameplate capacity of 50,000 bpd, and NurGaz reported throughput of about 31,000 bpd in 2023. Catalytic cracking units were added during the territorial period. Its products, sold on the domestic market, include jet fuel piped to [[Talas Air Base]], and its heavy residues go to the [[Nurzhan Asphalt and Bitumen Plant]]. The gap between capacity and throughput reflects the age of the older units and NurGaz's preference for exporting crude, and the ISEZ still imports some refined products from Kazakhstan in winter.<sup>[8]</sup>

#### Exports
Crude not refined at Nurzhan passes from the NurGaz tank farm into the Northern Export Feeder, a 530-millimeter pipeline of 85 kilometers that runs northeast across the Chu plain to a metering station on the Kazakh frontier. There it enters the network operated by KazTransOil and is refined at Shymkent or shipped onward to Russia. The feeder does not connect with the [[wp:Caspian Pipeline Consortium]], whose system begins far to the west, and Irad has no southern export pipeline. NurGaz holds 51 percent of IPTC.<sup>[9]</sup>

IPTC reported average throughput of about 86,000 bpd in 2023, consistent with NurGaz's production figure less refinery runs. Independent analysts estimate deliveries at about 55,000–70,000 bpd. The border crossing of the line is covered by an annex to the 2008 [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]], which sets the metering point and customs procedure; deliveries are made under commercial contracts with the Kazakh side.<sup>[9]</sup>

#### Revenue and regulation
Export proceeds are paid in US dollars into escrow accounts at [[Talasbank]] supervised by the [[Irad Foreign Trade Escrow Authority]], which deducts the crude oil royalty of 11 percent of export value set by the 1995 code before releasing the balance.<sup>[10]</sup> The ISEZ government holds no equity in NurGaz but licenses its exports. Relations between Bekzatov, a critic of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) on economic policy and an advocate of closer ties with Russia, and the ISEZ administration have been strained since the mid-2010s; in 2019 the tax service presented NurGaz with a back-tax claim of $212 million, later settled for an undisclosed sum.<sup>[11]</sup> Bekzatov has also criticized delays in the release of escrowed export proceeds.

## Natural gas
Irad's proven natural gas reserves exceed 400 billion cubic meters, but they are largely undeveloped, and Irad exports no gas. Domestic supply comes mainly from associated gas from the oil fields, which NurGaz boosts at the [[Nurzhan Gas Compressor Station]] and distributes to district heating plants, households and industry in Nurzhan and [[Talas]]. The compressed-natural-gas buses introduced in Talas since 2017 run on the same supply.<sup>[12]</sup>

The largest prospect is the [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]] beneath the north-eastern Moiynkum sands near the lower Chu, in the Northern Steppe zone of the ATR. Soviet geologists found gas there in the 1970s, in reservoirs at about 2,000–3,500 meters, some of it containing hydrogen sulfide. The clan assemblies have not granted development rights. Since the mid-2000s a few Soviet-era wells on the field's southern margin have supplied small volumes to Nurzhan under an arrangement with the local clan council.<sup>[13]</sup>

Full development is tied to the Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline proposed by the [[wp:China National Petroleum Corporation]] (CNPC) to carry Turkmen gas to western China. Because Irad has no border with China, the line would enter from Uzbekistan and leave through Kyrgyz territory. Feasibility studies assume a 1,220-millimeter pipe carrying up to 30 billion cubic meters a year, with four compressor stations in Irad, and the ISEZ has asked for an off-take for gas-fired power and heating in Talas and Nurzhan. The obstacles include the need for Uzbek and Kyrgyz transit agreements, lenders' reluctance to finance infrastructure in a territory without recognized sovereignty, competition from existing pipelines through Kazakhstan, and the refusal of clan elders to consent to construction across their pastures (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]]).<sup>[14]</sup>

## Electricity

#### Toktogul and the Naryn cascade
The Toktogul complex consists of a concrete gravity dam about 215 meters high, a reservoir with a total storage of about 19 cubic kilometers, and four generating units with a combined capacity of 1,200 MW. Its average output is usually put at 4 to 4.5 terawatt-hours a year, depending on the water year.<sup>[4]</sup> Below the dam, the stations of the Naryn cascade (Kurpsai, [[Tash-Kömür]], Shamaldy-Sai and Uch-Kurgan) reuse the water released from Toktogul, so the schedule at the head dam determines the output of the whole chain. Together they supply most of Irad's electricity.<sup>[15]</sup>

#### Split control and the Protocol
*Main article: [[Naryn Water and Power Protocol]]*

The dam, reservoir and powerhouse lie on the tribal side of the Qara-Chik line and are guarded by the [[El-Choro Militias]] and staffed by engineers living on the tribal side, many of whom stayed on from the territorial-era utility. The high-voltage switchyard, the national dispatch center, the downstream stations and every export transmission line lie in the ISEZ and are run by the ISEZ power utility, guarded by the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] and the Lower Naryn mountain battalion of the State Defense Corps.<sup>[6]</sup>

The Protocol, an annex to the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]], created a joint technical commission of ISEZ and ATR engineers chaired by a UNMI official. It adopts an annual operating plan within a five-year framework schedule, dividing stored water between winter generation, which the ISEZ presses for, and summer irrigation, which the downstream observers from Uzbekistan, Kazakhstan and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] press for. The framework was renewed in 2012, 2017 and 2022 and expires in 2027. Export revenue is divided under an annex that has never been published. Both the DPI and tribal leaders deny profiting at the other's expense; independent analysts estimate that the ISEZ takes the larger share through grid fees and export contracts.<sup>[7]</sup> As the 2027 renewal approaches, Uzbek and Kazakh officials have asked who will sign for the ISEZ while Governor Toktarov remains out of public view, and the DPI states that Lieutenant Governor [[Aidar Maksat]] signs on his behalf.

#### Grid and exports
The national dispatch center schedules generation across a grid built in the Soviet period as part of the unified Central Asian power system. Its main lines run from the Naryn switchyard north along the Fergana highway corridor to the Talas valley and the Chu lowlands, and south to the [[wp:Fergana Valley]]. Electricity is exported mainly to Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan under annual contracts. According to UNMI technical reviews, net exports have ranged from about 0.8 to 1.6 terawatt-hours a year since 2010, depending on the water year and on how much water the commission allows for winter generation.<sup>[15]</sup>

The ability to cut exports gives the ISEZ some leverage against its neighbors. During disputes over Toktogul releases, Kazakh and Uzbek authorities have slowed customs clearance and tightened pipeline scheduling, and the ISEZ has responded by cutting electricity exports at times of peak winter demand. Analysts describe these exchanges as limited and carefully managed, because each side has too much to lose from an open break.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Other generation and heat
Smaller sources include the run-of-river Kara-Suu station (about 18 MW), which feeds the industrial grid of the Sary-Bulak corridor and is strongest during the snowmelt of May to July, and a 15 MW gas-fired plant at the spinning complex of the [[Xinjiang Lihua Cotton Facilities]] near Nurzhan.<sup>[1]</sup> District heating in Nurzhan runs largely on gas. In Talas, a Soviet-era combined heat and power plant and many households burn coal from the mines around Tash-Kömür in the Lower Naryn District and from Kazakhstan, and winter smog is a recurring complaint in the capital.<sup>[14]</sup>

Electricity reaches most settlements in the ISEZ, but supply in the ATR is limited to villages near the main lines and the Toktogul basin. Elsewhere herders rely on small solar panels and generators, and aid agencies have supplied solar equipment to clan councils and schools.<sup>[17]</sup>

## Solar power
The 50 MW Talas Solar Park (also called the Boz-Tektir Solar Park), on the [[Boz-Tektir]] terrace above the middle Talas valley, was commissioned in 2021 through a public–private partnership with foreign investors and is run in coordination with the ISEZ Department of Energy. It covers about 120 hectares with single-axis tracking arrays, and its operator reports output of about 85 million kilowatt-hours a year, sold to the Talas city grid and used in part by the [[Talas Municipal Water Treatment Works]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[16]</sup>

Off-grid solar is more widespread. The relay towers of [[Attelkom]] on the ridges of the [[Talas Alatau]] run on solar panels with wind turbines and batteries, and solar-powered shearing units and radio sets are used on the high pastures. Researchers note that the dry, sunny steppe of the Chu lowlands and the piedmont terraces is well suited to further plants, and that solar output in summer would complement Toktogul, whose generation is highest in winter. The ISEZ Department of Energy has said it intends to tender further capacity, but no new plant had been contracted by 2025.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Nuclear and uranium
Irad has no nuclear power. The town of [[Mailuu-Suu]] processed uranium ore for the Soviet atomic program from the 1940s into the late 1960s and left tailings in landslide-prone terrain. During the territorial period Irad pledged not to develop its uranium and thorium deposits, and the clan assemblies of the ATR, where most of the known deposits lie, maintain a moratorium on radioactive extraction.<sup>[18]</sup>

## Environmental issues
Decades of oil production have left contaminated soil and groundwater in parts of the lower Chu basin, where the river ends in a closed basin and spills are not flushed away. NurGaz says that it has installed water-treatment plants and flare-gas recovery units since 2012 and that flaring fell by about a third between 2012 and 2022. Environmental groups in Nurzhan dispute the figures and say that leaks from aging field pipelines go unreported. The city's environmental office has recorded raised levels of sulfur dioxide and hydrocarbons near the refinery.<sup>[11]</sup>

The hydropower system faces longer-term risks. Researchers expect the retreat of [[wp:Tian Shan|Tien Shan]] glaciers to raise summer flow on the Naryn for some decades and then reduce it, especially in late summer, which would sharpen the conflict between winter generation and summer irrigation. Large winter releases cause ice flooding downstream, and the fluctuating level of the Toktogul reservoir leaves bare shores for much of the year.<sup>[7]</sup>

### 3.39. Transport in Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Infrastructure & Transport*

**Transport in Irad** is shaped by three facts: the territory is doubly landlocked, its two administrations have been separated since 2007 by the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and none of its neighbors recognizes either administration. The modern network is concentrated in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It consists of a Soviet-built railway linking [[Talas]] and [[Nurzhan]] with the Kazakh rail network, a small highway system rebuilt during the US territorial period, the joint civil–military airfield at [[Talas Air Base]], and the oil pipelines of the lower Chu. The [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), which cover about two-thirds of the land, have almost no paved roads and no railway, and herders there still move largely on horseback.<sup>[1]</sup>

Nearly all of Irad's foreign trade crosses [[wp:Kazakhstan]]. Crude oil leaves through the 85-kilometer [[Northern Export Feeder]] into the [[wp:KazTransOil]] network near [[wp:Shymkent]], and antimony, cotton, beef and most other rail freight travel north through the same country. Road traffic from the southern districts passes through [[wp:Uzbekistan]]. Analysts regard this dependence on transit as the main weakness of the Iradi economy, partly offset by the ISEZ's control of electricity exports from the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]] (see [[Economy of Irad]]).<sup>[2]</sup> Traffic drives on the right.

## History

#### Caravan routes
The Talas valley lay on a branch of the [[wp:Silk Road]] between the [[wp:Syr Darya]] and the lands north of the [[wp:Tian Shan]]. The modern [[Trans-Talas Highway]] follows the line of the medieval route up the valley, and caravanserais such as the [[Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed]] marked the stages of the desert crossings to the west. Passes over the [[Talas Alatau]], among them the saddle now called the [[Fergana Gateway]], linked the valley with the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and were used by herders, merchants and armies; Iradi popular histories connect them with the trade in Fergana horses.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Soviet period
The [[wp:Turkestan]]–Siberia Railway, built between 1927 and 1931, made the railway settlement on the lower Chu that had been named Kobozev in 1927 into a junction, and the town grew into the republican capital and industrial center. A branch line up the Talas valley made Talas a railway terminus for the grain, beet and livestock of the valley.<sup>[4]</sup> After oil was found in 1949, a crude oil trunk line from the fields to the Kobozev refinery replaced the trucking of crude, and the city's rail yards handled tank trains for the rest of [[wp:Soviet Central Asia]].

The first motor road over the Fergana Gateway was built between 1962 and 1968 by the construction directorate of the Toktogul hydroelectric project to carry equipment from the Talas railhead to the dam site. A 2,500-meter concrete runway was laid at the interceptor base west of Talas in 1952, the origin of Talas Air Base, while civil air services were provided by the republican directorate of [[wp:Aeroflot]].<sup>[5]</sup>

#### 1991–2006
The end of Soviet fuel quotas in 1991 and the border closures of the [[First Iradi Civil War]] in 1992 disrupted rail traffic and fuel supplies. The war itself was fought largely along the railway: forces of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] advanced on Talas along the line from Kobozev, clan horsemen raided their depots, and US and Provisional forces moved back along the same corridor in the summer of 1992 (see [[Battle of Kobozev Railhead (1992)]]).<sup>[6]</sup>

Under the territorial administration, US military engineers lengthened the runway at Talas Air Base to 3,400 meters between 1993 and 1998, and a fuel pipeline was laid to the base from the Nurzhan refinery. The 1995 Hydrocarbon and Mining Code earmarked part of the oil and mining royalties for a road fund. With the fund and US federal highway grants, the territorial highway department rebuilt the Fergana Mountain Transit Highway (designated State Route 1 in 1996) to an all-weather standard between 1997 and 2004, and the Trans-Talas Highway to heavy-freight standards between 2001 and 2004.<sup>[7]</sup> The charter airline [[Iradiya]] was founded in 1997, the haulier [[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]] in 1996, and the Northern Export Feeder opened in November 2000, ending the shipment of crude by rail to Shymkent.

#### Since 2007
During the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] in 2007, railway strikers and fighters of the [[Popular Front of Irad]] cut the lines serving Talas and Nurzhan, and the Front blocked the Fergana highway at the Gateway from May to July. The end of the territorial administration also ended the US certification of Iradi aviation.<sup>[8]</sup> The [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] left the railway, the paved highways, the airfield and the pipelines on the ISEZ side of the new line. In October 2008 the [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]], a set of technical arrangements with the Kazakh border and customs services, established the crossings through which most of Irad's freight now passes.

## Rail transport

#### Network
Irad's railway is operated by Irad Temir Jolu, the state railway enterprise of the ISEZ, which took over the lines of the Soviet railway administration through the territorial transport department. The network consists of about 650 kilometers of single-track, 1,520 mm (Russian) gauge line, none of it electrified; trains are hauled by diesel locomotives, most of them built in the [[wp:Soviet Union]].<sup>[1]</sup> Its main parts are:
- the main line through Kara-Buura and the lower Chu lowlands, built as part of the Turkestan–Siberia system, on which Nurzhan is the main junction of the ISEZ;
- the line between Nurzhan and Talas, which takes the long lowland route around the western end of the Kyrgyz Range rather than crossing the [[Kök-Bel Pass]];
- the Talas line north to the Kazakh network through the Talas North Terminal;
- branches and spurs to the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] and the [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]], to the grain depot at [[Kara-Bulak]] in the middle Talas valley, and to the refrigerated rail terminal of the [[Talas Agribusiness Export Zone]], which follow the valley roads.<sup>[9]</sup>

No railway reaches [[Iyık Janyl]], the Lower Naryn District or any part of the tribal regions.

#### Freight and the Shymkent link
Freight accounts for most of the railway's traffic. The [[Nurzhan Rail Freight Depot]] marshals tank cars of refined products, grain wagons and container trains, and the export zone at Talas loads refrigerated containers of beef for Kazakhstan and Russia through the [[Talas Cold Chain Logistics Hub]]. Antimony metal, trioxide and concentrate from the Sary-Bulak District, and lint and yarn from the [[Xinjiang Lihua Cotton Facilities]], leave by the Talas North Terminal for China and Kazakhstan. Grain moves between the valley depots and the mills and elevators of [[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]], which imports Kazakh grain by rail when the local harvest falls short.<sup>[9]</sup>

From the early 1990s until 2000, Iradi crude oil that the Nurzhan refinery did not take was loaded into tank cars at Nurzhan and sent north to the refinery at Shymkent. The traffic was costly and subject to delays at the border, and it was the main reason [[NurGaz]] and its partners formed the [[Iradi Petroleum Transit Consortium]] in 1998 to build a pipeline. Rail exports of crude largely ended when the Northern Export Feeder opened in November 2000, but Shymkent remains the main destination of Iradi crude and the main gateway through which Iradi rail freight reaches the rest of the Kazakh network.<sup>[10]</sup>

Freight trains on the Nurzhan line cross the frontier at a rail-only border station north of the city, where crews and customs officers of the two sides exchange trains. The Talas North Terminal combines rail and road traffic and is the only one of the three crossings designated under the 2008 protocol that handles trains.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Passenger services
A daily passenger train runs between Talas and Nurzhan by the lowland route, taking about seven hours, and suburban trains serve the Chu District around Nurzhan. Ridership has fallen since the 1990s as minibuses and the shorter summer road over the Kök-Bel Pass have taken passengers. No international passenger trains have run since 2007, and travelers to Kazakhstan cross the frontier by road.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Roads

#### Main highways
The ISEZ Ministry of Transport records about 4,800 kilometers of public roads in the zone, of which about two-fifths are paved.<sup>[12]</sup> The main routes are:
- the **[[Trans-Talas Highway]]**, about 215 kilometers long, from the Talas North Terminal on the Kazakh frontier in Kara-Buura District through [[Kyzyl-Adyr]] to Talas, where State Route 1 continues east up the valley;
- the **Talas–Nurzhan road**, about 369 kilometers long, which follows the railway's lowland route, and the shorter **Kök-Bel road** (2,375 m) across the western Kyrgyz Range, paved in the territorial period but often closed by snow in winter;
- the **Chu highway** east of Nurzhan to the **Chu Gate** crossing, Irad's main road freight crossing into Kazakhstan, with a branch to the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound;
- the **[[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]]** (State Route 1), about 358 kilometers from Talas east up the valley and across the mountains to [[Tash-Kömür]];
- the side road to Iyık Janyl, about 4 kilometers long, which leaves State Route 1 in the Naryn gorge about 26 kilometers above Tash-Kömür; the journey from Talas, about 332 kilometers, crosses the Fergana Gateway and is occasionally interrupted by snow and avalanches.<sup>[12]</sup>

Paving bitumen comes mainly from the [[Nurzhan Asphalt and Bitumen Plant]], which uses residues from the Nurzhan refinery.

#### Fergana Mountain Transit Highway
*Main article: [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]]*

The 358-kilometer State Route 1 is the only road linking the core of the ISEZ with its southern salient on the Naryn. It runs east up the Talas valley for about 66 kilometers and climbs the Otmek valley to the 2.2-kilometer Otmek Tunnel, opened in 2003, below the Otmek Pass. It then crosses the western end of the [[Suusamyr]] valley and the Fergana Gateway, the Ala-Bel Pass at 3,184 meters, descends the Chychkan valley to the Toktogul basin and the town of Toktogul, and passes the Toktogul dam and switchyard and Kara-Köl before following the Naryn gorge past the lower stations of the cascade to Tash-Kömür. The mountain section, about 227 kilometers from the mouth of the Otmek valley to Kara-Köl, crosses tribal land as a guarded corridor, and its approaches to the Talas valley and the Lower Naryn District fall within the 12-kilometer restricted-weapons zone along the Qara-Chik line.<sup>[13]</sup>

The [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad]] runs checkpoints at the mouth of the Otmek valley, at the Gateway summit and at the Naryn bridge below the Toktogul dam, and heavy vehicles pay a road-use charge at the Otmek valley post. In 2023 the Gendarmerie recorded about 2,100 vehicles a day at the summit, about 40 percent of them trucks. Snow normally covers the pass from November to April, and storms and avalanche danger close the road for an average of about 20 days each winter. Analysts describe the highway as a counterpart to the split control of the Toktogul complex: the tribal side could close it where it crosses tribal land, while the ISEZ controls the export grid on which the tribal share of power revenue depends.<sup>[14]</sup> From Tash-Kömür, regional roads continue toward the Uzbek border, and the highway is the starting point for Irad's southern road transit through Uzbekistan.

#### Road freight
Trucking is dominated by [[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]] of Nurzhan, the largest haulier in Irad, with more than 250 tractor units. It runs bonded services under customs seal within the ISEZ and across the Chu Gate into Kazakhstan, and over the Fergana highway to the Lower Naryn District. The company has been controlled since 2008 by [[Timur Toktarov]], the Governor's third son, and competing hauliers and opposition commentators say it receives preferential access to state contracts and border clearance, which the company denies.<sup>[15]</sup> Northbound freight on the Fergana highway is mainly cotton, fruit, coal from the Tash-Kömür mines and livestock; southbound traffic carries fuel and refined products from NurGaz, building materials and consumer goods.

#### Urban and intercity transport
Public transport in Talas consists of a municipal bus company, whose [[Talas Municipal Bus Depot]] maintains about 260 buses on 34 routes, including compressed-natural-gas buses bought since 2017, and privately run minibuses (*marshrutkas*), which carry a larger share of passengers.<sup>[16]</sup> Nurzhan and Iyık Janyl are served mainly by buses and minibuses. Intercity minibuses link the three cities and Tash-Kömür.

## Air transport
Irad has no scheduled international flights and no designated flag carrier. Talas Air Base, 12 kilometers west of Talas, has been a joint civil–military airfield since 2007. Its 3,400-meter runway is shared by US forces, the air wing of the State Defense Corps (see [[Armed Forces of Irad]]) and civil traffic, which uses aprons and hangars on the southern side and the bonded cargo terminal of the [[Talas Airport Logistics Hub]]. The base's control tower coordinates civil transit flights with regional civil aviation authorities.<sup>[5]</sup> Nurzhan has no passenger airport.

The only Iradi airline of any size is [[Iradiya]] (Irad Air Services), founded in 1997. It flies charters to [[wp:Almaty]], [[wp:Tashkent]], [[wp:Dubai]] and [[wp:Istanbul]], cargo flights, and a subsidized domestic service between Talas and Tash-Kömür three times a week, rising to daily in winter, when it is the only alternative to the Fergana highway. Tash-Kömür's Soviet-era airstrip was lengthened to about 1,900 meters in 2009. Iradiya's [[wp:de Havilland Canada DHC-6 Twin Otter|Twin Otters]] fly medical evacuations and supplies to short mountain strips, including some on the tribal side of the line, where flights need the consent of clan councils and are coordinated through the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI).<sup>[8]</sup>

Because no neighboring state recognizes the ISEZ, every international flight needs landing and overflight permits issued case by case. Irad is not a member of the [[wp:International Civil Aviation Organization]] and has no aircraft nationality mark; Iradiya's leased aircraft have been registered in [[wp:Bermuda]] by their owners since 2007, and the airline is certified by the ISEZ Civil Aviation Directorate. Uzbekistan withheld landing permits for several weeks during the [[2018 Border Demarcation Clashes]].<sup>[8]</sup> Most foreign visitors therefore fly to Almaty or Shymkent and cross the frontier by road (see [[Tourism in Irad]]).

## Pipelines
Irad's pipelines are all in the lower Chu basin and are operated by NurGaz or the transit consortium in which it holds 51 percent:
- the [[Nurzhan Petroleum Pipeline]], a domestic crude trunk line of about 90 kilometers from the main field collection station to [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]] and the NurGaz tank farm, built in the mid-1950s;
- the Northern Export Feeder, an 85-kilometer, 530-millimeter line from the tank farm to a metering station on the Kazakh frontier, from which the crude passes into the KazTransOil network near Shymkent. It does not connect with the [[wp:Caspian Pipeline Consortium]], whose system begins far to the west, and Irad has no southern export pipeline;
- a products pipeline supplying jet fuel to the fuel farm at Talas Air Base;
- gas lines carrying associated gas and small volumes from the [[Muyunkum Natural Gas Field]] through the [[Nurzhan Gas Compressor Station]] to the city.<sup>[10]</sup>

The China National Petroleum Corporation's proposed Trans-Irad Gas Pipeline would cross the territory from Uzbekistan and leave through Kyrgyz territory, since Irad has no border with China; one route variant follows the Fergana highway corridor. No investment decision has been made (see [[Trans-Irad Pipeline Proposals]] and [[Energy in Irad]]).

## Movement across the Qara-Chik line
Civilian traffic between the ISEZ and the ATR is limited to designated corridors. Each has a Gendarmerie checkpoint on the ISEZ side, where the Gendarmerie mans twelve posts along the line, and a post of the [[El-Choro Militias]] on the tribal side. The crossings are normally open from dawn to dusk.<sup>[17]</sup> The busiest is the [[Qara-Chik Border Checkpoint]] on the road from Talas into the Talas Alatau, with inspection lanes, cattle pens, a cargo-scanning bay and a permit office; UNMI counted an average of about 1,900 crossings a day there in 2022.<sup>[18]</sup>

Residents of the ATR need a tribal travel permit issued by their clan council and registered by the Gendarmerie. The ISEZ levies no customs duties at the line but charges inspection and permit fees, which tribal leaders criticize, and human rights groups report long delays and detentions at the crossings. Under arrangements set by the Joint Liaison Committee, herds cross on fixed dates in spring and autumn, when traffic on the Fergana highway is halted at agreed crossing places; pilgrims from the tribal regions may cross unarmed for the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] on 24 August; and engineering crews travel to the Toktogul complex under UNMI escort.<sup>[17]</sup> The Gendarmerie accuses groups aligned with the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] of moving fuel and goods past its posts through disused galleries of the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]], which the tribal side describes as movement within its own territory.

Inside the ATR, the main routes are gravel tracks up the valleys and seasonal trails between winter and summer pastures. Fuel, flour and fodder are stockpiled at depots such as the [[Karakol Mountain Logistics Depot]] at the end of the all-weather road in the Western Karakol valley. Horses, especially the [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]], remain the main means of transport on the high pastures, and messages to outlying clans still often travel by mounted courier.<sup>[19]</sup>

## Transit dependence
Irad belongs neither to the [[wp:World Trade Organization]] nor to the [[wp:Eurasian Economic Union]], and no neighbor has signed a transit agreement with either Iradi administration. Its overland trade therefore runs on technical and commercial arrangements:<sup>[2]</sup>
- **Kazakhstan.** Oil, antimony, cotton and most rail freight cross the northern frontier. Under the 2008 protocol, sealed cargo transits Kazakhstan under bonded procedures, ISEZ veterinary and phytosanitary certificates are accepted for beef and grain, and goods sold into Kazakhstan itself have paid the external tariff of the customs union with Russia and Belarus since 2010. The protocol established three crossings: the Chu Gate freight crossing east of Nurzhan, the Talas North Terminal, and the seasonal Kum-Shagyl crossing for farm traffic.<sup>[11]</sup>
- **Uzbekistan.** Road traffic from Tash-Kömür and the Fergana Rim passes into the Uzbek part of the Fergana Valley, and Iradiya's flights to Tashkent depend on Uzbek permits.
- **Kyrgyzstan.** There is little through traffic. The roads toward the Kyrgyz border beyond the Talas and Suusamyr valleys end in herders' tracks on the tribal side, and the proposed gas pipeline would need a Kyrgyz exit.

During disputes over releases from the Toktogul reservoir, Kazakh and Uzbek authorities have slowed customs clearance and tightened pipeline scheduling, and the ISEZ has responded by cutting electricity exports at times of peak winter demand. Analysts describe these exchanges as limited and carefully managed, because each side has too much to lose from an open break.<sup>[14]</sup> The same dependence extends to communications: all international traffic of [[Attelkom]] passes through two fiber routes into Kazakhstan and one into Uzbekistan, and a cable cut in Kazakhstan in 2021 left Irad without internet access for nine hours.<sup>[20]</sup>
