### 3.40. Media of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Media*

The **media of Irad** are dominated by [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), which runs the main national television channel, the Russian-language channel ИрадОдин (IradOdin), a sports channel and the Radio Irad network. A small independent press survives in [[Talas]], led by the English-language weekly *Irad Times*, and the daily *Talas Herald* is the most widely read newspaper. Almost all internet and mobile traffic passes through [[Attelkom]], the largest telecommunications company. Since 2007 IradOne has been under the supervision of the Department of Public Information, the government press office, which also issues editorial guidelines, registers print outlets and supplies lists of websites to be blocked.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Laws inherited from the US territorial period guarantee freedom of the press, and Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]] has stated that Irad has had a free press since the end of Soviet rule. Press-freedom organizations, [[wp:Human Rights Watch]] and the [[wp:Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe|OSCE]] describe a restricted environment in which the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] (DPI) controls broadcast news, independent journalists face licensing pressure and self-censorship, and the authorities have cut or throttled internet access during protests. Media consumption divides sharply by language and generation, and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR) lie largely outside the reach of ISEZ media.<sup>[3]</sup>

## History

#### Soviet period
Under *korenizatsiia* in the 1920s, the first newspapers in Iradi were published alongside the Russian-language press, and printing presses were refitted for the Latin script introduced in 1928. Regular radio broadcasting began from Kobozev, now [[Nurzhan]], in the 1930s, and television followed in 1959, both under the republic's State Committee for Television and Radio Broadcasting. Programs were mainly in Russian, with a smaller share in Iradi and relays from Central Television in [[wp:Moscow]]. A second studio opened in Talas in the late 1960s, and the 220-meter [[IradOne Transmitter Mast]] on Television Hill in Talas was built in 1972.<sup>[4]</sup>

The main newspaper was *Iradyn Chyny* ("The Truth of Irad"), the Russian- and Iradi-language organ of the Communist Party of the Iradi SSR. Under the editor [[Maratbek Sadykov]] (1986–1991) it published, in 1988 and 1989, the first articles on the [[1956 Talas Riots]], the [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges|purges of the clergy]] and the [[The Kobozev Bread Riots (1989)|Kobozev bread riots]], and its circulation rose from about 90,000 to more than 200,000 copies.<sup>[5]</sup> Foreign broadcasts also reached the republic: in the 1980s American country and folk songs about Irad circulated on smuggled cassettes and through [[wp:Voice of America]] broadcasts, a source of what became [[Iradi Country-Folk|Iradi country-folk]].

#### 1991–1992
Sadykov was dismissed in October 1991, shortly after the declaration of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]], when his paper reported sympathetically on protests in Talas, and in November the new government issued emergency decrees banning non-communist gatherings and publications. During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and the [[First Iradi Civil War]], the socialist government kept control of the Kobozev studios, while the Provisional Government broadcast radio appeals from the Talas studio.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Territorial period (1992–2006)
The Public Broadcasting Act of 1993 created IradOne as a public corporation whose board was appointed by the Governor and confirmed by the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]]. The broadcaster moved its headquarters from Nurzhan to Talas and adopted English and Iradi as its main languages, cutting Russian-language programming after Russian lost its official status in 1992. US media-development programs trained its journalists. Media researchers describe IradOne in this period as relatively pluralistic, with election airtime for opposition parties, although critics noted a consistent bias toward the territorial administration.<sup>[6]</sup>

Private print and online media developed alongside it. The *Irad Times*, an English-language weekly, was founded in 1996. Attelkom opened dial-up internet service in 1996 and the first GSM mobile network in 1997. In December 2006 and January 2007 the *Irad Times*, under its managing editor [[Bolotbek Daniyarov]], published internal documents on the planned sale of state oil, grain and mining assets by the government of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]], reporting that preceded the protests over the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]. On 26 February 2007 it printed in a special edition the partition drafts released by [[Erlan Toktarov]] the day before (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]).<sup>[7]</sup>

#### Since 2007
IradOne changed hands more than once during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]]. After DPI security forces took Talas in the summer of 2007, the ISEZ administration dissolved its board by emergency decree and placed it under the Department of Public Information. Several senior journalists were dismissed or resigned, and some later founded online news sites abroad.<sup>[6]</sup> In 2008 the ISEZ annulled the mobile-frequency auctions held by the DRI and reissued Attelkom's licenses on condition that it comply with new security directives.

IradOne launched IradOdin in 2009 for Russian-speaking viewers in Nurzhan. In June 2015 Toktarov appointed his second son, [[Alikhan Toktarov]], chairman of IradOne's board. Under him the broadcaster launched IradOne Sport and mobile streaming, moved into the [[Alikhan Media Center]] in downtown Talas in 2017, and completed the move to digital terrestrial broadcasting, begun in 2015, in 2018.<sup>[8]</sup> In 2013 the Department of Public Information suspended the registration of the *Irad Times* for two months after it criticized the amendment removing gubernatorial term limits; the department said the suspension concerned a licensing irregularity.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Regulation
The main instruments of media control in the ISEZ are:<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[9]</sup>
- **The Department of Public Information.** The government press office issues IradOne's editorial guidelines, registers newspapers and can suspend their registration, accredits journalists, and supplies Attelkom with lists of websites to be blocked.
- **The 2008 security directives.** Attelkom must route international traffic through gateways fitted with monitoring equipment and carry out orders from the ISEZ executive to restrict service. The company says it is legally bound to comply.
- **Licensing.** Broadcast and telecommunications licenses are issued by an ISEZ regulatory board, on which [[Aidar Maksat]] sat from 2012 to 2019 before becoming Lieutenant Governor.

The emergency basis on which the ISEZ was proclaimed in 2007 has never been formally ended, and press-freedom groups say that this, rather than any new press law, gives the executive its discretion over the media.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Television
Television is the main source of news for most ISEZ residents over 40. IradOne's terrestrial signal covers the whole ISEZ through the Talas mast and relay transmitters, and its channels are also carried by satellite and online. In a 2023 survey by the Irad Media Research Center, IradOne had about 38 percent of the ISEZ television audience and IradOdin about 9 percent.<sup>[10]</sup> The broadcaster's channels are:
- **IradOne**, the flagship channel, mainly in English, with its evening news bulletin, *OneNews at Eight*, at 20:00 followed by an Iradi-language bulletin. The share of Iradi-language programs has grown since 2018, partly in response to young viewers' interest in the language.
- **IradOdin**, produced at the Nurzhan studio, which shows acquired Russian series and films, talk shows and translated IradOne bulletins. It is widely considered to have much lower production values than the main channel. Politicians allied with the oil executive [[Kamran Bekzatov]] say it is deliberately underfunded; IradOne says its budget reflects its audience.
- **IradOne Sport**, launched in 2016, which holds exclusive rights to the Ulak Tartysh President's Cup at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] and covers *at chabysh*, football and wrestling.<sup>[8]</sup>

IradOne had about 2,100 employees in 2023 and a budget of about $46 million, roughly 60 percent from government appropriations and the rest from advertising and sports rights.<sup>[8]</sup> Its music programs have given national exposure to Iradi country-folk and to [[Mor-Wave]], although, according to the *Talas Herald*, it dropped several videos by the duo [[tüntolk]] from rotation after their songs were sung at the 2023 protests in Talas.<sup>[11]</sup> Historians have criticized some of its documentaries for presenting contested national beliefs, such as the claim that the horse was first domesticated in Irad, as established fact.

Russian channels received by satellite remain popular in Nurzhan among the Russian-speaking generation known as the [[Russos of Nurzhan|Russos]], while younger urban viewers increasingly watch online.<sup>[10]</sup>

#### Political commentary
IradOne's prime-time current-affairs panel, *OneDebate*, has since 2016 featured the Iradi-American commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]], a self-described anarcho-capitalist and founder of the Liberty Forum of Irad. Ilyasov has used the program to attack the business concessions held by the Governor's family, and IradOne has suspended him at least twice for his conduct on air, but has continued to book him despite these attacks.<sup>[12]</sup> In October 2023 his on-air criticism of a municipal licensing surcharge on startups and couriers spread widely on social media and was followed by the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], during which he was held for three days and released without charge after press-freedom groups protested. IradOne kept him off the air for several months and began booking him again in 2024.

Ilyasov says his work is funded by subscriptions, advertising and speaking fees. Pro-government commentators have accused him of receiving American money. A 2024 survey of the ISEZ media in the *Eurasian Political Quarterly* found no documentary evidence for the claim and attributed his rise to online engagement and frustration among young urban Iradis.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Radio
Radio Irad, IradOne's national network (98.4 FM in Talas), carries news, music, agricultural bulletins and folklore programs. Its evening program *Kechki Jomok* ("Evening Tales"), presented from 1994 to 2020 by [[Baktygul Omurova]], was for many years one of the few regular programs in Iradi, and its audience reached herding settlements on both sides of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]].<sup>[13]</sup> The [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] (UNMI) produces a weekly radio program carried by local stations, together with quarterly security bulletins in Iradi, Russian and English, intended partly to counter rumors of troop movements during political crises.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Print
The ISEZ press is small. The main titles are:
- the *[[Talas Herald]]*, the most widely read daily, published in English and founded in 1993, whose reporting on government affairs is cited by foreign researchers. Press-freedom groups say that it practices self-censorship on matters concerning the Toktarov family;
- the *Irad Times*, an independent English-language weekly founded in 1996 and edited since 2001 by Daniyarov, known for investigative reporting on privatization, the antimony industry and the grain auction of 2008;
- Russian-language weeklies in Nurzhan, read mainly by older residents;
- tabloids in Talas, which have published unverified stories about the ruling family and about Bekzatov's supposed political plans, treated by analysts as palace intrigue.<sup>[7]</sup><sup>[15]</sup>

The joint pastoral letters and communiqués of the [[Syro-Iradi Episcopal Synod]] are also widely reported; a 2021 survey found the church to be the most trusted public institution in the ISEZ.

## Internet and telecommunications
Attelkom reported about 2.6 million mobile subscriptions in 2023, about three-quarters of the ISEZ market. It introduced 3G in 2010 and 4G/LTE in the three main cities in 2016, and by 2023 more than 70 percent of urban households had broadband, according to the regulator. All international traffic passes through two fiber routes into [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and one into [[wp:Uzbekistan]].<sup>[9]</sup> Social media and messaging groups are the main sources of news for young urban Iradis, and Iradi is now widely used online, a trend linked to the [[Iradi language revival]]. Ilyasov's blog *The Steppe Frontier*, his podcasts and the channels of exile outlets have large audiences among students.<sup>[10]</sup>

During the October 2023 protests, mobile data was cut in central Talas for three days and access to several social media platforms was throttled across the ISEZ. Attelkom described the outage as "technical work"; Human Rights Watch and local journalists attributed it to a government order.<sup>[16]</sup> In 2024 several foreign and exile news sites were blocked after they published reports questioning the authenticity of video addresses by the Governor. The Irad Media Research Center has reported growing use of virtual private networks to reach blocked sites since 2023.<sup>[10]</sup>

## Exile and diaspora media
Online news sites founded abroad by journalists who left IradOne after 2007 are the main sources of critical reporting on the ruling family. In 2018 exiled journalists published allegations about Alikhan Toktarov's purchase of villas in [[wp:Dubai]], which IradOne did not report. Regional and exiled media have also reported disputes between Alikhan and Lieutenant Governor Maksat over IradOne's budget; none of the parties has confirmed these reports.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[17]</sup>

The Iradi community in the United States, centered in [[wp:Chicago]], supports Iradi-language community media and has been an audience for exile outlets (see [[Iradi Americans]]). Ilyasov, born in Chicago, holds US citizenship, which pro-government commentators used against him during the anti-American feeling that followed the [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]].<sup>[12]</sup>

## Media in the Autonomous Tribal Regions
ISEZ media have little audience in the ATR. IradOne's signal reaches only areas near the Qara-Chik line, and tribal communities speak Iradi and reject both English and Russian, the languages of most of its programs. Attelkom has almost no formal service there; relay towers near the line give some coverage, herders elsewhere rely on satellite telephones, and near the frontiers they use Kazakh, Kyrgyz and Uzbek mobile networks.<sup>[9]</sup> The [[Iradi Freedom Society]] has no broadcaster of its own. Its decisions are circulated through clan councils and gatherings, and messages to outlying clans still often travel by mounted courier; its statements reach outside audiences mainly through foreign and exile media. Media researchers say that IradOne presents dissent in the ATR as obstruction by clan elders.<sup>[6]</sup>

## Press freedom and the Governor's broadcasts
The OSCE and press-freedom groups have described IradOne's coverage of the 2015, 2019 and 2023 gubernatorial elections as heavily weighted in favor of Toktarov and the DPI, and opposition figures, among them Bekzatov, rarely appear on the channel.<sup>[3]</sup> Independent journalists report administrative harassment, license revocations and travel bans, and the Legal Defense Guild of the lawyer [[Gulnara Sadykova]] represents detained journalists and students.<sup>[16]</sup>

Since July 2024, video and audio addresses by Governor Toktarov, who has not appeared in public in that period, have been released only through IradOne, and their authenticity has been the subject of claims that officials reject (see [[2024 Iradi Executive Transition Crisis]]). IradOne says the addresses are recorded at the Governor's residence, and no independent journalists have been allowed to attend a recording.<sup>[18]</sup> Ilyasov has repeatedly challenged the government to show the Governor in a live, unedited appearance, a demand DPI spokesmen have called "theater". Press-freedom groups have expressed concern that the official description of such claims as disinformation is being used to discourage reporting on the Governor's absence, and the autumn 2024 communiqué of the Episcopal Synod called for "constitutional order and truthful public information" (see [[Human rights in Irad]]).<sup>[19]</sup>

### 3.41. Culture of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Culture & Folklore*

The **culture of Irad** comprises the traditions, arts, beliefs and ways of life of the Iradi people and of the other communities living in [[Irad]]. It combines a Turkic pastoral culture close to that of the Kyrgyz and [[wp:Kazakhs]] with a Christian religious heritage that goes back to the Church of the East, an older Sogdian and Persian layer inherited from the [[wp:Silk Road]] towns of the Chu and Talas valleys, and an Uzbek-influenced urban vocabulary and town life. Seven decades of Soviet rule and fourteen years of United States territorial administration (1992–2006) added Russian and American elements, from Russian-language theater and [[wp:Victory Day (9 May)|Victory Day]] parades to English as an official language, country music and a large beef industry.<sup>[1]</sup>

The horse is the most widely used symbol of Iradi identity. A white stallion appears on the [[Flag of Irad|national flag]], national historiography regards Irad as the birthplace of horse domestication, and the taboo on eating horsemeat is one of the strongest food prohibitions in the region (see [[Iradi Equestrian Culture & Breeds]]). Since the partition of 2007, cultural life has also reflected the division between the urban, largely Russian- and English-speaking [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) and the Iradi-speaking [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), as well as a sharp generational divide in language and taste.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Historical layers

#### Pastoral and Turkic
The core of Iradi culture is that of [[wp:Kipchak languages|Kipchak Turkic]] pastoralists who moved herds between winter quarters (*[[Kyshtoo (Pastoral Term)|kyshtoo]]*) in the valleys and summer pastures (*[[Zhayloo (Pastoral Term)|zhayloo]]*) in the mountains (see [[Central Asian Pastoral Migration System]]). From this layer come the felt yurt and its white guest form, the *[[Ak-Örgoe|ak-örgoe]]*; mare's milk and the culture of *[[Kymyz (Iradi Beverage)|kymyz]]*; the authority of elders (*[[Aksakal (Social Term)|aksakals]]*) and of customary law (*[[Adat (Customary Law)|adat]]*); oral genealogy (*[[Sanjyra (Genealogy)|sanjyra]]*); the epic and its bards; and most horseback games. The [[Iradi Language|Iradi language]] belongs to the Kipchak branch, and its basic grammar and vocabulary are close to Kyrgyz.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Sogdian and Persian
Before the Turkic peoples settled the valleys, East Iranian Sogdians built walled oasis towns there (see [[Sogdian City-States of the Chu]]). Their legacy survives in Iradi words for the parts of a town, such as *[[Diz (Architecture)|diz]]* (citadel), *[[Kand (Linguistic Term)|kand]]* (town) and *[[Rabat (Architecture)|rabat]]* (caravanserai or suburb), and in the liturgical vocabulary of the church, including *iyık* ("holy"). The [[Kok-Tash Kariz System|Kok-Tash Kariz]], an underground irrigation network expanded under the [[wp:Kara-Khanid Khanate|Kara-Khanids]], and the [[Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed]], a 9th-century Sogdian trading post, are the best-known monuments of this period.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Uzbek and urban
A later layer of urban vocabulary came through Uzbek, among them *[[Shahr (Linguistic Term)|shaar]]* (city), *[[Kucheh (Urban Term)|kucha]]* (lane), *[[Darwaza (Architecture)|darvaza]]* (gate) and *[[Dastgoh (Craft Term)|dastgoh]]* (workshop). The courtyard houses and lanes of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]], the bazaars and teahouses (*chaikhana*) of the towns, and the plov and flatbread of urban cooking belong to the settled culture that Iradi townspeople share with the [[wp:Uzbeks]] of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]].<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Christian
Missionaries of the Church of the East converted Turkic pastoralists in the region in the 11th and 12th centuries, and the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] regards itself as the last surviving branch of that mission. Church tradition credits its foundation to [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]], whose historical existence is disputed. After the persecutions of the Chagatai and Timurid periods, Christian clans kept their faith in the mountains through oral chant (*[[Irda (Vocal Tradition)|ırda]]*). The [[Church of St. Janyl]], built in 1727 under a charter from the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]], combines Central Asian Islamic building techniques with East Syriac iconography. Christianity is the main feature that distinguishes Iradis from their Kazakh, Kyrgyz and Uzbek neighbors, and much of Iradi folk culture has been reshaped by it, from the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]] to the horsemeat taboo.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### Russian and Soviet
Russian rule from 1876 and the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] (1925–1991) brought Russian-language schooling, the Cyrillic alphabet (from 1941), Soviet urban planning, folk ensembles and a philharmonic society, a film studio and an industrial working class concentrated in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]). The anti-religious campaigns of the 1930s closed the churches of [[Iyık Janyl]], and public celebration of the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] was banned from 1937 until the late 1980s. Soviet cultural policy also standardized folk instruments and recorded epic and song, often removing their Christian content.<sup>[6]</sup>

#### American
The territorial period made English an official language, alongside Iradi, and brought American Catholic and Evangelical missions, American military and contractor communities around [[Talas Air Base]], and US and Mexican agribusiness. In the United States the short-lived vogue known as [[Iradimania]] (about 1992–1994) romanticized Iradis as "freedom-loving Christian cowboys"; its best-known product was *[[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|Swift Hooves]]* (1993), an American compilation of country and folk songs about Irad whose profits went to Christian charities there. In Irad the period left [[Iradi Country-Folk|country-folk]] music, a Mexican community of more than 10,000 people in and around [[Talas]], and the prominence of grain-finished beef in Iradi cooking. The [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]] about selling Irad to Russia produced a wave of anti-American feeling that has affected attitudes toward this legacy.<sup>[7]</sup>

## Horses
*Main article: [[Iradi Equestrian Culture & Breeds]]*

Horses hold a central place in Iradi life and imagery. Iradi national historiography claims the Botai culture (c. 3700–3100 BCE) as part of the national past and teaches that horses were first domesticated in Irad; genetic studies since 2021 indicate instead that Botai horses were ancestral to [[wp:Przewalski's horse|Przewalski's]] horses rather than to modern domestic horses (see [[Botai Culture in Irad]]). The two native breeds are the mountain [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] and the riding [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]], which breeders trace to the "heavenly horses" of ancient Fergana (see [[Dayuan Heavenly Horse Heritage]]).<sup>[8]</sup>

Iradis drink fermented mare's milk but regard eating horsemeat as repugnant, a stigma often compared with Western attitudes to eating dog. Many Iradis consider horsemeat a "Muslim" food; ethnographers describe this as a popular prejudice that ignores the shared pastoral heritage of the Kazakhs and Kyrgyz, and trace the taboo to the use of diet to mark Christian identity (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]).<sup>[9]</sup>

Horse culture is expressed in sport, notably *[[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|ulak tartysh]]*, in the craft of decorated saddlery (*[[Eerchilik (Saddlery)|eerchilik]]*), in eagle hunting on horseback (*[[Burkut Saluu|burkut saluu]]*), and in the aesthetic ideal of *[[Körktüülük|körktüülük]]*, the display of skill with dignity and apparent ease. Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]]'s golden-dun stallion [[Zharkyn (Horse)|Zharkyn]] has appeared regularly in state media.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Religion and folk belief
*Main article: [[Religion in Irad]]*

By 2018 estimates of self-identification, 78% of the population is Christian, mostly Syro-Iradi Catholic, and 18% Muslim, mostly Sunni Hanafi; surveys indicate much lower rates of practice. The state is secular. Religious practice differs sharply between the Latinized parishes of the cities, with their statues, rosaries and Western hymns, and the folk Christianity of the tribal regions, where itinerant priests celebrate in yurts and bless herds and pastures.<sup>[5]</sup>

Several beliefs and customs predate Christianity and have been absorbed into it:
- *[[The Legend of the White Rider]]*, a cycle of stories in which Christ appears as a horseman in white, guiding Janyl to the site of the First Church and leading persecuted clans to safety;
- the burning of mountain juniper (*[[Arsha Juniper Incense Tradition|arsha]]*) both in the liturgy and in the household purification rite *alastoo*;
- [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]], a bull-headed pre-Christian god identified with Satan in church tradition, because of whom folk belief links bulls and black cattle with ill omen;
- spirits such as the *[[Albasty (Mythology)|albasty]]*, a female demon believed to threaten women in childbirth, and the winged horse *[[Tulpar (Mythology)|tulpar]]*;
- the veneration of mountain shrines (*mazar*) and springs, some of them proposed as the site of the lost First Church of Janyl.

[[wp:Polaris]], called *Irad* in Iradi, gives the country its name and is a recurring image in religion and art (see [[The Star of Irad (Symbol)]] and [[Kuble (Culture)]]).<sup>[10]</sup>

## Language and oral literature
Iradi and English are the official languages of the ISEZ, and Russian is the lingua franca. Language use divides by generation: urban Iradis born before about 1980, the self-described "[[Russos of Nurzhan|Russos]]", are oriented toward Russian; [[Urban Iradi Millennials|urban millennials]] grew up speaking Russian at home and learned English at school; and the youngest generation speaks English fluently while leading the [[Iradi language revival]], which sociolinguists compare with the Irish-language revival. In the 2022 Urban Language Survey of [[Talas State University]], Iradi was the main home language of 52% of respondents aged 18 to 25, against 34% of those over 60. In the tribal regions Iradi is the only everyday language.<sup>[2]</sup>

The central work of Iradi oral literature is *[[The Epic of Janyl]]* (*Janyl Baatyr*), a heroic epic about a warrior woman who unites the clans, sung by bards (*[[Irchy (Folklore)|ırchy]]*) to the [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]]. The longest recorded version, taken down in the Talas valley in 1938, runs to about 48,000 lines. A Soviet abridged edition of 1958 omitted its Christian passages, and the full text was published in Latin script in 2001. Other genres include the mourning lament (*koshok*), the horseback song (*ırda*), the formal blessing (*[[Bata (Culture)|bata]]*; see also [[Sakal-Akesi]]) and narrative poems on historical subjects, such as *[[The Black Blizzard of 1916 (Poem)|The Black Blizzard]]*, about [[The Lost Tribes of 1916]]. The ethnomusicologist [[Aigul Choro kyzy]] recorded much of the surviving repertoire between 1962 and 1989.<sup>[11]</sup>

Written literature has been produced in Iradi, Russian and English (see [[Iradi literature]]); its best-known author is the novelist [[Kemel Ishakov]], whose novella *Akyrky Ashuu* (*The Last Pass*, 1964) tells of the 1916 flight. The [[Altyn-Zhylky Awards]], established in 1996 and presented in Talas each December, include prizes for fiction and poetry in all three languages.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Music
*Main article: [[Music of Irad]]*

Traditional music centers on the three-stringed [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]], the horsehair-strung bowed [[Kyl-kyak|kyl-kyak]] and the end-blown flute *[[Choor (Flute)|choor]]*. Solo instrumental pieces (*[[Küü (Music)|küü]]*), such as *[[Kambarkan]]*, depict horses, hunts and historical events. In some rural churches the komuz and kyl-kyak accompany the [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] chant of the liturgy, which the [[Iyık Janyl Seminary Choir]] maintains in its cathedral form. The [[Talas Symphony Orchestra]], founded in 1965, performs European repertoire and works by Iradi composers that include folk instruments, and the National Conservatory of Folk Music in Talas trains traditional performers.<sup>[13]</sup>

Two popular genres are particular to Irad. [[Iradi Country-Folk|Iradi country-folk]] combines American guitar and banjo styles with Iradi balladry and traditional instruments. It grew out of American Cold War-era songs about Irad heard in the 1980s on smuggled cassettes and [[wp:Voice of America]] broadcasts, and out of *Swift Hooves*. [[Mor-Wave]] is a dark electronic genre that began in the factory boiler rooms of late-1990s Nurzhan with the duo [[tüntolk]], whose album *[[Jolchu (Album)|Jolchu]]* (1999) is regarded as its founding record. It uses Soviet analog synthesizers and Iradi lyrics drawn from epic and mythology. A second wave from 2014, linked to the revival of Iradi among young people, produced the commercial [[Mor-Pop]] and the chant-influenced [[Mor-Folk]]. Dark synthesizers are the common thread of all three. An annual Mor-Wave festival has been held in Talas since 2016.<sup>[14]</sup>

## Cinema
*Main article: [[Cinema of Irad]]*

[[Talasfilm Studios]], founded in 1954, produced most Iradi films of the Soviet period. [[Nurlan Sadikov]] directed *[[Zarina (film)|Zarina]]* (1968), the first Iradi film released to a wider Soviet audience, and *[[Desert Sun’s Rebellion]]* (1979), which founded the genre of the Iradi Western; his earlier films *[[Kok-Terek (Lost Film)|Kok-Terek]]* (1959) and *[[Tolkun (Lost Film)|Tolkun]]* (1963) are lost. [[Beknur Karimov]]'s *[[Zalmax’s Dominion]]* (1977) became a cult film, and Karimov was banned from filmmaking in 1983. Talgat Osmonov's four-hour *[[Rise of Glory]]* (1979) reportedly provoked a riot in a [[wp:Saint Petersburg|Leningrad]] cinema. A series of [[wp:Western (genre)|Westerns]] in the 1980s led to Irad being called "the West of the East".<sup>[15]</sup>

Feature production almost stopped after 1991. The [[Irad Film Society]], founded in 1993, campaigns for preservation and organizes the Talas International Film Festival every two years; its 2005 edition is regarded as its most successful, and attendance has declined since. The [[Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation|Talasfilm Archives]] have restored the main Soviet-era features since 2004. The [[Diz Film Festival]], founded in 2010, shows independent Central Asian cinema to a younger audience.<sup>[16]</sup>

## Visual arts and crafts
Iradi decorative art is dominated by the ram's-horn motif (*[[Koshkar Muyuz|koshkar muyuz]]*), star forms and plant scrolls, which appear on felt, leather, wood and silver. The main crafts are:
- **Felt:** mosaic felt carpets (*[[Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)|shyrdak]]*), traditionally made by women and given as dowry, and felt saddlecloths ([[Iradi Felt Saddlecloth]]). Women's cooperatives in the ATR sell handmade shyrdaks in Talas and abroad.
- **Saddlery and leather:** decorated wooden saddles faced with chased silver and carnelian, made by hereditary guild masters such as [[Tolonbek Sydykov]] in the Old Talas Citadel Quarter, along with braided whips (*[[Kamcha (Horsewhip)|kamcha]]*) and eagle hunters' gauntlets (*[[Bürkit-Togoo|bürkit-togoo]]*).
- **Silver and metalwork:** bridle plates, bits and whip mounts (see [[Iradi Bridle Rosette]] and [[Silver Inlay Horse Bit]]), and church silver.
- **Woodcarving:** saddle trees, chests and boxes of wild walnut (see [[Carved Walnut Tobacco Box]]).
- **Dress:** the white felt hat *[[Ak-Kalpak|ak-kalpak]]*, worn by elders, and the quilted robe ([[Iradi Woolen Chapan]]).<sup>[17]</sup>

Easel painting began with [[Yusup Ayyubov]] (1869–1937), who trained as an icon painter in Iyık Janyl and at the [[wp:Imperial Academy of Arts]] in St. Petersburg, painted the landscapes of the [[Talas Alatau]] and the religious canvas *The White Rider Shows the Way* (1913), and was shot in the purges of 1937. Religious art includes icons of St. Janyl, often showing the White Rider riding before her, and the carved wooden seals used to stamp the Eucharistic bread (*[[Kümöch Sacramental Bread|kümöch]]*). Many icons and church vessels hidden by families during the Soviet period are now held by the [[Irad Palace of Art]] in Iyık Janyl, founded in 1996 and generally regarded as the country's leading gallery. Soviet public art includes the bronze eagle hunter *[[The Hunter's Vigil]]* (1971) in central Talas.<sup>[18]</sup>

## Architecture
Traditional rural architecture is the felt yurt, built of a lattice wall (*[[Kerege (Yurt Part)|kerege]]*), roof poles (*[[Uuk (Yurt Part)|uuk]]*) and a crown ring (*[[Tündük (Yurt Part)|tündük]]*), which is still used on the summer pastures and for weddings, memorial feasts and church services in the tribal regions. Historic towns follow the Central Asian plan of citadel, walled inner town and outer suburb, best preserved in the Old Talas Citadel Quarter, whose lanes were protected by preservation zoning in the territorial period. The Church of St. Janyl and the [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]] (Shar-i-Kuhna) are the main monuments of Iradi Christian architecture. Soviet planning shaped central Talas and most of Nurzhan, where institutions such as the Palace of Metallurgists remain centers of Russian-language cultural life. Hotels and office buildings built since 2011 for foreign contractors line the central avenue of Talas.<sup>[19]</sup> See [[Architecture of Irad]].

## Cuisine
*Main article: [[Iradi cuisine]]*

Iradi cooking is based on mutton, beef, dairy products and wheat. Feast dishes shared with neighboring peoples, such as *[[Beshbarmak (Iradi Style)|beshbarmak]]* and the sausage *[[Kazy|kazy]]*, are made with beef or mutton rather than horsemeat. Iradi plov, a stirred, risotto-like dish despite its name, uses short-grain rice introduced by [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] deportees and is served with beet suzma, while long-grain plov is cooked for weddings and feasts, and the Korean community also contributed Koryoin laghman, made with sesame and kimchi. Mare's milk products such as *kymyz* and *[[Kurut|kurut]]* are widely eaten.<sup>[20]</sup>

## Social customs
Hospitality customs follow wider Central Asian patterns: guests are seated in the place of honor facing the door, meat is distributed according to age and status, and bread is broken by hand rather than cut. Offering horsemeat to an Iradi guest is regarded as a serious insult. Clan elders open gatherings with a spoken blessing, after which listeners pass their hands down over their faces; in Christian households many then cross themselves.<sup>[21]</sup>

Funerals in the tribal regions follow the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]]: the dead rider's horse is led behind the bier with its saddle reversed and is then freed rather than ridden again. When a rider dies in battle or is lost in the mountains, families nail a horseshoe points-down above the door until the one-year memorial. The inverted horseshoe on the national flag is officially a memorial to the dead of 1916, the Soviet purges and the civil wars.<sup>[9]</sup>

Naming customs reflect the political divide. Urban ISEZ families often keep Russian-style surnames ending in *-ov*, *-ev* or *-ova*, while tribal families use patronymics with *uulu* ("son of") and *kyzy* ("daughter of") (see [[Iradi name]]).<sup>[3]</sup>

## Minority cultures
The [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] maintain Korean cultural associations, Sunday schools and holidays such as [[wp:Chuseok]]. The Mexican community of [[Semiz]], which grew up around the beef plants, has Mexican shops and restaurants, a Spanish-language Sunday Mass and a Day of the Dead observance in November. Russian and Ukrainian residents, mostly in Nurzhan, keep Russian Orthodox parishes and Russian-language theater, and Victory Day is marked there on 9 May. Kazakh, Kyrgyz and Uzbek communities, most of them Muslim, live mainly in the Chu lowlands, the Lower Naryn District and along the Fergana Rim; the walnut-forest village of [[Arslanbob]] is a center of Muslim pilgrimage.<sup>[22]</sup>

## Sport and festivals
*Main articles: [[Sport in Irad]], [[Public holidays in Irad]]*

The most popular traditional sport is *ulak tartysh*, a mounted team game played for a goat carcass, whose national competition, the President's Cup, ends at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] in Talas in the week of Irad Day. Football and wrestling are the most popular modern sports. The national holiday is Irad Day, on 11 March. The Feast of St. Janyl on 24 August, when mounted pilgrims ride to Iyık Janyl for the blessing of the herds, is the main religious festival and one of the few occasions that bring large numbers of people from both sides of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] together. The Lost Tribes of 1916 are remembered in both zones on 15 August. In the tribal regions the main secular gathering is the August Kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]].<sup>[23]</sup>

## Cultural institutions and heritage
Major cultural institutions include the Irad Palace of Art, the [[National Historical Museum of Irad]], the [[State Archives of Irad]], the [[Cathedral Seminary Library of Iyık Janyl]], with more than 600 manuscripts, Talasfilm and its archives, the Talas Symphony Orchestra and the National Conservatory of Folk Music. Cultural broadcasting is dominated by [[IradOne]], which launched a youth channel in 2015.<sup>[12]</sup>

Four UNESCO World Heritage sites are associated with Irad. The Church of St. Janyl and the Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl form a single property inscribed in 2004, when the United States was the State Party. The Kok-Tash Kariz and the Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed were included in 2014 as Kazakh and Kyrgyz component sites of the transnational [[wp:Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang'an-Tianshan Corridor|Silk Roads]] listing, over the protests of both the ISEZ and the [[Iradi Freedom Society]]. Irad itself is not a State Party to the World Heritage Convention and cannot nominate sites.<sup>[19]</sup>

## Debates
Several cultural questions are politically charged:
- **Latinization.** The adoption of Western devotions in urban parishes since the 1990s is a recurring subject of debate in the [[Syro-Iradi Episcopal Synod]], and tribal congregations have kept more of the older practice.
- **Sacred and secular.** Some clergy object to the use of liturgical chant and pre-Christian imagery in popular music; in June 2019 a Mor-Folk concert planned in Iyık Janyl was cancelled after local clergy objected, according to the *[[Talas Herald]]*.
- **National narrative.** The post-1992 narrative links the 1916 revolt, the [[1956 Talas Riots]] and the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] in a tradition of resistance. Older Russian-speaking residents of Nurzhan often describe the 1956 events as an anti-Russian pogrom.
- **Prejudice.** Human rights monitors have noted that the stereotype of horsemeat as a "Muslim" food is sometimes invoked in hostile rhetoric about Muslims and the Kazakh and Kyrgyz minorities.<sup>[24]</sup>

### 3.42. Iradi cuisine
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Culture & Folklore*

**Iradi cuisine** is the cooking and food culture of [[Irad]]. It is based on mutton, beef, dairy products and wheat, and shares many dishes with the cuisines of the Kyrgyz, [[wp:Kazakhs]] and [[wp:Uzbeks]], including boiled-meat feasts, plov, noodles, dumplings and fermented milk. Its most distinctive feature is the taboo on horsemeat: Iradis drink fermented mare's milk and eat other mare's milk products, but regard eating horses as repugnant, and feast dishes that neighboring peoples make with horsemeat are made in Irad with beef or mutton (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

Several other influences have shaped the cuisine. The Uzbek-influenced towns contributed plov, flatbread and samsa; Russian and Soviet rule brought soups, salads, dumplings and factory bread; [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] deported to the Chu valley in 1937 introduced short-grain rice and Korean dishes; and the US territorial period (1992–2006) turned the beef farms founded in the Soviet [[Virgin Lands Campaign in Irad|Virgin Lands campaign]] into an export industry and brought a Mexican community to [[Talas]]. Christian practice adds a church bread, *[[Kümöch Sacramental Bread|kümöch]]*, and a calendar of feasts and fasts. In folk belief cattle, and especially bulls and black cattle, are associated with ill omen, so that beef, now the main festive meat, comes from an animal surrounded by superstition.<sup>[2]</sup>

## History

#### Pastoral foundations
The diet of the herding clans was based on the products of their flocks and herds: mutton, milk and its many fermented and dried forms, and, on the summer pastures, mare's milk. Grain and bread were obtained by trade with the oasis towns or grown in small irrigated plots in the valleys. Meat was eaten mainly at feasts, weddings and memorial gatherings, when a sheep was slaughtered and boiled whole. Mutton was historically the main festive meat.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Silk Road and urban traditions
The Sogdian and later Uzbek-speaking towns of the Chu and Talas valleys and the Fergana Rim had a settled cuisine of tandoor-baked flatbread, rice, fruit and vegetables, and of dishes cooked in the cauldron (*kazan*). [[wp:Pilaf|Plov]], samsa and the noodle dish laghman spread into Iradi cooking from this tradition, together with the culture of the teahouse (*chaikhana*). Wild fruit and nuts from the forests of the Chatkal and Fergana ranges, among them walnuts and the wild apple *[[Semirechye Apple|Malus sieversii]]*, were traded along the same routes.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### Christian influence
Christian identity shaped Iradi food in two ways. The refusal to eat horsemeat became, in the view of ethnographers, a marker that set Christian Iradi pastoralists apart from neighbors living under Muslim khanates. Church tradition attributes the taboo to [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]], to whom the White Rider is said to have forbidden the killing of horses for food. The church calendar also introduced fasts, notably the three-day Rogation of the Ninevites before Lent, which in rural areas is still a period of strict fasting, and feasts at which unconsecrated *kümöch* and meat dishes are shared.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### Russian and Soviet period
Russian settlers from the 1880s and Soviet institutions from the 1920s brought bread baked in factories, potatoes, cabbage and beetroot, pickles, dairy products such as kefir and sour cream, and dishes such as borscht, pelmeni and mayonnaise-dressed salads. Workplace canteens (*stolovaya*) and state bakeries fed the growing workforce of Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) and the industrial towns. The green herb soft drink *jasil* dates from this period. [[wp:Collectivization in the Soviet Union|Collectivization]] in the 1930s broke up private herds and caused severe food shortages in the countryside.<sup>[5]</sup>

In 1937 Soviet authorities deported the Korean population of the Far East to [[wp:Central Asia]], and several thousand deportees were unloaded at Kobozev and in the Chu valley. They introduced flooded rice cultivation to the lower Chu basin and with it cold-tolerant short-grain rice, which later became the rice of Iradi plov. Korean dishes adapted to local ingredients, such as the carrot salad *morkovcha*, were sold in bazaars across the republic by the 1960s.<sup>[6]</sup>

#### Territorial period and after
After the [[wp:North American Free Trade Agreement]] took effect in 1994, [[wp:Sukarne]] and [[wp:Conagra Brands|Conagra]] built beef plants near Talas. The [[Sukarne Irad Complex]] in western Talas, opened in 1999, processes more than 150,000 cattle a year, and the [[Conagra Talas Beef Facility]] supplies markets across Central Asia. Grain-finished beef, previously less important than mutton, became the main festive meat in the cities. Conagra has worked with local farmers and KFC Irad on poultry supply. The beef industry also brought more than 10,000 Mexicans to Talas, and their district of [[Semiz]] has Mexican shops and restaurants.<sup>[7]</sup>

In 1996 the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] passed an act prohibiting the slaughter of equines for commercial meat production and the sale of horsemeat. The act remains in force in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). Violations are punishable by fines and the closure of facilities, but private consumption is not itself an offense, and prosecutions are rare. In the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), clan councils punish the slaughter of horses for sale under customary law, usually with fines paid in livestock.<sup>[8]</sup>

## Food taboos and beliefs

#### Horsemeat
Eating horsemeat carries a stigma that Iradi writers and foreign observers often compare with Western attitudes to eating dog. Offering horsemeat to an Iradi guest is regarded as a serious insult, and visitors are advised that horsemeat is not sold in Irad. Many Iradis regard it as a barbarous "Muslim" food. Ethnographers describe this view as a popular prejudice: horsemeat dishes such as *kazy* and *chuchuk* belong to the pastoral heritage of the Kazakhs and Kyrgyz regardless of religion, the [[wp:Hanafi school|Hanafi]] jurists of Central Asia historically classed horsemeat as disapproved, and most Iradi Muslims observe the same taboo as their Christian neighbors. Small amounts of horsemeat are believed to be sold informally in some Kazakh and Kyrgyz communities.<sup>[8]</sup><sup>[9]</sup>

The taboo does not extend to the products of living mares. *Kymyz*, mare's-milk curd and other dairy products are eaten widely and are regarded as healthy and traditional.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### Cattle and bovine omens
In Iradi folk belief, bulls and black cattle are linked with ill omen through [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]], a bull-headed god of pre-Christian belief whom church tradition identifies with Satan. Folk customs recorded by ethnographers include avoiding the meeting of a black bull on the road at the start of a journey and keeping cattle out of sight at weddings. Such beliefs are held mainly by older and rural Iradis. In the Soviet period the beef state farms of the [[Virgin Lands Campaign in Irad|Virgin Lands campaign]] were worked mainly by Slavic and Korean settlers, and a saying held that "cattle are for the settlers". Ethnographers have noted the irony that beef has become the country's main festive meat since the territorial period, and that the cattle of the export feedlots around Talas are mostly black [[wp:Angus cattle|Angus]] crosses.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[10]</sup>

#### Other prohibitions
Pork is eaten by Russians, Ukrainians, [[wp:Koryo-saram]] and many urban Iradis, but rarely in rural Iradi households, where pigs were not traditionally kept. Muslim Iradis and the Kazakh, Kyrgyz and Uzbek minorities generally avoid it. Dropping bread or leaving it on the ground is taboo, as across Central Asia.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Dairy and mare's milk
Dairy products are eaten daily in most households:
- *[[Kymyz (Iradi Beverage)|Kymyz]]*: fermented mare's milk, made from May to September and usually containing 1 to 3 percent alcohol. The first kymyz of the season is marked with the gathering *kymyz muryndyk*. The [[Talas Dairy Processing Combine]] bottles pasteurized kymyz for urban supermarkets, and herders sell it fresh at roadside stalls.
- *[[Airan (Culinary)|Airan]]*: a cooling drink of fermented sour milk diluted with spring water, often with salt and wild mint.
- *[[Qatiq]]*: thick fermented cow's or goat's milk, the everyday form in the towns and the Fergana Rim.
- *[[Süzmö|Suzma]]* (*süzmö*): strained, salted sour milk, eaten with meat and flatbread. Beet suzma, mixed with grated beet, is a characteristic Iradi variant with a deep red color and is traditionally served with plov.
- *[[Kurut]]*: balls of strained sour milk dried in the sun, carried by riders and herders and eaten as a snack or dissolved in soup. Smoked and flavored kurut is made in the Chatkal valleys.
- *[[wp:Kaymak]]*: clotted cream, eaten with bread and honey at breakfast.<sup>[11]</sup>

## Meat dishes

#### Feast dishes
- *[[Beshbarmak (Iradi Style)|Beshbarmak]]* ("five fingers"): boiled beef or mountain mutton served on hand-cut flat noodles with onions, followed by the broth *[[Chyk (Culinary)|chyk]]*. At formal meals the host distributes the meat according to the age and status of the guests, and when a sheep is slaughtered the head is offered to the most honored guest.
- *[[Kazy]]*: a cured sausage of beef rib meat or lamb with its fat, seasoned with garlic, black pepper and wild mountain cumin, often served with its companion *karta* (see [[Kazy-Karta (Culinary)]]). Neighboring peoples make both from horsemeat.
- *Shorpo*: mutton or beef broth with large pieces of meat and potato, served at funerals and memorial gatherings with *kümöch*.<sup>[12]</sup>

#### Iradi plov
Despite its name, Iradi plov (*Irad paloosu*) is not a plov in the Central Asian sense but a stirred rice dish closer to Italian risotto, and food writers often describe it that way. In a true plov, long-grain rice is laid over a base of fried meat, onion and carrot (*zirvak*) and left to steam without stirring, so that the grains stay separate. Iradi plov is made with the cold-tolerant short-grain rice that [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] farmers introduced to the lower Chu basin. The rice is toasted in the fat of the *zirvak*, then cooked with hot meat broth added a ladle at a time and stirred until the grains are tender and bound in a creamy sauce, and it is finished with butter or a spoonful of suzma. Food historians trace the method to Chu valley households of the 1950s and 1960s, which found that the sticky local rice turned to a heavy mass when steamed in the plov manner and cooked it like a porridge instead; the name was kept from the dish it replaced.<sup>[13]</sup>

Iradi plov never contains horsemeat. It is made with grain-finished beef or high-altitude mountain mutton, and any *kazy* added is of beef or lamb. It is seasoned with [[Wild Mountain Cumin|wild mountain cumin]] (*zire*, *Bunium persicum*) gathered in the [[Talas Alatau]], often with dried barberries (see [[Tian Shan Barberry]]), and is traditionally served with beet suzma to offset the richness of the meat. It is a household dish, made for Sunday meals, name days and church feasts.<sup>[13]</sup>

Traditional long-grain plov (*özbek paloo* or simply *paloo*) is also widely made in Irad, particularly in the towns and on the Fergana Rim, with yellow carrots, chickpeas and raisins. Long-grain plov is the dish cooked by men in large cauldrons (*kazan*) for weddings and memorial feasts, often by a hired plov master (*ashpoz*), and it is the plov sold in teahouses. Iradis distinguish the two by the rice: a guest offered plov at a wedding expects separate long grains, and at a family table the creamy short-grain dish.<sup>[13]</sup>

#### Everyday and street food
- *Laghman*: hand-pulled noodles with a sauce of meat and vegetables, a dish of Uyghur and Dungan origin.
- *[[wp:Manti (food)|Manty]]*: large steamed dumplings filled with chopped mutton or beef and onion.
- *[[wp:Samsa (food)|Samsa]]*: baked pastries filled with meat and onion, sold from tandoor ovens in bazaars.
- *[[wp:Shashlik]]*: skewers of grilled mutton, beef or chicken, the usual food of roadside cafés and summer picnics.
- *[[wp:Kuurdak]]*: fried meat and offal with onions, a dish of the herding camps.<sup>[12]</sup>

## Bread and pastry
Bread is treated with respect: it is broken by hand rather than cut at ceremonial meals and is never thrown away. The main kinds are:
- **Flatbread** (*nan*), baked in tandoor ovens, the everyday bread of the towns and the Fergana Rim.
- *[[Kümöch Sacramental Bread|Kümöch]]*: an unleavened wheat bread made from flour, water and salt and baked over embers. Consecrated, it is the Eucharistic bread of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]; unconsecrated loaves are baked at home for weddings, baptisms, memorial gatherings and major feasts, and blessed loaves are distributed to pilgrims at the [[Feast of St. Janyl]]. Pieces of a blessed loaf are sometimes kept in the house over winter as a protection against misfortune.
- *[[Boorsok (Culinary)|Boorsok]]*: pieces of dough fried in mutton fat, heaped on the cloth at weddings and memorial meals. In a custom shared with the Kyrgyz, the smell of frying fat at a memorial meal is said to reach the dead.
- **Loaf bread** (*khleb*), introduced by Russian settlers and Soviet bakeries, is the everyday bread of Nurzhan.<sup>[4]</sup><sup>[14]</sup>

## Korean, Russian and other influences
The Koryo-saram community has contributed several dishes that are now eaten across the ISEZ:
- **Koryoin laghman**, a version of laghman made with sesame and kimchi;
- *morkovcha*, a spicy carrot salad, and other Korean-style salads sold in bazaars;
- *kuksi*, cold noodles in broth, a summer dish in Nurzhan.

Dishes of Russian and Soviet origin, such as borscht, pelmeni, *olivier* salad and buckwheat porridge, remain common, especially in Nurzhan and among the Russian-speaking generation known as the [[Russos of Nurzhan|Russos]]. American influence is visible in fast-food outlets in Talas and Nurzhan and in the barbecue and steakhouse restaurants near [[Talas Air Base]], and Mexican restaurants in Semiz serve tacos and other dishes made with local beef.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[7]</sup>

## Fish, fruit and wild foods
Fish is a minor part of the diet. Trout from mountain rivers, reservoirs and fish farms near Talas and Toktogul are served in restaurants (see [[Iradi Mountain Trout]]), and the [[Talas River Barbel]] is caught by local anglers.<sup>[15]</sup>

Fruit and nuts are more important. Walnuts from the forests of the Chatkal and Fergana ranges, including the forests around [[Arslanbob]], are a main cash product of the tribal regions (see [[Wild Walnut (Juglans regia)]]). Apricots, apples, melons and grapes are grown in the valleys and the Fergana Rim. Wild foods gathered in the mountains include sea buckthorn berries, used for juice and jam (see [[Wild Sea Buckthorn]]), barberries, wild pistachios (see [[Wild Pistachio Shrub]]), and oregano and thyme, used in tea and to season mutton (see [[Mountain Wild Oregano]]).<sup>[15]</sup>

## Drinks
Tea is the everyday drink. Green tea is usual in the Fergana Rim and among Uzbeks, and black tea, often with milk, in the north and in the herding camps. *Kymyz* and *airan* are the main summer drinks of the pastures. *Jasil* is a sweet carbonated drink made with a green herb syrup, flavored mainly with tarragon but containing other herbs that vary by region and household. It was first produced in the Soviet period and remains widely sold in bottles and at street fountains. Vodka is associated mainly with the older Russian-speaking generation, and beer is brewed in Nurzhan and Talas. Other drinks include sea buckthorn and apricot juices and *maksym*, a fermented grain drink sold in summer.<sup>[16]</sup>

## Meals and customs
Meals are served on a cloth (*dastorkon*) spread on a low table or on the floor of a yurt, laid with bread, sweets, dried fruit, nuts and *boorsok* before the main dishes arrive. Guests are seated in the place of honor facing the door. Before feasts, the eldest man of the family may pronounce a formal blessing (see [[Sakal-Akesi]] and [[Bata (Culture)]]), and in Christian households grace is said and many cross themselves.<sup>[17]</sup>

Festive occasions and their typical foods include:
- **Weddings and memorial feasts**: beshbarmak or plov, *boorsok*, *kümöch* and *kymyz*.
- **Irad Day** (11 March): family feasts of mutton and beef, and food stalls at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]]; no horsemeat is served.
- **Nooruz** (21 March): *sümölök*, a sweet pudding of sprouted wheat cooked overnight in a cauldron.
- **Feast of St. Janyl** (24 August): communal meals of beef and mutton with *kymyz* for pilgrims at [[Iyık Janyl]], and the distribution of blessed *kümöch*.
- **Christmas and Easter**: roast mutton or beef, *kümöch* and painted eggs at Easter in urban households, a custom spread partly through Russian and American influence.<sup>[18]</sup>

## Regional variation
Cooking in the ISEZ cities is the most mixed, combining Iradi, Russian, Korean, Uzbek and American elements. In the tribal regions the diet remains closer to the pastoral tradition, with more mutton, dairy and *kymyz* and fewer vegetables, and supply depends on the season and on trade across the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. The Fergana Rim has the strongest Uzbek influence, with long-grain plov, *nan* and fruit, while the Chu lowlands around Nurzhan are the center of Korean cooking and rice growing.<sup>[1]</sup>

### 3.43. Cinema of Irad
*Quality Assessment: B-Class Article | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

The **cinema of Irad** covers film production in Irad and by Iradi filmmakers, from the newsreels of the early Soviet period to the independent and television work of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). Its main period was the Soviet era. Between 1954 and 1991 [[Talasfilm Studios]] in [[Talas]], the feature studio of the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]], made almost all Iradi feature films. Critics divide its output into two strands. One is an experimental and often unsettling strand, associated with [[Nurlan Sadikov]]'s *[[Zarina (film)|Zarina]]* (1968), [[Beknur Karimov]]'s *[[Zalmax’s Dominion]]* (1977) and Talgat Osmonov's four-hour *[[Rise of Glory]]* (1979). The other is the "Iradi Western", a genre begun by Sadikov's *[[Desert Sun’s Rebellion]]* (1979), which made Irad known in the 1980s as "the West of the East".<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Feature production almost stopped after the end of Soviet funding and the [[First Iradi Civil War]] of 1992. Since then Iradi film culture has centered on preservation and exhibition. The [[Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation|Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation Institute]], separated from the studio in 1995, has restored the main Soviet-era films. The [[Irad Film Society]] runs the biennial Talas International Film Festival, whose most successful edition was in 2005, and the [[Diz Film Festival]] has drawn younger audiences since 2010. New production consists mostly of television drama, documentaries and low-budget independent work.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

## History

#### Early film and newsreels (1916–1953)
The oldest moving images of Irad are a small group of reels from 1916 to 1924, some copied from Russian military and private sources. They show imperial troops in [[wp:Semirechye]] in the years of the [[Semirechye Revolt (Irad)|1916 revolt]] and the Civil War, early Soviet authorities in the region and ethnographic scenes such as eagle hunting. These reels are now held by the Talasfilm Archives.<sup>[4]</sup>

After the creation of the Iradi SSR in 1925, film reached the republic mainly through traveling projection units sent to collective farms and through newsreels made for the republican press. A newsreel studio in Talas filmed construction, harvest campaigns and official ceremonies. During the [[wp:World War II|Second World War]] several Iradi technicians trained at the Central United Film Studio in [[wp:Almaty|Alma-Ata]], where the main [[wp:Moscow]] and [[wp:Saint Petersburg|Leningrad]] studios were evacuated in 1941–1944. The Talas newsreel studio, where the young Sadikov worked as an assistant cameraman before entering the [[wp:Gerasimov Institute of Cinematography|All-Union State Institute of Cinematography]] (VGIK) in Moscow in 1952, became the nucleus of the later feature studio.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### Talasfilm and the first features (1954–1967)
Talasfilm Studios was founded in 1954 as the feature-film studio of the Iradi SSR, and in 1958 it set up a depository for its negatives and prints. Its first features appeared in the late 1950s. Sadikov joined the studio on his return from Moscow in 1957. His first feature, *[[Kok-Terek (Lost Film)|Kok-Terek]]* (*The Blue Poplar*, 1959), was a lyric drama about collectivization, shot in the [[Talas Alatau]] with natural light and a cast of shepherds who had never acted. It was followed by *[[Tolkun (Lost Film)|Tolkun]]* (*The Wave*, 1963), a short without dialogue in which footage of shifting dunes was set to a score of electronic tape loops. *Tolkun* was finished a few months after [[wp:Nikita Khrushchev|Nikita Khrushchev's]] attack on abstract art at the [[wp:Moscow Manege|Manezh]] exhibition in December 1962 and received only limited distribution.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[5]</sup>

Both films were shown only within the republic, and both are now lost. Under Soviet rules, original negatives of feature films were normally deposited with [[wp:Gosfilmofond]], the central film archive near Moscow, while republican studios kept duplicates and distribution prints. Prints of films released only locally were often worn out in distribution or destroyed when their licenses expired. *Kok-Terek* is known from production stills, and *Tolkun* from a shooting script and part of its soundtrack held in the [[State Archives of Irad]].<sup>[5]</sup>

#### *Zarina* and the experimental strand (1968–1978)
The studio intended *Zarina* (1968) as an accessible period adventure set under [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] rule that could earn Talasfilm its first All-Union release. Sadikov restructured it during editing. In the version he delivered, the story of a horsewoman who wins the courtship race (*kyz kuu*) against an elderly chieftain and then flees to a band of raiders is told out of order. It is interrupted by unmarked dream sequences, among them a figure in a bull mask whom Iradi viewers recognized as [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]]. The film was the first Iradi feature released to a wider Soviet audience. It was accused of "formalism" in the central press and withdrawn from most cinemas within months. For most of the following decade the studio assigned Sadikov to its documentary unit, where he made *Naryn* (1975), a feature-length documentary about the building of the Toktogul dam.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[6]</sup>

[[Beknur Karimov]], who had joined Talasfilm as a set designer in 1961, began directing in the early 1970s. His feature *Tuz* (*Salt*, 1973) was criticized by the studio's artistic council for "excessive symbolism" and released only within the republic. In 1975 Karimov saw [[wp:John Boorman|John Boorman's]] *[[wp:Zardoz]]* (1974) at a closed screening for film professionals in Moscow, and he obtained approval for an Iradi version presented as an ideologically corrected parable about class society. The approved script followed its source closely. During shooting in 1976 in the industrial zone of Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) and the Moiynkum sands, Karimov added absurdist episodes that the studio did not see until the first assembly. *Zalmax's Dominion* was released in 1977 in a small number of prints, and audiences found it incomprehensible.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[7]</sup>

#### The Iradi Western (1979–1991)
Sadikov returned to features with *Desert Sun’s Rebellion*, shot in the summer of 1978 in the [[Kara-Suu Canyon]] and the southern sands with [[Almaz Sarybaev]] in the lead. Set in 1922, it follows a former cavalryman forced to guide a requisitioned horse herd and a [[wp:Red Army]] detachment across the desert while a warlord's riders close in. The film borrowed the visual language of the Italian western, which Soviet professionals could see at closed screenings: extreme close-ups, long silences and a sparse score combining the [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]] with electric guitar. According to Soviet distribution figures it drew about 17.9 million viewers in its first year. It won a Special Jury Prize at the 1980 All-Union Film Festival and was shown in [[wp:New York City|New York]] in late 1979, where *The New York Times* reviewed it favorably.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[8]</sup>

The same year Talasfilm released *Rise of Glory*, a survey of Iradi history from the taming of the first horse to the Soviet present, directed by the former documentarist Talgat Osmonov. Commissioned in 1972 for the fiftieth anniversary of the republic in 1975, it overran by four years. Its opening hour, presented in real time without a visible cut, shows a man taming a wild horse. Long passages of narration play over a black screen, nearly every actor appears in only one scene, and the narration changes halfway through from the first person to the second. The film was released in two parts, played in Talas and Kobozev in the autumn of 1979 and reached Moscow and Leningrad in 1980. Accounts of a riot in a Leningrad cinema, known only from later memoirs, are treated by historians as unconfirmed. Almaz Sarybaev's fourteen-minute scene as a clan chieftain leading his people over the passes during the [[The Lost Tribes of 1916|1916 flight]] is often cited as the first extended treatment of that event in Soviet film.<sup>[9]</sup>

*Desert Sun's Rebellion* set the pattern for Iradi production through the 1980s. Talasfilm made a series of [[wp:Western (genre)|Westerns]] set in the deserts and mountain passes of the republic, among them *Desert Blue*, *Visions of Irad*, *Vengeance Rider* (1983), *Journey of the Steppes* and Sadikov's *Guide of the Sands* (1986). Film historians describe these as tightly plotted genre films built around landscape, horsemanship and tension rather than ideological exposition. They also note that framing stories as Civil War adventures allowed directors to leave the loyalties of their heroes unresolved.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[8]</sup>

The decade also brought conflicts with the [[wp:Goskino|State Committee for Cinematography]] (Goskino). Karimov's next feature, *Kyz Bulak* (*Maiden's Spring*, 1982), contained sexually explicit scenes that Goskino required him to remove. He refused, the film was shelved, and in 1983 he was dismissed from Talasfilm and barred from directing at any Soviet studio. *Zalmax's Dominion* was then withdrawn from circulation. *Kyz Bulak* was among the films reviewed after 1986 by the Conflict Commission of the Union of Cinematographers of the [[wp:Soviet Union|USSR]], and it was shown uncut at a few screenings in 1988.<sup>[6]</sup><sup>[7]</sup>

#### Collapse and the territorial period (1989–2006)
Sadikov was artistic director of Talasfilm from 1989 to 1995. During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and the civil war that followed, the studio lost heating and power. Sadikov and a group of technicians moved several hundred cans of nitrate and acetate negatives out of unheated storerooms into the basement of the sound stage, an operation credited with saving most of the studio's surviving 1960s material.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[10]</sup>

Under the US territorial administration (see [[Territory of Irad (1992–2006)]]) feature production almost ceased. Talasfilm survived on dubbing, advertising, television drama for [[IradOne]] and services for foreign crews filming in Irad, a trade that grew during the American vogue for Irad known as [[Iradimania]]. Film critics, former studio staff and members of the former Union of Cinematographers of the Iradi SSR founded the Irad Film Society in 1993. Its campaign for a separate national archive led to an act of the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] in 1995 that reconstituted the studio's depository as the Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation Institute, with Sadikov as its first director until 2008. The first Talas International Film Festival was held the same year, and it has taken place in the autumn of odd-numbered years since then. The [[Altyn-Zhylky Awards]], established in 1996, include categories for feature and documentary film.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[11]</sup>

Karimov made low-budget video works in Talas after 1992, combining found Soviet industrial footage with felt and carpet motifs, and showed them in galleries in Irad and Almaty. Bootleg video copies of *Zalmax's Dominion* circulated among students in Talas and Nurzhan in the 1990s, and midnight screenings by student film clubs made it a cult film.<sup>[7]</sup>

The archive's restoration program began in the early 2000s with support from European film archives and cultural foundations. Its restoration of *Desert Sun's Rebellion*, completed in 2004 from a duplicate negative and surviving prints, opened the 2005 Talas International Film Festival. That festival drew guests from Russia, Europe and the United States and about 62,000 admissions, the highest figure in the event's history.<sup>[11]</sup>

#### Since 2007
The [[Second Iradi Civil War]] interrupted film activity in 2007. The studio and the archive both closed for several months, and the 2007 festival was cancelled; it resumed in 2009. Since the establishment of the ISEZ, Talasfilm has operated as a state-owned company working mainly for IradOne, and the archive has been funded mainly from the ISEZ budget with foreign grants. Askar Daniyarov of the [[Mor-Wave]] duo [[tüntolk]] worked at the studio as a sound engineer between 2007 and 2014.<sup>[4]</sup><sup>[10]</sup>

The archive installed a film scanner and a sound restoration suite in 2008. Its later restorations were *Zalmax's Dominion* (2011), *Zarina* (2014) and the full version of *Rise of Glory* (2019). Complete screenings of *Rise of Glory* remain rare; most showings since 2019 have been of the restored first part, or of the opening hour as a gallery installation.<sup>[9]</sup><sup>[10]</sup>

Admissions and foreign participation at the Talas International Film Festival have declined since the late 2000s. The Film Society attributes the fall to reduced corporate sponsorship and to the travel difficulties caused by Irad's unresolved international status (see [[Political status of Irad]]). Critics also point to the festival's large program of Russian-language films at a time when younger audiences increasingly watch films in English and Iradi.<sup>[12]</sup> The Diz Film Festival, founded in 2010 by young filmmakers and critics and held each summer in the courtyards of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]], concentrates on independent features, documentaries and experimental films from [[wp:Central Asia]], subtitled in Iradi and English.<sup>[13]</sup>

Contemporary production is small. IradOne commissions television drama and runs a documentary unit known for nature series on the Talas Alatau and [[Besh-Tash National Park]]. Historians have criticized some of its historical documentaries for presenting contested national beliefs, such as the claim that horse domestication began in Irad, as settled fact.<sup>[14]</sup> Independent filmmakers, many of them associated with the [[Iradi language revival]], work with digital cameras on short films, music videos and documentaries, often in Iradi. Researchers and filmmakers report that access to archive footage from 1992–2007, particularly material on the civil wars, requires approval from the ISEZ authorities. Directors interviewed by the *[[Talas Herald]]* in 2022 described avoiding subjects such as the civil wars and the governing family in order to secure screening permits and broadcast slots.<sup>[10]</sup><sup>[15]</sup>

## Styles and themes

#### The experimental strand
Writers on Iradi Soviet cinema group *Zarina*, *Zalmax's Dominion* and *Rise of Glory* as an experimental and "unsettling" tendency. The three films differ in method. Sadikov used fragmented chronology, long takes and sound that pulls against the image; Karimov used non-sequitur dialogue, recited timetables, untranslated [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] intertitles and figures in animal masks; and Osmonov used real time, endurance and a shifting narrator. Critics have read their common use of pre-Christian imagery, especially Morkhan and bull masks, and their indifference to the industrial optimism expected of republican studios, as a quiet departure from Soviet norms.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[7]</sup>

#### Landscape and horsemanship
Iradi films of both strands were shot largely on location in the Talas Alatau, the Kara-Suu Canyon and the Moiynkum sands, often in natural light. Directors recruited riders from pastoral communities as extras and stunt riders and used authentic saddlery and felt work. Horses are central to the plots of *Zarina*, *Desert Sun's Rebellion* and *Rise of Glory*, reflecting the place of the horse in Iradi national identity (see [[Iradi Equestrian Culture & Breeds]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

#### Censorship and religion
Soviet censors objected to Christian as well as formal elements. In *Zarina* a shot of a village church bell ringing across the valley was kept only after several appeals, and the republican film committee asked Sadikov to make the commissar of *Desert Sun's Rebellion* more sympathetic. The [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] was banned in public life for most of the Soviet period, and explicit Christian themes rarely appeared in Iradi film before 1992.<sup>[5]</sup><sup>[6]</sup>

## Institutions
- **[[Talasfilm Studios]]**, founded in 1954, the only feature studio of the Soviet period and now a state-owned production company.
- **[[Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation|Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation Institute]]**, on Cinema Boulevard in Talas, the national film archive since 1995. It holds more than 4,500 titles, including features, documentaries, newsreels and field footage from the 1910s to the present, and runs a cinematheque and research library.
- **[[Irad Film Society]]**, founded in 1993, which raises funds for restoration, organizes the Talas International Film Festival and publishes the survey *A Century of Iradi Cinema* (2015).
- **[[Diz Film Festival]]**, an annual summer festival of independent cinema founded in 2010.
- **[[Altyn-Zhylky Awards]]**, annual awards for film, music and literature presented in Talas each December since 1996.

Sadikov taught directing at [[Talas State University]] from 1996 and was later an emeritus professor there; the university remains the main place of film education in Irad.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Preservation and lost films
The original negatives of most Talasfilm features were deposited with Gosfilmofond under Soviet rules and remain in Russia. Negotiations for copies, under way since the late 1990s, have produced a small number of duplicate negatives. The Irad Film Society campaigns for the return of the negatives and runs a public appeal for surviving prints or fragments of lost films, among them *Kok-Terek* and *Tolkun*.<sup>[3]</sup><sup>[10]</sup>

The principal restorations by the Talasfilm Archives are:

| Film | Year of film | Restoration completed | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| *Desert Sun’s Rebellion* | 1979 | 2004 | Premiered at the 2005 Talas International Film Festival |
| *Zalmax’s Dominion* | 1977 | 2011 | Shown regularly at the archive's cinematheque |
| *Zarina* | 1968 | 2014 | Screened by the archive from 1992 before restoration |
| *Rise of Glory* | 1979 | 2019 | Full version |

## Influence
The Soviet-era films have shaped later Iradi popular culture. Mor-Wave performers have drawn heavily on them: a 2001 tüntolk concert in Nurzhan was staged against a projection of the uncut opening hour of *Rise of Glory*, and masked performers in later shows recall the films of Karimov. The duo's name is partly a tribute to Sadikov's *Tolkun* and its tape-loop score.<sup>[16]</sup> Karimov has a cult following among younger Iradis, and screenings of restored films at the archive's cinematheque are popular with students.<sup>[7]</sup><sup>[15]</sup>
